# Michel Henry, *Généalogie de la psychanalyse. Le commencement perdu* — Commentary

References are to the PUF/Épiméthée edition (1985; 2nd ed. 2003), cited by page. Freud is cited as Henry cites him, by French translation and *Gesammelte Werke* (GW, volume, page). The book grew out of seminars given at Osaka University in October–December 1983 and lectures at Osaka, Tokyo and Kyoto; it is dedicated to Yorihiro Yamagata.

Four misreadings are guarded against throughout. First, Henry's "unconscious" is not a hidden content waiting to be made conscious: the life Freud calls unconscious is, for Henry, revealed in itself, only not in the mode of representation. "Invisible" therefore never means absent, latent or dark in itself. Second, the *cogito* Henry recovers is not thought, reflection or self-representation; it is the affective self-givenness of life, and its certainty does not depend on the truth of what is seen. Third, Henry does not oppose affect to Freud in favour of consciousness; he sides with Freud's affect against Freud's representational and energetic metaphysics. Fourth, "genealogy" is not a history of ideas: the "lost beginning" is not an origin in time but the permanent ground, life's self-revelation, that each epoch's thought passes over.

## A Late Heir

Henry opens with a methodological claim. When a form of thought ages, its prospects tell us less than the way it came to maturity. A genealogy of psychoanalysis will say more about it than its present successes or failures. Psychoanalysis, with its therapeutic efficacy increasingly disputed, already wears "l'habit gris de l'idéologie" (the grey garb of ideology, p. 5). Philosophy must explain this decline because psychoanalysis presented itself as a total revolution in our understanding of the innermost being of man, the Psychè, and so as an overturning of traditional philosophy. The diagnosis of decline is not empirical polemic. It sets up the book's thesis: what fails in psychoanalysis is what it inherited, not what it invented.

He then denies psychoanalysis the status of a beginning. Its decline is not its own fault; it is "non pas un commencement mais un terme" (not a beginning but an end, p. 5), the end of Western thought's incapacity to grasp "ce qui seul importe" (what alone matters). Freud is "un héritier, et un héritier tardif" (an heir, and a late heir). What must be discarded is therefore not Freud but the presuppositions of classical philosophy that he took up unknowingly and carried to their final consequences. Henry disclaims any interest in mere disagreement with a doctrine. His target is the "fond impensé" (unthought ground, p. 6) from which the doctrine springs and which will govern whatever comes after Freud unless it is exposed. The subtitle's "lost beginning" is announced here negatively: Freud stands at the end of a history that began by losing something.

Henry answers the question of when the unconscious entered modern thought: at the same moment as consciousness, and as its "exacte conséquence" (exact consequence, p. 6). Descartes gave consciousness its modern sense. Before him "conscience" meant moral conscience, a way of judging man's dignity and rank among beings. With Descartes it takes on a radical ontological meaning: it names appearing as such, "non pas quelque chose mais le principe de toute chose" (not something but the principle of everything), the original manifestation through which whatever can exist becomes a phenomenon for us. The claim that the unconscious is born with consciousness is the hinge of the whole book. It means that the unconscious is not a discovery about a region of the mind but the shadow cast by a particular decision about what manifestation is.

Descartes posed this concept at such depth that its original reach was never preserved, not by Husserlian phenomenology, which claimed to bring it to completion, and not even by Descartes himself. Henry points to the repeated but neglected fact that the *cogito* is achieved only with the *epochē* of the world: the suspension not merely of beings but of the world's phenomenality as such, the ecstatic dimension (the "Dimensional extatique") from which thought has borrowed its possibility since the Greeks. What Descartes sought is thus reached neither as the opening of an Open granted by an original Nature (a glance at Heidegger's *physis*), nor as Platonic *idea*, nor as perception, nor as modern representation, but by rejecting all of these as "le tout autre de l'ek-stasis" (the wholly other of ecstasis, p. 7). Hence the provocation: "Je pense chez Descartes veut tout dire sauf la pensée. Je pense veut dire la vie" (In Descartes "I think" means anything but thought; it means life). Henry calls this what the Second Meditation calls the soul. The thesis anticipates Chapter I, where the *videre videor* bears the full weight of the claim, and it repeats in compressed form the argument of *L'essence de la manifestation* that immanence is a mode of phenomenality distinct from, and prior to, transcendence.

Descartes' scientific ambitions, or rather his legitimate philosophical claim to ground science by securing its conditions and giving it a "certain" foundation, were superimposed on this original project, diverted it, and finally consigned it to oblivion. The attempt at a radical phenomenology able to discern, beneath the phenomenality of the visible, a deeper dimension in which life reaches itself before the world arises, "tourna court" (came to nothing, p. 7). Consciousness entered philosophy doubled: it named both the visible and the invisible, the older revelation reached only in the *epochē* of the world. This doubling was simply lost. Henry calls the loss a "déviance historiale" (historial deviation), deliberately borrowing Heidegger's vocabulary of the history of being while turning it against him: the path toward the Beginning was abandoned and the philosophy of consciousness headed toward the world, toward a transcendental theory of knowledge and science, and so toward technical mastery. Kant is the culmination. Where the philosophy of consciousness becomes an ontology of representation, defined as the relation of a subject to an object in general, the critique of the Cartesian soul becomes systematic and bars modern man from his innermost being. The paragraph lays out the sequence of Chapters II–IV in advance: Cartesian ambiguity, Husserlian and Heideggerian reinterpretation, and the Kantian paralogisms.

Freud enters at exactly this point. He borrows his concept of consciousness, by his own account, from the philosophers and from common sense: what he calls conscious is "la conscience même des philosophes et celle du grand public" (the very consciousness of the philosophers and of the public at large, p. 7, citing the *Abriss*, GW XVII, 81). His *Bewußtheit* explicitly designates representational consciousness, as Chapter IX will show. Once consciousness has been defined as representation, the claim that the ground of the psyche escapes it acquires an "immense" ontological significance. It asserts that the original essence of being withdraws from the medium of visibility in which philosophy and science have sought it since Greece. Henry's formula follows: in a philosophy that reduces phenomenality to the transcendence of a world, "l'inconscient est le nom de la vie" (the unconscious is the name of life, p. 8). The formula is the book's central thesis, and it cuts both ways. Freud is right that the ground of the psyche is not representable; he is wrong to conclude that it does not appear.

Freud is therefore philosophically an epigone. Schopenhauer had already ended the reign of the metaphysics of representation by declaring that representation exhibits nothing that could count as real being, and he added a second, equally decisive thesis: the being that never comes forth as object for a subject, and that defines reality precisely through its refusal of "ob-stance" (standing-over-against), is life. Henry's hyphenations (*ob-jet*, *ob-stance*) mark the root sense of the object as what is thrown or stands in front, the structure of exteriority that life eludes. The interpretation of being as Life is called "l'événement crucial de la culture moderne" (the crucial event of modern culture, p. 8), the moment culture returns to the Beginning. Chapter V develops this.

Schopenhauer's invention repeats Descartes' and makes possible a philosophy of the fundamental determinations of existence: body, action, affectivity, the ground on which thought always builds. A new set of themes appears with him: bodily life, instinct, sexuality, love, shame, cruelty, and even the concrete problems Politzer called "drames", such as the choice lovers make of each other. The reference to Politzer is pointed. Politzer had attacked Freud's metapsychology in the name of the concrete "drama" of the person; Henry locates the origin of that concreteness in Schopenhauer rather than in Freud, and makes it depend on the ontology of life rather than on a psychology of the first person.

The assertion that the wholly other of representation alone defines true being escapes speculation, Henry argues, only if this other reveals itself in itself, in its own domain. Otherwise the unrepresentable is just the Kantian noumenon, a speculative entity that German idealism will try to reabsorb into thought, and so into representation, losing it again. Only a radical phenomenology that grasps the essence of life as the essence of original appearing can hold life where it is, "en nous, comme cela même que nous sommes" (in us, as the very thing we are, p. 9), and free it from the fantasies of a world-behind-the-world. The criterion is decisive for everything that follows: a non-representational reality must still be phenomenological, or it lapses into metaphysics.

Schopenhauer lacked the means to build this phenomenology. He showed, brilliantly, that the other of representation can never be perceived in it, and he located its accomplishment in a primitive bodily life that also identifies us with it. But he inherited Kant's theory of inner sense, which reduces absolute subjectivity to the ecstasis of time and so to a representation. That prevented him from giving phenomenological meaning to the immanence that defines the Will. The Will falls back under the yoke of Western thought, whose fate is either to appear in the light of temporal ecstasis or to sink into night: "ou la représentation, ou l'inconscient" (either representation, or the unconscious, p. 9). Henry concludes: "La vie est perdue au moment même où elle est nommée, et Freud est déjà là tout entier" (Life is lost at the very moment it is named, and Freud is already there in full). The disjunction representation-or-unconscious is the trap that the whole book dismantles. The critique of Kant's inner sense (Chapter IV) is what binds Schopenhauer, and therefore Freud, to it.

With Nietzsche the alternative breaks for an instant. Life is restored to appearing as its own essence, on one condition: that appearing be recognized in its non-ecstatic dimension, as its "initial et éternel parvenir en soi" (initial and eternal coming into itself, p. 9), which Henry identifies with the Eternal Return of the Same and with life. Nothing of the splendour of the world, of Apollonian appearance, is denied. Rather, the phenomenon is seen against the ground of its originating possibility, the "screen" of the original Night that engenders it, Dionysus. Dionysus is then taken for what he is: not an unconscious "qui n'est rien du tout" (which is nothing at all, p. 10) but his own pathos, being's endless self-experience in suffering and joy. Henry's reading of the Eternal Return as the immanent self-arrival of life, rather than as a cosmological doctrine or a test of affirmation, is the interpretive bet of Chapter VII.

On this basis an ontology emerges in which affectivity is the revelation of being in itself, "la matière dont il est fait, ... sa substance et sa chair" (the stuff it is made of, its substance and its flesh, p. 10). This ontology deciphers Nietzsche's figures of life, the strong, the noble, the animals, all who have entrusted their destiny to a primal suffering. It also clarifies a shift Schopenhauer glimpsed but did not conceptualize: all representational faculties (seeing, memory, thought) now have their principle in a power that is no longer intentional consciousness, namely life. The phrase "sa chair" anticipates the late *Incarnation*; here it marks that affectivity is not a quality of being but what being consists of.

A philosophical reading of Freud now becomes possible. In its essential analyses and in therapy, psychoanalysis demands that representational thought (perceptions, images, memories, dreams, symbolic, aesthetic and religious productions) be subordinated to a power of another order; it implicitly rejects a metaphysics of representation. Henry then turns Freud's repressing and operative instance into life itself, "l'affect consubstantiel à cette phénoménalité ... qui n'est jamais inconscient" (the affect consubstantial with this phenomenality, which is never unconscious, p. 10). The conclusion, announced "sans ironie": placed at the heart of the unconscious, affect makes the unconscious phenomenological in its essence. The appeal is to Freud's own doctrine that affects, strictly speaking, cannot be unconscious (*Das Unbewußte*, § III), which Henry will exploit in Chapter IX.

Henry anticipates the objection. In Freud the affect is not the final ground of the system; it is only the psychical representative of the drive, and the drive in turn represents in the psyche the energetic determinants of the 1895 *Project*. One would thus be led back to natural processes. The objection describes an unacknowledged return of the metaphysics of representation. The Psychè whose specificity Freud had defended becomes an ersatz of a biological, even physico-chemical, essence, and the scientific schema of explanation regains its rights, dismissing phenomenology once more. Henry's sarcastic summary: "La vie que nous vivons n'est qu'un effet de ce que nous ignorons" (the life we live is only an effect of what we do not know, p. 11), and science must deliver us from the illusion we are, the "éternelle pensée de l'Occident" (eternal thought of the West).

The reply: the world-behind-life invoked to explain life borrows all its features from life. The "excitation" invested in the double neuronal system of the *Project* is just the name for affection, that is, for phenomenality. Exogenous excitation is the transcendental affection of "living tissue" by the world; endogenous excitation is self-excitation, the auto-affection that constitutes the essence of absolute subjectivity as Life. Referring affectivity to the somatic ground of the drive therefore refers it only to itself. Henry draws a striking consequence. If the principle of inertia turns into the principle of constancy, if the system can never entirely discharge its quantities of energy, this is because life, as self-impression, "ne peut justement se débarrasser de soi" (precisely cannot rid itself of itself, p. 11). The metapsychological principle becomes a transcription of an ontological structure, the passivity of life toward itself analysed in *L'essence de la manifestation*, §§ 52–70.

The entropic schema therefore yields to life's unceasing coming into itself. Discharge of affects and the unbearable pressure of unused libido designate only the subjectivity of life when its self-experience reaches its height and becomes unbearable. Anxiety, which Freud called the common currency of all affects, is the anxiety of life at being unable to escape itself. Freudianism, both in its transcendent constructions and in its best phenomenological texts, harbours what our age has most lacked; that, despite its contradictions and absurdities, explains its "étrange succès" (strange success, p. 12). The analysis of anxiety links Freud to Kierkegaard's *Concept of Anxiety* and to the "suffering of oneself" developed in *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 70.

Hence psychoanalysis does not belong among the human sciences to which it is now attached; it is "bien plutôt l'antithèse" (rather their antithesis, p. 12). When objectivity extends its reign of death over a devastated universe, life's only refuge is the Freudian unconscious, and under every pseudo-scientific attribute of that unconscious a living determination of life hides and acts. Henry turns Marx's formula on religion: psychoanalysis is "l'âme d'un monde sans âme ... l'esprit d'un monde sans esprit" (the soul of a soulless world, the spirit of a spiritless world). The borrowing is apt. In Marx, religion is both an expression of real distress and a protest against it; for Henry, psychoanalysis is both the disguise of life under objectivism and life's protest against objectivism. The theme of a barbarism produced by objectivity returns in *La barbarie* (1987).

Life tolerates only briefly the mask least suited to it, that of a world-behind-the-world. It cannot long have its law outside itself, since it is its own law and constantly undergoes itself as what it is, as being's pathos and suffering. The time for it to cast off this mask is coming, perhaps already here. The prediction answers the opening diagnosis of decline: psychoanalysis fades not because life has ceased to matter but because life rejects the objectivist form in which psychoanalysis expressed it.

Henry now specifies what kind of history the book tells. Freudianism is often presented as an empirical history of the individual: what happens to him results from what happened before, his childhood, his relation to the Father and the Mother, the trauma of birth. Every explanation of this kind, and history in general, is naive because it moves a problem back into the past where it reappears intact. "Expliquer l'amour d'un adulte par celui qu'il avait pour sa mère, c'est expliquer l'amour par l'amour" (to explain an adult's love by the love he had for his mother is to explain love by love, p. 12). The Father makes the idea of God intelligible only to someone who has not seen that both figures represent one ontological structure: life constantly experiencing itself as that of which it is never the ground. Birth's distress accounts for anxiety only in a being already constituted as affective. Henry's objection is transcendental, not empirical: genetic explanation presupposes the very capacity it claims to produce. The structure "experiencing oneself without being one's own foundation" is the passivity of auto-affection, and it governs the later interpretation of the Freudian Father and of religion in Chapter VIII.

It would be equally naive to take this genealogy for a history of doctrines whose predictable outcome would be psychoanalysis. Henry grants the historical facts: when Freud came to Paris a psychology of the unconscious, presented chiefly as the condition of memory, filled the philosophy textbooks; the concept of the unconscious shared by Bergson and Freud was taught in schools before they "discovered" it. But establishing these ideological sequences explains nothing about why the unconscious is affirmed as the innermost and deepest being of man. That this affirmation recurs among Descartes' contemporaries as an objection to the eidetic definition of the psyche as pure phenomenality, in Leibniz, Schopenhauer, Hartmann, Bergson, Freud, or in Rabier's textbook, concerns only the history of ideas. The point is that a constant recurrence calls for a constant cause, which no history of influences can supply.

Even history should have prompted reflection: the repeated formulation of a psychical unconscious cannot be an occasional discovery or a one-off invention. If the term refers to what is deepest in us, and so to being itself, perhaps being produces this designation and does not stop producing it. Henry's answer, framed as a question, carries the book's genealogical principle: life itself, in its "invincible retrait du monde" (invincible withdrawal from the world, p. 14), escapes the phenomenality of ecstasis in which all thought moves and so leads thought astray, until thought declares that whatever does not show itself to it as object or "vis-à-vis" is only the Unconscious, "le privé en soi du pouvoir de la manifestation" (what is in itself deprived of the power of manifestation). The cause of the concept of the unconscious is the very thing the concept misdescribes.

"Généalogie n'est certes pas archéologie" (genealogy is certainly not archaeology, p. 14). The barb targets Foucault, whose *Les mots et les choses* described "epistemic structures" and the ideological horizons governing modern thought. The historial deviations through which the unconscious enters our world cannot be simply observed or described in that way, since they proceed ultimately from "le vouloir de la vie de demeurer en soi" (life's will to remain in itself). Life leaves the field free for the world's appearing while secretly founding it, and so says itself to thought, which can never bring it into view, as the unconscious. Henry extends this to the whole of Freud's speculative apparatus: the neurological imagery of 1895, the hypotheses stacked to infinity, the picturesque agencies and their sometimes burlesque games are not as absurd as they seem. Freudian mythology has the seriousness of every mythology, since all of them rise from the same secret ground that we are; that is why one believes it easily, recognizing oneself in it. Genealogy, for Henry, explains the history of a concept by the essence of what it fails to grasp, not by the conditions of discourse.

More deliberately than other thought, Freud's has contested the rights of objectivity, and in it scientific categories burst under the weight of original phenomenological determinations. Freudianism can therefore be called "une sorte d'ontologie" (a kind of ontology, p. 14): its discourse on the unconscious depends on the fundamental structures of being and exposes them in its own way. This discourse does not merely repeat classical philosophy unknowingly (the unconsciousness of pure or "transcendental" consciousness, the reversal of the philosophy of consciousness into a philosophy of nature) and with it the great deficiencies of Western thought. It goes further, to the unthought of that thought, the place where life's "inlassable et invincible venue en soi" (untiring and invincible coming into itself, p. 15) bursts through us in the invisibility of our night. The reversal into a philosophy of nature names the trajectory from Kant and Fichte to Schelling and Schopenhauer that Chapters IV–V reconstruct.

Life, finally, is indifferent to the thoughts of it, though they all proceed from it. Reducing being to the thought of being, even to the more essential thinking that belongs together with being in their original "co-appartenance" (belonging-together), is "pur idéalisme" (pure idealism, p. 15). The target is Heidegger's *Zusammengehören* of thinking and being. To understand psychoanalysis in its provenance from being is therefore not to make it one of being's moments, "figures" or "epochs", as in Hegel's phenomenology or Heidegger's history of being. If our primary relation to being is not an ecstasis, and Henry says that this is finally what psychoanalysis means, then thought cannot be wholly trusted, its errancy matters less, and "le destin de l'individu n'est plus tout à fait celui du monde" (the individual's fate is no longer quite the world's). Whether life is denied by objectivist science, conceived adequately by the radical phenomenology the book will build, or left to mythology, it continues its work in us, giving us to ourselves in "le pathos de son souffrir et de son ivresse" (the pathos of its suffering and its intoxication). Henry thus closes by withholding from his own book the role he refuses to Hegelian or Heideggerian thought: philosophy does not complete life; life is complete without it. The prologue is dated Osaka, 5 November 1983.

## Chapter I. "Videre videor"

The Cartesian project still fascinates, Henry begins, because it coincides with the project of philosophy as such: a first and radical philosophy is the search for the Beginning. This search is not a quest for a method that would lead there, since no method is possible without an assured starting point already lodged in the Beginning. Cartesianism's crucial intuition was to affirm that its procedure belongs to what comes first and makes it, and everything else, possible. Henry then separates two senses of beginning. Cartesianism is the beginning of modern philosophy, something new; but what is new presupposes much before it, and so does the order of Descartes' reasons. The Beginning is not the new but "l'Ancien et le plus ancien" (the Ancient and the most ancient, p. 17). Schelling's complaint that Descartes presumed to discard a tradition no single man could rebuild (*Ages of the World*) is faithful to Descartes' words, since Descartes says he will write as if no one had touched the matter before him. But it misses his intention: to return to the most initial moment of the Beginning, that by which it begins "et ne cesse de commencer" (and never stops beginning, p. 18). The Beginning is thus not temporal priority but a permanent originating; this is the sense of the book's subtitle.

What begins in the radical sense is being, since nothing would be had being not already deployed its essence to gather all beings into it. But where does the initiality of this beginning lie? What is already there before any thing when the thing appears is appearing itself. Henry refines this immediately. Appearing as the coming-to-appearance of a thing is still only the beginning of the being (*l'étant*). Appearing is initial in the most original sense only insofar as "il apparaît d'abord lui-même et en lui-même" (it first appears itself and in itself, p. 18). Only thus is appearing identical with being and its ground: as a self-kindling light that illuminates not something else but itself, the "apparaître de l'apparaître" that expels nothingness and takes its place. Being is the phenomenological effectiveness of appearing in its capacity to constitute an appearance by itself. It is the beginning, "non pas le premier jour, mais le tout premier" (not the first day, but the very first). Two levels of appearing are set out here that structure the whole chapter: the appearing of things, and the self-appearing of appearing. Henry's thesis will be that they differ in kind.

Descartes, in his language, calls appearing as such "thought". He found what he sought, the radical beginning and being, "je pense, je suis", at the moment he considered thought in itself. Henry corrects his own formulation. Descartes did not reject things in order to keep only the fact of their appearance; he rejected things and their appearance, with which they are always mixed in ordinary consciousness, so as to keep only pure appearance abstracted from everything that appears in it. The correction anticipates the next step: even the appearance of things, taken as pure appearance, will be put in doubt, so that the residue of the *epochē* is narrower than the Husserlian "phenomenon".

Five remarks advance this "repetition" of the *cogito*, a term borrowed from Heidegger's *Wiederholung*. The first answers Heidegger's objection in *Sein und Zeit* (§ 6, p. 24), that the Cartesian beginning is not radical because it presupposes an implicit pre-understanding of being: how could one say "I am" without knowing confusedly what being is? Descartes does not say "I am", Henry replies; he says "therefore I am". The *sum* is not posited without presupposition. It results from the systematic elaboration of the precondition that alone makes a proposition about being possible, and that precondition is appearing, which Descartes calls thought. Determining it is the very content of the *cogito*. Henry cites the *Principles* (I, 8): "nous sommes par cela seul que nous pensons" (we are by the sole fact that we think, his emphasis). The *ergo* is turned into Henry's weapon against Heidegger: the being-question is answered by the analysis of manifestation, not presupposed by it.

Throughout the *Meditations* and the *Replies*, the positing of the *sum* follows from that of the "I think". Appearing opens the field in which it reveals itself, a field constituted by it and its revelation; and being is nothing but what flashes forth as the phenomenological effectiveness of that field. The "therefore" thus expresses "une définition phénoménologique de l'être" (a phenomenological definition of being, p. 19). This is why Descartes could only shrug at Gassendi's suggestion that he might as well infer his existence from walking. Descartes answers that "je me promène, donc je suis" holds only insofar as the inner knowledge I have of walking is a thought (Fifth Replies, AT VII, 352). Moving from thought to being does not leave being undetermined but points to its essence; the idea of a phenomenological ontology takes root in Descartes. The Gassendi exchange gives Henry a textual basis for his distinction between walking as a worldly process and the "seeming to walk" that is certain; it returns at p. 29.

The third remark concerns substance. Being, in Descartes' language, is substance, the thing. In the Cartesianism of the Beginning (that of the Second Meditation, not of the *Regulae*) "thing" in "thinking thing" points to nothing beyond appearing in its actual accomplishment. It does not mean an appearance (*Schein*, *Erscheinung*) that would leave reality behind it, revealing it only mediately and thus hiding it. The thinking thing is what shows itself where what shows itself is "le se montrer lui-même" (showing itself as such, p. 20). To know what a thing, what being is, Descartes need not consult animals, plants or ideas, which no longer exist after the doubt; he need only grasp the flash of appearance, its "Parousie". The substantiality of the thinking thing is "la matérialité de la phénoménalité comme telle" (the materiality of phenomenality as such, p. 21). Descartes mocks Gassendi for supposing that the idea of a thing in general requires ideas of animals, plants and stones (Fifth Replies, AT VII, 362). By grounding the idea of thing exclusively on the thinking thing, Descartes rejects every interpretation of being from beings, and takes the first steps of what Henry now names "une phénoménologie matérielle" (a material phenomenology). Here the term that will title Henry's 1990 book is coined. Material phenomenology considers not the fact of appearing as distinct from what appears (Husserl's and Heidegger's ontological difference), but the content of appearing, its pure phenomenological "stuff". *Res cogitans* means a thing whose substantiality is that of pure phenomenality.

Henry sets aside what follows: the "chute fatale" (fatal fall, p. 21) by which thought becomes the principal attribute of a substance beyond it, substance proper is reserved to God, and thought becomes a created substance juxtaposed to body within a transcendent metaphysical construction. Whether this drift belongs to Descartes or marks the return of scholastic and theological conceptions is left aside; the note refers to Gilson, Laporte, Gouhier, Gueroult, Alquié and to Marion's two books on Descartes' grey ontology and white theology. What matters is a principle. Any split in our being between its self-showing and what would withdraw from phenomenality would not only taint the first eidetic definition of the psyche at the dawn of modernity, it would ruin the problematic. If being is not the flash of appearing, how could the *cogito* reach the *sum*? Being heterogeneous to appearing would resemble whatever is separated from phenomenality, that is, a being: "un animal, une plante, une idée, un dieu" (p. 22). The paragraph states in advance the formal argument against the unconscious: a psyche partly withdrawn from manifestation would collapse into a thing.

The Cartesianism of the Beginning comes down to an essential difference between what accomplishes the work of appearing and what is incapable of it: soul and body. The soul takes its essence from appearing; the body by principle lacks the power of manifestation. "Soul" in this sense has nothing to do with what we now call "thought": thinking that, conceiving that, imagining that, judging that. This soul is not the *meinen* (intending, opining) of modern philosophy. Henry finds proof in the polemic with Bourdin (Seventh Replies, AT VII, 559): Bourdin replaced the true difference, that incorporeal things think and corporeal ones do not, with a non-essential one, that the former consider that they think. Hence doubt does not lead to the *sum*. Doubt is a "considering that", a *meinen*, and so is the certainty into which it turns. What leads to the *sum* is the appearing that reigns in doubt as in "I walk", insofar as each is a determination of the soul. Henry thus detaches the *cogito* from the reflexive and propositional reading that dominates both the neo-Kantian and the Husserlian tradition.

The fourth remark: since thought designates appearing in its original form, the soul–body difference is "ontico-ontologique" (ontico-ontological, p. 23). The body expresses what is heterogeneous to manifestation, so all bodily determinations, the eye for instance, are blind: "C'est l'âme qui voit et non pas l'œil" (it is the soul that sees, not the eye; *Dioptrics*, VI). Animals, though they have eyes, do not see, and not only moles. Cartesian mechanism is therefore not primarily a theory of biological life; several texts treat the human body, like Goldstein, as an organic unity (*Passions*, art. 30). Mechanism formulates the irreducible heterogeneity of beings to the truth of being. The *cogito*'s reduction enacts this difference by striking out what appears, "the body", in favour of appearing, "the soul"; and this striking-out does not merely suspend the body's being-sense, as in Husserl, but casts it into nothingness. A note adds that Descartes' own example of gravity, which would require knowledge of the earth's centre and hence a mind, shows mechanism to mean the thought of beings as alien to appearing. Henry's reading of the body here is provisional; *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps* and *Incarnation* will recover a subjective body that this Cartesian dualism cannot see.

The fifth remark applies essence and existence to the Cartesian beginning. Their use is improper if the dichotomy springs from presupposing the facticity of a being and then asking what it is, so that essence as beingness (*Seiendheit*) conceals being; the bracketed German marks Heidegger's critique. To the objection that I know directly that I am but not what I am, Descartes answers bluntly that "l'un ne se démontre pas sans l'autre" (one is not demonstrated without the other, p. 24). In the Beginning essence and existence are not dissociated: when appearing deploys its essence, that is existence in the original sense, being there for us. The question of the essence of appearing therefore leads to the heart of Cartesianism, the *videre videor*.

The *cogito* finds its most ultimate formulation in *videre videor*, "it seems to me that I see". Henry recalls the context in the Second Meditation and *Principles* I, 9. Descartes has performed the radical *epochē*, doubting the earth he walks on, his room, the whole world that may be dream. Yet he sees all this, even if the appearances are false. But the *epochē* also covers Descartes as a man in the world, his body, legs and eyes. What then can seeing, hearing, feeling warmth mean for a being without eyes or body, who perhaps does not exist? "At certe videre videor, audire, calescere" (AT VII, 29): at least it seems to me that I see, hear, grow warm. Henry poses the obvious phenomenological answer as a question. Does not pure vision, reduced to itself, remain? And with it what is seen, as mere phenomenon: trees with coloured shapes, or at least appearances of shapes, men in hats, or at least patches and clothes? Would these appearances, taken as such, not remain indubitable? This is the Husserlian reading of the reduction, in which the *cogitatum qua cogitatum* survives with the *cogito*. Henry raises it in order to reject it.

The Cartesianism of the Beginning answers no. These shapes are not as I believe I see them, since I believe I see real forms that may belong to dream. A vision not of the eyes also sees that two and three make five, that a triangle's angles equal two right angles; and all of this may be false, as Descartes supposes. If clearly seen contents can nonetheless be false, it can only be because "la vision elle-même est fallacieuse" (vision itself is deceptive, p. 25): sight is of such a nature that what it sees is not as it sees it, perhaps is not at all, believing it sees something when there is nothing, or nothing when everything is there. The doubt is thus moved from the objects of sight to the act of seeing. This is the decisive turn: the evil genius attacks not content but the form of manifestation.

The familiar path of the *epochē* gives way underfoot. What the *epochē* first produced was the clear difference between what appears and appearing as such, freeing the latter as ground. Now it is this ground that wavers, since appearing, insofar as the text calls it seeing, is put in question. Seeing is rejected because the seen is not as we see it, and even the appearance taken as mere appearance may not be what it appears to be. Hyperbolic doubt achieves full scope only in subverting the eternal truths; but subverting essences is possible only if it first calls into question "le milieu de visibilité" (the medium of visibility, p. 26) in which such essential contents are seen. It is this medium, and the seeing grounded in it, that loses its power of manifestation. Henry's reading of the doubt on eternal truths is phenomenological rather than theological: the "created truths" doctrine matters because it compromises the light, not because it exalts divine will.

What, then, is seeing? With the human eye barred by the reduction, vision is returned to its nature, the pure fact of seeing, which presupposes a horizon of visibility, a transcendental light that Descartes calls "natural light". Things, mathematical essences included, can be seen because they bathe in that light. To see is to look toward and reach what stands before the gaze, so that it is seen only through the "ob-jection" of what is thrown and set before. Before being that of a thing or an essence, this ob-jection is that of being-set-before as such, of the pure horizon: "l'ouverture de l'ouvert comme différence ontologique" (the opening of the open as ontological difference, p. 26). Ecstasis is the condition of possibility of the *videre* and of all seeing. Henry thus identifies Descartes' natural light with Heidegger's clearing and Husserl's horizon in a single structure. But it is precisely this original ecstasis that the reduction abandons. What then remains to it?

"At certe videre videor." Descartes posits that this vision, however deceptive, at least exists. In the Cartesianism of the Beginning, to exist means to appear. *Videor* designates exactly that: "la semblance primitive" (primitive seeming, p. 27), the original capacity of appearing by which vision shows and gives itself to us, whatever the credibility of what it sees or of its seeing. Henry rescues *semblance*, a rare word, to capture the double sense of *videor*: "I seem" and "I am seen" or "it appears to me". The crucial question of Cartesianism, perhaps of all philosophy, follows. Is the seeming that reigns in *videor*, by which seeing first appears to itself, identical with the seeming in which seeing reaches its object and constitutes itself as seeing? "L'essence originelle de la révélation est-elle réductible à l'ek-stasis de la différence ontologique ?" (Is the original essence of revelation reducible to the ecstasis of the ontological difference?) This is the thesis of *L'essence de la manifestation* posed as a reading of a single Latin phrase.

"En aucune façon" (in no way, p. 27). If *videor* merely doubled *videre*, if the same essence were meant in both, how could the doubling confer what the essence lacked by principle, the ability to constitute the beginning and ground itself in the certainty of its self-revelation? Descartes' critique of seeing cannot be forgotten. If seeing has been discredited as a principle of legitimation, since its vision may be false, how could it be entrusted with legitimating itself? Seeing occurs in ecstasis as a grasp that is not only doubtful but radically mistaken if the evil genius so wills. If the appearance that grasps this seeing and gives it to itself were seeing again, it would double its uncertainty. A principle destroyed by the *epochē* "ne peut plus se sauver lui-même" (can no longer save itself, p. 28). The primitive appearance that runs through the *videre* and makes it an "absolute phenomenon" must therefore be structurally heterogeneous to the appearance that seeing itself is. The argument is a regress argument against reflection: a self-manifestation built on the model of seeing would inherit the defect of seeing.

When Descartes says "it seems to me that I see", he does not mean "I think that I see", as if *videre* were the *cogitatum* of a *cogito* named *videor*. That would follow only if *videor* were homogeneous with *videre*; the being of the first seeing would then be given in the ecstasis of a second, a "seeing that", as its intentional correlate. Such a reading would ruin the *cogito* by substituting the uncertainty of seeing for the certainty of thought, and it contradicts Descartes' general critique of reflection, which presupposes the certainty of thought instead of founding it. Henry enlists Alquié: Descartes affirms "non pas la conscience réfléchie de voir mais bien l'impression immédiate de voir" (not reflective consciousness of seeing but the immediate impression of seeing, p. 28). The text confirms it: seeming to see, hear and grow warm "is properly what in me is called feeling (*sentire*), and this, taken precisely, is nothing other than thinking" (AT VII, 29). Henry's whole reading hinges on this sentence of the Second Meditation, where *sentire* is identified with *cogitare*.

In feeling, then, Descartes deciphers the original essence of appearing expressed in *videor*, the ultimate ground. Thought unfolds as feeling, with the flash of a manifestation that exhibits itself as it is, the radical beginning the *epochē* sought. Descartes repeatedly says we feel our thought, feel that we see, hear, grow warm. "Je sens que je pense, donc je suis" (I feel that I think, therefore I am, p. 29) is Henry's reformulation. Seeing is thinking one sees, as Descartes himself says, "ce que je ne distingue plus" (which I no longer distinguish); but thinking one sees is feeling that one sees. *Videor* is the feeling immanent to seeing that makes it an effective seeing, "un voir qui se sent voir" (a seeing that feels itself see). *Principles* I, 9 confirms this: replacing the walking done with legs and the seeing done with eyes by the knowledge "qui fait qu'il me semble que je vois ou que je marche" (that makes it seem to me that I see or walk), Descartes declares the conclusion absolutely certain because it relates to the soul, which alone can feel or think. The letter to Plempius of 3 October 1637 opposes animal vision, mere retinal impressions moving the limbs, to ours, "dum sentimus nos videre" (when we feel that we see). A note shows that the first Cartesians understood this: Dilly holds that my vision makes itself felt to be "sans qu'il soit besoin d'autre chose" (without needing anything else), and Régis, following Augustine's *De libero arbitrio*, that the soul knows its sensations by themselves; Henry refers to Geneviève Lewis's study of the unconscious and Cartesianism. The note establishes that the immanent reading is historically available, not Henry's invention.

What does feeling mean? In "sentimus nos videre", does feeling refer to the same power in which seeing unfolds? After all, seeing is a mode of feeling, like hearing or touching. Descartes would endorse Heidegger's thesis that sight and hearing are possible only on the ground of Dasein's "de-severing" (*Ent-fernung*, the bringing-near that presupposes distance). In Cartesianism sensible vision presupposes a transcendental seeing: perceiving men in hats in the street implies the idea of man, and this idea too has an aspect discovered by pure intelligence in ecstasis. Intelligence, sense and imagination share one condition. Is not feeling in general a seeing, insofar as sense means affection by a foreign being and so presupposes the ecstasis in which being is given as other? Henry states here the strongest case for reducing feeling to ecstasis, the one *L'essence de la manifestation* attributes to Kant and Heidegger: sensibility as receptivity of what is other.

Three Cartesian theses forbid reducing the feeling immanent to thought to *videre*. The first, already shown, is that the certainty of the beginning does not lie in deceptive seeing. The second is that the soul cannot be felt. This does not merely exclude sensory feeling, the fact that the soul cannot be touched or seen (letter to Mersenne, July 1641). The whole problematic moves within the reduction, as Descartes insists: it concerns "la seule pensée de voir et de toucher" (only the thought of seeing and touching, Fifth Replies, AT VII, 360). It is this thought of seeing and touching, insofar as it bears the ecstasis of feeling, that the knowledge of the soul escapes: the original appearing in which thought feels itself immediately in the *videor*. Descartes' definitions of thought postulate this immediacy: thought is "tout ce qui est tellement en nous que nous en sommes immédiatement conscients" (all that is in us in such a way that we are immediately conscious of it; Geometrical Exposition, AT IX, 124), "ce qui se fait en nous de telle sorte que nous l'apercevons immédiatement par nous-mêmes" (*Principles* I, 9). The second thesis thus distinguishes two feelings: the soul cannot be the object of a feeling, yet it is feeling.

The concept of feeling thus splits in two. On one side, the feeling at work in seeing, hearing, touching and in the understanding as *intueri*, the transcendental seeing grounded in ecstasis. On the other, the primitive feeling of thought in "sentimus nos videre", "le se sentir soi-même" (feeling oneself, p. 31), which gives thought originally to itself and is the original self-appearing of appearing. This self-feeling does not merely differ from ecstatic feeling; it excludes it, and the concept of immediacy formulates this exclusion. Since ecstasis founds exteriority, thought, by excluding it, takes on its essence as "une intériorité radicale" (a radical interiority). The Cartesian phrase "so in us that we are immediately conscious of it" points to this mode of being-in as the expulsion of transcendence, the first essence of consciousness. It also explains the enigmatic *cognitio illa interna*, "cette sorte de connaissance intérieure qui précède toujours l'acquise" (that inner knowledge which always precedes acquired knowledge, Sixth Replies, AT VII, 422). Henry's "interiority" must not be read spatially; it is defined negatively by the absence of ecstatic distance, as in *L'essence de la manifestation*, §§ 49–50.

The third thesis is that appearing in its original self-revelation ignores ecstasis. It results from Descartes' refutation, in the Fifth Replies, of an "extraordinary" text of Gassendi. Rising for once above his sensualism, Gassendi grasps the essence of knowledge: the transcendental structure of feeling as the condition of every particular sense and of all thought. Sensible seeing is possible only if an original space opens between it and the seen; in this exteriority what is seen attains its condition of being-seen, and vision is nothing but the opening of this distance, nothing but ecstasis. Hence a faculty, not being outside itself, "ne peut former la notion de soi-même" (cannot form the notion of itself); that is why the eye does not see itself, nor the understanding conceive itself. For Gassendi, self-knowledge requires self-affection, but only in the form of seeing, in the light of exteriority: the eye sees itself in a mirror because there is space between them. And what holds for the eye holds for the mind. "Donnez-moi donc un miroir" (give me a mirror, p. 33), Gassendi says, and you will know yourself, not directly but by reflection; otherwise you can have no idea of yourself (AT VII, 292). Henry praises Gassendi because he states the ontological monism of Western thought with perfect clarity: all self-manifestation is reflection, all reflection requires distance.

Hence, for Gassendi, there are no innate ideas, only ideas acquired from outside, since exteriority is the medium of all reception and all possible experience. Descartes rejects these presuppositions, which in fact govern the history of Western thought: it is not the eye or the mirror that sees itself "mais bien l'esprit, lequel seul connaît et l'œil et le miroir et soi-même" (but the mind, which alone knows the eye, the mirror and itself; AT VII, 367). Seeing spread out in its ecstatic structure, eye and mirror, is not the first effectiveness of phenomenality. Seeing can see only if it is first possible as seeing, perceived in itself, so that this inner apperception of ecstasis precedes it and is not constituted by it. Henry accumulates names for this apperception: "l'Un de la Différence, l'intériorité radicale de l'extériorité radicale" (the One of Difference, the radical interiority of radical exteriority, p. 33), inner knowledge, the *videor* of the *videre*, what Descartes calls mind. The formula "interiority of exteriority" is exact: auto-affection is not a second region beside the world but the self-givenness of the very power that opens the world.

This answers Hobbes, who grants that "I exist" depends on "I think" but asks where knowledge of "I think" comes from (Third Objections, AT VII, 173–174). The project of the Second Meditation now takes shape. It concerns neither the soul nor the body but "the knowledge of the soul" and "the knowledge of the body". In the reduction, the body that is struck out is the being; this is why ontological determinations seemingly proper to the soul, such as gravity understood as a tending toward a goal, were denied it. "Knowledge of the body", by contrast, leads back to the ontological dimension of appearing: it names not knowledge of some thing called body but a mode of knowing as such, a mode of appearing with its own structure; likewise "knowledge of the soul". Two questions follow: what does the dissociation of two pure modes of appearing mean, and what does the primacy of one over the other mean, a primacy whose legitimation is the sole task of the Second Meditation? Henry thus reads the title of the Second Meditation ("that the mind is better known than the body") as a thesis about two modes of phenomenality, not two substances.

"Knowledge of the body" is seeing as such: by the eyes, or what remains after the reduction, sensible vision, touch, imagination, inspection of the mind. Any seeing, as ecstasis of what is thrown before it, presupposes the self-appearing of this vision, the self-revelation of its ecstasis, as different from it and prior to it. Only thus does "la connaissance que nous avons de notre pensée [précéder] celle que nous avons du corps" (the knowledge of our thought precede that of the body, *Principles* I, 11). If it were identical in its mode of appearing, it could not be its presupposition. "Precede" here is transcendental, not chronological: the *videor* is the condition of any *videre*, as the Beginning is the condition of any beginning.

The heterogeneity and priority of the knowledge of the soul cannot remain an assertion or a demonstration, for example by arguing that knowledge of the body requires a prior immediate knowledge of itself. "Le commencement ne saurait être établi par une raison de principe et le cartésianisme est une phénoménologie" (the beginning cannot be established by an argument from principle; Cartesianism is a phenomenology, p. 35). It must be a material phenomenology. It deals not with contents of knowledge or "phenomena", asking which can be posited by virtue of their privileged mode of givenness (Husserl's procedure with adequate and apodictic evidence), but with the mode of givenness itself. That mode must not merely be described in its structure, which does not establish its specificity; its pure phenomenality must be brought to appearance, or rather brings itself to appearance by its own power. Material phenomenology reads in this accomplished phenomenality the structure of its accomplishment, and "structure" here means only "le comment du mode selon lequel se phénoménalise la phénoménalité" (the how of phenomenality's self-phenomenalization, p. 36), a how identical with its effectuation. This is the methodological centre of the chapter. Henry refuses a transcendental argument for auto-affection, since that would make the beginning depend on thought; it must attest itself.

Can the Cartesian project reach the point where phenomenology becomes material? The opposition between *videre* and *videor* is founded only if, as an opposition between two primitive modes of phenomenalization, it concerns their phenomenological effectiveness: appearing differs each time in the materiality of its pure appearing. And if the project also claims a hierarchy, such that only what is given in one mode escapes doubt, it must show how in that primitive upsurge whatever is phenomenalized shows itself as it really is. In the other mode, though it too is pure phenomenality, nothing of the kind occurs, and the seeing on which knowledge has been built since Greece is struck with nullity, not provisionally but by an impotence inherent in its phenomenality. What is seen is always foreign to the reality of seeing and to its own reality: it appears in its exteriority to itself, and vision is this self-exteriority, which can find being only in its immanence to itself, as the *videor* that inhabits seeing. Henry here extends the Cartesian privilege from certainty to reality: what is given ecstatically is not merely uncertain but is, as given, unreal in itself. The thesis prepares the later claim that representation by itself never contains life (Chapter IV and Schopenhauer in Chapter V).

This interiority cannot remain a mere concept or structure, a formal anti-essence of ecstasis. It can be legitimated only within a material phenomenology, by reference to an effective appearance, to the "substantialité et ... matérialité phénoménologique pure" (substantiality and pure phenomenological materiality, p. 37) of that appearance. Only by recognizing in its mode of presentation something like a total self-exhibition can one affirm that such a manifestation is absolute and indubitable, beyond any reduction. Henry then asks the decisive textual question. Did Descartes ever explicitly designate the phenomenological substance of appearance as self-attesting, self-presenting as it is, the ground of all absolute truth and of his doctrine? Did he ever explicitly oppose to it the mode of *videre* as incapable of containing these conditions in "le cristal de sa phénoménalité pure" (the crystal of its pure phenomenality)? The chapter's interpretive thesis stands or falls on the answer.

*The Passions of the Soul* answers. Article 26, which belongs to the treatise's prescientific thesis (the action of body on soul through nerves and animal spirits) and stands at the antipodes of the reduction, suddenly returns to it. It evokes sleep, and waking, which nothing allows us to distinguish radically. What the sleeper or waker thinks he sees or feels in his body is nullified; feeling and seeing are again denied access to truth, while self-feeling, original affectivity and all its modes are stamped "du sceau de l'absolu" (with the seal of the absolute, p. 37). They reveal themselves in their affectivity as they are, and no illusion has power over them. Descartes: even asleep or dreaming, one cannot feel sad or moved by any passion "qu'il ne soit très vrai que l'âme a en soi cette passion" (without its being very true that the soul has this passion in itself, p. 38). The *videor*–*videre* opposition is thus repeated within a material phenomenology, determined by the phenomenological content of the two modes, which appear to Descartes as passion and perception. One can be deceived in perceptions of external objects or of parts of one's body, not in the passions, "d'autant qu'elles sont si proches et si intérieures à notre âme qu'il est impossible qu'elle les sente sans qu'elles soient véritablement telles qu'elle les sent" (because they are so close and so interior to our soul that it cannot feel them without their truly being as it feels them; art. 26, AT XI, 349). The textual centre of the chapter is thus a passage from the *Passions*, not from the *Meditations*: affect, not thought, is the Cartesian evidence that survives the dream argument. This is the doctrine Henry will set against Freud's claim that affects can be displaced, disguised or repressed (Chapters VI and IX).

Is the phenomenological determination of interiority as affectivity eidetic, coextensive with original appearing, if the passions in Descartes' specific sense name only some modes of thought? What principle restricts the name "passions" to some modalities of experience? Their determination by the body. A Cartesian passion, joy or sadness, unfolds in radical immanence, does not see and contains no seeing; it presents itself as pure interiority. But the affectivity that affects its self-feeling is not, for Descartes, the essence or possibility of that self-feeling; it derives from the action of the body. Yet in the Cartesianism of the reduction, "le « corps » n'existe pas" (the "body" does not exist, p. 39). The "explanation" of affectivity by bodily action is not just absurd but cannot even be advanced here. Unless the reduction is provisional? But the reduction is nothing but reading in subjectivity what it is, the flash of first appearing in its own content, which cannot be altered afterward by the philosopher's arbitrary decision. The affectivity of thought, when thought is alone in the world, must therefore be explained from thought's own essence, as "l'auto-affection en laquelle la pensée se révèle immédiatement à elle-même" (the auto-affection in which thought reveals itself immediately to itself). Affectivity is the original feeling, the self-feeling of feeling, the *videor* in which *videre* experiences itself and attains the reality of an experience of vision. Henry here performs on Descartes the move he will perform on Freud: an affect that a theory attributes to an external cause (body, drive, quantity of excitation) is shown to be possible only as self-affection. The reduction functions as the argument: once the body is bracketed, the affect cannot be its effect.

As the ultimate possibility of thought, affectivity reigns over all its modes and secretly determines them. Even in Descartes the reign of passion extends strangely. Though in the narrow sense passions are only perceptions related to the soul itself (joy, sadness), "toutes nos perceptions ... [sont] véritablement des passions" (all our perceptions, those related to external objects and those related to our body, are truly passions; art. 25, AT XI, 347). For Descartes they are passions not only because of their intrinsic affectivity but because they have their cause in the body: a known cause for perceptions of objects and of the body, an unknown one, which the treatise means to make known, for passions related to the soul. But the affectivity immanent to thought, as its first and unchallengeable phenomenological content, has nothing to do with a supposed causation by a body that has fallen under the reduction. Far from founding this affectivity, every explanation by the body or any other cause presupposes it "comme cela même qu'il s'agit d'expliquer" (as the very thing to be explained, p. 40). Only because affectivity has already unfolded phenomenologically can it become the object of the causal questions that Cartesianism, and the *Passions* in particular, pursue once they move outside the reduction and forget it. The same structure of argument will be turned on Freud's derivation of affect from the drive and of the drive from quantities of excitation.

Descartes himself, "involontairement sans doute mais invinciblement" (involuntarily no doubt, but inescapably, p. 40), is forced to recognize that passion in its affectivity does not depend on the body. *Passions*, article 19, considers perceptions caused not by the body but by the soul: the perceptions of our volitions, called "actions" because we experience that they come directly from the soul. Yet these volitions, which exist for us only as perceptions, far from excluding the concept of passion, fall under it as soon as one considers not their specific mode as willing but the original apperception that gives them to themselves. Descartes: we cannot will anything without perceiving by the same means that we will it, and though willing is an action of the soul, "c'est aussi en elle une passion d'apercevoir qu'elle veut" (it is also a passion in it to perceive that it wills; AT XI, 343, Henry's emphasis). Stronger than the Cartesian prejudice that will devalue it, affectivity proves constitutive of the essence of thought. Under the name "passion" it is the primordial *aperceptio*, "la passivité insurmontable de l'apparaître à l'égard de lui-même" (the insurmountable passivity of appearing toward itself, p. 41), its immanent auto-affection. Descartes then retreats: since perception and volition are one thing, naming follows the nobler, so we call it an action. But they are not one thing. Volition is a mode in which thought experiences itself as the source of its activity, a cause of itself, and so an "action", opposed to the other modes, the passions, in which the soul receives what it is from the things represented. Perception means the immanent apperception by which every mode of the soul is a mode at all, the universal essence of thought, and it is this that article 19 calls passion. The original concept of passion thus "domine l'opposition des « actions » et des « passions »" (governs the opposition of actions and passions) and founds both. Descartes is right after all that perception and volition are one, in the sense that this apperception ignores ecstasis and the will remains one with the power that gives it to itself in its affectivity. Henry distinguishes two concepts of passivity that ordinary readings run together: the ontic passivity of a mode determined from outside, and the ontological passivity of every mode toward itself. Only the second defines life, and it holds equally for action; this will become decisive in reading Maine de Biran's effort and Schopenhauer's Will.

The structural phenomenological dissociation of *videor* and *videre* is the indispensable precondition for the classical debate on what Descartes means by thought. The Second Meditation gives two definitions. One is by essence: I am precisely only a thinking thing, "mens, sive animus, sive intellectus, sive ratio", terms whose meaning was previously unknown to me (AT VII, 27). The other is by enumeration of modes: a thing that doubts, understands, affirms, denies, wills, does not will, imagines and feels (AT VII, 28). The first should suffice. Rejecting both traditional conceptions and naive anthropological knowledge, cumulative, confused and unelaborated, Descartes aims at once at an eidetics of a phenomenological essence, indeed of the essence of phenomenality, defined as mind, intellect and reason. "Mais que veulent dire ces termes ?" (But what do these terms mean? p. 42) The question frames the following units as a test of which definition can carry the Beginning.

From the first *Regula* these terms are joined to equivalents: *intellectus*, *bona mens*, *naturale rationis lumen*, *humana sapientia*, *universalis sapientia*, *scientia*, and in *Regula* II *cognitio certa et evidens*. Their content is clear. What is meant is evidence, or rather its condition, the natural, that is transcendental, light; human wisdom is given at once as universal wisdom. This light is transcendental as the ground of all knowledge and science, of its evidence and certainty, and in its effective phenomenality it constitutes both reason and intellect. The context of the Second Meditation confirms this. The concept of *mens* emerges as the fundamental power of knowledge, an *intellectus*, an *inspectio* of the mind apart from sense and imagination, or a *ratio*, the capacity to perceive the pure ideas in the mind, whether of extension or of thinking substance. The first definition, then, defines thought by the ecstatic light of the *videre*.

But these considerations belong only to the end of the Meditation, or to the *Regulae*. The First Meditation and the beginning of the Second, the whole phenomenological process leading to the *sum*, ignore or rather reject the definition of *mens* as *intellectus*. The doubt nullifies all anthropological and scientific knowledge only because it shakes their common ground, the transcendental light, the *sapientia universalis* that *Regula* I describes as always one and the same whatever its objects, as sunlight is not changed by what it illuminates (AT X, 360). This ontological horizon, recognized in its irreducibility to beings and as the condition of knowing them, the ultimate possibility of understanding ideal contents, falls under the reduction. Natural doubt relied on reasons; metaphysical doubt sweeps away all reasons, and *ratio* itself wavers. If thought is to be the stable foundation the Cartesianism of the Beginning seeks, its definition as "animus, intellectus sive ratio" is "décidément impossible" (decidedly impossible, p. 43). Henry inverts the usual hierarchy: the Second Meditation's definition by essence, which commentators privilege, is the one incompatible with the *cogito*.

That definition is secretly dependent on another problematic, which reappears at the end of the Second Meditation. There *mens* is examined not in itself, in the immediacy of its appearing, but as the condition, indeed the essence, of knowledge of the body: we know body "a solo intellectu" (by the faculty of understanding alone, AT IX, 26). The analysis of the wax and of the men in hats circumscribes the essence of *videre*, the "knowledge of the body" grounded in the ecstasis of seeing, pure seeing as "inspection of the mind"; it is an analysis not of body but of understanding. This knowledge, problematic in itself and unable to be the beginning, refers necessarily back to the knowledge of the soul exhibited in the *cogito*; a note cites the Fifth Replies' argument that every attribute known in other things counts as an attribute of the mind that knows them. That *mens* is not reducible to the *intueri* of intellect and reason is attested by a further text: the mind, as soon as it is infused into the infant's body, begins to think and "simulque sibi suae cogitationis conscia sit" (is at once conscious of its thought; Fourth Replies, AT VII, 246). Henry's irony is pointed: unless man's essence is mathematical activity and he is already preparing for the École Polytechnique in his mother's womb, the thought in question is not understanding but revelation in its most original form, "l'immanence muette de son premier être à soi dans l'affectivité du pur se sentir soi-même" (the mute immanence of its first being-to-itself in the affectivity of pure self-feeling, p. 44). The infant text also prepares the book's later concern with an infantile psyche that Freud will declare unconscious.

If the first definition, by the supposed essence, is really that of the knowledge of the body and cannot produce the beginning, consider the second. Despite its enumerative form, does it not lead to the essence, since the plurality of modes forces us to conceive their unity, which lies in their common essence? And can the attribution of these modes to one essence be explained if that essence is the understanding? The question sets up a test case: if the unity of the modes cannot be the intellect, it must be something else.

Concretely: are feeling and imagining homogeneous with intellectual intuition of simple natures, reducible to it? One solution is found in Descartes himself, and Gueroult's *Descartes selon l'ordre des raisons* develops it: sense and imagination are not proper modes of pure thought but intervene accidentally, through the union of soul and body. Thought identified with understanding could then take on modes contingent to its nature. Eidetic analysis confirms this, since in the Sixth Meditation one can think without imagining or feeling but not conversely: the power of imagining "n'est en aucune sorte nécessaire à mon essence" (is in no way necessary to my essence, AT IX, 58), and I can conceive myself whole without the faculties of imagining and feeling (AT IX, 62). But the Second Meditation unfolds entirely within the reduction and ignores the body and its supposed action on the soul. The inherence of sense and imagination in thought cannot be explained by a construction transcendent to the phenomenon. Must one wait for the Sixth Meditation? No: the reduction is not provisional; it requires that problems be posed and resolved on the plane of phenomena, excluding the hypothetical constructions of science and dogmatic philosophy. Sense and imagination must be shown to belong to thought as they present themselves, by the power of their own phenomenality, which is identically that of thought. That is why they are modes of thought, and why the enumerative definition does not discriminate among them: "ils ont tous le même droit" (they all have the same right, p. 46). Henry's refusal of a "provisional" reduction is his principal disagreement with the standard reading, Gueroult's included: the order of reasons does not license reinstating at the end what the beginning excluded.

The second definition therefore opposes the first only in appearance. The first, thought as pure essence of appearing, as "mind", omits sense and imagination because it springs from the reduction: the body has fallen and with it feeling and imagination as psycho-empirical faculties. But the reduction bars psycho-empirical feeling only to free the pure field of appearing, in which feeling and imagination, identified with their pure appearing, are promoted to "phénomènes absolus échappant à la réduction" (absolute phenomena escaping the reduction, p. 47). They enter the second definition as equal in dignity to appearing itself, as modes of its effective accomplishment. The reduction thus operates twice, as in Husserl: it excludes the empirical faculty and restores the same lived experience as pure. Henry's difference from Husserl lies in what grounds the restoration, which the next units identify.

The question must therefore be asked within the reduction: can sense and imagination, in their intrinsic phenomenological content, belong to thought understood as intellect? Once they are no longer taken naively as psychophysical powers and their acts as ontic processes, once the transcendental question of their possibility is posed (how they bring to phenomenality what they feel and imagine, and how they themselves come to appear), sense and imagination, as "thought of feeling" and "thought of imagining", oppose understanding less than it seems. Leave aside, Henry says, the crucial problem of their proper tonality. Their affectivity remains unquestioned in Descartes, who will address it only after forgetting the reduction, "explaining" it by the body and so excluding it from the ontology of pure thought. Granting this, do not sense and imagination give what they feel and imagine within the space and light of a seeing, and so through the essence the first definition calls *intellectus sive ratio*? This transcendental seeing immanent to their exercise, their power to go beyond themselves toward a content, is the "sorte d'intellection" (kind of intellection) Descartes grants them, which makes them "espèces de pensées" (kinds of thoughts; letter to Gibieuf, 19 January 1642). One can thus conceive how they appear as modes of thought reduced to that intellection. This is the strongest version of the intellectualist reading, granted in order to show its limits; the reserved question of "tonality" is the one Henry's answer will turn on.

Yet seeing itself has fallen under the reduction. Sense and imagination cannot be retained in the second definition as absolutely certain modes because they are founded on seeing; nor could the intellect itself, unless it were supported at its root by a more original mode of appearing, irreducible to it and unchallengeable. That not the *videre* but the more original seeming of the *videor* determines both the certainty of thought and the inherence of its modes will be shown by one mode cited no fewer than five times in the second definition, "dubitans, affirmans, negans, volens, nolens": the will. Henry reads doubting, affirming and denying as modes of will, in line with the Fourth Meditation's theory of judgment.

All Cartesianism formulates the difference, even the opposition, of understanding and will; the theory of judgment and much of the method rest on it. More than that, the recognition of appearing in its first flash, of the essence of thought and of being in it, is achieved in the *cogito* from the will, of which doubt is a mode. Hyperbolic doubt is not natural doubt, which asks the understanding for reasons; it is doubt against nature and against the understanding, a mode of the infinite will, identical in me and in God, which can will anything absolutely and unconditionally, "qui peut vouloir que le vrai soit faux" (which can will the true to be false, p. 48) and that seeing, even evidence bathed in its light, not be seeing. The understanding plays no part in the reduction except to be rejected by it; how could it be the beginning to which the reduction returns? The reading links the evil genius, the will's infinity and the created eternal truths into one structure: the power that can suspend the light cannot itself be in the light.

Yet must not the will also reveal itself, if it is to be something and not nothing? Is it not, despite its infinity, a mode of thought, and so dependent again on the understanding it meant to exclude? This is the characteristically Cartesian paradox that an infinite will is a mode of a finite essence. The paradox is real only if grasped phenomenologically. The finitude of the understanding is neither doctrine nor concept: it designates appearing itself, insofar as appearing is identified with understanding. What is finite is the transcendental seeing, or rather its ground, the horizon of visibility opened by ecstasis, "cette place pure de la phénoménalité extatique" (this pure place of ecstatic phenomenality, p. 49). Descartes' whole method, insofar as it trusts *intueri* and its light, describes the obstacles met and the detours taken by an understanding whose gaze moves within an essentially finite horizon. This finitude forces intuition to perceive one thing at a time: concentrating light on one thing throws everything else into shadow. Such seeing is "identiquement et plus encore un ne pas voir" (identically, and more so, a not-seeing), so that all it does not see becomes the object of an indefinite quest. The method tries to exorcize this finitude surreptitiously: extending the light step by step, claiming that the passage between intuitions is itself an intuition, recommending that a chain of intuitions be run through so often and so fast that they seem to be one, and deduction reduce to intuition (*Regulae* VII and XI). The finitude Henry assigns to ecstatic phenomenality is the same as Heidegger's finite transcendence; but whereas for Heidegger finitude is the essence of manifestation, for Henry it is the mark of one mode of manifestation, which cannot be the original one.

"En vain" (in vain, p. 50). These expedients feed on an irreducible phenomenological situation: each new content offers itself to the light of seeing only if the preceding one "lui fait le sacrifice de sa propre présence" (sacrifices its own presence to it). To hold a chain of reasons in the mind, or the data of a single problem in memory, the rest of what is slips into night. This structure determines the very content of what is seen even when the content seems to disclose itself as it is. A simple nature is simple only as the correlate of one intuition, whose unity circumscribes it. The proof that simplicity stems from the mode of givenness and not from the content is that the Cartesian simple is infinitely rich: a relation referring to other relations, an essence bearing a multitude of implications, virtualities and potentialities to be intuited in turn in an endless phenomenological elucidation. Surrounded by a fringe of shadow formed by its horizon of potentialities, the simple nature is never wholly clear and distinct; hence *Regula* XII: we cannot conceive seven distinctly without including, more or less confusedly, three and four. But this indefinite play of references belongs not to the given, which in itself has no representational potentiality, but to its mode of givenness. "Ce n'est donc pas l'essence, ce n'est pas l'étant qui est fini, c'est le lieu où il paraît" (it is not the essence or the being that is finite, but the place where it appears, p. 51). Finitude is an ontological structure of phenomenality whose essence is ecstasis, and understanding is finite because it sees in the medium ecstasis opens. Here Henry describes horizon-intentionality in Husserlian terms (implications, potentialities, the fringe of the halo) and lodges in it the origin of the representational "unconscious": what is not actually seen but implied is a potentiality of the horizon. The book's final section, "Potentiality", and the critique of Freud's latent representations in Chapter IX are already prepared: the "unconscious" of representation is merely the horizon structure of ecstatic seeing.

The question remains how the will can inhere in thought defined as understanding. How could an infinite will reveal itself in its infinity if its revelation is entrusted to an essentially finite power, a finite place where anything appearing shows only a partial aspect and overflows it on every side? If the will refuses to deliver its essence as an aspect offered to seeing, or as an indefinite series of aspects, if it has no external faces whose sum would yield its essence, that is because it is possible and infinite only "comme puissance" (as power, p. 51). Power is never graspable under an aspect or an image, in the "outside itself" of any exteriority; it experiences itself only inwardly and comes into its own power, to seize and deploy it, only through "cette épreuve muette de soi et dans sa passion" (this mute self-experience and in its passion). This is why Descartes characterized as passion the original *aperceptio* in which the will lives itself as willing, coming directly from the soul.
