Here the explicit exclusion of the body rules out explaining the affectivity of this apperception by the body, as was claimed for sense and imagination. The concept of power that will govern Schopenhauer's Will, Nietzsche's will to power and Freud's drive is fixed here: a power is real only as self-experience, never as an aspect. The book's last section takes up exactly this under the title "Potentiality".

What the passion is that lets the will reveal itself all at once, in the infinity of its power, now comes fully to light. It is thought in its most original essence: not the *videre* of the understanding in its finite ecstasis, but "la première semblance du videor" (the first seeming of the *videor*, p. 52), first appearing as it appears to itself in the auto-affection of radical immanence. The opposition of *videor* and *videre*, and the breakdown of thought into two fundamental modes of phenomenality, now become significant. Sense, imagination and will do not belong to the seeing of the understanding; nor does feeling (*sentiment*), absent from the enumeration only because it is its "unité inaperçue" (unnoticed unity). What grounds the inherence of all these modes in one essence is the original *aperceptio*, coextensive and "co-intensive" with their being, the "kind of intellection" each bears as what reveals it to itself, which understanding also bears, since *videre* is possible only as *videre videor*. Henry's answer to the debate on the two definitions is that neither intellect nor enumeration gives the essence: the essence is the affective self-revelation that the enumeration omits because it is in every item.

That the regression to first appearing was made in the *cogito* not from a specific mode of thought, the understanding, but by excluding it, through "l'acte obscur et la passion infinie d'une volonté aveugle" (the obscure act and infinite passion of a blind will, p. 52) rejecting all the intelligible, should have given pause. It should have shown that the most initial thought glimpsed by Descartes at the dawn of modern culture had nothing to do with the thought that, through theories of knowledge and science, would lead that culture to a universe like ours. This inaugural thought, in its withdrawal from the world, its irreducibility to seeing and the radical subjectivity of its immediacy to itself, deserved another name, which Descartes gave it: soul, "ou, si l'on préfère, le nom de la vie" (or, if one prefers, the name of life). But Cartesianism itself could not hold to this narrow ridge of original meanings; to understand our world, one must examine its decline. The "blind will" names the point where Descartes touches Schopenhauer: the will that reaches the Beginning is blind because it does not see, not because it is unconscious. The last sentence opens Chapter II, on the decline of the phenomenological absolutes.

## Chapter II. The Decline of the Phenomenological Absolutes

Only a material phenomenology can hold firm the distinction between *videor* and *videre*: one that refers each basic concept of phenomenality to its actual accomplishment, so that these pure manifestations, "ces cristaux d'apparaître" (these crystals of appearing, p. 53), are recognized in the radical difference of their phenomenological substance. A phenomenology or phenomenological ontology that forgets this reference remains "un pur conceptualisme" (a pure conceptualism): its propositions have only the form of apodicticity and lend themselves to an indefinite free play. What can appearing mean if the concrete phenomenality of exhibition is not itself exhibited? What can it mean for appearing to give itself or withdraw, to give itself in withdrawal, for the truth of being to deliver itself as the truth of beings while concealing its own truth, unless truth here names the phenomenological matter of an effective mode of phenomenality? The last question targets Heidegger's *aletheia* and the epochal withdrawal of being: Henry charges that "giving in withdrawal" is a formal schema with no assignable phenomenological content.

No concept is without reference, that of phenomenality least of all. As soon as the word "appearing" is uttered, an implicit understanding of an effective mode of phenomenality has taken place, and not just any mode. What first presents itself as the referent of every concept of phenomenality is the visibility of the world: the transcendental horizon thrown before us by ecstasis, whose prior visibilization makes every being visible. The phenomenality produced in "le processus d'extériorisation de l'extériorité" (the process by which exteriority exteriorizes itself, p. 54) grounds the "natural" or "universal" light of Descartes and, in turn, seeing and *intueri*; *videre* refers to it. In its apparent autonomy this mode seems so original that it underlies the mostly implicit conceptions guiding philosophy since Greece. It took the extraordinary break of the reduction to rout these presuppositions, gathered alike in Plato's *idea* and the *ratio* of the *Regulae*. Then, for a moment, the reverse side of things appeared to philosophical consciousness: what never separates from itself, never goes outside itself, never presents itself as a world, what has neither face nor outside and that no one can see, "la subjectivité dans son immanence radicale identique à la vie" (subjectivity in its radical immanence, identical with life). Henry's "face" (*visage*) is a pointed word: Levinas's face is still, for Henry, a mode of the world's exteriority.

The concept of consciousness, entering Western philosophy, thus split mysteriously, designating both the visible and the invisible, thought and life, linked and founded one upon the other. But the original seeming on which seeing depends also falls under the requirements of material phenomenology: it is not a concept but shows itself in the effectiveness of its phenomenological matter. Did Descartes really conceive affectivity as this matter, as the phenomenological substance of the auto-affection by which seeing affects itself and experiences itself seeing? Chapter I answered for the Cartesianism of the Beginning; Chapter II asks about Descartes' doctrine.

Here the historial deviation takes place by which modern philosophy loses the essence of life from the start and can continue only as a philosophy and history of "thought" in today's sense. On one side, Descartes recognizes the affectivity of thought, or more restrictively, that thought can take a properly affective form in some modes, sensations and feelings (poorly distinguished), the passions of the soul at any rate. Calling them passions of the soul means first that they belong to thought. Descartes' analysis of them is in many respects phenomenological, even if its concepts will need examining. *Principles* I says feelings are both "clear" and "confused", and § 68 that we will not err about them if we distinguish "ce qu'il y a de clair d'avec ce qui est obscur" (what is clear from what is obscure). That feelings are clear and can be known clearly and distinctly (§ 66) means that they are phenomenological matters, modes of thought, as certain as the *cogito*: the soul cannot feel them otherwise than they are, because their being consists in auto-affection. That they are "obscure" or "confused" means only that their phenomenality is not the transparency of an intelligible light but affectivity, irreducible in its tonality. Descartes also calls them confused in another sense, which applies only to sensations: clear as phenomenological matters, they are confused not in their affectivity but in the judgment that naturally attaches to them and refers them to external things and first to one's own body, as if the hand were hot or painful. We believed these feelings to be "dans nos mains, dans nos pieds" (in our hands and feet, § 67); we know pain and colour clearly when we consider them as thoughts, but when we judge them to subsist outside thought we have no conception of what they are (§ 68). Henry separates two Cartesian senses of "confusion": the affective tonality of a feeling, which is no defect, and the false judgment projecting it into bodies, which is. The rest of the chapter traces how Descartes collapses the first into a privation.

These "magnificent" texts reaffirm the ontico-ontological difference that forbids attributing determinations of appearing to beings. Precisely because affectivity is referred to appearing and can have no site but there, its inherence in the soul can only mean its active role in the process that constructs phenomenality, since essence of the soul means the ultimate possibility of the power that produces phenomenality. Yet before this "intuition aveuglante" (blinding intuition, p. 56) of affectivity as appearing's first coming to itself, the Cartesian gaze turns away. Affectivity is not the essence or substance of thought; it befalls thought as an accident, from elsewhere, constraining it from outside and impairing its power of revelation and transparency. Then affectivity, from clear, that is illuminating, becomes "obscure". Obscurity is never, in Descartes, the phenomenological index pointing beneath ecstasis to where appearing arises in its immediacy; it marks a decline, an alteration by a foreign power, thought's "trouble". The principle of the reduction is flouted again: beings, once excluded from the conditions of phenomenality, return as one of them, and feeling, a pure phenomenological element, becomes "l'effet, d'ailleurs mystérieux, de l'étant en lui" (the effect, mysterious at that, of beings within it, p. 57). This is the exact template of the later Freudian move: affect as the psychical effect of a non-phenomenal cause.

If affectivity no longer constructs appearing from within and supplies its immediacy with phenomenological matter, where does the power by which phenomenality comes about lie? In ecstasis: its light becomes the sole essence of phenomenality, and affectivity only its alteration. The immanence required by Descartes' definitions of thought and of the idea can survive as a demand, since ecstasis could not unfold if the power unfolding it did not remain in itself, if ob-jection were not held in its radical interiority, if the idea lacked material reality. But it is now only a prescription, fragile once its phenomenological footing is gone. Light supplies that footing, slipping into the place that immediate appearing, in its invisible withdrawal, always leaves free. Thus the Beginning is forgotten. Because ecstasis first stands within itself, light, by an occultation that rebounds on its own origin, covers it and presents itself as the sole substance of phenomenality. "Le cogito est démembré" (the *cogito* is dismembered, p. 58): the seeming of the *videor* is abolished in that of the *videre*, I think means I see, and "thought" is no longer life but its opposite, knowledge. Henry explains the forgetting structurally, not psychologically: immanence is forgotten because it never appears in the light, so the light can always seem self-sufficient. This is the mechanism of "oubli" that the book opposes to Heidegger's forgetting of being.

The end of the Second Meditation, undermined by a deep contradiction, already shows how the *videor* is clouded over by the *videre*. On one hand Descartes recalls that knowledge of the soul is easier and older than that of the body, so all powers used to know body must first be known in themselves, by an immanent seeming. On the other, under pretext of grasping this knowledge of the soul better, since it is immanent to knowledge of the body, it is knowledge of the body that becomes the theme and guides the analysis. What that knowledge is gets determined by what is needed to know body: the vision of extension, or its idea, a vision of understanding rather than sense or imagination. The rivalry between these three faculties, now faculties of knowledge, leaves the field to seeing alone, grasped in its purity apart from the trouble and obscurity of sense and imagination. The Meditation closes paradoxically by excluding affectivity from appearing and reducing appearing to seeing, defined in turn by that exclusion, "comme si l'auto-affection originelle du voir ... avait cessé d'être un problème" (as if the original auto-affection of seeing had ceased to be a problem, p. 58), the problem of the *cogito* itself.

The Third Meditation accentuates the slide through a double occurrence that reverses the meaning of Descartes' procedure. First, the *cogito* is replaced by its relation to the *cogitatum*, and the *cogitatum* becomes the theme. Descartes does not yet aim to increase knowledge but to found it definitively, as if the *cogito* had not done so. The forgetting of the *videor* as the immediacy that reveals seeing to itself, indubitably, as its material reality, yields to another project: legitimating seeing mediately by divine veracity, to be read in the idea of God as a *cogitatum*. One must therefore inventory *cogitata*, secure them as such, withdraw them from the reduction, all while presupposing the infallibility of the seeing to be founded. The truth of the movement is this: the *cogitatum* now escapes the reduction by itself; being-thought, taken as it is given, is indubitable, and ideas such as that of God, in their objective reality, are beyond doubt provided one does not ask whether a reality corresponds to them. But being a *cogitatum qua cogitatum* means being seen. What validates any objective content is "l'être-vu comme tel" (being-seen as such, p. 59), and being-seen, taken as a pure condition independent of what is seen, is sight itself, seeing launched into the space of light opened by ecstasis. Once its original seeming is overlooked, the *cogito* is nothing more than such seeing. Its appearing is then identical with that of the *cogitatum*: one appearing runs through both, and the *cogito* is merely "le nom de l'apparaître du cogitatum" (the name for the appearing of the *cogitatum*, p. 60). We fail to notice this only because we retain in the *cogitatum* what is thought and not its condition. Henry here anticipates his critique of Husserl in Chapter III: an intentional *cogito* that is merely the correlative pole of its *cogitata* has no being of its own.

In Cartesian terms, the distinction between the formal and the objective reality of the idea, which seems to drive the Third Meditation, is also lost. If objective reality means the specific representational content (soul, God), it is easily distinguished from formal reality, the idea's belonging to thought. But if belonging to thought means being seen, being represented, and objective reality means the content in its objective condition, its being-seen as such, then the formal reality of the idea, of thought reduced to the form of representation, is just objective reality understood as the condition of objectivity. "Le cogito cartésien est déjà un cogito kantien" (the Cartesian *cogito* is already a Kantian *cogito*, p. 60). A general presupposition of Western philosophy comes to light: reduced to a seeing, to a condition of objectivity identical with its structure, "la subjectivité du sujet n'est plus rien d'autre que l'objectivité de l'objet" (the subjectivity of the subject is nothing but the objectivity of the object, p. 61). A long note fixes the terms: the "material reality" of the idea is the reality of the soul itself, its proper phenomenality identical with its being, and its "formal reality" is the same reality specified as a determinate thought; both are ontologically homogeneous with the soul, as the Third Meditation says (each idea requires no formal reality but that which it borrows from the mind of which it is a mode). The note sets the sense in which "formal reality" is used for the rest of the book. The final formula, subjectivity as the objectivity of the object, will define Kant's transcendental subject in Chapter IV and Heidegger's Dasein in Chapter III.

The Third Meditation, then, does more than shift attention from *cogito* to *cogitatum*: it reduces the *cogito* to the condition of the *cogitatum*, the *videor* to the ecstasis of the *videre*. This catastrophic reduction, in which the original being of subjectivity and life is abolished, is not merely surreptitious; it is claimed in the famous thesis that governs the method: the *cogito*, once established, is not only the first truth but the criterion of all truth. I am certain that I am a thinking thing; in this first knowledge there is nothing but a clear and distinct perception of what I know; hence the general rule that whatever I conceive very clearly and distinctly is true (AT IX, 27). What is this perception? Might it be the perception of a feeling, its self-revelation? It is now a perception of what I know, a seeing in which I see that the deceiver cannot make me nothing while I think I am something, or two and three make more or less than five, "ou choses semblables que je vois clairement" (or similar things that I see clearly, AT IX, 28, Henry's emphasis). The text repeats it: I cannot doubt what natural light makes me see is true, as it made me see that from my doubting I could conclude I was. Clear and distinct seeing is evidence. Evidence is the criterion of all truth, and the need for divine veracity to confirm it shows only that it is the sole possible criterion, the one to be secured. Henry's reading of the truth rule is the hinge of the chapter: the criterion of truth is drawn from the *cogito* only once the *cogito* has been reinterpreted as an evidence.

As the sole criterion of truth, the *cogito* is also one of the truths it grounds, the first: it has become an evidence. "Je vois que, de ce que je doute, je puis conclure que je suis" (I see that from my doubting I can conclude that I am, p. 62). An evidence here means the content of an evidence, an ontic content. As a truth, even the first, the *cogito* is no longer the transcendental condition of truth in general but is subject to it and presupposes it like all others: the first intuition in the chain of deduction, the first reason in the order of reasons. "Le cartésianisme des Regulae submerge celui des Méditations" (the Cartesianism of the *Regulae* submerges that of the *Meditations*). It does so on condition that the condition of all truth present itself as the first proposition of science, which it can do only because its condition continues to unfold its essence: it is in the natural light that I see that from doubting it follows that I exist. Henry thereby relocates Gueroult's "order of reasons" in the history of the forgetting: making the *cogito* the first link of a chain is already its loss.

Henry draws the distinction sharply. On one side, scientific knowledge, which thematizes particular truths and the first of them, the *cogito*, as philosophy's beginning; on the other, absolute knowledge, the appearing that makes scientific and philosophical knowledge possible. Thematized in philosophy, appearing remains its ground. *Cogito* means two things: a certain intuition, and its condition. But appearing, thought of as the condition of all knowledge, intuition and evidence, is nothing but the light of ecstasis. In this circle, where appearing is the condition of the privileged intuition the *cogito* is, and the *cogito* is the thematization of its own condition, where seeing is both form and content, nothing appears but seeing and its condition, the light in which its essential immediacy, the essence of life, is hidden. Hence the sentence that gives the book its subtitle: "dans le commencement de la philosophie, parce qu'il est un mode du savoir, le commencement s'est perdu" (in the beginning of philosophy, because it is a mode of knowledge, the beginning was lost, p. 63). The "lost beginning" is thus a precise structural claim: the Beginning is lost by being made the first item of knowledge.

But because immediacy is also the ultimate presupposition of knowledge, it does not let itself be forgotten so easily. Two features mark Cartesianism. As thought shifts from *cogito* to *cogitatum* and immanence is abolished in ecstasis, every concept of Cartesian phenomenology slides from its first sense, referring to the *videor*, toward a cognitive sense supplied by the "ob-jection" of the object. Yet original immediacy persists as their unnoticed ground, and Descartes' most brilliant insights point back to it. The result is an amphibology (an equivocation of concepts across two orders, in Kant's sense) affecting every key term (thought, idea, apperception, perception, natural light, evidence, clarity, distinction, confusion, obscurity) and making the discourse "proprement illisible" (strictly unreadable, p. 63) unless the radical dissociation of *videor* and *videre* is kept in mind. The rest of the chapter is a lexicon of this amphibology.

Immediacy is the target of Descartes' definition of thought, as shown, and as the naming of sensations, feelings and passions as "thoughts" suffices to recall. The Cartesian idea, in its original sense, means the same and must be understood, on pain of irreversible misreading, as fundamentally different from what we call an idea, a representation of a tree, a triangle, God. The Cartesian idea excludes representation, seeing, *intueri*; it is anything but an idea of the understanding, the aspect of what is uncovered in the light of ecstasis, the intelligible: "l'idée ou le sentiment de la douleur" (the idea or feeling of pain, *Principles* I, 46). Descartes affirms the absolute singularity of the idea of the mind, its difference from all ideas of things, sensible or intelligible: he no longer doubted he had a clear idea of his mind, which was present to him and so conjoined, and that this idea was entirely different from that of all other things (Sixth Replies, AT VII, 443). Henry reads "idea" against the Lockean "way of ideas" that made the Cartesian idea the paradigm of representation.

So essential is this singularity that Descartes twice gave a technical definition: by "idea" I mean "cette forme de chacune de nos pensées par la perception immédiate de laquelle nous avons connaissance de ces mêmes pensées" (that form of each of our thoughts by the immediate perception of which we are aware of these same thoughts; Second Replies, AT VII, 160). The idea is the immediate revelation of thought to itself: it throws each thought back into itself and discloses it to itself, revelation of thought itself and not of something other or objective. Only the idea so taken lets us understand its "formal reality": not something formal, the form of a content outside it, but, with no exteriority, what is one with its content, a content radically immanent and identical with the thought. This is why Descartes' examples of the idea as the original essence of thought are limited to immanent modes: when I will or fear, I conceive at once that I will and fear, so this willing and fearing are counted among ideas (Third Replies, AT VII, 181). Sensations, tickling, pain, feelings must be considered "comme des idées qui sont seulement en notre âme" (as ideas that are only in our soul, *Principles* I, 67). The definition by "immediate perception" is the Cartesian text closest to Henry's auto-affection; he reads "perception" here as the *videor*, not as an inner seeing.

The ultimate sense of the formal reality of the idea clarifies its "innateness". Innateness does not merely mean being in us before experience; it concerns the idea's nature as defined by a phenomenality that excludes ecstasis. It designates affectivity as the original dimension of appearing, such that whatever appears can do so only in this form, so that a being is never affective in itself, and placing an affective character in it is nonsense; at most a being can take on such a character in its appearing, in the formal reality of its idea. Descartes showed this for sense experience, the affective nature of sensations being innate, belonging to the essence of their phenomenality, not to the being supposed to cause them. The letter to Mersenne of 22 July 1641 says the sense organs bring us nothing like the idea awakened on their occasion, so "cette idée a dû être en nous auparavant" (this idea must have been in us beforehand, p. 66). Henry turns the innateness of sensory qualities, usually read as a thesis about origins, into a thesis about the ontological site of affectivity.

Descartes also means by idea its objective reality, its representational content. Strictly, not all ideas have such content, and according to the original concept they have none, as with sensations, volitions and passions. That thoughts exist without representational content, independently of seeing and its ecstasis, shows that the original dimension of phenomenality is not constituted by representation; this discovery made Cartesianism a philosophy of radical subjectivity and of life. Yet some thoughts present a representational content, and to these Descartes curiously reserves the name idea: some of my thoughts are like images of things, "et c'est à celles-là seules que convient le nom d'idée" (and to these alone the name idea is suited, AT IX, 29). Remarkably, representation, and hence ecstasis, appear as the mark of certain ideas only to be immediately devalued. This is the decisive and misunderstood claim that objective reality never coincides with reality and gives it only in image: "les idées sont en moi comme des tableaux ou des images" (ideas are in me like pictures or images, AT IX, 33), so that the ontological deficit of what is seen belongs to its being-seen, to representation and ecstasis. Kant's "progress" in identifying the conditions of the representation of the object with the conditions of the object, and Husserl's in affirming that intentional sight reaches being as it is, may be illusory: being given in representation, in its exteriority to itself, "ne peut précisément plus constituer l'être tel qu'il est en lui-même" (can precisely no longer constitute being as it is in itself, p. 67). Henry rehabilitates the "picture theory" of ideas usually treated as Descartes' error: its truth is that representation as such derealizes.

For Descartes, seeing, even when founded in the immediacy of its *videor* and so certain, remains struck by an ontological impotence: it reaches only being's double, its image. The mode of being by which a thing is objectively in the understanding through its idea is "imparfaite" (imperfect). The idea of the sun is not the sun "as it is in the sky" but the sun as it is "in the understanding", an unreal double, a copy. Following scholastic usage, Descartes calls the sun's reality its formal reality and its representation its objective reality; formal reality, reality as such, escapes objective reality by principle. "La représentation constitue et définit la dimension ontologique de l'irréalité" (representation constitutes and defines the ontological dimension of unreality, p. 67). The strange and much-contested thesis that Cartesian man is enclosed in his representations and cut off from things takes on a boundless meaning. First, it reaffirms the ontico-ontological difference, the inability of beings to accomplish revelation by themselves and their need to submit to it as to a foreign power: a being gives itself only in representation, as object, and is absorbed in it. What it is in itself, outside this space of light, escapes us, and only divine veracity can assure us that it is in itself as it is objectively in the understanding. Henry's equation of representation with unreality is the basis of the later analysis of the Freudian "representative" (*Vorstellungsrepräsentanz*): what is represented of the drive is precisely not its reality.

But formal reality in Descartes is not only or first that of the thing whose objective reality is in the understanding; it is the formal reality of the idea itself, the material reality of thought. That reality is not the objective reality of the idea, that it cannot present itself in ecstatic ob-jection, that ecstasis opens the medium of unreality while what escapes it is reality, the formal and substantial reality of thought, appearing in the immediacy of its self-appearing: this is "la définition ou la condition de la vie" (the definition or the condition of life, p. 68), which the Cartesianism of the Beginning keeps in view beyond all later misreadings. Hence divine veracity intervenes twice in constituted Cartesianism: once to guarantee the sight of what is seen, once to legitimate the belief that a formal reality of a thing in itself corresponds to what is seen. Likewise the ontological concept of finitude doubles: it designates not only the finitude of the place of ecstasis but, more radically, "l'irréalité principielle de ce Dimensional extatique" (the principial unreality of this ecstatic Dimension) and of all that appears in it. Finitude thus has two senses for Henry: the restriction of the visual field (one thing at a time) and, more deeply, the unreality of whatever is merely shown.

However unreal and finite the objective reality of the idea, it is what the Third Meditation thematizes; the modalities of representation now guide reflection and define its teleology. The idea becomes the title for a metaphysics of knowledge. With the idea taken in its objective reality, the phenomenality of seeing installs itself at the centre and claims to be the medium, if not of all revelation, at least of the only one in which knowledge and science can advance, finding there their objects and their own condition. Once its idea is considered in its objective reality, as a proposition of science and philosophy's beginning, the *cogito* means only a simple nature, thought, implying another, existence, the implication itself being a simple nature. Must not this proposition be thought, and does not the objective reality of the idea of the *cogito* presuppose its formal reality? But what is formal reality here, if not the light in which seeing is possible, in which "thought", "existence" and "the link that unites them" bathe? Once understood from its objective reality, the formal reality of the idea is lost, "confondue avec une condition de l'objectivité" (confused with a condition of objectivity, p. 69). The analysis of the *cogito* into simple natures follows *Regula* XII, where "I am, therefore God is" figures among necessary conjunctions.

Thus the basic concepts of phenomenality drift into amphibology, when their primordial sense is not definitively occulted. That sense, for the formal reality of the idea, referred exclusively to its immediacy, to its self-revelation as an immanent mode of thought like a pain or a volition, apart from objective reality, seeing and what is seen. But since this formal reality is also the condition of seeing in its own immediacy, it is also the condition of ecstatic seeing and everything it sees, of all objective reality. The reference of objective reality to formal reality as the reality of immediacy fades in favour of a reference to the form of seeing alone; formal reality, "la forme de la perception" (the form of perception), as Descartes also says, comes to designate only this form of seeing; "et l'amphibologie cède la place à l'occultation" (and amphibology gives way to occultation, p. 69). Henry distinguishes stages of the loss: equivocation, then forgetting. Descartes remains amphibolous; his successors are simply blind.

In this occultation the destiny of Western thought, and first of Cartesianism, is knotted. In the Third Replies Descartes repeats the essential definition: by "idea" I mean whatever is "la forme de quelque perception", for who conceives anything without perceiving that he conceives, and so has "this form or idea of intellection"? The context may mislead. Having called God "an infinitely intelligent substance", Descartes must answer Hobbes, who asks by what idea he conceives God's intellection (AT VII, 187–188). Descartes appeals to the original structure of the idea, the self-revelation constitutive of its formal reality. But since the idea in question is that of intellection, of conceiving something, *intueri* and its correlate, one easily imagines that the ultimate ground invoked is intellection in its specificity, all the more since the theme is the objective reality of the idea of God, to be exhibited in its objective components. The point is philological and precise: the definition is sound, the example misleading, and readers have taken the example for the definition.

But every problematic, every science and philosophy itself, obeys such a thematics: it aims objectively at a reality and readily takes the conditions of its knowledge for the conditions of reality. Concerning the Beginning, this confusion is precisely that of *videre* and *videor*, the absorption of the second into the first. In philosophical discourse, Cartesian first, the concepts of phenomenality float in total phenomenological indeterminacy; appearing becomes a formal concept again, and its metaphors ignore the irreducibility of its concrete effectuation. Or rather, since the concept cannot remain formal, "c'est le voir de l'ek-stasis ... qui lui fournit son contenu" (it is the seeing of ecstasis at work in knowledge that supplies its content, p. 70). A purely formal concept of phenomenality, the claim implies, is never really neutral: it is always tacitly filled by the model of seeing. This is Henry's charge against Husserl's and Heidegger's formal concepts of manifestation.

*Principles* I, §§ 29–66, offer a striking example of the constant sliding of phenomenal concepts from immanent to ecstatic meaning. Ecstasis governs the determination of appearing from the outset. God, not as the objective reality of an idea but as its condition, the transcendental condition of truth, identical with appearing and its ground, "most truthful", is "la source de toute lumière" (the source of all light, § 29). That this is the light of ecstasis is clear from its being the light of knowledge: what it illuminates takes the form of the object; its modes of illumination, as it concentrates on the object and holds it in view, are clarity and distinction; what is so seen is "true", manifest in this light. Hence the natural light God gave us "n'aperçoit jamais aucun objet qui ne soit vrai en ce qu'elle l'aperçoit" (never perceives any object that is not true insofar as it perceives it clearly and distinctly, § 30). God as light anticipates Malebranche's vision in God at the end of the chapter.

When, by contrast, the immanent modes of thought, "ideas" without objective reality, appear (§§ 39 and 41: sensation, feeling, will, freedom), the problematic faces the only modalities of appearing that escape both the reduction and ecstasis: they cannot show themselves in its light; interiority is their essence, and by a necessity stronger than amphibology the vocabulary itself points to it. The freedom of our will is known without proof, "par la seule expérience que nous en avons" (by the sole experience we have of it, § 39); we would be wrong to doubt what we perceive inwardly and know by experience to be in us (§ 41). Yet to circumscribe this phenomenality, prior to ecstasis and stronger than the reduction, Descartes uses the words of ecstasis, subsuming two irreducible orders under one monotonous terminology. "Apperception" means both the sight of the object in natural light and, amphibologically, the internal self-affection of immanent modes. The same concepts of clarity and distinction designate both revelations: when someone feels a sharp pain, his knowledge of it "est claire à son égard" (is clear to him, § 46); if he does not mix in the false judgment that inserts the pain in the wounded part, he perceives clearly only the feeling in him; and there remain "les sentiments, les affections et les appétits" (feelings, affections and appetites) of which we can have clear and distinct knowledge (§ 66). The texts on freedom matter because freedom, like the will in Chapter I, has no representational content; it is the purest case of a Cartesian "idea" that is pure self-experience.

In their original meaning, however, as § 45 defines and distinguishes them, clarity and distinction are modes of knowledge; they presuppose seeing and its ecstasis and qualify the ways it is accomplished, its attention, and correlatively the ways its object is offered. The famous text: knowledge must be not only clear but distinct; I call clear what is present and manifest to an attentive mind, "de même que nous disons voir clairement les objets lorsque, étant présents, ils agissent assez fort, et que nos yeux sont disposés à les regarder" (as we say we see objects clearly when, being present, they act strongly enough and our eyes are disposed to look at them); distinct, what is so precise and different from all else that it contains only what manifestly appears to one who considers it properly. Henry stresses the visual analogy within the definition itself: clarity is a property of an object to an attentive look.

How then could clarity, a modality of light, name what knows no light, "l'affection sans extase où se produit la vie" (the affection without ecstasis in which life occurs, p. 72)? It cannot. Restored in full, § 46 reads: he perceives nothing clearly but "le sentiment ou la pensée confuse qui est en lui" (the feeling or confused thought that is in him). Everyone understands having clear knowledge of a confused reality, provided they do not understand Descartes. He does not mean that one sees clearly that a reality is confused because the relations implied in it are not yet clearly seen, as when I see clearly that seven confusedly contains three and four. Clarity and confusion do not refer to two things, a clear knowledge and a confused content, but to one and the same property of one thing: clarity names the appearing of the feeling; confusion or obscurity, its phenomenological specificity, the matter of its phenomenality as affectivity. The clarity of feeling, of thought in its material reality, is identical with its confusion and has nothing to do with the clarity of knowledge and evidence, of the objective reality of the idea, which is opposed to its confusion yet bound to it by an essential law. The phrase "clear confused thought", a crux for Cartesian scholarship, receives Henry's most ingenious reading: not a contradiction or a two-level description, but the exact formula of auto-affection, manifest and non-luminous at once.

The radical elucidation of the phenomenal concepts that Cartesianism implicates and confuses, Henry says, can therefore be set out in two theses, numbered by him. The first: clarity, as identical with confusion and obscurity, indicates the immediacy of appearing, one essence, clear insofar as it accomplishes phenomenality, obscure insofar as the matter of that accomplishment is affectivity. Clarity and obscurity, identical in essence, cannot change into each other, being always "identiquement le Même" (identically the Same, p. 73): the original dimension in which life experiences itself in the invisible, so that nothing growing in it ever leaves it, nothing outside it ever enters it, so that "ce qui est vivant l'est à jamais" (what is living is so forever). The formula prepares Henry's reinterpretation of the Freudian thesis of the indestructibility of unconscious wishes (Chapter VI): what Freud ascribes to timeless unconscious representations belongs to life's inability to leave itself.

The second: clarity, as opposed to confusion and obscurity, is that of ecstasis, one essence, clear in opening the place where light concentrates, obscure in that this place is surrounded by shadow, the unthematizable horizon of all ecstatic exposition. A being is clear or obscure as it comes to the condition of object; it never bears these characters in itself, only in its ex-position. Clarity and confusion are thus pure phenomenological determinations consubstantial with ecstatic phenomenality. These opposed determinations never go one without the other, but pass into each other as beings pass through them. Thus is built the law of the world's phenomenality: each ontic determination reaches clarity only as another yields its place, and each can run through the continuous series of degrees from clarity to confusion and obscurity, a pure possibility prescribed by essence, every clear determination being able to become confused or obscure and conversely. Henry here gives the Leibnizian continuum of perceptions its phenomenological source, preparing the next section.

The obscurity that belongs to ecstasis as its horizon, where a being sinks when it leaves the place of presence, the limit-point and declining mode of the world's phenomenality, has nothing to do with the intrinsic obscurity of what knows no ecstasis. The first is readily converted into its opposite, the clarity of evidence, which is the teleology of Cartesian method and of all science. The second, "l'obscurité du sentiment et de la vie" (the obscurity of feeling and life, p. 74), rejects such a possibility by principle. Two "darknesses" must never be confused: the shadow of the horizon, which can become light, and the night of life, which is never meant to. Freud's unconscious, as the next unit shows, conflates them.

If *videre* establishes its primacy over *videor* in thought and casts it into oblivion (it unfolds necessarily as forgetful of its immediacy, which is never before its gaze), if the concept of consciousness that guides Western philosophy means exclusively seeing and its determinations, then "l'inconscient se définit à partir d'elles comme le mode limite de la phénoménalité du monde" (the unconscious is defined from them as the limit-mode of the world's phenomenality, p. 74), where all that was conscious is lost and from which its parts can re-emerge one by one. Consciousness reduced to seeing tends invincibly toward becoming conscious of this, and the teleology of knowledge and science is fixed. Meanwhile life continues its untiring self-coming in eternal withdrawal. It is the Obscure, and the Unconscious designates it too, in amphibology, so that what is now at issue cannot present itself as an object "et que toute prise de conscience est ici pur non-sens" (and every becoming-conscious is here sheer nonsense, p. 75). The unconscious is thus itself amphibolous: it names both the horizon-shadow of representation and the night of life. Henry's whole critique of Freud follows from separating them. "Where id was, there ego shall be" makes sense for the first, none for the second.

Moreover, by defining our being as appearing and the soul as thought, Cartesianism raised many problems. If the matter of the psyche is phenomenality, if, as the First Replies say categorically, nothing can be in me of which I am in no way conscious, where can the innate ideas that make up my mind's nature, or its multiple potentialities, be located? The more radically the soul is defined as consciousness, the more sharply it is contradicted: only a part of our being, the most superficial, comes into the light. Not only innate ideas but all our ideas escape conscious presence. And their temporality: what becomes of memories we no longer think of? This is the classic argument Freud invokes in his "Justification of the Unconscious": consciousness contains only a minimal content at any moment, so most of what we call conscious knowledge is latent for long periods, thus psychically unconscious; considering all our latent memories, "il deviendrait parfaitement inconcevable de contester l'inconscient" (it would become quite inconceivable to deny the unconscious; *Das Unbewußte*, I, GW X, 265–266). Henry's citation is strategic: Freud's first and most "obvious" argument for the unconscious is the Leibnizian argument from the finitude of attention.

If what is at stake is the finitude of the place of light, by which only "a minimal content" of beings is offered to consciousness while "the greatest part of what we call conscious knowledge" remains latent, then consciousness means the phenomenality of ecstasis and seeing within it, whose clear actualization implies the obscurity of its horizon. The objective reality of the idea and its transcendental condition are at issue. But if the psyche, insofar as it is something other than the empty form of seeing, designates its material reality, the reality of life in the radical immanence of auto-affection, where there is no ob-jection, object, finitude, horizon, knowledge or known, then Freud's argument does not concern it. "Quand elle prétend viser la vie ... la philosophie de l'inconscient est un non-sens" (when it claims to aim at life, the philosophy of the unconscious is nonsense, p. 76): the unconscious is what has not yet entered the light of ecstasis, can do so, then withdraw, all historial determinations from which life is exempt in principle. "Il n'y a pas d'opposition irréductible entre la conscience et l'inconscience, mais entre celles-ci et la vie" (there is no irreducible opposition between consciousness and unconsciousness, but between them and life). This is the book's most quoted formula. It reclassifies the Freudian unconscious as a mode of representational consciousness, its horizon, and sets life outside the whole conscious–unconscious axis.

Descartes had mocked the attempt to reduce the soul's phenomenality to that of contents actually perceived and juxtaposed in the light of ecstasis. To Revius, who objected that infants in the womb lack an actual notion of God, he replied that he had never written or thought that such ideas were actual, or "des espèces distinctes de la faculté même que nous avons de penser" (species distinct from the very faculty we have of thinking; *Notae in programma*, AT VIII-2, 366). What is at issue is not that consciousness can hold only one representational content clearly at a time while others remain virtual, but the dimension of phenomenality in which this necessarily happens. For the soul to hold its whole being in its own appearing, one must leave aside objective reality, where ideas can be exhibited only one after another, and consider the power that produces them all. When I say an idea is innate, I do not mean it is always present to thought, "car ainsi il n'y en aurait aucune" (for then there would be none), but that we have the faculty of producing it (Third Replies, AT VII, 189). The dispositional account of innateness, often read as a concession to empiricism, becomes for Henry the decisive alternative to a reservoir model of the unconscious.

The shift from objective reality to the power of producing ideas removes the finitude that requires representational contents to be actualized successively only if it is first a shift from objective to formal reality. Because the soul is wholly present to itself in its formal reality, in radical immanent auto-affection, the power of producing ideas is wholly present to itself as well. Ideas or memories we no longer think of therefore do not reside "dans le réceptacle d'un inconscient grossièrement imaginé par Freud, par Bergson et par tant d'autres" (in the receptacle of an unconscious crudely imagined by Freud, Bergson and so many others, p. 77). Their only existence is potential, their capacity to be produced by a power; their phenomenological status is that of the power, the invisible self-immanence in which every power and force, and life's superabundant potency, forms and comes into itself. Henry's theory of memory here, power to reproduce rather than store of traces, will govern his reading of Freud's mnemic traces and of Bergson's *Matter and Memory* in Chapter IX and "Potentiality".

The scholastic determinations by which, with Descartes, one tries to think the soul's essence or the being of things escape confusion only when taken up by the fundamental structures of phenomenality recognized in the *cogito*. Actuality, virtuality, potency, faculty always have two senses, "et la philosophie commence avec leur dissociation" (and philosophy begins with their dissociation, p. 77). When a virtual representation becomes actual, the determinations of ecstatic phenomenality are at stake: an intuitive content enters the light, stands before the gaze, becomes its theme. When it leaves the centre of the clearing, reaches the margins of consciousness, finally crosses the horizon of all presence, it is virtual again. Virtuality then designates the "fabulous place" invented by mythologies of the unconscious to preserve for what was exposed in the space of ecstasis its standing and consistency, with the same characteristics, once it is no longer there, as if presence consisting in ob-jection and ex-position could persist without them. The ex-posed and juxtaposed, "l'extatique-horizontal" (the ecstatic-horizontal), defines the general law of being even without ecstasis and horizon, without the light of consciousness they produce. "Conscient et inconscient sont le Même" (conscious and unconscious are the Same, p. 78): the ex-posed and juxta-posed, except that in the second they lack the light belonging to all exposition. Actuality and potentiality likewise have the same structure, with consciousness in the first case and without it in the second, "comme si la conscience était indifférente à cette structure qui la constitue" (as if consciousness were indifferent to the very structure that constitutes it). This is the formal critique of Freud's topographical unconscious in a single argument: a representation that persists unconscious is a representation with the light switched off, which is incoherent, since representation just is the light.

Life never actualizes itself, never enters the finite place of light, but holds itself wholly outside it in the immediacy of its omnipresence to itself. For life, actuality, virtuality and potentiality have another sense: actuality designates the auto-affection in which potentiality is effective, the reality of the possibility consubstantial with every power. Actual is not only what comes for a moment to the condition of something standing-over-against, but more essentially what never enters it, what persists in its "infrangible attachement à soi" (unbreakable attachment to itself, p. 78): life's untiring accomplishment. Depending on whether one aims at the ecstasis of the soul's seeing or the seeming in which seeing remains eternally in itself, one cannot ask the soul the same questions. Of the intuition of the *cogito* and its evidence, one may ask, as of all that is exposed to a seeing: "cela est certain, mais combien de temps ?" (this is certain, but for how long? AT IX, 21). But if the *cogito* is understood not as an intuition but as what excludes all intuition and evidence, as "l'essence sans visage de la vie" (the faceless essence of life), then of him who is so considered in the material reality of his own thought one must say that he cannot fail to know it in himself "par une expérience continuelle et infaillible" (by a continual and infallible experience; Sixth Replies, AT VII, 427). Henry turns the Second Meditation's temporal restriction of certainty ("as long as I think") into a mark of the ecstatic *cogito* only; the immanent *cogito* is continual, a thesis that grounds his rejection of Husserl's temporalizing of the living present in Chapter III.

The immediate posterity of the *cogito* testifies to the amphibology of its concepts. Leibniz sensed at once that life does not resolve into the clarity of knowledge, that it has a nocturnal dimension irreducible to the light the *Regulae* had made the condition of science and, more deeply, of man's being and relation to the world. But instead of examining this oldest essence of being and life, he kept his gaze on being-in-the-world and imagined it deprived of its innermost condition yet still doing its work in deploying and maintaining the universe. "Au lieu de dire : il y a une aperceptio sans perceptio, il déclara au contraire : il y a une perceptio sans aperceptio" (instead of saying there is an apperception without perception, he declared on the contrary that there is a perception without apperception, p. 79). We are never without perceptions but often without apperceptions, namely when there are no distinguished perceptions (*New Essays*, II, 19). "En même temps qu'une définition totalement erronée de la vie et justement comme son origine, le concept qui allait devenir plus tard le concept opératoire de la psychanalyse, celui de perception inconsciente, était né" (together with a wholly erroneous definition of life, and as its origin, the concept that would become the operative concept of psychoanalysis, unconscious perception, was born). The chiasmus is Henry's thesis in miniature: the truth is apperception without perception, life without representation; Leibniz inverts it into representation without self-experience, and that inversion is the Freudian unconscious.

With *perceptio* without *aperceptio*, and the claim that such perception is unconscious, Leibniz advances "la thèse la plus philosophique et la plus antiphilosophique" (the most philosophical and most anti-philosophical thesis, p. 79) of Western thought, the one that weighed most on its destiny. That every *perceptio* without *aperceptio* is unconscious means that no perception is possible or subsists by itself, no seeing can see anything unless it first reveals itself to itself as seeing, in apperception. Leibniz senses that there is no thought without a formal reality of thought, without this original apperception, only to misunderstand its nature by reducing it to that of perception itself, so that the decisive intuition of immediate appearing glimpsed in Cartesianism "est aussitôt perdue" (is at once lost, p. 80). The thesis is philosophical because it recognizes that perception needs apperception; anti-philosophical because it conceives apperception as one more perception.

Can one even speak of reducing apperception to perception? Leibniz in fact knows only perceptions, of two kinds: perceived or "notable" ones (pain is a "notable perception"), and unperceived, obscure or unconscious ones. Why are the latter unperceived? Because "trop petites pour être aperçues" (too small to be noticed, *New Essays*, II, 20); too small because too many, crowding the closed space of light that every *perceptio* bears. The finitude of ecstasis, excluding all or nearly all beings, makes them both too many and too small. Why do beings crowd into the clearing? Because to the phenomenological conception of perception Leibniz adds a psychophysical, prescientific one: the soul, always wholly united to its body without limit and through it to all bodies in the universe, is ceaselessly affected by them, receiving a multitude of impressions it perceives without noticing. Two conceptions of affection are superimposed. One is crudely realist, unaware that to affect means to give to be felt, to appear; it is ontic, outside the reduction, presupposes the union and identifies affection with the "action" or "impression" of one body on another or on the soul, missing the double sense of "impression". The other is ontological, resting on the finitude of seeing. Their confusion produces the theory of insensible, obscure or unconscious perceptions: "expressing" the body's impressions, the soul's impressions make up its infinitely rich content, which it cannot hold entirely in its finite view, so it attends only to some, the rest being like objects around us in sleep: objects always strike our eyes and ears, and the soul is touched "sans que nous y prenions garde : parce que notre attention est bandée à d'autres" (without our noticing, because our attention is bent on others, *New Essays*, II, 1). The double sense of "impression" (physical imprint and felt impression) is the same equivocation Henry found in the *Project*'s "excitation" in the prologue, and he will track it in Freud.

The drift of the concept of apperception becomes evident. The problem of thought's self-immanence in the *videor* is posed when Philalethes says it is hard to conceive that a thing can think "et ne pas sentir qu'elle pense" (and not feel that it thinks), and Theophilus replies that this is "the crux of the matter". Leibniz establishes the possibility, indeed necessity, of thought without apperception not by analysing apperception but by setting it aside and replacing it with the finitude of seeing. Because to apperceive means to see, the content held in the finite horizon overflows it and is lost in the night. Thought can think without feeling that it thinks because "nous pensons à quantité de choses à la fois mais ne prenons garde qu'aux pensées qui sont le plus distinguées" (we think of many things at once but attend only to the most distinguished thoughts). To be without apperceptions is to be without distinguished perceptions. The reduction of apperception to perception, and to distinct perception, becomes blinding when apperceiving means reflecting on, Theophilus's ultimate argument: we cannot always reflect expressly on all our thoughts, or the mind would reflect on each reflection to infinity and never pass to a new thought; in noticing a present feeling I would have to think that I think of it, and that I think of thinking of it, and so on; "il faut bien ... qu'il y ait enfin quelque pensée qu'on laisse passer sans y penser" (there must finally be some thought one lets pass without thinking of it, *New Essays*, II, 1, Henry's emphasis). The regress argument Henry used in Chapter I against a reflective *videor* is here found in Leibniz, but drawn to the opposite conclusion: Leibniz infers unconscious thought; Henry infers non-reflective self-revelation.

That "some thought" must pass without being thought follows from the impossibility for perception or reflection to disclose the soul's whole content. But this impossibility is far more radical than Leibniz imagines. It does not tolerate even the exception of the content we actually perceive or reflect on; it excludes a priori every content of this kind, "parce que l'aperception n'est ni un voir partiel ni le voir total d'une intuition infinie, mais l'exclusion de tout voir possible en général" (because apperception is neither a partial seeing nor the total seeing of an infinite intuition, but the exclusion of all possible seeing, p. 82), the dimension of radical immanence where ecstasis does not occur. Henry accepts Leibniz's premise and radicalizes it: not some, but all thought escapes reflection, and that is why all thought is self-revealed.

Malebranche's position was far more pertinent and profound. He grasped at once the essential dichotomy of the structures of phenomenality: that *cogito* means not one thing but two, different and opposed, so that their co-belonging at the origin is one of philosophy's major problems. Is it not extraordinary that "le plus cartésien des cartésiens" (the most Cartesian of Cartesians, p. 82) said of the phenomenon Descartes made his starting point exactly the opposite of what Descartes had said: that the *cogito* is not an evidence, let alone the clearest, but "un abîme d'obscurité" (an abyss of obscurity); not a knowledge, let alone the first, but that of which we have no conceivable knowledge; that the soul is not easier to know than the body but unknowable; that what can be known of it is known by analogy with the body; that the idea of the soul cannot ground knowledge because, ultimately, it does not exist? Yet more surprising: with these propositions, opposed term by term to Cartesian theses, Malebranche, far from departing from them, offers "pour la première fois et peut-être la dernière, une répétition radicale" (for the first and perhaps the last time, a radical repetition, p. 83) of them, going to the most initial point of the beginning Descartes had glimpsed. Henry's reading of Malebranche as the radical repeater of the *cogito*, rather than an occasionalist dissident, runs parallel to Alquié's *Le cartésianisme de Malebranche* (1974), though Henry places the radicality in the inner feeling rather than in the vision in God.

That the soul is "obscure", in Malebranche's repeated words, means first that it is not illuminated by the light of ecstasis, because it does not bear ecstasis in itself and is not constituted by it. The soul as obscure escapes by principle the world's phenomenality. It is not nothing: expelling transcendental exteriority does not push it into the nothingness of non-phenomenality but lets the effectiveness of first appearing show in its phenomenological materiality, designating it in its radical interiority as affectivity. Whereas bodies and their properties are known by their ideas, "nous ne la connaissons point par son idée ... nous ne la connaissons que par conscience" (we do not know the soul by its idea, we know it only by consciousness; *Recherche*, III, 2, 7, § 4). This consciousness, exclusive of *videre* and identical with the seeming of *videor*, is an "inner feeling" by which we feel what happens in us, such that nothing happens in us that we do not feel by a feeling constitutive of the soul and all its modifications, "toutes les choses qui ne peuvent être dans l'âme sans qu'elle les aperçoive par le sentiment intérieur qu'elle a d'elle-même" (all the things that cannot be in the soul without its perceiving them by the inner feeling it has of itself; *Recherche*, III, 2, 1, § 1). The Cartesian *aperceptio* thus receives, "en tant que trouvant sa structure dans l'intériorité et sa substantialité phénoménologique dans l'affectivité" (as finding its structure in interiority and its phenomenological substance in affectivity, p. 84), a radical ontological determination. Malebranche's distinction between knowledge "by idea" and knowledge "by consciousness or inner feeling" is the historical anticipation of Henry's distinction between ecstatic and immanent manifestation.

Unfortunately Malebranche too could not stay on this summit. The very radicality of his intuition of the soul's immanence and of the exclusion of representation from it led him to the paradox that all determinations taking the form of representation must be excluded from this sphere: "les idées qui nous représentent quelque chose hors de nous ne sont point des modifications de notre âme" (ideas that represent something outside us are not modifications of our soul; *Recherche*, III, 2, 7, § 4). He misses the ultimate intuition of the *cogito*: that the original seeming in which seeing appears to itself and is an effective seeing, "un voir qui se sent voir" (a seeing that feels itself see), gathers and essentializes itself precisely in immanence. By denying representations their inherence in the soul, their formal reality in favour of their objective reality alone, and no longer seeing that the self-interiority of ecstasis is its unsurpassable condition, Malebranche inaugurates the inextricable situation in which exteriority, left to itself, hypostatized and resting on itself, raises the question of its receptivity: how could any "subject" open to it and hold its gaze there? Rather, the problem of this subject and its subjectivity, which is only the self-immanence of ecstasis, is left without solution. Malebranche makes the opposite error to Leibniz: Leibniz reduces apperception to perception; Malebranche separates them so completely that perception no longer belongs to anyone. The "intelligible extension" of the vision in God is ecstasis without a self.

Moreover, even when Malebranche recognizes the soul's essence in radical immanence, he remains prisoner of the prejudice that reduces all phenomenality to ecstasis. Setting ecstasis aside no longer leads to the original dimension of appearing but signals its absence, an insurmountable ontological deficit. The soul's obscurity then becomes its intrinsic non-phenomenality, a kind of brute facticity: a "sentiment confus qui te frappe mais ... sentiment sans lumière, qui ne peut t'éclairer" (confused feeling that strikes you, but feeling without light that cannot enlighten you, *Méditations chrétiennes*, IX, § 18, Henry's emphasis). Insofar as it makes itself felt without making itself known, feeling no longer accomplishes revelation by itself but asks it of a foreign power; it is no longer the self-feeling of auto-affection but, asking another instance to reveal it, is in itself "blind": "sa situation est redevenue celle d'un étant" (its situation has become that of a being again, p. 85). To what power must feeling now appeal? To the idea, to ecstasis. No philosophy situates pure phenomenality more explicitly or exclusively in exteriority as such, transcendental exteriority, "intelligible extension". Malebranche's "felt but not known" is the formula of the Freudian affect without representation, which "needs" to be bound to a representation in order to be known.

The thesis that we have no idea of the soul thus changes meaning entirely. It no longer determines apodictically the inner structure of revelation; entrusting revelation to the power of exteriority, it states assertorically that the soul in fact lacks this power and is delivered to night: "je ne suis que ténèbres à moi-même" (I am only darkness to myself; *Méditations chrétiennes*, IX, § 15). That all phenomenality consists in the light of ecstasis, which Malebranche calls vision in God, appears in the fact that the soul itself can be illuminated only by exposing itself in an external image, its archetype in God; the soul's true being and light is its own exteriority to itself, its Idea, which exists, but in God, in exteriority. God has merely refused us its contemplation on earth, since its splendour would distract us from our daily tasks ("if you had the idea of your soul, you could think of nothing else", *Méditations chrétiennes*, IX, § 20). "Le philosophe qui s'est le plus approché du Commencement est aussi celui qui s'en écarte le plus" (the philosopher who came closest to the Beginning is also the one who departs from it most, p. 86). Malebranche, like Leibniz, belongs to the destiny of Western metaphysics, whose historial determination is "l'occultation du videor par le videre" (the occultation of the *videor* by the *videre*). The chapter's two heirs mark the two paths by which the Cartesian beginning is lost: Leibniz makes the soul's depth an unconscious representation, Malebranche makes it a darkness whose light lies outside it. Freud, Henry will argue, combines both.

## Chapter III. The Insertion of the "Ego Cogito" into the "History of Western Metaphysics"

With Heidegger the denaturing of the *cogito* is immediate and complete. The beginning essence of phenomenality held in view in the *videor* is not even reduced to or confused with that of the *videre*; its existence is never considered or suspected: "je pense veut dire je me représente" (I think means I represent to myself, p. 87). Heidegger's case against Descartes must therefore be placed: it concerns not the original *cogito* but its decline, the moment the Beginning was already lost. Henry states the stakes. He is not interested in whether Heidegger's reading is historically contestable or partial, but in what is at stake in it, the ultimate nature of truth and being. Heidegger inscribes his reading in a history of Western metaphysics paradoxically identified with that of being, in which the *cogito* determines "in a new way the essence of knowledge and truth" (*Nietzsche* II). This is not an ideological shift: on Heidegger's account the essence of being itself changes. The target text throughout is the chapter "Die Metaphysik als Geschichte des Seins" and the analysis of the *cogito* in *Nietzsche* II (1961), which Henry cites in Klossowski's French translation.

What is this change? The ground of all things, the *hypokeimenon*, "das von sich aus schon Vorliegende" (what already lies before from itself), the *subjectum*, is now interpreted as man. In each epoch being sends itself to us by withdrawing its ownmost being; with the *cogito*, inaugurating modern metaphysics, this clouding reaches its extreme, when man takes himself for the *subjectum*, usurping the prerogatives of being. Man posits himself as the ground of truth, the centre and measure of beings in every domain. This "liberation of man for the new freedom" rejects any other truth (revealed, biblical, Christian) and extends man's "autonomous legislation" to all beings; but because this legislation must be "sure of itself", man must first make sure of himself. The *cogito* claims this self-foundation of man as the self-assurance, the self-certainty, of the ground of all truth. Henry reconstructs Heidegger fairly before contesting him; the vocabulary of *Sicherung* and *Gewissheit* is Heidegger's.

Measured by what it actually aimed at, the *cogito* excludes man rather than placing him at the centre, and does so in the reduction: what remains has no eyes, ears, body or belonging to the world, only first appearing in the pure phenomenological effectiveness of its self-appearing. The idea of man appears in constituted Cartesianism only later, once the gaze has slid from *cogito* to *cogitatum* and, in the system of representation, one *cogitatum*, the strange idea of God, shows that this system does not support itself. "L'homme n'intervient dans le cartésianisme qu'au moment où il se découvre fini, ens creatum" (man enters Cartesianism only when he discovers himself finite, a created being, p. 89), not at all as a ground. Henry turns Heidegger's anthropological reading against him: in Descartes "man" appears with the Third Meditation's finitude, not with the *cogito*.

How, by Heidegger's own account, does man claim this role? Not as man but as *cogito*. What claims to be the *fundamentum inconcussum veritatis* is not a being but an ontological structure, the structure of pure phenomenality. In Heidegger's commentary "man" arbitrarily interferes with the Cartesian "subject" or with subjectivity dubbed "human". The only question is that of the essence of phenomenality, of "thought", and of its possible reduction to representation. The distinction between subjectivity and man is axiomatic for Henry: subjectivity is a mode of manifestation, not an anthropological fact, and "humanism" is a misdescription of it.

That reduction, for Heidegger, characterizes Cartesianism and modern subjectivity. *Cogitare* means *percipere*, "taking possession of something ... disposing it toward oneself, in the manner of setting something before oneself, 're-presenting' it". Because they mean "setting before oneself", *cogitare* and *percipere* mean the same as *Vorstellung* in its double sense, *vorstellen* and *Vorgestelltes*, the bringing-before-oneself and what has been brought before oneself, "in the widest sense what is made 'visible'". In this disposing, the represented is not just given but placed at one's disposal, secured as what man can master. Because of its link with the *dubitare* it rejects yet springs from, *cogitare* relates to the indubitable: its setting-before admits nothing as surely posited, as true, that has not been sanctioned before it as such. But if representing is "a setting in full security", what makes it possible, "what must be posited as secure?" (p. 90). Heidegger's hyphenated *Vor-stellen* is the model for Henry's own hyphenations (*ob-jet*, *pro-poser*); Henry adopts the analysis of representation and denies only that it is the essence of thought.

A decisive question has so far been passed over, as Cartesianism itself and all modern philosophy passed over it: the question of the ego. What does it mean that the regression to the beginning and universal principle of all things ends in positing an ego, and a particular one, who says not "it is thought" or "there is" but "I think, I am"? Is it because this movement is carried out by an empirical individual, Descartes or anyone who repeats it? But the ego arises with the *cogito*, at the end of the reduction and through it, when there is no longer any empirical individual or world. "Ego cogito veut tout dire sauf l'homme" (*ego cogito* means anything but man, p. 90). It means that in the original upsurge of appearing ipseity is implied as its very essence and innermost possibility. Descartes expressed this contemporaneity of phenomenality and ipseity only thus: it is so manifest of itself that it is I who doubt, understand and desire "qu'il n'y a rien par quoi cela puisse être expliqué avec plus d'évidence" (that nothing can explain it more evidently; Second Meditation, AT IX, 22). The ego question opens the chapter's central thesis: selfhood belongs to the essence of manifestation, not to a being called man. This is the core of *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 50 onward, on ipseity as auto-affection.

How much beginnings matter in philosophy appears from this: though Descartes referred the link of ipseity and thought to what is most ultimate in thought, because he did not pursue its elucidation further the being of the ego remained totally undetermined, inviting the most gratuitous and contradictory assertions throughout the history of philosophy, down to Husserl, Sartre and Merleau-Ponty, "pour ne rien dire de l'évacuation du « sujet » ... par les sous-pensées groupées sous le titre de « structuralisme »" (to say nothing of the eviction of the "subject" by the sub-thoughts grouped under the title "structuralism", p. 91). Kant himself, as Chapter IV will show, could not ground the simple proposition "I am". Heidegger's commentary in *Nietzsche* II is valuable because it faces this question, not ontically as that of man, despite appearances, but as tied to the pure essence of phenomenality. When the *cogito*, reduced to "I represent", must be grounded, and the question arises "what must be posited as secure?", the ego answers, not simply as joined to representation but as its intrinsic possibility and what makes it certain. Every *ego cogito* is *cogito me cogitare*: every "I represent something" represents "me", who represents something; all human representing is "a 'self'-representing", as Heidegger puts it, in a way that "lends itself easily to misunderstanding". Henry singles out Heidegger as the one modern thinker who connects the ego to the essence of phenomenality, which makes his error instructive rather than merely external.

This does not mean that in every representation the self presents itself as a correlate, that representing Freiburg cathedral I must also represent myself beside it, vaguely, as an object. More essentially, by a necessity of essence, the self that represents projects itself before itself and implicates itself in its representation: everything represented is represented to the self, before it, toward it. The self is thus presupposed in all representation, not a posteriori as an object it discovers, but "a priori comme appartenant à la constitution du champ" (a priori as belonging to the constitution of the field, p. 92) in which discovery occurs, since the field is thrown by the self, before it, toward it; this back-reference to the self is identical with the structure and opening of the field. Henry gives Heidegger's analysis its strongest form: the self as the transcendental pole of the field of representation, close to Kant's "I think" that must be able to accompany all my representations.

Because he confuses the self with man, Heidegger can write that in every act of representing it is man who represents to himself, to whom the represented is disposed, so that man represents himself along with what he represents, "not afterward but in advance", since he brings the represented before himself each time. If the self is thus implied as the "toward which" of everything represented, then every consciousness of object is identically and first self-consciousness, consciousness of the self thrown to the horizon of representation as its ground. The Self underlies representation, stretching beneath it as that from which it springs and to which it returns. "Pour l'acte de représenter ... le Soi de l'homme est essentiellement ce qui en constitue le fondement. Le Soi est subjectum" (for representing, the Self of man is essentially its ground; the Self is *subjectum*, p. 92). The equivalence "all consciousness of object is self-consciousness" is the thesis of Kant's transcendental deduction; Heidegger reads it as the Cartesian legacy, and Henry will grant the equivalence while reversing its order of grounding.

Thus a theory of the ego and of the "I am" is formed, offered as an explanation of *ego cogito ergo sum*. Again it springs from, and merely reads off, the decisive presupposition that *cogito* means "I represent". There is no inference from *cogito* to *sum* but the recognition, in the structure of representation, of the "I" that necessarily unfolds in it and ultimately is identical with it. Because every object in its representation is ob-jected, op-posed to the one who represents it, the representer is already there as the one who has disposed the object toward himself and so, more fundamentally, has disposed himself: by this self-disposition, man "can say 'I'". Leaving "man" aside, there is in fact no inference, since the *sum* of the representer, his disposition toward himself in his representation, is identical with it. *Cogito* and *sum* mean the same: I dispose toward myself, I represent. The "I" of "I am" is no less known in representing than the represented object, and "no induction, however conclusive, could ever reach the certainty" of this self-disposition (p. 93). Henry and Heidegger agree that the *ergo* is not an inference. They disagree about what the identity of *cogito* and *sum* consists in: self-representation for Heidegger, self-affection for Henry (Chapter I).

Nowhere in Descartes is there the slightest hint of a problematic in which ipseity depends on and is intelligible from the structure of representation. The brief, enigmatic and brilliant irruption of the ego in the Second Meditation occurs at the last moment of the reduction, when doubt is alone in the world, when there is no world and so no representation. Descartes then holds "un élément purement immanent réduit à lui-même" (a purely immanent element reduced to itself, p. 94), to its material reality apart from all objective reality, and reads in it the ipseity of the ego, as identical with its essence, the supreme essence, since nothing could explain it more evidently: "tam manifestum est ut nihil occurrat per quod evidentius explicetur". That essence is the essence of manifestation. Henry restates the double evidence to be restored: the original essence of phenomenality excludes "representedness", and precisely by this exclusion it essentializes itself as a Self. The Latin phrase becomes a proof text: Descartes says the ego cannot be explained by anything more manifest, so it is not derived from a prior structure such as representation.

For the first part, the text of the *Passions* (art. 26) suffices. Pushing the reduction to its limit, it holds that whatever is represented, far from being secured by representation, is doubtful, as whatever I seem to see or imagine in pure representation, "in a dream"; whereas, asleep or dreaming, one cannot feel sad or moved by a passion without its being very true that the soul has it. The immanence of affective determination, sadness, the material reality of the idea, alone is the site of absolute certainty and truth, which, as self-certainty and self-legitimating truth, consist in the first self-appearing of appearing.
