Representation has nothing to do with this original upsurge: a pain is wholly what it is in the immanence of its affectivity without first being set before itself. "Pour être certaine de soi elle n'a pas besoin de cette venue à l'ob-stance, sa souffrance lui suffit" (to be certain of itself it needs no coming into ob-stance; its suffering suffices, p. 94). Considered in itself, apart from its being represented in the body or the object, sensation receives its being in the auto-affection of its affectivity. In the exteriority of representation, outside thought's immanence, "nous ne concevons en aucune façon quelle chose c'est que cette couleur, cette douleur" (we have no conception of what this colour, this pain is; *Principles* I, 68). The pain example carries the argument because pain is the clearest case of a phenomenon whose certainty owes nothing to an object-pole.

The Cartesian critique of secondary qualities reveals its "abyssal" meaning: a final division between what is dead and what is living. Sensations are life; they grow where life lavishes its being, where there is no ecstasis or world, in the radical interiority Descartes calls the soul. Henry concedes to the phenomenologists that there are transcendent qualities (the sky is blue, the river serene) and that it does seem my foot hurts. But the quality spread on the thing, colour on the surface, pain in the foot, is only "la représentation irréelle, l'ob-jection d'une impression réelle vivante" (the unreal representation, the ob-jection, of a real living impression, p. 95), which affects and impresses itself only in its affectivity. Where the self-feeling that makes it a pure affective tonality occurs, there is no space, neither of the thing nor of the organic body into which the impression is projected. Three proofs: in dreams there is no real space, yet the wall seems yellow; the amputee feels pain in a foot he lacks, so pain has no being but its self-impressing; most radically, pure feelings such as sadness and joy have no objective reality, only material reality, and cannot owe their being to a representation they do not contain. Henry rehabilitates the much-criticized Cartesian doctrine of secondary qualities, not as physics but as ontology: it locates sensation's reality in life, not in the world. Merleau-Ponty's description of qualities as belonging to the perceived world is conceded at the level of appearance and denied at the level of reality.

Heidegger's claim that representing (*percipere*, *co-agitare*, *cogitare*, *repraesentare in uno*) is a basic feature of all human comportment, "even non-cognitive", must be not only contested but reversed. In Descartes non-cognitive "comportments" (feelings, passions, will) are wholly foreign to representing, and this heterogeneity defines the original dimension of the *cogito*. Hence cognitive comportments too, insofar as they belong to the *cogito*, the idea in its formal reality as a mode of the soul, ignore representing. Heidegger's parenthesis is an amalgam: the original *perceptio* and *cogitatio* have nothing to do with *repraesentare in uno*, as shown by the definitions of *cogitatio* by immediacy, by the many uses of *perceptio* for immanence (the perceptions caused by the soul "are the perceptions of our volitions"; passions are "perceptions, feelings or emotions of the soul referred particularly to it", *Passions*, arts. 19 and 27), and by the whole problematic of the *videor*. Henry turns Heidegger's etymological gloss, *co-agitare* as gathering-before, against the lexical evidence of Descartes' own uses of *perceptio*.

If one cannot go back further than this principial immediacy, it must also ground the essence of ipseity. That Descartes did not elucidate this ultimate occurrence does not prevent him from locating in it, in thought's most primitive seeming, what makes thought not only being or "there is" but an "I think, I am". The original phenomenalization of phenomenality occurs as ipseity insofar as appearing appears to itself in immediate auto-affection without distance, independently of ecstasis and representation: what affects it and shows itself to it is itself, its own reality and nothing unreal, so that, affecting itself and constituting the content of its auto-affection, it is a Self, "le Soi de l'ipséité et de la vie" (the Self of ipseity and of life, p. 96). "Car le Soi est l'identité de l'affectant et de l'affecté" (for the Self is the identity of the affecting and the affected, p. 97): the being in which there is nothing but itself, which is itself all that it is. Descartes calls this the soul; Henry calls it life. Life experiences itself, and whatever affects it does so only on the prior condition that it affects itself. Everything living bears this essence, and only the living can be affected by anything other, by the world. This is the book's definition of the Self, stated in its most compact form: ipseity is the identity of affecting and affected. The last clause matters for Freud: a drive "affecting" the psyche presupposes a psyche that first affects itself.

Because ipseity resides in the original essence of thought, in the *videor* that precedes and enables every *videre*, the claim to ground the ego on the seeing of representation is a paralogism, all the more misleading because it can invoke an "appearance" that must be read and deconstructed. It is true that every representing, as a representing to oneself, as an act of setting-against, implies that the representer projects itself to the horizon of its act as that toward which everything opposed is opposed. But "ce n'est pas parce que l'ob-jeté ... s'oppose à lui que le représenter est un Soi, c'est parce qu'il est un Soi ... qu'il peut se représenter ce qu'il se représente" (representing is not a Self because the object is opposed to it; it can represent what it represents because it is a Self, p. 97), can project itself beyond the opposed, can and must first oppose itself to itself, so that all consciousness of object is self-consciousness. The Self is implied in representation as its *subjectum* only because it is presupposed by it, as what representation does not produce or explain but presupposes as its other, "comme le fondement qu'elle est impuissante à fonder" (the ground it is powerless to ground). "Paralogism" is a deliberate Kantian word: Heidegger's derivation of the self from representation is the counterpart of the rational psychologist's derivation of a substance from the "I think", and Chapter IV will turn the charge on Kant himself.

Every proposition in which Heidegger ties the ego to representation and derives it from it must be reversed. To the claim that in the *ego cogito* the "I" is understood as the self to which representing as such essentially returns, and so is what it is, Henry replies that it is because the I is already understood, already essentialized in itself outside the structure of representing, that representing "is thus what it is": what is already in possession of itself as a Self in order to represent anything. The reversal is grammatical as much as philosophical: the reflexive "se" of *se représenter* presupposes a self; it does not create one.

The statement that because the relation to the one who represents belongs to representing, and all representedness gathers toward him, the one who represents and so can call himself "I" is subject "in an emphatic sense", that toward which everything returns, likewise inverts the order. The one to whom everything represented refers can call himself "I" only if he already is one, in and by himself, on the ground of the essence of ipseity. Otherwise the "I" of "I represent to myself" would be like the tree said to be reflected in the river, which sends its image back to it, "comme si un pronom réfléchi suffisait à faire surgir ... l'ipséité de cet ego" (as if a reflexive pronoun sufficed to make the ipseity of this ego arise, p. 98). The tree reflected in the river is Henry's counter-image to Gassendi's mirror in Chapter I: reflection structures reference without generating selfhood.

One will object that the tree does not really represent its image or transcend toward it. Indeed: it could not, since representing can set before itself what refers back to its self, can project itself as the Self toward which representedness unfolds, only if it is first a Self. Pro-tentional and retro-referential relations presuppose ipseity rather than constituting it. The presupposition is double: ipseity is immanent to representation as its condition, since there is no self-representing without it; and, though implied in representation, it is not explained or grounded by it. "Ce n'est pas parce qu'il y a un « devant soi » ... qu'il y a un Soi" (it is not because there is a "before oneself" that there is a Self, p. 98), but because there is a Self that anything can be determined in relation to it. The Self exists neither in the "before" nor in the "toward", which are not even possible by themselves. It is "un phénomène de la vie" (a phenomenon of life, p. 99), arising in the radical interiority of its auto-affection and identical with it. That "before" and "toward" exist only as "before oneself" and "toward oneself" means: interiority is the condition of all exteriority, the Self the condition of representation. The formula "interiority is the condition of exteriority" restates, in the vocabulary of the self, the thesis of *L'essence de la manifestation* that immanence is the essence of transcendence.

Heidegger's paralogism is easier to see when referred to its Cartesian context. Descartes did seek an unshakeable ground of truth, a final assurance and certainty, and thought he found it in the *ego cogito*. Because the ego must support the edifice of knowledge, its consistency as thought must first be established. The question is how this consistency takes shape, what in the *ego cogito* makes it self-legitimating and self-grounding so as to ground everything else. Heidegger's answer: "la consistance de moi-même en tant que res cogitans consiste dans la sûre et stable détermination du représenter, dans la certitude conformément à laquelle le soi est porté devant soi-même" (the consistency of myself as *res cogitans* consists in the secure and stable determination of representing, in the certainty by which the self is brought before itself, p. 99). Henry isolates "consistency" (*Beständigkeit*): Heidegger reads certainty as the stability of what stands before, a temporal and ecstatic notion.

Two interlocking theses are advanced here. First, the ego is defined as representing: "I am insofar as I represent". Second, the ego is certain of itself precisely as representing, because it holds itself firmly in the act of setting itself before itself; certainty arises "in the secure and stable determination of representing", by which the self is brought before itself. In this certain structure of a Self firmly held before itself, all truth and certainty about what is gathered into the structure and brought to the condition of object become possible. Once the Self is defined as self-representing and assured of itself as such, it is assured of everything it represents: my being is essentially determined by representing, and my representing, as the measuring *repraesentatio*, "décide de la présence de tout représenté" (decides the presence of everything represented, p. 100), that is, of its being as a being. The certainty of everything set before the Self rests on the prior certainty of the self's self-positing. "Ce sur quoi toutes choses se trouvent replacées comme sur un fondement inébranlable est la pleine essence de la représentation même" (that on which all things are set back as on an unshakeable ground is the full essence of representation itself). Henry separates the two theses because he will accept neither: the ego is not representation, and certainty is not the stability of self-positing.

But in the Cartesianism of the Beginning everything represented, valid only as set upon the *subjectum* of representation, everything appearing in this self-proposing of the Self, is swept away by the reduction, cast out of all possible certainty and nullified. The appearance produced in self-opposition cannot make itself sure of itself, since the seeing that moves in it is doubtful. Far from being an "unshakeable ground", "la pleine essence de la représentation même" (the full essence of representation itself) "se désagrège et part en lambeaux" (falls apart in tatters, p. 100). The radicality of Descartes' effort is measured by this rejection of a representation that might be illusory, and by the fact that, despite it or rather through it, a path is opened to a true ground. Heidegger's reading thus attributes to Descartes as his foundation exactly what the doubt destroys.

That this ground is the ego is possible on one condition: that it not be the ego of representation, whose ipseity would have its essence in representation and be constituted by it. Such an ego would be lost with representation in the reduction. More than that: no ego is possible in and through representation, since the structure of opposition is that of otherness: whatever shows itself to the Self and affects it in this structure is by principle other than it. Affected by this other, the Self cannot be affected by itself and its own reality; it cannot be a Self, "ce qui s'affecte soi-même et dont tout l'être est constitué par soi" (that which affects itself and whose whole being is constituted by itself, p. 101). The argument is a reductio: a self constituted by alterity would be the contrary of a self.

Further, for Descartes representation never presents reality, formal reality, but only the objective reality of the idea, an image, a double, a copy, an unreal equivalent that figures reality and refers to it but is not it. What comes in representation is "à la fois l'autre et l'irréel" (both the other and the unreal), the contrary of the Cartesian ego, the Self that bears reality in itself and defines it. That a self proposes itself to itself in representation can be understood only restrictively: the Self projected in representation is only "un soi-représenté" (a represented self), not the real Self that projects and sets before itself, but an unreal Self "co-ob-jeté" (co-projected) at the horizon of representation as the term accompanying every object insofar as it means "thrown before the self, to it, toward it". This co-projected Self belongs to that meaning as its term, unreal like it. Henry thus grants Heidegger and Kant a represented "I", the formal pole of objectivity, but declares it unreal; it is the "empty subjectivity" of Chapter IV's title.

On one hand, this represented Self is possible only from the real Self, as its mere representation, its projection. On the other, it is not this unreal self that is affected: "rien d'irréel, rien de représenté ne forme jamais le lieu d'une affection possible" (nothing unreal or represented is ever the site of a possible affection, p. 101), but only what originally affects itself: the real, living Self. It is to this Self, before it, toward it, that everything represented is in reality represented; that the represented still implies an unreal self in its representedness merely expresses the real process of representation, the fact that everything represented is presented to a real Self. The claimed reading of ipseity in the structure of representation confuses the Self that projects and is affected with the projected Self, reducing the first to the second, which is only its representation and presupposes it. The criterion "only what affects itself can be affected" decides the issue: the pole of representation cannot be the bearer of experience.

That representation is never the ground in the Cartesianism of the Beginning is also clear because it cannot define the essence of certainty and truth any more than of ipseity. A being becomes true and certain only in representing, as set before it and shown to it. But it derives truth and certainty from its representation only if representing is itself first true and certain. For Heidegger, everything held in the setting-before is true because that setting is firm and secure. Not for Descartes: holding firmly before oneself, representing, seeing, is still doubtful. Heidegger writes that the true is what representing brings clearly and distinctly before itself and disposes toward itself so as to posit it securely; this security is certainty; the true in this sense is the real; the reality of the real lies in the stability and consistency of what is represented in certain representation, which excludes the inconsistency of a representing that "posits here and there", going back and forth while it doubts; "le représenter, libre de doute, en est un clair et distinct" (representing free of doubt is clear and distinct, p. 102). A note states that where Heidegger's text has "man" as the subject of this sentence, Henry substitutes "representing" for the sake of intelligibility, consistent with his claim that "man" stands in for a structure. Heidegger's identification of Cartesian certainty with the stability of the represented underlies his link from Descartes to Nietzsche's will to power as securing of permanence; Henry contests its first link.

But Descartes' doubt strikes not only the unstable representing that "posits here and there" but all representing as such, including the stable one that holds the represented firmly in the clarity of evidence. Because doubt is not a modality of representing but "loge dans son essence" (lodges in its essence, p. 102), what is "free of doubt" can no longer be this representing, however clear and distinct. The whole Cartesianism of the Beginning seeks a ground of representation, an absolute certainty which, far from consisting in "the security of representing", which has none of its own, must establish it. This task falls to the ego, not the ego projected in self-representing, as doubtful as its representation and whose possibility, the essence of ipseity, must first be recognized. Only a problematic that stops relying on representing, its seeing and what it sees, can ground it: one that first establishes the existence of this seeing, its self-feeling, which subsists even if every representation is false. Descartes sought absolute certainty "dans l'anti-essence de la représentation" (in the anti-essence of representation, p. 103), not in it. "Avant d'étendre à l'infini le règne de la représentation et de la science, avant d'offrir à son saccage toute la terre, le cartésianisme du commencement l'avait préalablement marqué d'une limite infranchissable" (before extending the reign of representation and science to infinity, before offering the whole earth to its plunder, the Cartesianism of the Beginning had first marked it with an uncrossable limit). Henry concedes Heidegger's diagnosis of technology but locates its origin in the decline of the *cogito*, not in the *cogito*; the original *cogito* is the limit of technical reason, not its source.

Heidegger's interpretation is not innocent: from its serious failings it draws the benefit that is probably its unavowed aim, the insertion of the ego into "the history of Western metaphysics". Cartesianism's originality is not denied. In this history running from Plato to Nietzsche's doctrine of values and the domination of the earth by will, it accomplishes a reversal, "le retournement (*Umschlag*) de l'idéa en perceptio" (the turning of *idea* into *perceptio*), which is "decisive". Yet this reversal grows more and more relative, since in Heidegger's own terms it marks not an interruption but the coming to light and release of an essential feature of the *idea*, "at first veiled and held back". This feature, that of what "makes possible and conditions", is neither secondary nor derivative; it makes Western thought a metaphysics, an interpretation of being and its truth from beings, as what conditions them, the condition of the possibility of beings (p. 104). Henry's objection to "insertion" is that a history in which Descartes merely releases a feature latent in Plato's *idea* leaves no room for a beginning; the *cogito* is made a moment of a continuous destiny.

How does the Platonic *idea* already make beings possible, giving itself at the dawn of Western thought as their a priori condition? By presenting itself beforehand to man's sight as the aspect of a being, the visible form in which it becomes visible, so that only this primitive relation of the gaze to the visibility of the *idea* gives access to the sensible world. Philosophy's task is therefore to turn the gaze from the sensible content that occupies it to what opens it to that content. This a priori intelligibility of the *idea*, as constituting the opening to beings, is their way of being, their beingness (*Seiendheit*); Platonism is already a metaphysics insofar as it thinks the truth of being no longer in itself but as beingness, preparing Aristotle's and Kant's "categories" of beings. Henry summarizes Heidegger's "Plato's Doctrine of Truth" and *Nietzsche* II without objection here; the objection comes in the next units, at the point where both Greek and modern appearing prove to be the same ecstatic visibility.

How then is the turn of *idea* into *perceptio* defined? The *idea* holds from itself the visibility that will be that of beings, their presence; it thus presents itself as *hypokeimenon*, what lies beforehand as the underlying ground of what it grounds, from itself. Greek appearing, though the appearing of beings, arises from itself and produces itself. It still shines with this property through Plato's *idea*, which sets itself in advance before man, preceding and transcending his gaze, offering itself to him, opening him to its light and through it to beings. The self-arising character of Greek appearing, *physis*, is what Heidegger contrasts with modern representation; Henry will argue that self-arising in *physis* is still the arising of an outside.

When Descartes interprets being as "I think", that is, by Heidegger's lights, as "I represent", it is grasped as beingness, an a priori condition of the possibility of beings: a being is posited in being only insofar as it is represented, set before, ob-jected, disposed at the disposal of the one who objects it; its beingness is its capacity to be represented, its "représentéité" (*Vorgestelltheit*, representedness). "Representedness as the property of being makes the represented possible as the being", and the represented thus stands "obstantially" as object. But the visibility that comes to beings from representedness no longer arises of itself: man now confers it on beings, since he produces representedness. He represents, throws before himself and disposes toward himself the field in which beings will be represented. "L'idéa devient le perceptum de la perceptio" (the *idea* becomes the *perceptum* of the *perceptio*, p. 105), what man's representing produces before itself; the essence of the *idea*, from sight and presence, turns into representedness for and by the one who represents. Henry renders *Vorgestelltheit* by the neologism *représentéité*, echoing *Gegenständlichkeit*, to keep the structural sense.

Because man's ego, throwing itself before itself, produces representedness as the essence of visibility and so as the a priori condition of beings, man thereby posits himself as *sub-jectum* and absolute ground of all things. "Man is the *subjectum* in this distinguished sense." "Man is in that he represents himself in this way." In Descartes' metaphysics man is "par excellence le fondement sous-jacent à toute re-présentation de l'étant" (the ground underlying all representation of beings and their truth, p. 105), on which all representing must be set and sets itself. Henry assembles Heidegger's formulas so that the slide from ego to man, criticized above, is visible in Heidegger's own words.

Modernity thus begins with the definition of man as Subject. Everything is only through him. Because the being of beings is representedness, their capacity to be represented by man, and only what is disposed toward him, securely posited as object for the Subject-man and so known, has validity, method is nothing but this holding and firm disposition of what is thereby made certain. Man, through his representation, gives the measure, and since representation is his affair, his self-representing, he gives himself as the measure. But beings measured in this way are guaranteed only if the measure is itself secured, if man has first made sure of himself in this self-representation, "il demeure décisif que ... l'homme soit certain et sûr de lui-même" (it remains decisive that man be certain and sure of himself, p. 106), and this happens in the *cogito*. The chain measure–security–self-certainty is Heidegger's reconstruction of the link between Descartes and Protagoras's *homo mensura*, taken up in the next unit.

It is then easy to oppose to these "basic metaphysical positions of Descartes" those of Greek thought, of Protagoras, for whom man, far from recovering the world in representing it, is defined by his prior belonging to the region of the unconcealed; beingness is no longer representedness but presence within the unconcealed, so that man is the measure not by taking himself as measure but by measuring himself against unconcealment and its limits. For Plato or Protagoras the prior is never man but the truth of being, even as beingness, to which he attunes himself. These well-known Heideggerian theses, legitimated by repetition, have an appearance of truth regarding a fallen Cartesianism and its role in forming modern culture, and a global pertinence to the world of knowledge and technology. Both lose their force when referred to the original *cogito*, whose radical reduction has excluded man and his power of representation, or rather its essence. Not only is the paradoxical intervention of a being, man, in an analysis of pure phenomenality contestable ("jamais le mot homme n'intervient dans les textes de la réduction si ce n'est pour être rejeté", the word man never appears in the texts of the reduction except to be rejected, p. 107); the structure of pure phenomenality, far from reducing to representation, excludes it. Henry distinguishes the validity of Heidegger's history as a description of modern culture from its invalidity as an interpretation of the *cogito*.

The first of these questions, man's intervention, deserves a brief return, because "man" is finally only "un prête-nom, un masque" (a front, a mask, p. 107) for a problem of another order, an ontological one. The interpretation of modern subjectivity as man's uprising and claim to be Subject, as if subjectivity could be defined by man's enigmatic irruption into it rather than by an eidetic analysis of the structure of pure phenomenality, is less naive than it seems, since through "man" the ultimate possibility of appearing is at stake. When man becomes the subject he supplies its content, and since everyone knows what man is, everyone knows what the subject is: identified with man, the Cartesian subject offers no mystery. But this subject-man, even reduced by Heidegger to the subject of representation, designates only the structure of representation and what ultimately makes it possible, an ontological structure, "tout sauf un étant" (anything but a being). The anthropological reading makes subjectivity seem familiar and so conceals its enigma; this is one root of the later "death of the subject" in structuralism.

Consider first the Greek world. Man occupies a modest place there, since Being is understood "insofar as it 'is' Being", as *hypokeimenon*, that is, as *physis*, "l'éclore à partir de soi-même ... le fait de s'ouvrir dans l'ouvert" (emerging from itself, essentially setting itself in emergence, opening itself in the open, p. 108). Only because it belongs to *physis* and emerges in its emergence can a being, so appeared and put into view, offer itself to man's sight. The *idea* is just this putting-into-view of the being, a consequence of *physis* on one side, and what gives man access to beings on the other. But the second property tends to veil the first. Because the *idea* opens man's access to beings and so determines itself as their a priori condition, it presents itself as the source of their appearing, which lies however in *physis*. It is no longer emergence in *physis* that grounds the putting-into-view, but the reverse. Once the putting-into-view is not thought from the emergence of *physis* and floats freely before man's gaze, why should it not find its principle in that gaze, in man himself? The concealment of *physis* by the *idea* makes possible the turn of *idea* into the Cartesian *perceptio*: "je, moi, l'homme, fais advenir toutes choses à l'apparition" (I, me, man, bring all things to appear, and am their master). Henry's summary shows that in Heidegger's own narrative the passage from Greece to Descartes requires no change in the structure of appearing, only in its assignment; visibility remains the model throughout.

However modest man's place in Greek thought, does he not play an essential role there? He is the one who can contemplate the *idea*, open himself to it and see it. More originally, pre-metaphysical Greek man immersed in *physis* can attune himself to the emergence of presence it dispenses. But to think in Greek, turn rather to Heidegger and ask how, in his own philosophy, man stands toward its fundamental question. For the charge against metaphysics of giving man a role cannot stand if, in Descartes, the Greeks and Heidegger alike, man enters the question of being neither as a being nor in his relation to beings. Henry turns the charge back: if Heidegger's own thought assigns man an essential role in the truth of being, he cannot reproach Descartes for the same.

Not in his relation to beings: man can relate to beings only if he first relates to being. Only in the light of being, having first opened to its clearing, can man open to what is lit in it, to beings. More original than man's opening to beings, and enabling it, is his opening to being as such. In this prior opening the problematic interrogates no longer beings; it is no longer metaphysics but the thinking of being. Henry summarizes the ontological difference as it structures the relation of man to being in the *Letter on Humanism*.

Nor as a being: man relates to being through thinking, not as a being. "La pensée accomplit la relation de l'être à l'essence de l'homme" (thinking accomplishes the relation of being to the essence of man, p. 109; *Letter on Humanism*). Beings are first eliminated from the question of being by substituting man's essence for man. This substitution rejects metaphysical humanism, every conception of man as a being, *homo animalis*, and by specific difference *animal rationale*, in favour of a humanism that perhaps no longer deserves the name, the true *humanitas* of *homo humanus*: a "strange" humanitas in which man is nothing but being, a moment of pure appearing and, as thinking, what rests in it and belongs to it. Such is the new situation of a problematic placed beyond metaphysics: considering not man or beings but what grounds both and owes them nothing, "la pure relation de la pensée à l'être" (the pure relation of thinking to being). Henry sets up the parallel: Heidegger, like Descartes, substitutes for man an essence of appearing, so the difference between them must be sought elsewhere.

One will not ask how such a problematic could criticize the *cogito*, which had done nothing but set aside man and beings, explicitly rejecting the definition of man as rational animal to promote a wholly new essence of *humanitas* as the eidos of appearing, pure appearing identical with being. The question is rather: in this radical reduction of being to appearing, taking the form of an essential connection of thought and being, where lies the ultimate difference between Descartes' and Heidegger's non-metaphysical positions? Behind Heidegger's "man" lies not exactly being but thinking and, identically, a certain conception of being. As thinking, man, a transcendental man rid of categories proper to beings, is nothing but ek-sistence: he opens to being in the ecstatic existential determination of throwing himself into exteriority and standing in it, the place of all standing, the place of being. Being is the "there" as standing-before, setting-before, offering-itself. Thinking joins what stands before it and so draws it. How? By ek-sisting in it. How does being enjoin thinking to join it? By standing before it, opening to it so that thinking opens to it. In this mutual opening lies the *Ereignis*, "le phénomène originel, l'étreinte première où la phénoménalité surgit" (the original phenomenon, the first embrace where phenomenality arises, p. 110). Are the opening of thinking to being and of being to thinking the same? "L'œil par lequel l'être me regarde et l'œil par lequel je regarde l'être n'est-il qu'un seul et même œil ?" (Is the eye by which being looks at me and the eye by which I look at being one and the same eye?) The closing question echoes Eckhart's sermon ("the eye in which I see God is the same eye in which God sees me"), which Henry cited abundantly in *L'essence de la manifestation*; there it described immanence, here it is posed of an ecstatic relation, to show that Heidegger's "same" is an identity of two openings, not a self.

Between thinking and being there is no reciprocity. If thinking "accomplishes the relation of being to the essence of man", it "neither constitutes nor produces this relation itself". If thinking is ek-sistence, it reaches being by throwing itself outside itself into the truth of being, accomplishing the "ecstatic relation of the essence of man to the truth of being"; but this relation is not what it is on the ground of ek-sistence; rather, "the essence of ek-sistence is existential-ecstatic from the essence of the truth of being" (*Letter on Humanism*). All of Heidegger's texts after *Sein und Zeit* reaffirm the reversal by which the ultimate possibility of transcendental truth lies not in man, in thinking, but outside him, in the prior dimension of being's truth; as *Sein und Zeit* already said, "being is the *transcendens* pure and simple". Hence what is essential "is not man but being as the dimension of the ecstatic of ek-sistence" (p. 111). Being opens this dimension in which "es gibt das Sein" (there is being): being gives the "there is", gives itself and grants its truth. If ek-sistence throws itself into being's truth, it is because being first threw it into this projection: "ce qui jette dans le projeter n'est pas l'homme, mais l'être lui-même" (what throws in projecting is not man but being itself, which destines man to the ek-sistence of being-the-there as his essence). The critique of Descartes finds its precise motive here, if in the *cogito* read as "I represent" it is man, the ego, who throws before himself the space of representedness. The motive is explicit when Heidegger, having said that only the field of view in which being's clearing unfolds can draw man's ek-sistence toward it ("Anblick erst zieht Hin-sicht auf sich", the look first draws the regard to itself), deplores the surrender by which this field "gives itself over" to the regard it raised and loses itself in it, in the *perceptio* of the *res cogitans* as *subjectum* of *certitudo* (*Principle of Reason*). Henry's reconstruction isolates the *Kehre* as the decisive move: what Descartes attributes to the ego, the later Heidegger attributes to being. The question will be whether this changes the structure of appearing at all.

The regard ek-sisting in being's truth thus rests not on itself but on that truth and its prior opening. How is ek-sistence grounded in this opening, how does it open to it? Are there two "openings" or one, one eye? Commenting on the "es gibt", the self-giving of being, Heidegger writes: "le don de soi dans l'ouvert au moyen de cet ouvert est l'être même" (the self-giving into the open by means of this open is being itself, p. 112). Ek-sistence stands in this open not by itself but by being's gift, by means of the open. "Being transmits itself to man only insofar as the clearing of being comes about", and that it comes about is being's affair, not man's: "l'être est le destin de l'éclaircie" (being is the destiny of the clearing). Henry's question of "one eye or two" now becomes whether the openness of ek-sistence has any phenomenality of its own or merely borrows the openness of the open.

How is the clearing destined? Does its coming about, being's destiny, not include man as a necessary moment of its accomplishment? Would being transmit itself to man only insofar as its clearing occurs, while its clearing occurs only insofar as it transmits itself to man? Significantly, almost every text affirming ek-sistence's belonging to being adds a finality: being throws man into ek-sistence so that he may guard its truth, as if the clearing were lit only insofar as man ek-sists in it. "Man belongs to the essence of being and remains destined to the understanding of being from this belonging" (*Nietzsche* II). Thinking, "belonging to being because thrown by being for the truthful guarding of its truth and claimed by being for this guarding, thinks being" (*Letter*). Man is "thrown" by being into its truth "afin qu'ek-sistant de la sorte il veille sur la vérité de l'être" (so that, ek-sisting thus, he watches over the truth of being, p. 113); ek-sistence matters as being brings man about as the one who ek-sists "for vigilance toward the truth of being". The enigma of thrownness (*Geworfenheit*) is clarified: man is thrown, unfolds as "the ek-sisting response of being", because "called by being itself to the safeguarding of its truth". Hence "the essence of man is essential for the truth of being". Henry exposes a circle in the *Letter*: being grounds man, yet being's truth needs man. The circle is not a confusion; it points to a structural requirement the next units identify.

Why should man, or rather ek-sisting, belong essentially to the truth of being, to its innermost possibility? Why is the clearing lit only insofar as man ek-sists in it? Henry asks, as Heidegger himself demands, for "the essential help of phenomenological thinking". What does the clearing of being consist in phenomenologically, what is its specific phenomenality? What does ek-sisting in it consist in? One thing is known: the phenomenality of ek-sisting is identical with that of the clearing (p. 113). The clearing grounds ek-sisting because it supplies its phenomenality; every ek-sisting is an ek-sisting in it. To ek-sist is to stand outside in exteriority, in and by it. This exteriority is the clearing of being, "das Sein als die Dimension des Ekstatischen der Ek-sistenz" (being as the dimension of the ecstatic of ek-sistence). It is not space but what lets space show itself: every spatial medium and every space-time unfolds its essence "im Dimensionalen", in the dimensional, which is being itself (p. 114). Henry's term "le Dimensional", used since the prologue, is Heidegger's own (*das Dimensionale*). Henry now uses it to fix the referent of Heidegger's concepts: the clearing is exteriority as such.

If ek-sisting stands in the exteriority of being's ecstatic dimension and borrows its phenomenality from it, how does this exteriority come about? It is not simply there like a stone, a tree, a man, or a clearing in the forest. "L'extériorité s'extériorise" (exteriority exteriorizes itself, p. 114), in the transcendental process that throws outside itself and so pro-jects what stands before as exteriority. Every exteriority is "naturante-naturée" (naturing-natured): naturing as production, throwing-before; natured as what is thrown before, the ob-jected. Only as naturing-natured is exteriority constitutive of and identical with phenomenality. The Spinozist pair *natura naturans/naturata* names a duality inside ecstasis between the act of exteriorizing and the exteriorized field; Heidegger's "clearing" names only the second.

All effective phenomenality opens the path to itself: "la voie d'accès au phénomène est le phénomène lui-même" (the access to the phenomenon is the phenomenon itself, p. 114). Exteriority is the Open; the Open accomplishes opening, opens itself and so leads to itself. Insofar as being is lit in the ecstatic dimension of exteriority, man is led by it to it, opens to the Open as it opens to him, and ek-sists in it. One text suffices: "In-der-Welt-sein" names the essence of ek-sistence with regard to the lit dimension from which the "ek-" of ek-sistence unfolds (*Letter*). Henry grants the principle that a phenomenon gives access to itself, and will turn it: the access to the act that opens the Open cannot be the Open.

But the Open presupposes its prior Opening: not the opening it accomplishes in opening to man, as the clearing that is the way to itself, but the Opening of the Open itself, "non pas l'ouverture qu'il rend possible mais celle qui le rend possible" (not the opening it makes possible but the one that makes it possible, p. 115). This is the transcendental process that throws the Open, the original Exteriorization that exteriorizes exteriority and unfolds it as the ecstatic dimension of being, so that it may be the clearing in which man can ek-sist. Man can ek-sist in being's truth only on condition of a much more primitive Ek-sisting, "celui qui jette originellement devant soi" (the one that originally throws before itself), which threw the Open and constituted the ecstatic dimension. This is the argument of *L'essence de la manifestation*, §§ 11–15, against "ontological monism": transcendence requires an act of transcending, which cannot itself be in the transcended.

More than that. The exteriority in which Exteriorization exteriorizes itself subsists in its condition as the place of ecstatic phenomenality only as long as the process producing it continues, only as long as exteriorization keeps exteriorizing, setting before itself but also referring to itself and retaining what continually goes outside. What goes out is not lost but retained and kept in the unity and coherence of the dimension. "La réceptivité de l'horizon extatique" (the receptivity of the ecstatic horizon, p. 115) is the condition without which the horizon, no longer held or retained, could not be seen, and not being seen would no longer offer itself as a horizon of visibility. Receptivity is Kant's word, and the argument repeats Heidegger's *Kantbuch*: transcendence must receive the horizon it forms. Henry will claim that this receptivity, which Heidegger interprets temporally, is auto-affection.

That man belongs to Heideggerian being, thrown by it into its truth "for the truthful guarding of its truth", "so that he watches over the truth of being", ek-sisting "for vigilance", "as the ek-sisting response of being", "called by being to safeguard its truth", and finally as "the shepherd of being", means: the truth of being neither lights itself nor subsists by itself. The exteriority of the dimension phenomenalizes itself only insofar as, unfolded in the transcendental process of exteriorization, it is also received by that process, held and maintained in the original act of its exteriorization. The reception of the ecstatic horizon as the condition of its phenomenological formation, receptivity as the transcendental condition of being's truth, "tel est le procès primitif auquel l'homme prête son nom" (such is the primitive process to which man lends his name, p. 116), the process in which the clearing finds its prior possibility. This is why Heideggerian being needs man, why man is not a mysterious synthetic addition to an already complete truth of being: the guarding he is required for, as reception of the horizon in which it is lit, is the transcendental condition of this truth itself. Henry's thesis solves the circle of the *Letter*: "man" is Heidegger's name for the self-reception of ecstasis, the condition of the clearing's phenomenality.

How then does man guard being? Not as man, a being, but by his essence, ek-sisting. Man reaches being's truth by ek-sisting in it, since this truth is the ecstatic dimension of existence. But how does being itself reach its truth, the ecstatic dimension, if not by ek-sisting in it, in the ob-jected into which one comes by throwing oneself? Only ek-sisting can reach being's truth as being's own prior work: not man's ek-sisting, but ek-sisting as such, the transcendental process of Ek-stasis that throws the ecstatic before it and reaches it in throwing it. "L'homme n'est pas le gardien de l'être" (man is not the guardian of being, p. 116). Man guards being only insofar as being first guards itself, holding the Open in the throw by which it opens it. Man does not ek-sist afterward, on his side, into an Open already opened by being. "Il n'y a qu'un seul ek-sister" (there is only one ek-sisting), that of the Ek-stasis in which being's work is accomplished. "L'homme lui-même n'ek-siste pas" (man himself does not ek-sist, p. 117); he ek-sists only on the ground in him of being's process. Henry answers his own question about "one eye" negatively for Heidegger: there is one eye, but it is ecstasis, and man is merely its locus.

But how can man open to his own ground, his essence, unite with the transcendental process that bursts through him so as, one with it, to throw himself with it into the Open and reach the clearing? Ask rather being: how does the transcendental process that throws the Open first reach itself, in the process, in Ek-sisting, so as to be what it is and do what it does? Again it is not man, a being, who can accomplish being's innermost possibility, "l'Unité en laquelle l'être parvient originellement en soi" (the Unity in which being originally comes into itself, p. 117). Supposing this Unity accomplished and its ultimate possibility shown, how could man, since he does not accomplish it, unite with it so as to enter by it into Ek-sisting and thereby into the Open? The question shifts from man's relation to being to being's relation to itself; that is the move from Heidegger's problematic to Henry's.

Or is not the coming of Ek-sisting into itself, as auto-affection, as what originally affects itself, as identity of affecting and affected, the essence of ipseity, and as such that of man himself? "Car l'homme est celui qui dit Je" (for man is the one who says I, p. 117). He does not say I because he can speak; he speaks insofar as he says I, and can say I on the ground of the essence of ipseity in him. Finding his essence in ipseity, man is born in being, comes about in it at the same time as it. "L'homme ne crée pas l'être, il est créé par lui" (man does not create being; he is created by it), because being, affecting itself originally in the self-appearing of its coming to itself, determines itself each time as an ego. Henry's answer reconciles the two claims in conflict: being is prior to man (with Heidegger) and being is essentially a Self (with Descartes). The ego is being's way of coming to itself, which is why it is "created" and never self-founding. The priority of saying "I" over speech is a counter-thesis to Heidegger's "language is the house of being" and to Lacan's subject of the signifier.

Here the crucial intuitions of the Cartesianism of the Beginning call to be meditated once more: that Cartesianism which, knowing nothing of man and having in its night only the self-appearing of appearing identical with being, says "ego cogito, ego sum". The Cartesian grasp of the Beginning is formulated by the identity of three words: ego, cogito, sum; being accomplishing itself as "thought" (appearing) also accomplishes itself as ipseity. Only in this identity does Descartes grasp the essence of the soul, an appearing whose self-appearing is identically ipseity and life. In being's initial coming to itself, man is produced as a living being, who on the ground of primitive ipseity, carried and constituted by it, can say "I": "l'homo humanus dont l'humanitas transcendantale puise aux sources de l'être" (the *homo humanus* whose transcendental humanity draws from the springs of being, p. 118). Henry claims for Descartes the title Heidegger reserved for the post-metaphysical "humanism" of the *Letter*.

Also to be pondered is that Descartes said of this original connection of thought and egoity only that nothing more evident offers itself by which to explain it. This connection is established at a time when evidence is not yet born, within the transcendental process of exteriorization that produces exteriority. Of this production, in which there is neither exteriority nor evidence, Schelling will say that it is unconscious; it is the veiling from which every unveiling arises. "Un tel « inconscient » doit être soigneusement distingué de celui qui appartient à l'horizon de l'ek-stasis" (such an "unconscious" must be carefully distinguished from the one that belongs to the horizon of ecstasis, p. 118). But this original unconscious is so only for a philosophy lacking an adequate concept of phenomenality, which reduces phenomenality to the world and its knowledge; that philosophy is the philosophy of the unconscious. It is contradictory, since all exteriority, naturing-natured, phenomenalizes itself only if its naturing is itself effective. Only a material phenomenology can return to the Beginning: one that uses the formal concepts of phenomenality only as indices and asks what makes them possible, the concrete phenomenological substance they refer to. It alone gives "the essential help of phenomenological thinking" and splits the concept of appearing into its principial material dichotomy. That this theme remained unthought by Descartes does not prevent its real accomplishment in the reduction, the separation of the radical immanence of the *videor* from ecstasis; the amphibology of all Cartesian key concepts proves it. Here Henry names the second unconscious, Schelling's unconscious production, preparing Chapter V; its "unconsciousness" is again an artefact of a philosophy that knows only ecstatic phenomenality. The book now has three "unconsciouses": the horizon-shadow, the night of life, and the hidden act of exteriorization; the last two are the same life.

To this amphibology the monotony of Heidegger's basic concepts of phenomenality stands in striking contrast. Being keeps man in its guard so that he may become its guardian. Whether this guarding is being's throwing of man into its truth or man's ek-sisting in it, a single Phenomenon makes up the double guarding and makes it reversible, "un seul espace de lumière qu'on traverse successivement dans les deux sens" (one space of light traversed successively in both directions, p. 119). Being relates itself to man; it is this relation: "being is itself the relation insofar as it brings ek-sistence to itself in its existential, ecstatic essence and draws it back to itself". How does man relate to being? By the same relation. This relation is the co-belonging of being and thinking, the *Ereignis* (*Identity and Difference*). Hence "man is never first man on this side of the world as 'subject' ... rather he is first in his essence ek-sisting in the openness of being". What is innermost in this man and his *humanitas* is a radical exteriority; the "subjectivity" of this "subject" is that of the "world", what is "plus « objectif » que tout « objet » possible" (more "objective" than any possible "object", *Sein und Zeit*, § 69). The quotation from *Sein und Zeit* on the world's "objectivity" seals the charge of the prologue: in Heidegger, subjectivity is the world.

Because man's phenomenological essence is exteriority, that of being, because there is one essence of phenomenality and one accomplishment of it, one light, the concepts that formulate it are univocal. Henry lists the families of Heidegger's metaphors: sheltering and concealing (*Bergen*, *Verborgenheit*, *Unverborgenheit*), jointure (*Fuge*), belonging (*Zugehörigkeit*), provenance (*Geschichte*, *Geschehen*), coming (*kommen*, *vor-kommen*), destiny (*Geschick*), light (*Licht*, *Lichtung*), look (*Blick*, *Anblick*), openness (*offen*, *Offenheit*, *Offenbarung*, *Offenbarkeit*, *Erschliessung*, *Erschlossenheit*), sight (*Sehen*, *aussehen*, *Gesicht*, *Ansicht*), being and the there (*Da-sein*), positing and standing (*Setzen*, *Stehen*, *hinausstehen*, *Stand*, *Bestand*, *Gegenstand*, *stellen*, *her-stellen*, *vorstellen*, *zustellen*), truth and guarding (*wahr*, *wahren*, *gewahren*), reign, world, throw, essence (*walten*, *Welt*, *werfen*, *wesen*), relation (*Verhältnis*), sojourn (*Aufenthalt*), house (*Haus*). "Ces métaphores de la pensée ont une même référence phénoménologique" (these metaphors of thinking have one and the same phenomenological reference, p. 120). The list is the counterpart of the Cartesian amphibology in Chapter II: Descartes' terms equivocate between two modes of appearing; Heidegger's, however various their etymologies, refer to one.

This monotony makes possible a method, what Marx against Stirner called the method of "appositions": imperceptible slides of meaning by which, gradually leaving one concept, one easily reaches another. From ethics to ontology is only a step once one notes that *ethos* "means sojourn", "the domain open to the presence of the god" (who himself designates this presence), and that *nomos* "is not only the law but more originally the assignment hidden in the decree of being", the assignment that alone "enjoins man to being" (*Letter*), both finally having their essence in this "injunction". The reference to *The German Ideology* is a polemical gift: Heidegger's etymological chains are compared to Stirner's word-play, which Marx ridiculed as producing identities by juxtaposition.

Yet the univocity of this discourse proves highly equivocal if the appearing to which it refers divides structurally, according to the phenomenological materiality of its effectuation, into two heterogeneous modes. "Openness" in the clearing of being, for example, cannot designate without distinction ek-sisting and "l'Unité plus originelle dans laquelle celui-ci parvient en soi" (the more original Unity in which ek-sisting reaches itself, p. 121). This Unity is not a means of reaching the light; in it is essentialized the lightless dimension where nothing ek-sists, where everything rests in itself in the immanence of life. The question of the insertion of the *ego cogito* into "the history of Western metaphysics" can be posed only in terms of this dichotomy. Henry inverts the verdict: Heidegger's univocity is itself an equivocation, since it uses one name for two modes of manifestation.

Once the *ego cogito* is arbitrarily reduced to "I represent", its insertion goes without saying: the phenomenality representation borrows can only be that of ecstasis (there is no other), and representing is only an improper way of thinking ek-sisting. How then does the *cogito* alter Platonic and Greek truth? It gives man the initiative in unfolding the relation: the ego represents, throws before itself and draws back the horizon of representability, everything being defined as what it can represent. But "que l'initiative revienne à l'être ou à l'homme dans le jet de l'ek-sister, la phénoménalité qu'il pro-jette ou en laquelle il est jeté, est la même, c'est la lumière de l'ek-stasis" (whether the initiative belongs to being or to man in the throw of ek-sisting, the phenomenality projected or thrown into is the same, the light of ecstasis, p. 121). The *Kehre* changes who throws, not what is thrown; Heidegger's history of being is a history of attributions within one phenomenality.

Heidegger tried to oppose Cartesian and Greek truth radically, as *Gegenstand* to *Gegenüber*, object to "what stands over against". In *Gegenstand*, the *Gegen* is defined by the casting-against that is the representing act of the subject; in *Gegenüber*, the *Gegen* discloses itself as what comes upon the perceiving man who listens and looks, what surprises him; the present thing is not what a subject throws to itself as object but what comes to perceiving man and what his sight and hearing set out as having come to them (*Principle of Reason*). Because modern man thinks himself master of the object he throws against himself, his attitude differs entirely from the Greek's listening to a presence that does not emanate from him, "the presence of the gods", so that for him the "over-against" was "the most unsettling and fascinating: *to deinon*" (p. 122). Henry presents Heidegger's best case for an epochal difference before showing that the shared *Gegen* annuls it.

More decisive than the attitude of Greeks or moderns toward what comes is the structure of this coming, the essence of being's truth. As long as it is thought by means of a *Gegen* that determines *Gegenüber* as much as *Gegenstand*, the difference between them is secondary, and the terms that arise from it bear "le Même" (the Same) within them: the clearing that stands in the openness opened by the *Gegen*, which constitutes *Be-gegnen*, encounter. Though it comes from the gods and not from men, the Greek "over-against" too is "ce qui est étendu devant nous" (what is spread out before us, p. 122). The common prefix carries the argument: *Gegen* is the ecstatic "against" in which anything can be encountered, whether projected by a subject or arriving from the gods.

Reduced to "I represent", the *cogito* is not only inserted into the history of metaphysics; it is homogeneous with what precedes it, with the most original truth of *physis*, since this too is constituted by the *Gegen*. Proof is the "extraordinary" fact that the history of metaphysics is the history of being itself: the same being, the same truth sends itself here as *physis*, there as *idea*, there as *cogito*. The identity of essence of appearing grounds the secret affinity among the epochs of being, between *Gegenüber* and *Gegenstand*. The epochs are not equivalent; each has its way of veiling and unveiling. But veiling and unveiling, and the nature of unveiling, belong to all and determine all equally. In modern times the clouding of being's truth reaches its extreme when man, making himself Subject, takes himself for being. Yet this mistake comes not from man but from being, as the way it sends itself in modernity. "Pourquoi l'être se joue-t-il à lui-même ce petit tour de se faire passer pour l'homme aux yeux de l'homme, c'est-à-dire en fin de compte à ses propres yeux à lui ?" (Why does being play itself this little trick of passing itself off as man in man's eyes, and so ultimately in its own? p. 122) The irony is exact: if the "subjectivist" error is a sending of being, Heidegger must explain why being presents itself as a subject, which his ontology cannot do.

Because "man" is only a front for the transcendental condition of being interpreted from the *Gegen*. The history of being is not as absurd as it seems; it is not left to accident; in it essence unfolds its prescriptions one after another. This is why man enters it, already in Greece, not only as the receptivity of ecstasis and its design but, more ultimately, as the ego: "non pas l'ego qui se représente mais l'ipséité qui, présupposée en toute représentation, l'exclut de soi insurmontablement" (not the ego that represents, but the ipseity which, presupposed in all representation, insurmountably excludes it from itself, p. 123). Henry answers his ironic question: being "passes itself off as man" because ecstasis needs a self-reception that it cannot supply, and it is this self, mistakenly anthropologized, that the history of being registers as "man".

But this "ego" is not itself inserted into the history of Western metaphysics, nor into that of being. It does not come about in Descartes' time or in the age it inaugurates; it is not a declension of ecstasis. It is there before ecstasis, "avant la Différence" (before the Difference, p. 123), the Beginning that has begun since the beginning and never ceases to begin, "l'initial apparaître à soi de l'apparaître, l'invisible venir en soi de la vie" (appearing's initial appearing to itself, life's invisible coming into itself). The chapter ends where Chapter I began: the Beginning has no history. "Before the Difference" sets the ego outside the ontological difference, and so outside every history of being; it has a history only in the sense that it is forgotten, which is the genealogy the book traces.

## Chapter IV. Empty Subjectivity and Lost Life: The Kantian Critique of the "Soul"

Henry announces the chapter's aim: Kant's critique of the paralogisms of rational psychology will make evident that the ipseity of the ego does not lie in the essence of representedness, which he will also call "representativity" or "representability", and cannot be grounded in it. Kant's metaphysics is a metaphysics of representativity understood as the condition of all that is, and so as the essence of being. How can something be for us? By being represented by us. We know only phenomena, and critical thought draws its legitimacy from this phenomenological presupposition. But "knowledge" and "phenomenon" mean only coming into the condition of being-represented, standing there before us, and so showing oneself, and so being. Thought usually sets knowledge on one side and the object on the other and then asks how they relate; yet "connaissance et objet sont identiques" (knowledge and object are identical, p. 125) if being an object is being represented and being represented is being known. Representativity is the common essence of knowledge and object, of phenomenon and being. The *Critique of Pure Reason* is the systematic search for the conditions of being-represented as such, the conditions of the possibility of experience as the possibility of its objects. Henry reads Kant's "highest principle of synthetic judgments" (the conditions of experience are the conditions of the objects of experience) as an ontological thesis: being equals representedness. The chapter will show that Kant himself proves this thesis insufficient.

The first condition is intuition. The opening sentence of the Transcendental Aesthetic says that whatever the way in which knowledge relates to objects, the mode in which it relates to them immediately, and toward which all thought tends as its end, is intuition (A 19/B 33). Thought is thus subordinated to intuition and serves only to make the singular object of intuition accessible and common, to "represent" it in turn in a concept. But intuition will prove to have its deepest essence in inner sense, which Kant calls a "restrictive condition" for an understanding like ours, which represents only objects given in intuition, in contrast to an intuitive or divine understanding whose representation would produce its objects. Intuition is placed at the heart of experience only to have its finitude recognized at once. The finitude of our understanding, which "can only think and must seek intuition in the senses", rests on that of intuition itself as a power of knowledge unable to create its objects and therefore bound to receive them. Henry follows Heidegger's *Kantbuch* (§§ 4–5) in placing finitude in intuition rather than in thought, preparing to turn the *Kantbuch*'s results against it.

How does intuition receive its objects? Its receptivity, the source of its finitude, cannot be defined from outside by contrast with the limit-concept of an *intuitus originarius*. It must be grasped in its internal, phenomenological positivity, if it means nothing but phenomenality as such, if being received as an object of intuition is showing oneself, being a phenomenon. This capacity of intuition to receive, that is, of the object to show itself, consists in establishing a relation by which what is to be received is placed in the condition of object, set before and so seen, intuited, known. The opening declaration of the Aesthetic affirmed that all knowledge consists in a relation to objects, and intuition counts as the condition of knowledge only as its "immediate" realization. Henry makes "receptivity" a phenomenological, not a creaturely, category: to receive is to let something appear as object.

Intuition that immediately puts at a distance and so ob-jects what it can then receive as object is pure intuition, presupposed by every empirical intuition, since any being can be intuited only if it is first put at a distance. There are not two kinds of intuition, pure and empirical, ontological and ontic, but "une seule essence de l'intuition" (one essence of intuition, p. 127), this original distancing. Space and time are pure intuitions, but they are intuitions only because they bear the original transcendence in which the ecstatic horizon is instituted, where every being will be gathered and made visible as object, "as object of a possible experience". Space and time are the modes of this transcendence. If outer intuitions are also inner, if time includes space, it is because the time that structures inner sense is nothing but the projection of the primordial horizon of objectivity forming the a priori dimension of all experience of objects: the essence of representativity. Henry adopts the *Kantbuch*'s priority of time over space (§ 33) and identifies time with ecstasis as such. This sets up the paralogism critique: if inner sense is time, self-knowledge through inner sense is ecstatic.

But thought is itself representation, more precisely its unity, insofar as it rests on intuition: "the representation that can be given before all thought is called intuition" (B 132). The ecstatic transcendence inhabiting the original intuitions of space and time, which create the pure ontological content they receive and by which beings are received as objects, the ecstasis that originally creates the pure manifold of space and time, is possible only if it synthesizes this manifold and holds it in the unity of its view. Thought is precisely the a priori synthetic unity of the manifold of intuition, and the manifold requires it: unless it is to be lost in its own dispersion, every intuition presupposes the binding power that makes it "one" intuition, "one" consciousness. The analytic unity of consciousness, without which nothing would be, rests on the synthetic unity of apperception (B 133). The categories are only the different ways thought synthesizes the manifold and brings it back to its unity. Henry's point is that apperception, Kant's "I think", is here wholly defined as the unifying function of ecstasis; it has no other content.

The question of the primacy of thought or intuition seems illusory if thought joins intuition to constitute the transcendental power of knowledge, if synthesis presupposes the manifold and makes it possible. Or does a synopsis already belong to intuition? Or is the most original synthesis that of the power on which intuition rests, the ecstasis of exteriority from which every manifold originates? This ecstasis inhabits thought too; it is "l'essence commune de la pensée et de l'intuition" (the common essence of thought and intuition, p. 128) and grounds their unity. The problem of the unity of the transcendental power of knowledge is not first that of intuition and thought but of what ensures unity in each, the unity of ecstasis as such. Kant labours to recognize and ground the inner coherence of the transcendental process of exteriorization because for him it is the condition of all experience as relation to an object in general. This is why Kantianism is a metaphysics of representativity: ecstasis is its essence, making possible all coming into being as coming into the phenomenon, into the condition of the Object. The "common root" question is Heidegger's in the *Kantbuch* (transcendental imagination as the root of sensibility and understanding); Henry answers it with ecstasis rather than imagination or time, which amounts to the same for the argument.

The meaning of Kant's critique is ambiguous. On one side, in an unprecedented philosophical vision, it thinks the transcendental condition of all experience, recognized as the essence of representativity. If this condition is also that of all possible being for us and so delimits an ontology, it soon appears that this ontology destroys itself. Pure thought and pure intuition compose the ecstatic structure of being they analyse; but this structure contains no being by itself, "cette condition de toute existence ne contient aucune existence et ne peut la produire" (this condition of all existence contains no existence and cannot produce it, p. 129). It must ask for existence from a radically heterogeneous element: sensation. Thus the second aspect of the *Critique* emerges, its "abyssal intuition": the essence that originally brings existence, "le pouvoir inaugural de l'être n'est pas l'ek-stasis et ne réside pas en elle" (being's inaugural power is not ecstasis and does not reside in it). Henry claims Kant as a witness against ontological monism: the *Critique*'s dependence on sensation shows that the horizon of being is empty.

This second meaning is the *Critique*'s proper one. Though its major analyses ground the coherence of representation, notably the inner determination of pure intuition by the concepts of the understanding, such determination is still nothing, being the determination of nothing, as long as it does not gather in the wholly other than itself: sensation, impression, *Empfindung*. Critique of knowledge does not merely expose its a priori condition as that of representativity and of every object; it is the critique of that condition, "la critique radicale de la représentativité elle-même" (the radical critique of representativity itself, p. 129), since representativity proves unable by itself to yield actual experience or exhibit any reality: it is "une forme vide" (an empty form). Representativity as empty form and reality as foreign to it are determined jointly and antithetically, and reality is entrusted to sensation alone: "sensation is what designates a reality" (A 175/B 217); space "is nothing but a mere representation", and nothing in it is real except what is represented in perception (A 374–375); the second postulate of empirical thought requires for reality "a perception, and consequently a sensation accompanied by consciousness" (A 225/B 272). Sensation thus plays the role of an origin; it is being itself, reality. Existence has its ground in it; experience as actual is possible only through it, and is always the empirical experience of an empirical existence. The double reading of the *Critique* as theory and critique of representation is Henry's own; it inverts the usual reading in which sensation is the unintelligible residue and form the source of objectivity.

Here we reach the heart of Kant's thought and its aporia. Insofar as Kantianism is the completed exemplification of a metaphysics of representativity, it is this metaphysics that is at issue and that will deliver its ultimate truth just as it is about to hide it forever. That sensation is the other of representation and what representation cannot produce means that the being of sensation, of impression, is not representativity and cannot be reduced to it. What is the being of impression, then, if not "l'auto-impression originelle en laquelle toute impression s'auto-impressionne elle-même" (the original self-impression in which every impression impresses itself, p. 130), the radically immanent essence of life excluding all ecstasis? "Self-impression" is the key term of Henry's late *Phénoménologie matérielle* and of his reading of Husserl's "Urimpression"; here it names the ontological status of Kant's matter of sensation.

But Kant takes impression as the wholly other of ecstasis only to bring it back to ecstasis at once, since sensation is only as intuited, received in inner sense, whose structure, identical with that of time, is the structure of ecstasis itself. With this reduction of the being of impression to representativity, the transcendental element inherent in sensation, affectivity as its a priori condition, "l'essence originelle de la révélation sans laquelle aucune impression ... ne serait jamais" (the original essence of revelation without which no impression would ever be, p. 131), is passed over in silence and replaced by representativity. Because he posits this condition as that of all experience, Kant not only misses the experience impression has of itself; he makes it and its affective support "un contenu mort, opaque, aveugle" (a dead, opaque, blind content), deprived of the light of phenomenality and needing to borrow it from representation. The "blind" intuitions without concepts (A 51/B 75) are read as the ontological degradation of affectivity to a matter that does not appear by itself. This is the Kantian equivalent of Malebranche's "feeling without light" in Chapter II.

In summary: "dans la représentation il n'y a pas de sensation possible" (in representation no sensation is possible, p. 131). Kant must therefore constantly add sensation to representation, yet, lacking the radical ontological means, he can add it only within representation, as what it represents, the ontic content of the sole ontological power, representativity. This sole and exclusive power is no power at all; it is marked by a fundamental deficiency, unable to exhibit in the exteriority it projects what escapes it in principle yet is the real condition of all actual experience and indispensable to it: "la sensation, la vie" (sensation, life). The formula "in representation no sensation is possible" parallels the Cartesian conclusion of Chapter II that representation is the dimension of unreality.

Kant's conditions for real experience are now intelligible. There is experience only as representativity, insofar as the powers of ecstasis have thrown out the pure manifold of transcendental exteriority, so that in this first coming of an outside, creative of a pure ontological medium of visibility, nothing is yet given to see. Because time as pure intuition is, as Kant says, only "an empty intuition", it cannot be an object of perception, and knowledge of the self of inner sense, whose form is time, receives nothing positive from it. Nor can space substitute for time. If knowledge of the empirical self is possible only with the support of space, it is not because space as pure representation is less empty than time but because it presents its content so that the categories, notably substance and causality, can be applied; but that content is then empirical, not merely ontological (the Refutation of Idealism). In every case the form of intuition is only the mode in which knowledge will be achieved, which happens only under the condition of sensation. Here the mutual conditioning of pure and empirical intuition is reversed: empirical intuition does not merely have its condition in pure intuition; it makes it effective and secretly grounds it. This ultimate truth of the *Critique*, that all actual knowledge rests on sensation, remains masked because sensation is considered only as empirical intuition, and so seems still dependent on intuition and on the representation that grounds it. Henry's reversal of pure and empirical intuition is his sharpest move against the *Kantbuch*, where the pure horizon is the condition of any encounter.

That sensation belongs necessarily to actual experience as the end for which the transcendental power of knowledge is only a means is shown by examining pure thought, which co-constitutes this power. Thought is the a priori unity of apperception synthesizing the manifold through its categories. Yet, Kant says, "it is very remarkable that we cannot see the possibility of any thing through the mere category, but must always have an intuition at hand to exhibit the objective reality of the pure concept of the understanding" (B 288). For "in themselves the categories are not cognitions at all but mere forms of thought for making cognitions out of given intuitions". That these given intuitions are empirical follows from the rest of the General Note on the System of Principles: the causal principle, for example, could be proved only "as the ground of the possibility of experience, and so of the knowledge of an object given in empirical intuition, not from mere concepts" (B 289), and more generally from the constantly repeated thesis that the category has only empirical use. Henry quotes the General Note because it was added in the B edition precisely to block rational psychology, and its emphasis on intuition prepares the paralogism argument.

The insurmountable prescription of the *Critique* that forbids us to take "a step outside the sensible world" is also expressed in the thesis that all knowledge is synthetic. "Synthetic" here no longer means thought itself as the binding of the manifold, but the fact that, reduced to the a priori synthesis of pure intuition, thought yields no knowledge, which requires a sensation, dis-jected in the ecstasis of space and time and reunified in the unity of thought, yet independent of it. A cognitive proposition is synthetic: it adds to a subject that would otherwise remain empty a predicate always resting on empirical intuition and so on sensation. Hence "from mere categories no synthetic proposition can be drawn", and no one has "ever succeeded in proving a synthetic proposition from pure concepts of the understanding" (B 289). Henry distinguishes two senses of "synthesis": the binding act of the understanding and the addition of an empirical datum; only the second yields reality.

The incapacity of the concept to supply by itself the content of actual knowledge and allow its progress will play a decisive role in the critique of the paralogisms. As the tautological repetition of itself, unable to prove that anything real corresponds to it, the concept is only a question whose answer must come from another element. The concept "turns always around itself and never takes us further in any question of synthetic knowledge", because "every synthetic solution requires intuition" (A 731/B 759). The image of the concept "turning on itself" will become the image of the "I think" in the paralogisms, where Kant speaks of "a perpetual circle".

The concept's poverty is even greater. It is not only that, left to itself, it remains the analytic doubling of a logical principle unable to become the concept of a reality, an object. More deeply, it denotes the concept's principled impossibility of first reaching knowledge of itself. This self-knowledge is not knowledge: it exhibits no reality, not even the concept's own, and cannot lead to grasping it; it is only "une conscience vide et formelle" (an empty and formal consciousness, p. 134) whose status remains undetermined and whose affirmation is groundless. The concept's inability to know itself anticipates the claim that the "I think" is, in Kant, not a self-knowledge and not a self.

The ontological poverty of the concept, unable to exhibit any reality and first its own, is not peculiar to it. It concerns the transcendental power of knowledge in general, and so intuition as pure intuition. It is the ecstatic structure as such, the essence of representativity, that is foreign to reality and deprived of it.
