This is why the condition said to be that of all experience must seek outside itself what it lacks in principle, why it becomes intuition, receptive and finite intuition. The finitude of intuition is not a proper character to be noted as an unexplained and fatal property of the human condition. It follows from a prior, unthought presupposition at the heart of Kantianism and every philosophy of representation: the reduction of absolute subjectivity to representation. Because subjectivity has no reality in itself, because, not experiencing itself and not giving itself to itself as what it is, "elle n'est pas une vie, elle n'est pas la Vie" (it is not a life, it is not Life, p. 135), it must seek the ontological element of reality outside itself. The ecstatic deployment of exteriority in pure intuition and concept is only the way in which "une subjectivité qui n'en est pas une" (a subjectivity that is not one) tries to reach being outside itself, in its representation. Henry here inverts Heidegger's *Kantbuch*: finitude is not the ground of transcendence; it is the consequence of having reduced subjectivity to transcendence. A subjectivity that is life would not need to receive reality from outside.

But exteriority is not being either. What it exhibits as it exhibits itself in the outside of the phenomenological horizon it constitutes is only the emptiness of that horizon, "ce n'est encore rien" (it is still nothing, p. 135); by Kant's own account the transcendental power of knowledge cannot ground actual knowledge, cannot posit a reality. What then is reality, insofar as ecstasis cannot posit it? Sensation, as said. But sensation is possible only as self-impressing, on the ground of the essence of original subjectivity as life. "Ce que la subjectivité kantienne cherche hors d'elle ce n'est rien d'autre que sa propre essence" (what Kantian subjectivity seeks outside itself is nothing but its own essence), the essence of Life. The empirical, contingent being that the transcendental power, reduced to an empty form, must join to itself to reach synthetic knowledge hides in itself "l'Apriori véritable" (the true a priori). That is why this contingent element is what is most necessary to authentic experience, which is possible only as a modality of life and presupposes it. The reversal of a priori and a posteriori is the chapter's thesis: the true a priori is the affective self-impression Kant consigns to the empirical.

That ecstasis, emptied of reality by itself and forced to find it outside, tries to reach it as the empirical manifold, as empirical intuition, is made manifest by the critique of the paralogisms. Rational psychology is a science of the soul, of the self, or rather of the essence of ipseity as the original essence of subjectivity, which in Kantianism has become the transcendental condition of the possibility of objects, ecstasis itself. But rational psychology is a pure, a priori science claiming real knowledge of the soul, of subjectivity, of "thought", by relying on thought alone, setting aside every empirical predicate as impure. Such a science of subjectivity based on subjectivity alone is possible only if subjectivity's essence consists in self-revelation. When subjectivity is reduced to ecstasis, when phenomenality is that of exteriority, all knowledge and science borrow their possibility from exteriority, self-knowledge included. Kant's critique of the paralogisms then unfolds its prescriptions as follows. Henry thus states the logic of the chapter: rational psychology fails not because the soul is unknowable but because, for Kant, all knowing is ecstatic. With a different concept of phenomenality, an a priori science of the soul would be possible: Henry's own phenomenology of life.

Rational psychology is a pure science of the soul resting on thought alone. But knowledge without empirical predicates is not synthetic. It can display only transcendental predicates, which are empty. Consider substantiality, simplicity, identity, distinct existence: these are certainly predicates of thought. But since it is not an actual subjectivity grounded in the phenomenon of its own essence, Kantian thought is only "l'unité problématique formelle d'une connaissance possible" (the formal problematic unity of a possible knowledge, p. 136), real only under the condition of empirical intuition. Transcendental predicates are only the predicates of this formal unity, its purely analytic doubling and explication; problematic and possible like it, they are in no way real predicates of a real being, the self. Rational psychology fails to define real knowledge of the self because it could do so only by empirical predicates resting on empirical intuition, which it claims to do without. Henry presents the four paralogisms (substance, simplicity, personal identity, ideality of outer relation) as a single argument from the emptiness of the logical "I".

It is not only on the side of intuition but also of the concept that the transcendental power of knowledge proves deficient, since it lacks a genuine concept of the self. A genuine concept is first more than a category: it is a concept of an object, determining an intuition, which rational psychology lacks by hypothesis. But that is not all: this concept would have to be the concept of the self. Even if the transcendental condition of knowledge can be likened to a concept in the broad sense, as the concept of an object in general, it is in no way the concept of a self, still less of my self. Consciousness, Kant says, is not "a representation that distinguishes a particular object" (A 346/B 404); it does not let me discern my own being in its specificity, since it gives me no particular concept of the particular object I call me. Relying only on thought (subjectivity) as Kant understands it, the logical condition of representation, "on ne possède donc ni concept ni intuition d'un moi" (one has neither concept nor intuition of a self, p. 137): every condition Kant's ontology requires for any knowledge, for any actual phenomenon, is missing. The double lack (no intuition, no individuating concept) is what makes Kant's "I" both empty and anonymous. Henry will argue that both lacks stem from the same cause, and that the ipseity of the self is given neither by intuition nor by concept but by auto-affection.

The failure of rational psychology raises a question: how can the being of our self be determined and known if not from pure thought? Further, where does the very idea of a self come from, since each of us speaks of himself only by saying "I, me"? The theory of inner experience answers, and it simply reaffirms the usual presuppositions of Kant's ontology. Inner experience will produce the being of the self by subjecting it to the conditions of experience in general, through the intuitive determination of a concept that yields knowledge of the empirical self. To be, the self must first be received in intuition: a specific empirical element, an impression, will be supplied; intuited in inner sense, in and through time, it will be subjected to the categories, which assign it, like any empirical datum, a definite place in the overall system of the universe, making it a "phenomenon" in the sense of an object of knowledge (p. 138). Henry treats inner experience as the positive counterpart of the paralogisms: having denied pure knowledge of the self, Kant offers empirical knowledge, which turns out to presuppose what it cannot supply.

The need to subject this impression to the categories leads Kant to reject psychological idealism. The categories of substance and causality apply only to a permanent object, which inner sense cannot exhibit, since it is only the temporal form in which everything flows and nothing remains. Only outer intuitions can supply the matter of an object subsumable under categories requiring something permanent. Inner life, the subjective succession of impressions in inner sense, can be organized by the category, thought and known, only if the knowing subject rests it on an objective universe and a permanent order of objects in space. This is the Refutation of Idealism (B 274–279), on which Henry spends no more than this summary.

Attention to the refutation of problematic idealism seriously hinders a properly philosophical understanding of Kant's theory of the self. The fundamental problems of the ultimate interpretation of being are passed over; interest shifts to the secondary question whether there is an autonomous subjective series, whether inner experience, empirical knowledge of the self in time, is bound to an external objective order. One pretends to ask whether there are two kinds of experience or only one, that of real phenomena determined by the categories. One fails to see that even if Kant had admitted an autonomous subjective series, its modalities, received in inner sense whose form is time, the original structure of ecstasis, remain subject to it and so are "representations", as Kant constantly calls them. The being of the self is thus reduced to representations and the object they form, "quand rien ne répugne davantage à l'essence de l'ipséité ... que l'être-représenté comme tel" (when nothing is more repugnant to the essence of ipseity than being-represented as such, p. 138). Here lies the aporia of any metaphysics of representativity that tries to determine the being of a self, as a radical critique of Kant's inner experience will show. Henry sets aside the debate between "realist" and "phenomenalist" readings of the Refutation: both share the premise that inner life is a series of representations.

In the ecstasis that grounds representation, here inner sense, what we have access to is exteriority, showing itself as the ontological element of pure otherness. Sense, as Kant expressly says, always means affection by something foreign, and first of all inner sense. Because inner sense is constituted by the ecstasis of time, which conditions the use of any natural sense, a sense can be a sense and relate us to any being, as foreign. The paralogism subtly included in Kant's theory lies in the very name "inner sense", which nothing authorizes, since its essence makes it the sense of exteriority, an outer sense and nothing else. To separate outer from inner sense, Kant has the difference between the pure intuitive properties of space and time. But that time's pure content differs from space's does not make it an inner content, nor the sense that exhibits it an "inner" sense. One may think rather that the exteriority of space rests on that unfolded by the ecstasis of time, so that space itself is in time and, as Heidegger will say, "in the world". This unavoidable ontological situation is masked by the presupposition that the pure content produced in the ecstasis of time refers to a self and belongs to it. This is wholly unfounded in Kant, indeed absurd, if ipseity draws its possibility from an affection whose content, the affecting, is identical with the affected and nothing exterior to it, if the ego is "par principe ce qui n'est pas susceptible d'être intuitionné" (by principle what cannot be intuited, p. 139). How would the intuiting power come to think that what it intuits in exteriority is a self, its own self, how would it seek it there and recognize it, "s'il ne le possédait d'abord en soi" (if it did not first possess it in itself, p. 140), as the Self it inhabits and is, before ecstasis and intuition? The argument has two steps: "inner sense" is a misnomer, since time is the form of exteriority; and recognizing anything in it as myself presupposes a prior non-intuitive self-possession.

Kant nonetheless insists that an intuition is needed to determine the being of the self. The theory of inner experience seeks it in the impression of "inner" sense; the paralogism of rational psychology consists in dispensing with it. What is the philosophical motive of this demand, and why could Kant go so far astray as to see in an intuitive manifold, by principle deprived of what makes the Self a self, the indispensable condition of its experience and existence? Henry's answer: "la reconnaissance tacite de la passivité inhérente à l'essence de l'ipséité" (the tacit recognition of the passivity inherent in the essence of ipseity, p. 140). Thought, for Kant, is pure spontaneity; that it does not contain the being of a self means that the self lacks the power to posit itself, so its being must be received, given in intuition. "Telle est la vue profonde de Kant" (such is Kant's profound insight): the self does not produce itself and can acquire its concept of itself only empirically, not a priori. A note adds that this is why Kant refuses to apply to the self as thinking being the concept of substance, "a subject existing by itself" (Henry's emphasis). But because Kant knows no mode of receptivity other than intuition, ecstasis, what must be received appears as other, in the element of exteriority, and so can no longer be a self. Henry credits Kant with the deepest insight of the chapter: ipseity is passive, not self-positing, against Fichte. Kant's error is only to model this passivity on sensible receptivity.

What is to be received and must supply the being of the self is no longer exteriority, which is pure otherness and, in any case, only a secondary receptivity relative to thought, since it is actually produced by the transcendental power of knowledge, by time as an original intuition creating its own content. Exteriority is here only the medium through which what is supposed to bring the being of the self, the impression of inner sense, must be received, as distanced by this original exteriority; in Kant's terms, as intuited in inner sense. "Mais la matière du sens interne est-elle susceptible d'exhiber en elle l'être d'un moi ?" (But can the matter of inner sense exhibit the being of a self? p. 141) The question shifts from form (time) to matter (the impression in inner sense).

It is hard first to say what this matter consists in. Insofar as inner sense is only the pure form of temporal intuition, ecstasis, it seems to have no matter of its own. Only by designating the way the mind apprehends the manifold of outer intuition can inner sense receive the transcendental meaning the schematism gives it, by which a pure time, devoid as it were of irreducible intuitive properties, can lend itself to the action of the category and mediate it. But there is no concept of the self that could, merely by subsuming a manifold, make it the specific matter of the object "self". It belongs to the manifold of intuition to specify the object. Since there is no concept of the self independent of intuition, intuition must ground such a concept, which becomes the concept of a self only by determining a specific intuition. Inner sense must supply a manifold that is no longer arbitrary. The demand is now precise: a manifold that is intrinsically mine.

That is exactly what it cannot do. Insofar as the matter of inner sense, the impression received in it, is received in and through the original exteriority of ecstasis, it is only something impressional, intuitive, sensible but transcendent, comparable in every respect to outer sensible intuitions, with nothing to distinguish it from them so as to refer it to a self rather than to any object. Such is the inescapable fate of every ecstatic conception of being and its claim to recognize impression as a phenomenon only in intuition: to have deprived itself, along with the inner essence of impression as self-impression and ipseity, "du lien originel qui ... unit cette impression à un « moi »" (of the original bond that, on the ground of its essence, unites this impression to a "self", p. 142). The mineness of an impression is thus not a relation added to it but its mode of givenness; once givenness is ecstatic, mineness is lost.

As an unconscious and vain attempt to overcome this difficulty, the second edition makes an effort to ground the specificity of the manifold of inner sense by linking it to the very self it should make known. This manifold ceases to be arbitrary if it contains impressions arising from the determination of inner sense not by outer objects but by the acts of the understanding pursuing knowledge, and so by the I belonging to them. The transcendental power synthesizes the manifold of outer intuition by applying its categories and constructing the object of perception; while turned toward the outer object, each transcendental act of determination affects inner sense internally, producing a "shock" (*choc*), the "counter-blow" of its exercise, which is the impression or sensation of inner sense. This sensation is no longer arbitrary, like those the mind refers to outer things. Arising from the self that constructs the universe, it is bound to it as its result and offers itself as the specific manifold of inner sense, specified by its inner relation to a self. Henry is describing the doctrine of self-affection in B 153–157 (§ 24), the "affection of inner sense by the understanding", which Heidegger in the *Kantbuch* (§ 34) made the essence of time. Henry's reading differs: it is a stopgap, not Kant's deepest insight.

With the specific sensation of inner sense, Kant has the two conditions his theory requires for knowledge, or rather existence, of the self. First, the empirical element: the existence of the self, like all existence, presupposes sensation. Even a priori representations acquire existence only as belonging to inner sense, as modifications of the mind. Kant does not confuse the transcendental power with inner sense; through this distinction he deliberately places the feeling or existence of the self on the side of inner sense, as a modality belonging to it, the repercussion in it of an original act of the power of knowledge. Believing he follows Descartes, Kant separates "I think" and "I am": the passage from the first to the second is not an inference but the act by which the mind affects itself, producing in inner sense, as it determines outer affections, an impression that is "la trace empirique de cet acte pur de détermination" (the empirical trace of this pure act of determination, p. 143). The existence of the self, the "I am", is this empirical impression that immediately doubles the "I think". In a famous note of the second edition, Kant calls it "an indeterminate empirical intuition" (B 422–423 note), not yet subject to the category: "existence is not yet a category here". This illustrates the uncrossable limit of the ecstatic definition of being: existence must be sought outside the pure representations of thought and intuition, in sensation, and posited independently of the category of existence. That the anomaly occurs for the existence of the self is not indifferent; but existence in general, despite the ecstatic presuppositions, escapes the general conditions of experience in Kant's ontology. The B 423 note, where the "I think" contains an indeterminate perception of existence prior to the category, is the most important Kantian text for Henry, since there Kant admits an existence given before any categorial objectification.

That knowledge of the self is constituted only with the categorial determination of this initially indeterminate empirical intuition changes nothing in the initial situation and invites a return to it. The impression of inner sense grounds the existence of the self on which knowledge of it will rest only if, besides existence, it contains a self, the second condition of the theory. Is this condition not met if the impression expresses the immediate resonance (hence not yet categorially determined) of the transcendental act of the "I think" in inner sense, and so the existence in it of this "I" (p. 144)? Henry poses the question to answer it negatively in the next unit by a circularity argument.

But the impression can bear this character of belonging to a self, as arising from it, only if the ipseity of this self has been established where it originally unfolds, in the transcendental power, as the I of the "I think", only if the existence of the transcendental Self has been recognized and grounded beforehand. Yet the critique of the paralogism keeps repeating that no self's existence can be deduced from pure thought, so much so that the first edition says the I of the "I think" must be taken only problematically. Kant thus claims both that the self of pure thought exists only in the form of the impression of inner sense, and that this impression is the existence of a self only insofar as it is supposed to come from this self of pure thought, "qui n'existe pas ou qui n'existe que dans cette impression" (which does not exist or exists only in this impression, p. 144). The circle is vicious: the impression is mine because it comes from the I, and the I exists only as the impression.

Moreover, it is not enough to assert that the impression of inner sense is produced by the transcendental subject. Unless it is to remain an idle speculative hypothesis, the origin of this impression, which determines its relation to a self, must be exhibited in its phenomenological effectiveness; this exhibition is that of the transcendental subject itself as it affects inner sense. Such affection is nothing but the essence of subjectivity insofar as it affects itself and is originally constituted as auto-affection. This affection is not merely phenomenological; it is "le naturant et l'effectuation première de toute phénoménalité" (the naturing and first accomplishment of all phenomenality, p. 144), appearing's self-appearing and its principial possibility. Nor is it linked to a self exterior to it whose mysterious trace or reflection it would bear; it is ipseity itself and its inner genesis. Further, this auto-affection is not only what affects (the transcendental self) but also what is affected (inner sense), grounding the possibility of being affected in general, because it is first affected by its own reality: as auto-affection, self-impression and the essence of every possible impression, it can be impressed by anything else and by the world. "Le sens interne ne reçoit pas des impressions venues d'ailleurs ... il est le lieu où elles se forment" (inner sense does not receive impressions coming from elsewhere; it is the place where they are formed, p. 145), because in everything that affects it in ecstasis it has already affected itself in the affectivity of its own essence. That is why it is an inner sense. Henry rewrites Kantian self-affection in his own terms: the identity of affecting and affected (Chapter III) is the true inner sense, and it is the condition of all outer affection.

But Kant's inner sense is the ecstasis of time: in it the affecting and the affected are different, external to each other, separated by exteriority as such, which constitutes affection itself, phenomenality. The conditions of the auto-affection that secretly defines it do not exist in inner sense as Kant understands it. One can say that this sense produces the content of its affection and so "affects itself". But as ecstasis it produces exteriority as the pure content of its affection; it is affected by that, in no way by its own reality, which is being-affected and its possibility. That the Same affects and is affected only posits the tautological doubling of this Same, "non son essence intérieure en tant que celle de l'ipséité" (not its inner essence as that of ipseity, p. 146). A long note acknowledges Kant's French commentators, Jean Nabert's "L'expérience interne chez Kant" (1924) and Pierre Lachièze-Rey's *L'idéalisme kantien* (1931), who showed that Kant himself saw that what matters in self-affection is inner sense's capacity to receive passively the impressions the subject produces in it, and that the *Opus postumum* (the *Übergang*) develops a theory of self-positing (*Selbstsetzung*) in which the mind first posits itself as passive so as to receive the impressions of its own acts; Lachièze-Rey writes that "the self makes itself object" and "posits itself as originally passive". Henry's objection is that this self-positing remains ecstatic: the self-posited self is a transcendent content that cannot receive in itself the impression produced in it, which is only referred to an ideal term. The theory repeats between determining and determinable self the situation of the *Critique* between transcendental self and inner sense, because it remains governed, despite Lachièze-Rey's disclaimers, by a philosophy oriented to knowledge of the object; Lachièze-Rey himself says that in the *Übergang* the self replaces the universe as correlate of mental activity and becomes "the imperative of knowledge". "Ce n'est pas un moi auto-posé ... mais seulement une subjectivité radicalement immanente qui est susceptible d'être affectée mais d'abord d'être un moi réel" (not a self-posited self, whose ipseity is itself only ideal, but only a radically immanent subjectivity can be affected, and first be a real self). The note situates Henry exactly: against Heidegger, who took Kantian self-affection as pure time, and against the French reflexive tradition (Nabert, Lachièze-Rey), which took it as self-positing. Both remain ecstatic.

One decisive condition of the self's existence, required by Kant himself, must not be forgotten: what inner sense must receive to contain this existence is not empty exteriority but a sensation. The condition of receiving an impression, as opposed to exteriority, is no longer ecstasis but self-impression, which constitutes identically the essence of the impression and its reception. And, by an abrupt shift in the critical problematic that deliberately breaks with an ontology of representativity, this impression contains existence, reality, and defines it. That the impression of inner sense defines the original dimension of existence and reality, and that this existence is that of a self, Kant's second express claim, holds precisely because, as self-impression, it defines both the original essence of subjectivity as being and life, and that of ipseity. Henry finds his own thesis implicit in Kant's two requirements: existence needs sensation, and the sensation must be mine; only self-impression satisfies both.

But the impression received in Kant's inner sense, intuited in the time of ecstasis, is only a representative sensation, spread on the surface of things and belonging to them, an outer empirical intuition like all others (one recalls the *Critique*'s embarrassment in separating the contents of the two senses), something impressional, sensible and affective which is "comme l'humus du monde, le dehors mystérieux d'un dedans qui ne s'y montre jamais tel qu'il est en soi" (like the humus of the world, the mysterious outside of an inside that never shows itself there as it is in itself, p. 147). Representative sensation is only the representation of original impression; in its exteriority the properties of absolute subjectivity, reality, existence, affectivity, ipseity, life, become unreal. That is why Kant could not find in the transcendent content of inner sense the real existence of the self he sought there. "Representative sensation" (*sensation représentative*) is a crucial coinage: the sensible quality as it appears on things is the representation of an impression, not the impression; Freud's "affect bound to a representation" will be read in the same way.

If the content of intuition, sensation insofar as it "belongs to sensibility", proves definitively blind, unable to exhibit the ipseity of the self, it remains to seek it on the side of the transcendental power, the I of the "I think", and Kant is forced to do so, returning almost despite himself to the rational psychology he claimed to discard. Not entirely. Rational psychology asserts that the being of the self belonging to pure thought can be known absolutely, from pure thought and as identical with it. In its inaugural Cartesian form its radical thesis means only this: because the soul, absolute subjectivity, thought, is the original coming of appearing into itself, which makes it possible and so being-in-itself, and because this coming into itself is the essence of ipseity, then thought so understood contains, as identical with it, both the "being" and the "knowledge" of the "SELF". Henry rehabilitates rational psychology in its Cartesian root: its error was not to claim an a priori knowledge of the soul but to construe that knowledge as representation.

But in a metaphysics of representativity that cannot subject to representation what remains its condition, the very act of positing and representing, this condition, the I of pure thought, escapes by principle the phenomenality it grounds. "Thought, taken by itself, is merely the logical function, and hence the sheer spontaneity of combining the manifold of a merely possible intuition, and it in no way presents the subject of consciousness as appearance" (B 428–429). For the I of pure thought to be a phenomenon, the text continues, an intuition would be needed, the self coming into the condition of being-represented. Lacking it, one can only say, in the paragraph's strange conclusion: "I represent myself to myself neither as I am nor as I appear to myself, but think myself only as any object in general from whose mode of intuition I abstract" (B 429, Henry's emphasis). For Henry this conclusion is the self-refutation of the metaphysics of representation: the condition of all appearing is said not to appear.

What leaves the presupposition of rational psychology intact in this radical critique is the designation as I of this mere "logical function", this "spontaneity of combining" that pure thought is. The *Critique* constantly distinguishes itself from rational psychology by repeating that no synthetic knowledge of the real being of a self or its real properties (identity, simplicity, permanence, immateriality) can be deduced from the I of pure thought. Yet, granted grudgingly and surrounded by reservations, the concession remains "énorme" (enormous, p. 148): it concedes the phenomenality and ipseity of the condition, in itself non-ecstatic and so non-phenomenal, of all ecstasis and phenomenality, in Kant's terms, the inherence of an I in pure thought. The concession is constant: "I am conscious of myself in the transcendental synthesis of the manifold of representations in general ... not as I appear to myself, nor as I am in myself, but only that I am" (B 157); "the I is indeed in all thoughts"; "the only condition accompanying all thought, the I, is in the universal proposition 'I think'"; "the proposition that expresses self-consciousness: I think". "Qu'en est-il donc du Je du je pense ?" (What then of the I of the "I think"? p. 149) The "consciousness that I am" of B 157 is Henry's evidence that Kant knows a self-givenness that is neither appearance nor thing in itself, without being able to conceptualize it.

The insurmountable difficulty is clear: Kantianism must define the being of the I independently of the conditions of being in general, abstracting from intuition, empirical and pure, and from the concept. When it must ground the existence of the "I think" taken not as an empirical proposition based on an intuition (a note recalls B 422–423: "I think", meaning "I exist thinking", is not merely logical but determines the subject with respect to existence and requires inner sense) but as the fact of pure thought, Kant's embarrassment becomes inextricable. It shows in his formulas: the "I think" is in turn a concept, a judgment ("this concept or, if you will, this judgment"), an expression, a mere presentation of thought ("it serves only to present all thought as belonging to consciousness"), "a formal proposition", the mere consciousness of a power of synthesis. The term "concept", which would replace the problem within the general constitution of experience, cannot be kept and is explicitly rejected: the representation "I" cannot even be called a concept but is "a mere consciousness that accompanies all concepts" (A 346/B 404), and "this I is as little an intuition as it is a concept of any object". When it is called a "proposition", what is expressed is the spontaneity of pure thought. That this thought is, in all Kant's expressions ("logical unity", "logically simple subject", "analytic proposition", "identity of the subject"), only an empty form means it is deprived of being. Being requires the transcendental deployment of the powers of relating to objects, ecstasis, and the reception of sensation as empirical intuition. Without it, how can the being of this pure, empty form be determined, apart from what it constitutes as object? Kant expressly says thought is given neither to concept nor to intuition. How then can it arise into being? The question is unavoidable "si l'être du je pense est celui du pouvoir qui intuitionne et qui pense, celui de l'ek-stasis" (if the being of the "I think" is that of the power that intuits and thinks, that of ecstasis, p. 150). Henry's catalogue of Kant's names for the "I think" is a symptom-reading: terminological instability marks a conceptually unplaceable phenomenon.

Wherever Kant tries to name the being of the "I think" in itself, the one expression he uses without at once correcting it is "intellectual representation". Negatively, this means that no empirical element, no sensation, enters it; the *Critique*'s explicit presuppositions, which seek the condition of existence and being in intuition, are suspended. Positively, it means that when I say "I think" I actually represent to myself that I think. "D'entrée de jeu Kant a substitué au cogito une représentation de celui-ci" (From the start Kant substituted for the *cogito* a representation of it, p. 150): for the mode of phenomenality of the original revelation that defines the *cogito*, the soul, Cartesian thought, a revelation of which Kant knows nothing, he substituted the phenomenality of representation, the only one he knows, produced in ecstasis, in thought as representation and in intuition. This explains the "astonishing" note in the second edition's critique of the paralogisms and its sudden passage from the "I think" as empirical proposition to the "I think" as "purely intellectual representation" (a note quotes Kant: though I called "I think" an empirical proposition, the I in it is not an empirical representation but "purely intellectual, because it belongs to thought in general", B 423 note): on the ground of the same ecstatic structure of phenomenality, the I can be defined both as (indeterminate) empirical intuition and as purely intellectual representation, and slide from one to the other. Henry identifies the Kantian paralogism proper: not rational psychology's inference to substance, but Kant's own substitution of a representation of the *cogito* for the *cogito*.

That Kant substitutes for the *cogito* its representation appears in the way he constantly treats it as an expression, the statement in which thought represents itself, statement and expression having taken its place and passing for it. The way Kant "calls", "designates", "expresses", "represents" the "I think" conceals and distorts its true being, reducing it each time to "the proposition 'I think'", to the content of a representation. But when the "I think", the condition of possibility of all representation and of its own representation, is reduced to the content of a representation, not only is its being clouded over; the condition of this representation and of all others collapses. A propositional "I think" presupposes the very "I think" it expresses; once the expression is taken for the thing, the ground disappears.

Kant carried a metaphysics of representativity to its end, to the extreme point where, claiming to ground itself, to subject its own condition to representation, it "bascule dans le néant et s'auto-détruit" (tips into nothingness and self-destructs, p. 151). With this condition of all representation, not only representation is lost, but its wholly other, this condition itself, the being of the "I think", "l'essence de la vie" (the essence of life). The chapter title's "lost life" is explained: life is lost not by being ignored but by being subjected to representation, which can only empty it.

In the technical texture of Kant's text, substituting for the *cogito* its representation as the condition of its coming into being as phenomenon is explicit. The "I think" is the condition of all unity, the synthetic unity uniting the manifold of intuition in one representation; it is the form of apperception. Is this form a phenomenon, does it bear the original essence of self-revelation that would make it, as apperception, a modality of life and the effectiveness of the first experience? Kant denies it: the form of apperception "is inherent in all experience" but "is not itself an experience" (A 354; Henry quotes it in a note). What would make it one, what would make the condition of all unity, the unity of consciousness, conscious of itself? That it be represented: "consciousness of self in general is the representation of that which is the condition of all unity" (p. 152). Henry's point is that Kant can conceive self-consciousness only as a representation of the condition, so that the condition itself is never self-conscious.

The Kantian proposition must be reversed, and the possibility that the condition of representation be represented rejected. Not only would the coming of its own condition, the "I think", into representation bring down the whole system, since the represented is always held by the "I think", posited before it and by it, which can never itself come into the condition of being represented. This logical prescription of the system of representation, which makes the I the logical condition of representation and experience, is only the speculative and still blind formulation of a far more radical phenomenological prescription: the principled impossibility for a thought originally constituted as immanent coming into itself, and so as ipseity, as "I think", to show itself in the phenomenological medium of exteriority, to be "represented". In its critique of the paralogism, "prononçant lui-même sa propre condamnation" (pronouncing its own condemnation, p. 152), Kantianism will establish this magnificently. Kant's logical point (the subject cannot be an object without regress) is grounded in a phenomenological one (immanence cannot appear in exteriority); this is how Henry can call the paralogism chapter an achievement while rejecting its premises.

The "I think" is, Kant declares, "the sole text of rational psychology" (A 343/B 401). But this text is something only as the "proposition" "I think", as purely intellectual representation, the "I think" as the object of this representation. Since the "I think" is by principle what cannot be represented, what appears at once is the poverty of this representation, the fundamental ontological poverty of rational psychology's only text. Once the paralogism is recognized, no longer that of rational psychology but of Kant himself, the substitution of its representation for the "I think", the critique of the paralogism appears as "une admirable description de cette représentation « la plus pauvre de toutes »" (an admirable description of this representation, "the poorest of all", p. 153), and rightly shows the impossibility of building a positive science on so narrow a basis. Henry's reading thus preserves the critique of the paralogisms as true of its object, while changing the object: Kant describes the emptiness of the represented I, not of the self.

The poverty of the representation "I think" is often shown by comparing its content with the conditions real objects of experience must meet; because it does not meet them, the content is declared empty or illusory. Its sterility is explained by its containing no manifold, or by its being not a concept but only the representation of the formal condition of thought, "the vehicle of all concepts". Henry distinguishes this external characterization of the poverty (measured against the conditions of objects) from the internal description that follows.

Sometimes, however, the emptiness of this representation is described for itself, as in the paralogism of simplicity. The first edition uses the word "simplicity" in two senses. First, a positive character supposed to determine the being of the self as a real predicate; that determination is the paralogism. But the term is kept in what follows to designate the being of the representation "I think", because this being is not one, marked by an essential poverty, that of a pure represented and empty unity. Simplicity designates precisely the poverty of an empty representation: "'I am simple' means no more than that this representation, I, ... is an absolute (though merely logical) unity" (A 355). Simplicity is that of "a something in general ... whose representation must be absolutely simple, just because nothing is determined of it, and nothing can be represented more simply than by the concept of a mere something" (A 355, Henry's emphasis). A note draws the conclusion: the simplicity of the representation does not mean knowledge of simplicity but its absence, the emptiness of the representation of a something in general. The two senses of "simplicity" repeat in Kant the amphibology Henry found in Descartes: a property of the self versus a property of its representation.

The poverty of the representation "I think" is also denounced in a text all the more remarkable because, at the very moment the self is confused with its representation, the poverty of its being appears as a phenomenological character of that representation: "in what we call soul everything is in continual flux, and there is nothing abiding, except (if one insists) the I, which is so simple only because this representation has no content" (A 381). So when Kant also says that the proposition "everything flows and nothing permanent remains in the world" is "not refuted by the unity of self-consciousness", one must understand that the poverty of being, insufficient to repel the idea of universal flux, is that of a representation, since for Kant the unity of self-consciousness is only the representation of the unity of consciousness. The Heraclitean flux of inner sense and the emptiness of the I are two faces of the same reduction of subjectivity to time; Henry's later "continual" self-experience (Chapter II, the end of the *cogito* reading) answers both.

The emptiness of the representation "I think", in which one represents only a something in general ("through this I, or he, or it, the thing which thinks, nothing further is represented than a transcendental subject of thoughts = X", A 346/B 404), explains how so undetermined a content can take on many later determinations, provided they keep the unreality of so empty a representation. On the ground of its ontological poverty, as a declension of the transcendental object that is the subject of thoughts, of which we know nothing, it will appear in turn as the I that constructs the universe (the subject of representation), as the object of inner sense, as the reference point of subjective representations, as an ideal term produced by Reason to unify inner phenomena, as an Idea, as an unknown and unknowable thing in itself, and finally as the passive self-posited self. These meanings are not contingent; they meet the needs of the system at its various stages; the distance between the determinable self-posited self and the determined, known empirical self measures the progress of the constitution of experience. No genuine determination is conferred by them. What the transcendental object Self brings them is its aptitude to serve as their subject, due entirely to its indeterminacy. "Comme les nuages du ciel peuvent prendre toutes les formes qu'on imagine parce qu'ils n'en ont aucune, le contenu de la représentation je pense, l'ego transcendant dont parleront à leur tour les phénoménologies contemporaines, est un fantôme conciliant" (as clouds can take any shape we imagine because they have none, the content of the representation "I think", the transcendent ego of which contemporary phenomenologies will speak in their turn, is an accommodating phantom, p. 155). The "transcendent ego" is Sartre's in *La transcendance de l'ego*; the jibe extends the critique to the French phenomenological reception of Husserl.

However undetermined and poor, the representation "I think" is still too rich, insofar as it is the representation of a self, insofar as the transcendental object = X it designates is rather a transcendental subject affected in its being by an ipseity that makes it, unknown as it is, not merely "this he" but "this I". Precisely because the representation "I think" includes an I, it supports all the specifications the system gives it as needed. What marks them decisively is that, whether as subject of knowledge to which every known object is referred as an "I represent", as noumenal Self not to be confused with the object of inner experience and limiting its pretension to reveal our true being, or as idea of the soul, a heuristic concept for separating inner from outer phenomena, "elles portent toutes en elles cette ipséité cachée qu'elles empruntent à la simple représentation « moi »" (they all bear this hidden ipseity which they borrow from the mere representation "I", p. 156). This representation is not as poor as it seems: its poverty permits its declension, but its unnoticed positivity makes all the determinations of the system that imply ipseity converge on it, "et elles l'impliquent toutes !" (and they all imply it!). Henry reverses his own previous unit: the represented I is empty as representation, yet it carries a stolen positivity, the ipseity that representation cannot generate.

The "mere representation I" secretly obeys the insurmountable prescriptions of the essence of ipseity, which are finally those of life. It is not the representation of the self but of a self, an ego always particular and individual, as Kant profoundly recognizes when he says that the representation "I am" governing pure psychology is "singular" and "individual in every respect". The paralogism occurs precisely when this essentially singular representation passes itself off as universal. A note forestalls an objection: when Kant says elsewhere that the I is not the representation of a particular object (p. 137 above), he speaks on another plane, of consciousness as the general transcendental power of knowledge not yet specified as a concept of object, before the problem of the being of the "I think" is posed as that of a purely intellectual representation. Why is the representation "I think" that of a singular, individual reality, if not because, on the ground of ipseity as auto-affection, whatever bears this essence, experiencing itself and having for the content of its being what it experiences, is necessarily this experienced, particular and singular reality that it itself is? "À l'essence de l'auto-affection il appartient de ne pouvoir s'accomplir phénoménologiquement que sous la forme d'une affection déterminée" (It belongs to the essence of auto-affection that it can be accomplished phenomenologically only as a determinate affection, p. 156). Kant rightly reproaches pure psychology for claiming to determine the object in itself independently of experience, by reason alone. But this experience could be that of the real, singular, individual self each of us is, of the soul and life, only on condition of not being its representation, of drawing its original phenomenological possibility from somewhere other than ecstasis. The principle that auto-affection is always a determinate affection grounds Henry's later thesis (*C'est moi la vérité*, *Incarnation*) that life is always the life of a singular living being; it also answers the charge that his "life" is an impersonal absolute.

To elucidate this nature of life further, and first to recover it, one must now turn to thoughts which, without Kant's extraordinary analytic apparatus or conceptual splendour, "n'en font pas moins signe plus sûrement vers l'Essentiel" (point more surely toward the Essential, p. 157). The transition announces Chapter V, on Schopenhauer: a philosophy of weaker conceptual means but truer orientation.

## Chapter V. Life Regained: The World as Will

Schopenhauer can appear today, despite his uncertainties, inconsistencies and theoretical weaknesses, as one of the most important philosophers in our history because he introduced a radical break: the explicit and decisive rejection of the interpretation of being as representedness. He does not ignore or minimize the field opened by representation; for him it co-constitutes the being of the world, defines it and is identical with it: "the world is my representation" (*World as Will and Representation*, § 1). He thereby continues and reinforces what must already, in 1818, be called the Kantian tradition: representation determines "the mode of all possible experience", and "everything that exists, exists for thought", the whole universe being object only in relation to a subject. A crucial element of this tradition, stressed in Chapter IV, that the subject "knows everything without itself being known", does not escape him. But a nuance appears in the first pages of the book, "une de ces nuances imperceptibles par lesquelles se met en place un monde infiniment proche de celui qui le précède et infiniment éloigné, un monde nouveau" (one of those imperceptible nuances by which a world infinitely close to its predecessor and infinitely far from it, a new world, sets itself up, p. 160). On one hand, Schopenhauer grasps, from Berkeley and against Fichte, that subject and object go together and form one and the same form, that of all representability: "the division into object and subject is the essential, original and common form of all representation". Hence no metaphysical breakthrough to the thing in itself can come from their opposition (one cannot, for example, assign freedom to the subject and necessity to the object), since "the subject is posited at the same time as the object and vice versa". The identity of the subject's being-subject and the object's being-object is Schopenhauer's insight into the one structure of the world as representability. On the other hand, he rejects this one structure as unable to contain and exhibit the essence of reality, which escapes all representation. The concept of representation, which has guided philosophy since Kant and, according to Heidegger, since Descartes, is devalued, absolutely and not relatively, if it means the principled heterogeneity of reality and representation, which falls back into unreality and defines it. Henry takes over Schopenhauer's subject–object correlation as his own description of ecstasis (Chapter III), and identifies Schopenhauer's novelty in the claim that this correlation, as such, is the domain of unreality.

This explains what is usually considered one of Schopenhauer's major misreadings, strange in a philosopher who discussed Kant closely: reducing Kant's thesis that we know only phenomena to phenomenalism, which allows Kant to be assimilated to Plato, for whom the sensible is the appearance of a deeper reality, and to Indian thought, for which the whole visible universe is illusion, the veil of Maya. Kant's whole doctrine aims to separate subjective appearance, the mere course of our representations, from the necessary order that makes them true and makes them "phenomena". But this is conceivable only if the field of representation can produce truth, and finally the reality of phenomena, only if it is itself constituted as such a power, as a power of intuition and thought. Once this power is withdrawn, once intuition and thought are considered not in their specificity but in their common essence, as representation, the phenomenality that consists in representation is disjoined from reality, and reality is no longer understood in its appearance and content. What Schopenhauer rejects is "le mode de présentation de la représentation ... sous la forme d'un « poser devant »" (representation's mode of presentation as a "setting before", p. 161), the very possibility of reaching reality from outside. In his "misreading" of the Kantian phenomenon his most extreme claim is revealed. Henry treats Schopenhauer's alleged misunderstanding as a deliberate shift of level: from the empirical reality of objects (Kant's question) to the reality of manifestation itself.

The misreading is still more significant given that Schopenhauer, far from ignoring the order of things (the true phenomenon) in contrast to their mere course (pure appearance), holds that causal thinking is already present in the amoeba and understanding at work wherever there is representation. His amalgam in the principle of sufficient reason, making causality coextensive with representation and placing it on a level with the a priori forms of sensibility, is intelligible only as the design of a thought which, deliberately going beyond the question of rational truth, the necessity of "phenomena", asks much more ultimately about the condition of possibility of transcendental truth itself, about the mode of appearing and phenomenological presentation as such. What is considered is representation, not the modalities of representing in it, their necessity or contingency. If, unlike Kant, contingency soon becomes in Schopenhauer the index of reality, it is only because it designates within representation, through its being questioned, what escapes it in principle. But how can representation indicate what escapes it? Representation is for Schopenhauer the sphere of unreality, so there is no real difference between the phenomenon, even scientifically determined, mere subjective appearance and ultimately the dream; India and Plato can both be invoked for this derealization; and the waking world, as representation, is homogeneous with dream, forming "les feuillets d'un même livre" (the leaves of one book, p. 162). But what authorizes reading this derealization in appearance as it actually appears, what allows us to say that what is exhibited there is not reality but leaves it outside? Is it the strange idea, which also implies a transcendent use of causality, that for there to be appearance there must be something appearing in it, otherwise phenomena would be appearances of nothing, phantoms? But why should what appears differ from appearance, why should reality not coincide with the content of appearance and be defined by it? To this crucial question there can only be a radical answer, which Schopenhauer gives in two basic assertions. Henry rejects the standard Kantian argument for the thing in itself (an appearance must be of something), which would be a transcendent use of causality; the derealization must be grounded otherwise.

The two theses. First, there exists a reality in itself wholly foreign to the world of representation, included neither in its mode of phenomenological presentation nor in its content. Because reality escapes the world of representation, that world is the reign of unreality and its appearance "une apparence « vide »" (an "empty" appearance, p. 163), unable to exhibit reality. Second, this reality is accessible to us, and it is because we have access to it that we know and experience the world of representation as mere appearance. This reality in itself is the will; the way it is given to us is our body. These theses must be elucidated systematically. The second thesis supplies what the first lacks: the derealization of representation is known not by inference but by contrast with an experienced reality.

The will at issue would be wholly misunderstood if taken in the usual sense, likened even for a moment to the will of classical philosophy, the mere fact of willing or not willing and, more ultimately, the pure power of willing or not willing as unconditioned and absolute. It has nothing to do with Descartes' free will, a will of indifference containing no principle of action, determining itself freely and assenting to a motive only by seeking it elsewhere, in the understanding, which finally tells action what its content must be, the will merely saying yes or no unconditionally. Schopenhauer's will has no use for the understanding; far from asking it for its law and modelling itself on it, it bears its law within itself; it does not stand before its action as before a possibility but "elle est cette action" (it is this action, p. 163), already decided, one with it and its content. Henry's contrast with the will of the Fourth Meditation corrects a possible misreading of Chapter I: the Cartesian will that reached the Beginning did so as the auto-affection of a power, not as a faculty of assent; Schopenhauer's will retrieves the first and discards the second.

It is not only a matter of reversing the classical thesis and, opening the way for psychoanalysis, asserting that we do not will something because we represent it as good and judge it so, but judge it good because we will it, that is, really because we desire it, and that is why we do it. Will in Schopenhauer does mean desire, but not in the usual sense of a subjective velleity, a mere preliminary to action, its inner but still unreal design, waiting for another principle to realize it. In Schopenhauer's will there is never this priority of desire over action, "mais une seule force jamais séparée de soi dont l'action n'est que le déploiement" (but a single force never separated from itself, of which action is only the internal and necessary unfolding, p. 164). Far from being separate from reality, able to precede, arouse or deny it, the will is identical with it, immanent to it, its very essence. The reversal "we judge good what we desire" is Spinoza's (*Ethics* III, 9 scholium) as well as Freud's; Henry's point is that Schopenhauer goes further by refusing the gap between desire and its realization, which the Freudian "wish" presupposes.

All misunderstandings of Schopenhauer's will, for instance assimilating it to Kant's, Hegel's or Schelling's, which are pure concepts of will reducing it to willing or not willing, are removed at once by noting that "Will" in Schopenhauer designates not pure will but something else entirely: "la vie" (life, p. 164). Will means life's will to live, so that all the essential determinations of the central concept, the will-to-live, are explained by life, not by "will". What does the will-to-live mean? Not that a pure will, first in itself, sets out to realize itself, to will something, life, as a reality external to it. Why would a will, starting from its own essence, will something quite other, this strange life with its complex properties that pure will could never explain? In Schopenhauer's will, the principle, the naturing, is not will; it is not will that wills, but life. Living comes first; it is reality, determines action and determines itself to action, to realize itself. What does the will-to-live will? Not willing as such, its exercise, but life. It wills itself "non pas en tant que vouloir mais en tant que vivre" (not as willing but as living, p. 165); it desires nothing but life's self-affirmation, a reiterated positing of its nature and all its determinations. The identification of Will with life is the hermeneutic key of the chapter, and it differs from standard readings that treat Schopenhauer's Will as a cosmic force or a metaphysical striving.

In summary: in the will-to-live, what wills is life, and what it wills is life. Schopenhauer's singularity lies in this: since willing, identical with life, is wholly external to pure will, life can never be reached from pure will. And if life thinks only of itself, posits itself by a movement of its own and not of will, and wills anything but the will, then the concept of "will to will" by which the modern world of technology has been characterized has nothing to do with Schopenhauer. Like Marx later, he rejects the concept of a formal, empty willing from which one can only reach this formal and empty will to will. Starting from life, one is at once in reality; the movement is a real movement, that of life and its indefinite recommencement. "Will to will" is Heidegger's formula for the culmination of metaphysics in technology (*Overcoming Metaphysics*); Henry exempts Schopenhauer from Heidegger's genealogy of Nietzsche and technology, and links him to Marx's "living labour", the subject of Henry's two-volume *Marx* (1976).

Why does life's self-affirmation take the form of reiteration? More precisely, why does life's self-relation, the sole content of the will-to-live, express itself as a willing? If reality is one with itself, wholly present to itself, how could it will itself, claim to close a gap separating it from itself that does not exist? Here a certain conception of life and reality must be considered. Schopenhauer conceives reality as essentially affected by a lack, not even a lack of itself but "un manque en soi" (a lack in itself, p. 165). Life never stops reaching itself, positing itself in being; it is indefinite reiteration, but what it reaches each time, what it posits as its own being, is this lack consubstantial with it. It is reality, but a reality constituted essentially by lack of reality, pursuing reality and eternally missing it. Since there is no reality other than this one, no reality can fill the lack, only repeat it indefinitely. Reality is a "réalité affamée" (hungry reality), an "unquenchable thirst", an image of Hell, which Schopenhauer symbolizes by Ixion's wheel. Because this reality is life's, Schopenhauer calls life a willing, the will-to-live: not an abstract will but one rooted in reality, identical with it as lack of reality. This interpretation also gives him the idea of a frightening time, real and empty at once, real because it is reality's own movement, empty because, determined by lack, it is the indefinite reproduction of lack. Henry describes Schopenhauer's will as desire structured by lack, the model Lacan's "desire" will inherit via Kojève and Hegel; the reading prepares Henry's critique: lack is a description of life seen from representation.

With the interpretation of life as will-to-live and its tragic themes, which made Schopenhauer's reputation in the nineteenth and twentieth centuries, the decisive meaning of the concept of life identified with will is not yet grasped, rather lost. Yet it is implied in the title of the main work, strange at first, with a shocking but only apparent asymmetry. If "the world as representation" sets the theme of appearing, understood as unfolding its essence as representedness, so that its claim is ontological, the opposed formula, "the world as will", has no less scope. Will cannot designate what appears, while the mode of appearing, appearing as such, would be constituted by representation. This must be firmly established. Henry reads the title as naming two modes of appearing, not two aspects of one thing appearing in one mode.

Will and representation face each other as reality and unreality. The unreality of representation belongs to it by principle. What is represented is not unreal in itself; on the contrary, the essence of the represented world is will, reality itself and the only reality, since outside will there is nothing. Insofar as what is to be represented enters representation and stands in it, it becomes unreal, insofar as representation cannot exhibit reality, "pour autant que la réalité n'est pas susceptible de paraître devant soi, de se donner à titre d'ob-jet" (insofar as reality cannot appear before itself or give itself as object, p. 167). Unreality is a mode of givenness, not a property of things: the same will is real or unreal according to how it appears.

What has been said of the unreality of representation holds also of the reality of will. Will does not constitute reality in itself, as will-to-live: proof is that while it is represented it floats before us as appearance and illusion, the veil of Maya. Will is reality only on condition of a mode of revelation that reveals it in itself, in its reality, so that this mode of revelation, as the mode constitutive of reality in general and identical with it, alone can reveal will as it is. "Will" first has the radical ontological meaning of circumscribing a mode of revelation in which reality can be revealed in itself, by which it is in fact constituted. "C'est l'apparaître sui generis de la volonté qui fait d'elle ... la réalité, telle est l'intuition abyssale de Schopenhauer" (it is the will's sui generis appearing that makes it, and alone can make it, reality: such is Schopenhauer's abyssal intuition, p. 167). Henry's crucial interpretive step: "will" is first a name for a mode of manifestation, auto-affection, and only secondarily a name for a striving content.

The concept of life therefore divides. To the first determination, still naive and "ontic", life as residing in the will-to-live and so as desire, endless desire, is added the essential, ontological determination: life now designates the mode of self-givenness of this willing, in which it experiences itself immediately, making it, in this self-experience, "non un simple vouloir-vivre mais un vouloir vivant" (not a mere will-to-live but a living will, p. 167). The rivalry between these two concepts of life, never clarified or even grasped, undermines Schopenhauer's philosophy from within and leads to its ruin; yet it gives it its unusual depth and strange power to fascinate us still. The distinction between "will-to-live" and "living will" is the chapter's interpretive centre; Henry will use it to explain both Schopenhauer's achievement and Freud's inheritance: Freud keeps the ontic concept (drive as endless desire) and loses the ontological one.

It is thus the dichotomy of appearing, its double process of realization and phenomenalization, in the immediacy of interiority and in representation, that doubles reality, given now in itself as it is, as will, now as an appearance which, unable to produce reality in itself, is a phantom: "the world as will and as representation". Essential texts show that will, before being will, is the mode of access to it, a mode of manifestation fundamentally different from and opposed to representability: "my will, insofar as I am conscious of it in a wholly different way [from intuitive representation], one that bears comparison with no other". The identification of will with a pure mode of appearing is explicit when Schopenhauer speaks of "another, absolutely different way [of being known], designated by the word will". A passage gives a quick but decisive elucidation, recognizing immediacy and rejecting the form of representation as opposition of subject and object, knower and known: "the concept of will is the only one ... that does not have its origin in the phenomenon, in a mere intuitive representation, but comes from the very depth of the individual's immediate consciousness, in which he recognizes himself directly in his essence, without any form, even that of subject and object, since here knower and known coincide" (§ 22). The § 22 text, where knower and known coincide, is the closest Schopenhauerian analogue to Henry's definition of the Self as identity of affecting and affected (Chapter III).

The connection between will and the original appearing in which alone it constitutes reality is revealed in the body, and this is Schopenhauer's second thesis: there is a reality in itself whose mode of manifestation is the body. It is explicit: "the thing in itself, insofar as it manifests itself to man as his own body, is known immediately". If the body is only the appearing of willing, it must itself divide according to the two modes of appearing, and one of Schopenhauer's most original claims, in the German context of the time, is this doubling of the body by the duplicity of its mode of manifestation: "this body is given in two quite different ways: on the one hand as representation in phenomenal knowledge ... and on the other hand, at the same time, as what is immediately known to everyone and designated by the word will" (§ 18). What matters here, besides the repeated link of will with an immediate mode of manifestation, is the "at the same time". There are not two realities of the body, two bodies, except in appearing. In appearing, as will and representation, the one body in itself (my body) takes on a double aspect: one that deserves the name, "ce pan d'extériorité" (this side of exteriority) by which my objective body resembles other bodies, and the "second side", which is no side at all, "qui ne présente aucune face à aucun regard, qui n'a plus de visage" (which presents no face to any gaze, has no countenance, p. 169), given only in itself, where, coinciding with the force that runs through me, I am one with it. The "at the same time" prevents a dualism of substances: there is one body with two modes of givenness. This is the thesis of Henry's *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps*, where it was credited to Maine de Biran; here it is credited to Schopenhauer.

One must not miss Schopenhauer's decisive insight into this radically immanent and absolute body, which, as thing in itself, he calls Will, often reserving "body" for its objective aspect: "every real act of our will is at once and inevitably a movement of our body; we cannot really will an act without at once perceiving that it appears as a movement of the body". The objective bodily change can in no way be considered the product or effect of willing; any problematic of the soul's action on the body is implicitly but definitively excluded. Every determination of the radically immanent force that constitutes our being is also given to us, at the moment it is accomplished and we inwardly are this accomplishment, as an objective displacement in space: "the act of will and the action of the body are not two different objective states connected by causality ... they are one and the same fact, given in two different ways: immediately, and as sensible representation. The action of the body is nothing but the act of will objectified, that is, seen in representation" (§ 18). The rejection of psychophysical causality matters for Freud: Freud's "drive" as a "frontier concept between the psychical and the somatic" presupposes the very causal gap that Schopenhauer abolishes.

That the lived body is by no means reduced for Schopenhauer to its appearance in "representative intuition", to what tradition regards as "the body" in its supposed opposition to "the soul", that it contains in its real being, as constitutive of it, the immediate experience of the will-to-live with which it is identical, Schopenhauer states clearly: "my body is the only object of which I know not only one side, that of representation, but also the second, that of will". Asked how the representation of the body differs from all others, the answer leaves no ambiguity: "this difference consists in the body's being knowable in another, absolutely different way, designated by the word will" (§ 18). The phrase "the only object" retains, Henry notes implicitly, an objectivist frame: the body is "an object" with a second side. The coming units show where this frame leads Schopenhauer astray.

That the lived body is not reduced to its representation like any other object but "is also will" is the decisive fact that will give us "la clé de l'univers" (the key to the universe, p. 171). The "double knowledge" of our body is therefore not merely a property of this body, though in one sense it is: only in and through our body do we experience the will-to-live immediately at the core of our being and identify with it. This situation gives rise to what Schopenhauer calls theoretical egoism, the assertion that there is only one will in the world, mine, one reality, everything else being the ghostly appearance of representation, the precarious condition of whatever is only object for me. But this purely theoretical and theoretically irrefutable assertion soon comes up against the analogy I discover between what I experience at the core of my being and the movements and forces running through nature. Henry now reaches the turning point of Schopenhauer's system, the extension of will to nature by analogy, which he will criticize as a relapse into representation.

The same willing manifests itself in me and in natural forces, exactly as the same willing reveals itself immediately in my immanent body and is represented in my objective body. And as in my body the same inwardly lived willing is translated "at the same time" into displacements in space, so I recognize at work as the principle of all movements around me in nature, not their cause, which is only apparent or "occasional", as Schopenhauer would say, but "le même pouvoir obstiné qui œuvre en moi et me jette chaque jour à mes désirs et à mes besoins" (the same obstinate power that works in me and throws me each day toward my desires and needs, p. 171). The analogy from the lived body to nature is Schopenhauer's step in § 19; Henry notes the occasionalist overtone of his treatment of causes, linking it to Malebranche.

"Mon corps est pour moi ... ce que la stèle de Rosette fut pour le déchiffrement des hiéroglyphes" (my body is for me, in my knowledge of the world, what the Rosetta Stone was for deciphering hieroglyphs, p. 171). It is a stone on which two texts are carved, one perfectly intelligible and known by heart, the other obscure, in strange characters, whose meaning will suddenly appear. The meaning of these feet and hands, nails and teeth, this voracious mouth, this sex and this eye, is what I have always known, what I am, the will-to-live that bursts through me and to which I abandon myself. As the first text allows me to read the second, my body unveils the secret of the whole book of the world: the movements of my hands, feet, fingers, gaze, nails and teeth resemble those I see in animals, the contractions of peduncles, tentacles, antennae and claws, all those mouths and sexes through which the same obstinate force surges, the same willing that wills what it apparently never obtains. Even in the mineral world, the structure of things, the stratification of rocks, magnetic fields, crystal configurations betray the same cohesive force that holds social groups and whole societies together. The Rosetta image is Henry's own and well chosen: a bilingual inscription in which one script is known from within. The bodily catalogue (mouth, sex, eye) anticipates Freud's erogenous zones, which Henry will read as organs of the will.

The veil thus lifts at once from all the hieroglyphs of the universe: they are only the phenomena and diverse representations of one will-to-live. But this will, the reality of all things, the thing in itself, reveals itself only in me, in my original body, whose immanent appearing is the immediate appearing of this willing. Schopenhauer's problem was to understand how the world of representation to which our experience seems reduced can be experienced by us as mere appearance, why we seek its "meaning", a "passage to what it may be outside representation" (§ 17). The answer: we have a body, which is in itself such a passage, the immediate experience of what goes on behind appearance and of which appearance is only appearance, the will. Henry restates his earlier point: the derealization of representation is known by contrast with the body's immanent self-experience, not inferred.

The whole Schopenhauerian construction rests on this fundamental thesis of the bodily experience of willing. Since we know for certain what will is ("we know and understand what will is better than anything else") and know, living it in our body, that it is endless willing; since the whole world is only the image of this hungry will, which we see entering into conflict with itself a million times and devouring itself in a monstrous universal struggle, the project of this philosophy takes shape: to put an end to this absurd willing, not by its self-suppression, which would still be a manifestation and affirmation of it, as suicide shows, but by its extinction, the ultimate and only way out, that the will no longer will. This is achieved in the saving experiences given to man: art, the ethics of pity and religion. Henry summarizes Books III and IV (art, pity, asceticism) only to show that the entire edifice depends on the phenomenological status of the will.

But if knowledge of the world, and hence ethics, rests on inner experience of reality as adequate experience of the thing in itself, Schopenhauer was unable to ground this first assertion, and the whole edifice wavers. What is at issue is the phenomenological status of the will, the mode of appearing from which it receives its reality and with which, ultimately, it coincides. The return to this question in chapter XVIII of the Supplements to Book II marks the collapse of the crucial thesis of the identity of appearing and willing and its absorption into the most classical conceptions. At first the primacy of self-knowledge is categorically reaffirmed on the ground of immediacy: "everyone knows himself immediately and has only mediate knowledge of everything else". Because of this immediacy self-knowledge is the principle for understanding all nature, formulating the central intuition of all essential metaphysics: far from being determined from the world, from scientific knowledge projected onto it as one object among others, the self is not only the starting point but the condition of possibility of everything else. "It is from ourselves that we must seek to understand nature, and not conversely seek knowledge of ourselves in that of nature." This immediate self-knowledge is that of the will, so "our will provides the sole opportunity ... of understanding from within a process that presents itself objectively; it provides something immediately known and not, like everything else, given only in representation". Because mediation is that of an appearing consisting in representation, immediacy means its radical exclusion, so that the will as thing in itself must reveal itself wholly independently of representation, from itself, as constituting by itself an original mode of revelation: "the thing in itself can come into consciousness only quite immediately, namely by becoming conscious of itself" (*es selbst sich seiner bewußt wird*). Henry quotes the German to mark the reflexive "sich seiner": the formula of auto-affection in Schopenhauer's own words, just before he abandons it.

This ultimate presupposition is precisely what Schopenhauer can neither hold nor ground, and the crucial theses are progressively abandoned. We learn that "this inner perception of our own will" cannot coincide with "complete and adequate knowledge of the thing in itself", because it is not "quite immediate". This means two things, both ruinous. First, metaphysical mediations, "intermediaries": the will creates a body and an intellect, establishing a double relation to the outer world and to itself, a reflective consciousness that is the counterpart of consciousness of the world. Here a new use of "objectification" takes effect, losing its strict ontological sense identified with the pure appearing of representation and designating an ontic process, creation in the strict sense.
