Second, and worse, regaining its ontological sense, the mediation the will must undergo is representation itself, because representation again becomes, as in Kant, the only conceivable mode of manifestation. Everything decisive about eliminating representation and its inability to exhibit reality is lost. The will enters experience only in and through representation, whose form is the opposition of subject and object, again the condition of all experience and of the self. A radical separation is instituted between knowledge and known, "entre la connaissance qui n'est pas connue et le connu qui est dépourvu du pouvoir de connaître" (between knowledge that is not known and the known that lacks the power of knowing, p. 175): representation absorbs the essence of appearing, while the will, deprived of it, is only "un contenu mort et en lui-même aveugle" (a dead and blind content). The phrase repeats exactly what Chapter IV said of Kant's sensation: Schopenhauer falls back into the Kantian situation.

Lacking sufficient elaboration, the capital assertion that the concept of will comes from the individual's immediate consciousness, "without any form, even that of subject and object, since here knower and known coincide", is contradicted and denied when it is said that "this knowledge of the thing in itself is not completely adequate". It is tied to the form of representation, it is perception, and so divided into subject and object: "for even in self-consciousness the I is not absolutely simple but consists of a knowing part, the intellect, and a known part, the will; the former is not known, the latter does not know". The metaphysics of opposition prevails again: "in the knowledge of our inner being too there is a difference between the being in itself of the object of this knowledge and the perception of this being in the knowing subject" (p. 176). What distinction then remains between inner experience of willing, as apprehension of an enigmatic object by a power different from it, and experience of the world in general, of natural forces that are enigmatic realities illuminated from outside by a knowing power that cannot penetrate them? Henry shows that the knower–known split within self-consciousness abolishes the privilege of the body: inner experience becomes one more outer experience.

Schopenhauer could not avoid this question of the difference between inner and outer experience: outer experience has the three constituents of the *principium individuationis*, space, time and causality; inner experience has only "the form of time and the relation of knower to known". This time, the form of inner sense as in Kant, is identical with Kant's time of representation: the deepest structure and ultimate condition of representation, the structure of opposition found in the "relation of knower to known". The opposition of inner and outer experience is no longer decisive; far from challenging representedness, it lies within it. It is no use saying that "despite all these imperfections the perception in which we grasp the impulses and acts of our own will is far more immediate than any other", that "it is the point where the thing in itself enters the phenomenon most immediately and is most closely illuminated by the knowing subject": this phenomenon, its light, is no longer that of willing itself but fundamentally different. And there is no other appearing than that of the subject in its difference from what it knows, of Difference: "my intellect, alone capable of knowing, is always distinct from the I as will" (p. 177). Of this sole mode of phenomenalization the will is forever deprived; it has become again the unknown and unknowable thing in itself. Asked what the will is apart from its representation, "this question will never be answered". "Ainsi Schopenhauer n'a-t-il pu mettre en cause l'essence de la représentéité que pour sombrer dans une philosophie de la nuit" (Schopenhauer could challenge the essence of representedness only to sink into a philosophy of night). Far from being removed, Western thought's overwhelming dilemma returns more forcefully: "ou la représentation ou l'inconscient" (either representation or the unconscious). The prologue's formula is now demonstrated: the unconscious is born when a philosophy that has found life can manifest it only through representation, and so declares it dark.

With this inability to recognize the specific appearing of willing, the whole philosophy of the body collapses, losing the extraordinary originality it owed to that of "un autre penseur de génie, Maine de Biran" (another thinker of genius, Maine de Biran, p. 177), who had developed it shortly before with the appropriate means. The original body is willing itself, in its immanent revelation, and that is why it can really be our body: the force we can exercise because we can reach it, and can reach because we coincide with it within a power of revelation that does not set it before us, outside us, as what we would be forever separated from. "Le corps — mon corps — n'est possible qu'irreprésenté et irreprésentable" (the body, my body, is possible only unrepresented and unrepresentable). If, as Schopenhauer recognized for a moment ("this identity of body and will"), body and will are identical, it is not only because they designate one force, but because force is possible only as identical with itself within a self-experiencing that is a self-possessing, the force's being one with itself as able to be what it is and do what it does. The self-revelation of will reveals it as it is and also makes it possible as will, force and body. "Corps, force, volonté sont notre demeure" (body, force, will are our dwelling, p. 178): their remaining in themselves is each time our remaining in ourselves, our Self; as long as they remain in themselves, they remain in us, and we can deploy them. This is the doctrine of *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps*: a power is exercisable only because it is given to itself; the "I can" is grounded in auto-affection. The dwelling image answers Heidegger's "house of being".

This ontological condition of the possibility of will and body is destroyed as soon as their specific mode of revelation, self-revealing, self-experiencing, self-possessing, the essence of life, is passed over and replaced by representation. This is exactly what happens in Schopenhauer when inner experience of the will is only its temporal representation. The unknown and unknowable essence of will is replaced by the succession of voluntary acts in time, appearing as objective bodily movements. But what lets me feel these acts as acts of will, distinguish them from natural processes or modifications of inner sense? They are only representations, and Schopenhauer had shown that the meaning of representations is not in them, that they can be lived as representations of the will only if the will is given to us otherwise. When the conditions of this original experience no longer exist, when willing and appearing are dissociated, the possibility of going behind the appearance of representation to grasp what it is the appearance of collapses. The argument recalls Chapter IV's on Kant's inner sense: a represented act bears no intrinsic mark of being mine or willed.

So it is with the particular representation I call my body. Having identified will and body, § 18 tended to separate them, reserving "body" for the representative intuition of bodily movement, the objective body: "the whole body is nothing but the objectified will, will become perceptible". But the objective body can be given as objectified will only on condition of an experience of the will that first reveals it in itself; only if we know what will is can the body appear as its objectification. "The will is the a priori knowledge of the body; the body is the a posteriori knowledge of the will." A posteriori means: insofar as we experience the will in ourselves, and with it our body as the immanent body we are, we can know that the objective body is only the representation of the first, of its force, drive, willing. But now this a priori knowledge of the will's being in itself is denied; it manifests itself only through its temporal phenomenal forms, those of our representative body and its acts. The representative body becomes the only possible manifestation of the will and the condition of its knowledge. Schopenhauer's thought closes in a circle: a priori condition of knowledge of the representative body, the will now finds in it the condition of its own knowledge. "Finally, the knowledge I have of my will, though immediate, is inseparable from the knowledge I have of my body. I know my will not as a whole, not in its unity, nor perfectly in its essence; it appears to me only in its individual acts, hence in time, which is the phenomenal form of my body as of every object; therefore my body is the condition of the knowledge of my will" (§ 18). The progressive dissociation of will and body, with the reduction of the immanent body to the body of representation, is not accidental but "la conséquence inéluctable et ruineuse" (the inescapable and ruinous consequence, p. 179) of the prior dissociation of willing and appearing and, ultimately, of the misunderstanding of appearing's original essence. Henry identifies a circle parallel to Kant's in Chapter IV: there the I existed only as the impression it was supposed to produce; here the will is known only through the body it is supposed to make known.

The clouding of the will's primitive mode of revelation corrupts the whole doctrine. The will whose inner knowledge was to reveal the world's enigma becomes the object of a negative discourse. Its essence is reconstructed antithetically from the phenomenal world by postulations that repeat all of Kant's uncertainties. Since the phenomenal world is structured by the *principium individuationis* and the a priori forms of sensibility everywhere promote plurality, the will, escaping this principle, must be one in itself, a universal force; hence the inference that all phenomena, all forces of nature in the mineral, vegetable and animal realms, are manifestations of one willing, that the force I feel in me, in inner sense, is identical with all the forces that tear the universe. The will's unity is thus a negative inference from the plurality of representation, not a phenomenological given.

Since the phenomenal world obeys the inflexible law of the principle of sufficient reason and everything here has a ground, the will is groundless, absolutely free in this sense; yet everything that flows from it, our acts and first our character, flows necessarily, not only for phenomenal knowledge, which is out of play, but in itself, insofar as this irrational will has determined itself timelessly. Since the phenomenal world is that of knowledge and the only mode of any knowledge, the will that escapes it is characterized by *Erkenntnislosigkeit*: unknown, unknowable and unknowing; its mode of being is blindness. "Blind will", which in Chapter I named the will's non-ecstatic self-experience, is here degraded into ignorance: the same word, two meanings.

*Vielheitlosigkeit*, *Grundlosigkeit*, *Erkenntnislosigkeit*, and also *Ziellosigkeit*, aimlessness: these negative determinations, whose theoretical meaning is to forbid applying the discourse of the world to the will, surreptitiously acquire a positive meaning on which Schopenhauer's pessimism is built, wholly illegitimately. Lacking reason, the will is only a blind force whose unleashing fills the universe with absurdities. Lacking an aim, it is endless striving, starting again indefinitely, as in natural forces such as gravity, "continual striving joined to the impossibility of reaching the goal", in the plant growing from bud to fruit, which is only the origin of a new bud, in animal procreation where the individual that accomplished it fades away while another begins the cycle again. In all manifestations of the will the aim is illusory, the starting point of a new process, to infinity. One in itself and only apparently plural, the will whose processes everywhere cross and collide comes into conflict with itself, pushing the absurdity of this world to the extreme. Purely negative determinations, resulting from the will's lack of phenomenological status, are thus changed into the pseudo-positive determinations that give Schopenhauer's universe its image. "C'est par là que Schopenhauer annonce véritablement Freud, en ouvrant une dimension que son caractère irréductible à toute connaissance propose comme réceptacle aux constructions chimériques de la spéculation" (It is here that Schopenhauer truly announces Freud, opening a dimension which, being irreducible to all knowledge, offers itself as a receptacle for the chimerical constructions of speculation, p. 181). The genealogical thesis is stated: what Freud inherits from Schopenhauer is not the discovery of life but its negative, unknowable form, which can be filled with any speculative construction (the economics of drives, the death drive, the repetition compulsion). Schopenhauer's "aimless repetition" is the ancestor of Freud's *Wiederholungszwang*.

The precariousness of these constructions comes to light over a decisive question that occasions the system's major contradiction: individuality. It is a classical thesis, taken up by Kant, that a being's individuality is sufficiently established by its place in space and time, since two realities at different places or moments are thereby distinct. Husserl will hold the same: if two exactly similar sounds are emitted, two "objects" identical in quiddity, their respective positions in phenomenological time give the principle of their real distinction. There is no need, as Descartes and Leibniz held, to appeal to ideas; diversity in concept (*Verschiedenheit*) is no longer the condition of real difference (*Vielheit*), which finds its sufficient condition in intuition. "For it is through space and time that what is one and similar in essence and concept appears to us as different, as many, whether in coexistence or succession" (§ 23). Space and time form the *principium individuationis*, a term inherited from scholasticism, and since they co-define representation, representation is the site of the difference and multiplicity of all beings. Because the will's noumenal essence is constructed negatively from the structures of representation, it is conceived as a single essence of which all voluntary acts and the forces, movements and forms proliferating in nature are only varied appearances, a diffraction through the prism of representation. Since representation opens a dimension of unreality, the plurality and individuality inherent in it are themselves only "appearance". Henry's mention of Husserl's two tones (*Zeitbewusstsein*, Hua X) shows that Husserl too individuates by temporal position, and so belongs to the same lineage.

This devaluation of individuality, and so of the individual, is visible in its relation to the species, identical with the Idea and, like it, an immediate objectification and true realization of the will, an eternal and timeless form, while the perishable individuals composing it are only its illusory refraction, indefinitely repeated in space and time. With this valuing of the species and devaluing of the individual, Schopenhauer again opens the way for Freud: "the species alone ... interests nature" (p. 182). The note cites Schopenhauer on the metaphysics of sexual love; Freud's view of the individual as the mortal vehicle of the germ-plasm (*Beyond the Pleasure Principle*) is the descendant.

But Schopenhauer also asserts the opposite: that individuality belongs to the will in itself and determines it originally. This original connection is seen first in the body as the inner experience of the will. The body appears radically individualized, so much so that only by binding itself to a body does the subject of knowledge, in itself a pure impersonal and undifferentiated gaze, become an individual: "the subject of knowledge, through its identity with the body, becomes an individual" (§ 18). Henry rereads the essential text in which the will is the individual not as reflected in intuitive representation but precisely as escaping it: "the concept of will is the only one ... that does not have its origin in the phenomenon, in a mere intuitive representation, but comes from the very depth of the immediate consciousness of the individual", in which "he recognizes himself directly in his essence, without any form, even that of subject and object" (p. 183). It is no longer possible to say that it is "through space and time that what is one and similar in essence appears as many", and the thesis grounding the *principium individuationis* in representation invincibly conflicts with that rooting individuality in the will itself. Henry's own thesis from Chapter IV, that auto-affection is always singular, is found in Schopenhauer's second line of thought, in conflict with the first.

The latter thesis, far from accidental, governs whole parts of the system, notably the a priority of character and the theory of style, so important in aesthetics (Schopenhauer's aesthetics had an enormous influence on nineteenth- and twentieth-century aesthetic doctrines and on art itself). The will's timelessness, its heterogeneity to the temporal structure of representation, grounds the intelligible character by which an individual always acts the same way, repeating the same acts in similar circumstances throughout his history. The multiplicity of these acts, scattered through the temporal form of representation, cannot constitute their individuality, their expressing one character; it presupposes it as an individuality belonging to the intelligible character and ultimately to the will, of which character is the immediate expression. Likewise the theory of style, the unity of an individual's manifestations and creations as springing from his body, refers to the individuality of that body and so of the will, "une individualité qui précède le temps au lieu d'en résulter" (an individuality that precedes time instead of resulting from it, p. 184). Intelligible character (from Kant's Third Antinomy) and style both require pre-temporal individuality; Henry's choice of examples anticipates his own aesthetics in *Voir l'invisible* (1988).

The contradiction between grounding individuality in representation and in the will is so strong that they turn into each other, as in the theory of art. Knowledge is no longer the principle of individuation; rising to the contemplation of Ideas and completed there, it now appears as what delivers us from individuality, which is shown to depend on the body and through it on the will. Schopenhauer thus interprets aesthetic experience and the disinterestedness Kant ascribed to it: as the coming of a subject freed from the individual's desires and passions, from the will, open to pure perception of the thing as it is in itself, independently of the chain of causes and reasons that explain its existence only for the illusory gaze of science, and independently of its situation relative to us, our motives and interests. This existence in itself of the thing, discovered in the pure experience of its beauty ("not the experience of this flower but of the flower in itself"), is "l'objectivation immédiate de la vie, c'est son Idée" (the immediate objectification of life, its Idea, p. 184), indifferent to time, to world events and to our cares. Aesthetic contemplation is Schopenhauer's first form of deliverance (Book III); Henry presents it as the point where representation, previously the principle of individuation, becomes the principle of de-individuation.

This existence in itself of the thing as the immediate objectification of life is that of the will, but of the will offered to an impersonal subject, a subject that no longer wills. The exchange of roles, or rather of metaphysical determinations, between representation and will is complete: originally inherent in representation and identical with it, individuality is now that of the will; one frees oneself from it by freeing oneself from the will, entrusting oneself to the pure light of impersonal knowledge, whose benefits Schopenhauer suddenly praises. Having described aesthetic experience as that in which "we turn the whole power of our mind to perception, sink ourselves completely in it, forget our individuality, our will, and continue to exist only as pure subject, as clear mirror of the object" (§ 34), having stressed "the suppression of individuality in the knowing subject", Schopenhauer concludes: "we found in aesthetic contemplation two inseparable elements: knowledge of the object not as a particular thing but as Platonic Idea, the permanent form of a whole species of things; and the self-consciousness of the knower, not as individual but as pure, will-less subject of knowledge" (§ 38). The "clear mirror" is the Gassendian mirror of Chapter I promoted to salvation: Schopenhauer's deliverance is the triumph of the *videre* over the *videor*.

The incoherence is somewhat lessened by noting the modification, or rather doubling, of Schopenhauer's concept of representation in aesthetic experience: alongside the spatio-temporal "phenomenon" stands the Idea, an archetype indifferent to the individuals that reproduce it monotonously. The Idea remains subject to "the most general form of representation", which "consists in being an object for a subject", but it escapes the "secondary forms" that alone make up the principle of sufficient reason, identical with that of individuation. "It is the secondary forms that draw from the Idea the multiplicity of individuals", as it is they that make the subject's knowledge individual knowledge. Individuality of phenomena and of the knowing subject thus lies exclusively in the *principium individuationis*, and one can conceive that, foreign to space, time and causality and having "no particular form of knowledge except the general form of representation" (§ 32), the will's immediate objectification in the Idea knows no individuality, whether on the side of the subject, become a pure impersonal mirror, or of the object, the Idea. It is significant that the saving experience of beauty abolishes both the multiplicity of individuals prey to the will's absurd struggles and this Will itself. But if, leaving the blissful world of the Ideas, one returns to the will, why is the will itself marked with individuality like phenomena? How could individuality reach it in its noumenal reality if it is explained only by the forms of sensible intuition and inheres in them (p. 186)? Henry grants the partial resolution (two levels of representation) and then shows that it leaves the will's own individuality unexplained.

This may be the moment to recall the tradition's indeterminacy regarding the ego and the essence of ipseity. The uncertainties of Schopenhauer's thought are one consequence among many of "le silence singulier de Descartes" (Descartes' singular silence) on this essential problem, at the very moment he placed it, unnoticed, at the centre of the development of culture (Chapter III). The question of individuality, recognized in its radical meaning, does not differ from that of ipseity; it aims at the same essence. Hence the gravest doubts about the possibility of accounting for anything like an individual from the forms of representation and their ultimate ground, the ecstatic structure of a world. If the individual draws its essence from the Self of ipseity and is possible only through it, and if ipseity lies in life's original auto-affection, which excludes all ecstasis, then the very claim to find the ground of the individual in representation and its forms "devient immédiatement absurde" (becomes immediately absurd, p. 186). Individuality and ipseity are identified: what makes something this individual is not its place in a field but its being a self.

Consider again more closely how Husserl accounts for individuality from temporal position, an account that benefits from the extraordinary contribution of nascent phenomenology to the concept of time. The question becomes explicit when the possibility of objective time must be grounded, when one must explain how, in the universal flow of immanent phenomenological data, a fixed, identical, objective order of these data can be instituted. Take hearing a lasting sound (an example that facilitates the reduction, setting aside transcendent apprehensions). The original phase in which this impression arises constantly changes into a phase "just past", while a new phase constantly arises. While the formerly original phase slides into the past and recedes, it remains "the same", with the same impressional content and the same temporal position (it will always precede the phase that follows it and follow the one that precedes it), and it is intended as such, as identical with itself as it sinks into the past. This self-identity of the sound phase, which makes its individuality, is the object of an intending, "une signification que nous lui accolons dans notre représentation" (a meaning we attach to it in our representation, p. 187), though an "immanent" representation bearing on the sensory content apart from the transcendent object, while the phase flows away and sinks into a more and more distant past and, at the limit, into the "unconscious". The reference is to Husserl's *Lectures on Internal Time-Consciousness*, §§ 30–33, on the constitution of temporal position and objective time. Henry's ironic quotation marks around "unconscious" connect retention's fading with Freud's latent memories.

But this representation of the phase as identical with itself, in its irreducible individuality, far from grounding individuality, presupposes it. Only because the phase is in itself identical with itself, ultimately because "elle est en soi un Soi" (it is in itself a Self, p. 187), can it, sliding into retention and still held by it, be represented as what it is, identical throughout its flow. The problem of its individuality is posed and must be solved at the level of the original impression, the *Urimpression*. The original impression (of this sound) is "absolutely unmodified", meaning it has not yet undergone the retentional modification, the first ecstatic dehiscence of sliding into the "just past". As such it is wholly present, "ou pour mieux dire vivante" (or rather living, p. 188), and it is in this original situation that it receives the seal of individuality, marking it forever as it later slides into the past. The *Urimpression* is the site Henry will make central to *Phénoménologie matérielle* (1990), "Hylétique et phénoménologie matérielle": Husserl saw it and then subordinated it to retention.

What is this original seal of individuality? Not the content of the impression but the fact that it is experienced now, absolutely, "elle et aucune autre" (it and no other). The now, as the source and definition of an absolute temporal position, absolutely individualizes the impression, while another now will individualize another: "the same sensation now and in another now has a phenomenological difference corresponding to absolute temporal position; it is the original source of the individuality of the 'this', and thereby of absolute temporal position"; and "the tone-point in its absolute individuality is held fast in its matter and its temporal position, the latter alone constituting individuality" (Husserl, *Lectures*, § 31). Henry quotes Husserl's thesis that position "alone" constitutes individuality in order to contest it.

But why does the now individualize? Why is the now, taken as a pure temporal position abstracted from the variable content of the impression, nonetheless bound to it? Why is individuation by the now demonstrated precisely with an impression, indeed the original impression, rather than with a stick, an ideology, an equation? Why, if what individualizes is a pure temporal position, is the pure form of the now invoked rather than that of the just-past, the past in general, or the future? Because the now can provide the ground and source of all individuation only insofar as its essence is identically that of ipseity, of life: "le s'impressionner soi-même" (self-impressing, p. 189) in which alone impression is possible as originally living, so that no dehiscence occurs in this self-impressing that constitutes all original presence, neither of the past, even just past, nor of the future, and so that in the radical immanence of this living presence there is no form separate from and opposed to a content, but only the content's presence to itself, its auto-affection. Henry's four questions turn Husserl's formal "now" into a disguised appeal to the living impression; the absolute "now" individuates only because it is the auto-affection of the impression, not a position in a series.

To return to Schopenhauer: the double regime of the concept of will, with respect to its individuality or non-individuality, obeys these prescriptions. As long as the will is considered an ontic reality, finally a fact, without asking about its ultimate possibility as force, thought slides easily from "the" will to a single universal principle of things whose plurality is credited to the intuitive structure of space and time, to ecstatic bursting, while the external designation of each element arising from this indefinitely repeated self-exteriorization of reality cheaply makes it an individual entity, if not quite an individual. Only when willing is linked to the original appearing that determines it as will-to-live and a modality of life does its individuality, unnoticed or even denied till then, surface "at the very depth of the individual's immediate consciousness", as in the many texts that link willing and individual and oppose both to representation, which, as aesthetic contemplation, as the pure essence of light, "the most delightful of things" (§ 38), is suddenly freed from the torments of individuality. The two concepts of life (ontic will-to-live, living will) now map onto the two theories of individuality.

This avowed recognition of the will's individuality is essential; it alone makes possible the doctrine's great analyses, of egoism, cruelty and pity, and gives them their seriousness. The theory of egoism repeats Schopenhauer's contradictions and pushes them to their limit. It rests at once on the *principium individuationis*, making individuality an appearance resulting from representation, and on the thesis linking the individual to the will in itself and identifying them. Because space and time are the forms of representation, the will, one in itself, must, insofar as it manifests itself in representation, "manifest itself through a plurality of individuals" (§ 61), so that this plurality does not touch the will's being in itself, whose essence remains wholly present and indivisible. An operative asymmetry arises between this will, which contains the totality of its being and wills everything, this hungry will knowing only its desire and abandoning itself to it, and the world of appearances floating before it, things that exist only for it, ready to be delivered to it and crushed, mere means for its preservation and growth (p. 190). The analysis prepares Freud's narcissism and aggressivity: the self that treats the world as means.

What this description lacks to be a description of egoism is the individual, a real individual, not one arising from dissemination in space or time, which is only the individuation of a part external to all others, but the individual that coincides with the will's being in itself and its infinite willing. If individuality appears only on the side of representation, it is only that of things, and illusory: destroying it, the will destroys only an appearance, and the struggle lacks seriousness. But § 61 speaks of the struggle of individuals as quite real, a *bellum omnium contra omnes*. In it, not only (apparent) individuals are flouted, mutilated, used and annihilated; "c'est un individu aussi qui bafoue, mutile, utilise et tue" (it is also an individual who flouts, mutilates, uses and kills, p. 191). The will that wills everything, ravages the earth and treats things and others as mere means is each time the will of an individual. Only in this coincidence of the will with an individual, of each individual with the whole essence of the will, is egoism possible. Henry's argument: cruelty requires a real agent; an illusory individual cannot be cruel.

§ 61 clearly shows this shift that gradually moves individuality from representation to will, making the will not an impersonal principle but each time the will of an individual, throwing him, with this infinite force in him, against all others in whom the same force dwells. Having declared that the phenomenal plurality of individuals does not concern the will and leaves its infinite, indivisible essence intact, and reaffirmed that it is only in this essence that the will resides, the text suddenly designates the individual as the place where the will discovers its being in itself, a discovery that engages him in the unchecked process of his destructive desire: "as for this essence in itself, reality par excellence, it [the will] finds it within itself, and only there. That is why each wants everything for himself, wants to possess everything, at least to rule everything, and would like to destroy whatever opposes him" (§ 61, Henry's emphasis). The will finds its reality "within itself" only as an individual; the text admits ipseity at the heart of the noumenal.

In man the will is joined to intelligence, a gaze that holds everything that is not itself, individuals included, as its mere object, existing only through it and disappearing if it disappears. But only insofar as this gaze is for itself the only gaze, by its immediacy, its ipseity, and only insofar as the will is that of an individual identified with the whole will, can Schopenhauer write: "every individual, as intelligence, really is and appears to himself as the whole will-to-live". Here the gaze is the will's own; the "subject" of representation and the "subject" of will coincide in the individual, who is nothing but their original self-appearing in themselves, their ipseity and the essence of life in them. "Thus each appears to himself as the whole will and the whole representing intelligence, while all other beings are given to him first only as representations, his representations; hence his own being and its preservation must come before everything in the world." This explains the singularity of egoism: "each individual, though tiny, lost and annihilated in a boundless world, nonetheless takes himself as the centre of the whole, valuing his existence and well-being above everything else" (§ 61). All this on one condition: "que l'individualité soit l'épreuve immanente de la réalité au lieu de sa représentation illusoire" (that individuality be the immanent experience of reality instead of its illusory representation, p. 192). Henry reads theoretical egoism, introduced earlier as irrefutable but absurd, as the truth of the individual's ipseity distorted into a claim about the world.

Many other difficulties make Schopenhauer's thought uncertain or contradictory. The fluctuation of the essential concept of objectification has been noted: most often it means the appearing of representation, presenting in exteriority what already exists in itself; sometimes a genuine creation meant to give effectiveness to what would otherwise lack it. The ambiguity appears in the will's double objectification according to the complete or incomplete form of representation. According to the complete form, whose essence is the *principium individuationis*, objectification yields the efflorescent multiplicity of individual manifestations, which, given representation's derealizing function, are so many illusions akin to dreams. According to the incomplete, "most general" form, the subject–object opposition without the *principium individuationis*, objectification brings forth the universe of Ideas, matrices of creatures and things, which, drawing the moulds in which the will-to-live is eternally accomplished, seem to form the infinitely real structure of the world: "the will, in its primary act of objectification, determines the various Ideas in which it objectifies itself, that is, the various forms of creatures of every kind" (p. 193). Aesthetic experience opens us to this first structuring of reality, not as illusion but as the will's "most adequate possible objectivity", which, revealing the secret of the universe, prepares us to detach ourselves from it. A note gives Schopenhauer's reason for the Ideas' reality: like the will, they need no ground; "only phenomena can be given a reason; the will needs none, nor does the Idea in which it objectifies itself adequately". According to this division of its concept, which occurs within representation, objectification of the will means now the passage to effective existence, now the arising of appearance, the unfolding of the veil of Maya. The double sense of *objectivation* (manifestation vs. creation) is the Schopenhauerian counterpart of the Cartesian amphibology of Chapter II; Freud's "representative of the drive" will inherit it.

This ambiguity is not chance; it refers to the central gap around which all the others are organized: "l'absence de tout statut phénoménologique positif du concept de volonté, ou plutôt sa négation" (the absence of any positive phenomenological status for the concept of will, or rather its denial, p. 193). On one hand, immediate knowledge is the principle and ground of all mediate knowledge. Inner experience of the will, its original appearing as our immanent body, makes us experience the world of representation as appearance and understand these appearances as the will's manifestations. The other of representation, the wholly other, gives us its key. Schopenhauer is precisely the thinker who, radically challenging the concept of the essence of being as representedness, opens the way to a philosophy of life, which he grasps under the title of will and will-to-live. But what, if not representation, can be the way of access to life, what original mode of revelation will give it to us in itself as it is, so that, more deeply, this mode of revelation will itself constitute the essence of life (p. 194)? The question is the one Henry's own philosophy answers: auto-affection is both the access to life and its essence.

Schopenhauer has no answer. When attempts to grasp the will's being in the depth of the individual's body have failed and the will in itself is delivered to the unconscious, everything one can know of it must be gathered from representation. Having asked the will for the secret of the world of representation, we see that world offer itself instead as the only possible testimony to the will's reality. "The will's only general consciousness of itself is the whole representation, the whole world it perceives". "The will, the will without intelligence (in itself it is nothing else), blind, irresistible desire ... thanks to the represented world offered to it and developing to serve it, comes to know that it wills and what it wills: this very world" (§ 54). So arises what will become the paradox of modern thought: the more representation is criticized and its claim to equal reality contested, the more our age defines itself against it and understands itself as "l'« ère du soupçon »" (the "era of suspicion"), the more the empire of representation extends, to the point of including everything, and the more it appears as the principle of all knowledge and so of all salvation. More than ever, just when it seems questioned, it constitutes the sole essence of manifestation and being. Thus an astonishing reversal of values occurs, "lequel trouve sa conclusion dans le freudisme : la mise en question de la représentation aboutissant à l'établissement de son règne sans partage et proprement à son diktat" (which finds its conclusion in Freudianism: the questioning of representation ending in the establishment of its undivided reign, its diktat). "Era of suspicion" is Nathalie Sarraute's title, and Ricœur's "masters of suspicion" (Marx, Nietzsche, Freud) is in the background. Henry's paradox is the book's thesis about psychoanalysis: it suspects consciousness, yet heals by making conscious, by representation. The cure's "becoming conscious" is the diktat.

Schopenhauer, however, had broken this diktat, not only by contesting representation's capacity to represent reality but by opposing to it, instead of the x of a mysterious entity, what he calls will, force. Here *The World as Will and Representation* opened the way to an entirely new thought. The opposition of force to representation ceases to be naive and precritical as soon as the more ultimate question arises of its essence, of the appearing that makes it possible as force, which is life. Then the thought of representation and of its ecstatic support truly trembles, because with the concept of appearing that of being falters. What Schopenhauer forbids, definitively, not on the level of history (which for him is the same errors indefinitely repeated) but on that of essences and the possibilities they govern, is an insidious attempt to erase the irreducibility of force and the unthinkable field it unfolds by reducing it to representation, by making the movement of force the movement of representation (p. 195). The insidious attempt is named in the next unit: Leibniz as read by Heidegger.

That attempt is Leibniz's, to which Heidegger gives indulgent attention. In principle it is a matter of denouncing once more the reign of representedness by showing how it determines Leibniz's concept of force. More subtly, the originality of force is denied by this explicit integration into representation, which still belongs to the history of being and originates in *physis*, so that the presuppositions of Greek truth reign, through their alteration, over the whole of "Western metaphysics", reducing every form of being to ecstasis. Leibniz is placed from the start in the extension of the *cogito* reduced to "I represent", as its avatar. The new essence of reality lies in representedness, in a *subjectum* whose actuality consists in representing, by which every being is held in being: "the *actualitas* of this *subjectum* [man] has its essence in the *actus* of *cogitare* (*percipere*)" (*Nietzsche* II, p. 196). Once the *actus* is that of *percipere*, all action is reduced to representing. Since this self-effecting is representing, it is not effecting something but effecting itself: "it relates in itself to itself (*auf sich zu*)", since representing is implicitly a self-representing. Hence: "in each time bringing something before itself, effecting performs a disposing-toward-itself (*Zu-stellung*) and so sets before itself, represents in a certain way what is effected. Effecting is in itself a setting-before, a re-presenting (*percipere*)". Henry implies that Heidegger's interpretation of Leibniz's *vis* as representation is a projection of his own reduction of all phenomenality to ecstasis.

With Leibniz two features are added to Cartesian perception to make it more clearly a self-effecting: unification and appetition. Since perception is, for Leibniz, the expression of the many in the one, the monad endowed with perception is in itself the original unifying that "disposes the many toward itself by unifying it from itself, and has in this representing the essence of its 'sistence in itself', its persistence, that is, its reality" (Heidegger). Since this unifying constitutes the monad's *actualitas* and determines it as self-effecting, self-effecting is nothing but the essence of representation, identical with an *actio* that constitutes its actuality and determines it as effective reality. Further, the *multum* that unifying representing disposes is not arbitrary but essentially limited: it is the world represented from the monad's particular point of view. Because each representing represents the universe only from a point of view and in a corresponding concentration, it is inhabited by a specific appetition that expresses, beyond its own world, its relation to the universe. Thus "in representing there operates an appetitive progression going beyond itself" (p. 197), by virtue of which all representing is essentially "transitory". Henry summarizes Heidegger faithfully; the objection follows.

Within each representation, by its finitude, that is, by the finitude of ecstasis, a movement arises by which it continually exceeds itself, an action by which each perception tends to change into a larger, more comprehensive one, a movement "qui n'est pas sans préfigurer le dépassement intentionnel de la conscience husserlienne" (prefiguring the intentional self-transcending of Husserlian consciousness, p. 197) and that seems endless. Thus movement and action, which are something else entirely and draw their possibility from an appearing that ignores and expels ecstasis, are reduced to a character of representation and explained by it. "This 'appetition'", Heidegger says, "is the basic feature of effective action in the sense of representation." And *Monadology* § 15 says: "the action of the internal principle which brings about the change or passage from one perception to another may be called appetition; it is true that appetite cannot always fully attain the whole perception toward which it tends, but it always obtains something of it and attains new perceptions". The internal principle of action is thus only the internal principle of representations, by which they constantly change into one another, "representing appetition". Appetition and perception are not two realities or two separable characters but the single essence of reality as effective reality consisting in the self-effecting of self-representing: "*perceptio* and *appetitus* are not two determinations that merely engender each other, but their unity of essence constitutes the simplicity of what is truly one, and so its unity, its beingness (*Seiendheit*)". The link between Leibniz's appetition (each perception tending toward another, never fully attained) and Husserl's intentional fulfilment matters for Freud: desire as the endless passage from one representation to another is the Leibnizian model, and Lacan's metonymy of desire is its heir.

Will it be said that the movement by which each representation is an appetition (*Anstrebung*), a striving toward more comprehensive unification, is just the movement of representation, without claiming to reduce every movement and force, still less their essence, to it? On the contrary, Leibniz means to define the essence of force in general in the change of representations. *Monadology* § 11 states: "and generally one may say that force is nothing but the principle of change", which Heidegger glosses: "here change does not mean any becoming-other in general, but the transitory essence of appetitive representation" (p. 198). The force–representation identification is Leibniz's own, on Heidegger's reading.

Not only does the appetition of representation define the essence of force; it thereby defines the essence of reality in general as effective reality, the being of every possible being. "Leibniz names the principle of beings as such: *vis*, force. The essence of force is not determined by generalizing after the fact an effective action experienced somewhere, but conversely: the essence of force is determined from the beingness of beings (*Seiendheit des Seienden*)." Henry lets Heidegger's formula show that, in the history of being, force is deduced from beingness, that is, from representedness.

As the being of every possible being, force designates the essence of subjectity, which since Descartes has become the essence of subjectivity; but this force, now the ground of all things, is nothing more than the movement of perception: "each *subjectum* is determined in its *esse* by *vis* (*perceptio-appetitus*)". That force, far from opening a new dimension of being, is reduced as "appetition" to the essence of representation and "son mouvement inoffensif" (its harmless movement, p. 198) is stated no less clearly: "with the universality of the representational essence of reality, appetition, the basic feature of re-presenting". "Harmless" is Henry's sarcasm: a force that is only the passage from one representation to another threatens nothing, whereas the force of life is the "drive" that Freud will try, in vain, to represent.

One then better measures the extraordinary break that occurs, within this history of Greek truth, with a thought that seeks the ground of reality no longer in representation or its ultimate ecstatic support, but in their rejection. Yet by setting representation aside as far as he could and opening the way to a philosophy of life, Schopenhauer inevitably raised many essential and new problems that must now be elucidated more radically (p. 199). The transition announces Chapter VI, "Life and its properties. Repression", where the problems raised by Schopenhauer are worked out and brought to bear on the Freudian concept of repression.

## Chapter VI. Life and Its Properties. Repression

"Life" therefore means two things. On one side, will, the will-to-live, a desire without aim, object or end, its indefinite reiteration. But this concept, whose grand and tragic implications Schopenhauer obstinately deduces, remains marked by a principled naivety: far from thematizing the essence of life, it does not even form its idea. It is not enough to assert that life is this hungry, restless willing, or any other mode of existence a problematic may privilege; one must first show how such willing belongs to life, how it is living. What constitutes the essence of life in it, as in any other determination bearing this original essence, is "son auto-affection indépendamment de la différence « du sujet et de l'objet »" (its auto-affection independently of the difference "of subject and object", p. 201), of Difference as such. To the ontic, precritical concept of life as willing must be opposed the ontological concept by which this willing is first something rather than nothing "dans l'immanence du souffrir primordial qui fait de lui un vouloir vivant" (in the immanence of the primordial suffering that makes it a living will). The chapter opens by restating the two concepts of life from Chapter V. "Primordial suffering" (*souffrir*) is Henry's term for the passivity of auto-affection, not pain as a content.

We begin to understand how, before Nietzsche, Schopenhauer's thought unfolds its hazardous assertions in a back-and-forth between these two concepts, since the unity of life and will constantly comes apart. Life, whose inner possibility is won by rejecting representedness and which, in post-Kantian philosophy, finds its site in the thing in itself, in the will with which it is then identified, does not entrust its essence to the will for long. Life is not a mysterious x but manifestation, what is there first, the effectiveness of a presence. It is what the will wills insofar as, as Kantian thing in itself, it is still unconscious. The will wills life, its manifestation, and, in a metaphysics that rejects representation only to posit the unconscious, it wills the only manifestation it knows, manifestation in the world, representation: "since what the will wills is always life, that is, the pure manifestation of this will in the conditions suitable for its being represented", it follows that "life, the visible world, the phenomenon, is only the mirror of the will", that "life must be the inseparable companion of the will", and that "wherever there is will there will be life, a world" (§ 54). Schopenhauer understood reality as hungry, bearing within it the lack of reality. The meaning of this hunger suddenly becomes clear as the will-to-live reveals its true essence: the lack is "celui de l'apparaître sans lequel aucune réalité ne peut s'exhausser hors du néant" (that of appearing, without which no reality can rise out of nothingness, p. 202), without which it is not even a fiction or a shadow. Schopenhauer's hunger and thirst are those of the German beginnings, of Boehme and Schelling, of everything obscure in itself that rises and strives toward light in order to find being there. Terrifying, then, the tension of what strives toward what will make its own existence possible, if what is at stake in this willing is its own possibility of being, "le simple pouvoir-vivre de ce vouloir lui-même" (the mere being-able-to-live of this willing itself). But in the field opened by Western metaphysics since Greek truth the only recognized mode of manifestation is ecstasis, which after Kant becomes representedness; so the will asks representation for its manifestation, for the possibility of living; it asks life of a medium of light from which life escapes in principle. More terrifying than its tension toward the effectiveness of its own life is the inevitability of its failure, since this will-to-live persists in seeking life where it is not. "Le pessimisme de Schopenhauer s'alimente secrètement à un contresens ontologique" (Schopenhauer's pessimism secretly feeds on an ontological misunderstanding, p. 203), which he neither inaugurates nor completes. The indefinite recommencement of desire, striving toward a reality that cannot come about in its opening to the world, and the joint repetition of failure, motivated by the ultimate structures of being, are what Schopenhauer constantly claims as proof of his theory of the will-to-live, whereas it is at most a philosophical nonsense. This is the decisive reinterpretation of Schopenhauer's (and Freud's) "lack": desire is the will's search for manifestation in the world, doomed because the world cannot give life. The lineage Boehme–Schelling (the "ground" striving toward light) is named as the origin of the model in which darkness strives toward light.

The dissociation of will and life runs through Schopenhauer's text. It shows in that, for the will, willing, self-affirmation, is not limited to the immanent exercise of its essence but consists in its manifestation, conceived only as objectification in the form of a world; and this manifestation, a representation, is also life. "The will affirms itself" means: "when, in its manifestation, in the world and life, it sees its own essence represented to itself in full clarity" (§ 54). Life is thus coined in a world of appearances whose proliferation and renewal are the sign of this relentless will in search of an existence, of the manifestation it lacks by itself. "The will-to-live strives violently toward existence and takes innumerable forms." Everywhere the will is described as an attachment to life that has its ground not in its object, life, but in the subject that feels it, in the willing deprived in itself of reality. This attachment of the will to life is identically its adherence to the body. The genital organs are the will's focus; sexual needs and all others express the incessant demand of the will-to-live in us, the obsessive witnesses of its thirst to exist (p. 204). Schopenhauer's "genitals as focus of the will" (§ 42) is the passage most often cited as anticipating Freud's libido. Henry places it within the "ontic" concept of life.

But the immanence of the will to the body, its identity with it, which made the first approach to the body in Book II so profound, is incompatible with the thesis that the will, abandoned to the unconscious, must seek the reality of its phenomenality outside itself, in the body, but a body now belonging to representation, to the "world" of life. Hence the decisive shift in the status of the body, while the thesis of the will's "affirmation" shows its ambiguity. At a certain moment this affirmation is no longer the will's self-affirmation, nor, for the body, the setting in motion of its powers and their free play, the unleashing of appetites and needs willed by them. Over the body, life and their desires is superimposed a sort of pure act of willing, the will, which can invest itself in them and assent to them, or refuse. Between this act and its bodily manifestation a space, a difference, a contradiction is instituted, "the contradiction between the will and its own visible form". This space is that of representation, as shown by the fragmentation of the will inherent in the *principium individuationis*, co-constitutive of all representation: "this act of affirming its attachment to the body, the will now repeats in an infinity of individuals" (p. 204). The "pure act of willing" that can assent or refuse is the Cartesian will of indifference Schopenhauer had rejected in Chapter V; it returns through the gap opened by representation.

Life, ceasing to coincide with the will and be defined by it, becomes its object, the object of its affirmation: "this life which is a matter of willing or not willing". Likewise the body. From this interval between will and life arise the categories of ethics, good and evil, or rather good and wicked. Life is evil since it is the tireless repetition of a desire that never reaches its aim, the body traversed and ground down by this desire until death. But true evil lies in the affirmation of life, in adherence to this wretched body; the wicked one is precisely the one who says yes to his drives, "the wicked by the energy he puts into affirming life". This is the ground of injustice: the wicked "does not content himself with affirming the will-to-live as it appears in his body, but pushes this affirmation to the point of denying the will as it appears in other individuals" (§ 62). Salvation lies in refusing this life, which causes the living being's perpetual misery before extending it to all around, since the affirmation of life in each inevitably despoils it in others. "Says yes to his drives" (*pulsions*) is Henry's deliberate Freudian word: Schopenhauer's ethics is already a moral topography of drive and renunciation.

Where this conversion finds its condition of possibility is easy to see: in the gap between will and life that allows the first no longer to will the second, "the will then detaches itself from life". This gap must receive its true sense: the detachment it promotes is not an ethical concept but "le dépliement de la distance phénoménologique par laquelle la volonté se donne à voir à elle-même sous l'aspect de la vie" (the unfolding of the phenomenological distance by which the will gives itself to be seen by itself under the aspect of life, p. 205); this self-seeing in the form of seeing life makes possible no longer willing life, the conversion of the will. As in classical philosophies, "comme dans le freudisme qui leur fait suite" (as in Freudianism, which follows them), salvation occurs through knowledge, which consists in representation and so in reflection, the two being one since the representation of the world is for Schopenhauer only the will's self-representation, its own reflection. Everywhere the aim is to strengthen "knowledge of the true nature of the world, so that this knowledge may become the supreme quieter of my will"; and "only through this knowledge can the will abolish itself ... nature brings the will to the light precisely because only in the light can it find its deliverance" (p. 206). The comparison with Freud is explicit: the cure, like Schopenhauer's salvation, works by bringing the will into the light. Henry's critique of the analytic cure is announced here: deliverance by representation presupposes that representation can act on life.

But in this description of salvation an imperceptible shift has occurred. The will was to deny life, to break its attachment to the body; but body and life are only the will's representation, its phenomenal accomplishment. For the will, no longer willing life means no longer willing itself, no longer willing. Thus, after "the will then detaches itself from life", this detachment presupposes the will's detachment from itself, its self-negation: "the will turns back: it no longer affirms its essence, represented in the mirror of phenomena; it denies it". In the same passage the "horror" of life and its pleasures becomes, in the ascetic, horror of the will itself: "this will he has come to abhor since he knows himself". The analysis of chastity shows that suspending bodily needs is only the will's self-suppression and its consequence: "chastity marks that the will abolishes itself together with the life of the body which is its manifestation" (§ 68). Denial of life is self-denial of will, since life and will are one.

Here the purely apparent, illusory character of the opposition between life and will is revealed, if every action on life is identically an action on will and every action of life an action of will. "L'opposition de la vie et de la volonté, c'est la connaissance ; son apparence, c'est l'apparence de la connaissance" (the opposition of life and will is knowledge; its appearance is the appearance of knowledge, its illusion, p. 206). Will one then grant this powerless appearance the power to deliver us from life and from the will itself, from the essence and ground of all power? Schopenhauer's philosophy meets the paradox that will haunt all the thoughts it engendered: to separate life and representation radically as reality and unreality, yet ask the second to act on the first, even to transform it from top to bottom. One of the major contributions of *The World as Will* is to affirm the primacy of the will and its determination of all knowledge, so that the intellect can only be its "valet", charged with justifying its undertakings, not inventing them. A consequence of this general critique of knowledge is the critique of ethics as located outside life and assigning it norms and commandments from outside: "an ethics that would claim to shape and correct the will is impossible. For doctrines act only on knowledge, and knowledge never determines the will itself" (§ 53, p. 207). Henry names "the paradox of Schopenhauer" that will become Freud's: the intellect is the will's servant, yet the cure, an act of knowledge, is to transform the will. Schopenhauer himself states the objection to his own soteriology.

These difficulties lead to a radical elucidation of the relation of will and life, taking as its guide what remains, beyond all contradictions, the doctrine's decisive intuition: the opposition to representability of what must be thought as the reality and essence of all things, and the inner understanding of this reality from that opposition. What unfolds its essence independently of representation, of the ecstatic Dimension that gives it light, remains in itself, in its radical immanence. "Ce qui demeure en soi, c'est la Vie" (What remains in itself is Life, p. 207). The stone does not remain in itself, nor does any being, nor the being of that being, its mode of being thought from it as its a priori condition. The a priori condition of beings is their capacity to be represented, which refers to the exteriorization of ecstasis and ultimately to the original emergence of *physis*. Thought for itself, being can be thought only as life. What Schopenhauer aims at first is not will but what escapes all representedness and, in its heterogeneity to it, constitutes being, movement, life. "Remaining in itself" is a precise criterion: a being "is" only for another (by being exposed); life is in that it holds itself.

Why what escapes representedness is thought as will, grasped as will-to-live, hunger and need, has been shown: Schopenhauer, unable to assign a rigorous phenomenological status to what he understands as the essence of reality, unable to think the most initial essence of appearing in its precedence over representedness, faces the bastard concept at the centre of his philosophy, "une réalité sans réalité, ... une vie qui ne s'éprouve pas elle-même" (a reality without reality, a life that does not experience itself, p. 208), not life but the will-to-live, the unquenchable need for a revelation it no longer constitutes in and by itself and asks of the "world". The "bastard concept" is what Freud will call the drive.

What constitutes the central element of Schopenhauer's discovery is not the will but its condition, one not derived from it but making it possible. This condition, the essence of life, is immanence. The will's immanence determines it wholly, its irreducible opposition to representedness; it makes the will reality, as long as it does not fall to the rank of a will-to-live seeking its reality in the world of unreality, and it disqualifies that world. Beyond these great themes, all the others receive their secret light from this immanence. The will is generally interpreted as endless desire. Not only because, as will-to-live, it strives vainly to realize itself and never ends its striving, but more originally because it has no end in the sense of an aim, and has none because, accomplishing itself wholly in itself and never going outside itself, "elle ne peut jamais se donner quelque chose comme la représentation d'un but" (it can never give itself anything like the representation of an aim, p. 208) nor institute the distance that would separate it from the aim, from itself. The will's relation to itself, its primitive being as immanence, underlies these too readily accepted assertions (p. 209). Henry now begins the positive reinterpretation of Schopenhauer's negative predicates: aimlessness is not the defect of a will that misses its goal but the mark of a power without ecstasis.

Hence the other Schopenhauerian formulation of the thesis that the will has no aim, the assertion that it is "empty", must also be understood, as Nietzsche will, by being reversed. That the will is empty means it is full: "elle a longueur, largeur, hauteur et profondeur et emplit tout son être" (it has length, breadth, height and depth and fills its whole being, p. 209); nowhere, at no moment, does it rise above itself or unfold beyond itself the vacuity of any space. That it is empty means: because it does not accomplish ecstasis or project any representation before its gaze, it wills nothing, no aim, pursues no end; no motive determines it, no reason. "La volonté est vide non par l'effet du vide de son regard, mais parce qu'elle n'a pas de regard" (the will is empty not because its gaze is empty, but because it has no gaze). That is why, being "independent of all knowledge", the beings the will inhabits "do not appear as tending toward an end that attracts them but as pushed by an invisible energy". These beings include men, "not drawn forward but pushed from behind; it is not life that attracts them, but need that presses them and makes them walk". So unusual is the original sense of life as immanence, so foreign to representedness, that Schopenhauer cannot help judging it by representedness. One who moves in the open space of representation by a principle that represents nothing is like a man who staggers: "an individual driven forward against his will struggles as best he can, and the resulting confusion often produces a comic effect". Yet despite, or rather because of, this incapacity to represent anything, because of its radical immanence, the will is wholly in itself, wholly itself; it never stops being what it is and willing. "The affirmation of the will", says Schopenhauer in an essential proposition, "is the will itself" (p. 210). Cut off from all reference to the world, beings and their being, given over to itself, the will is only its own essence, accomplishes only what this essence accomplishes: it wills, indefinitely, inexorably; it is "the tireless will", "a tireless spring", and since it is the being of all being, "every being wills without respite". The irreducibility of this drive, its ignorance of the whole world, the fact that its "sufficient reason is not in the outer world", determines it as "a blind impulse, an instinct without ground or motive", an action that finally takes its principle only from itself, in absolute indifference to all that is not itself. "Pushed from behind, not drawn forward" is the formula Freud's drive (*Drang*, pressure) will inherit; the "biblical" dimensions (length, breadth, height, depth, Eph. 3:18) give the will a fullness that is the exact opposite of lack.

This also explains the terrible character of the will, how, like the hordes of the strong of whom Nietzsche is about to speak, joyfully ravaging the earth, it bursts into a universe it ignores, because it bears not the least part of that universe in itself, having no representation of it. Schopenhauer described existence inwardly determined by the will as an alternation between the proliferation of cares, each giving way to another, and boredom, so unbearable that relapsing into preoccupation and its endless game, even manufacturing artificial aims, seems far preferable. But the principle of this deadly emptiness of boredom has already been found, which is also that of its Nietzschean reversal into the unspeakable experience of fullness: it expresses only the will's original condition in its heterogeneity to all representedness, its immanence (p. 210). Henry's interpretation of boredom inverts Heidegger's (*Fundamental Concepts of Metaphysics*), where profound boredom discloses beings as a whole: here boredom is the will's self-experience, misread through the lack of an object.

With the recognition of the will's original essence as immanence, one of the major discoveries of the philosophy of life is achieved: the status of action and its true possibility. Action, like everything, owes its possibility to being. As long as being is interpreted from Greek truth as ecstasis, the being of action is ultimately understood that way too. Action is pro-duction. To pro-duce Aristotle's silver chalice is to answer for the chalice's being there before us, ready for sacrifice. To pro-duce means to bring into presence, to lead something toward its appearing, to let it come forward. To convey the essence of production, Heidegger quotes the *Symposium*: "every occasion for whatever passes over and goes forward from the non-present into presence is *poiēsis*, is pro-duction" (*The Question Concerning Technology*). To produce is to pass from the non-present into presence. But presence is that of ecstasis, which is why it is accomplished as a passage from its contrary. "On the Essence of Ground" says precisely: "pro-ducing the world before oneself". Action is finally the action of *physis*, emergence (p. 211). The four causes of the chalice are from *The Question Concerning Technology*; Henry uses Heidegger's best text on *poiēsis* to define the ecstatic concept of action he will reject.

These presuppositions dominate not only Greek or Heideggerian thought. The metaphysics of representation is an avatar of this thought, and the ecstatic conception of action becomes in the modern world its explication from representedness. To act is not only to posit ends and dispose means, each time representing, throwing before oneself and referring to oneself what is to be done; it is to realize the doing and its object, to bring it into existence, into the state of the ob-jected. Action, not only in its circumstances but in itself, is objectification, the process of a representation. Only because the being of action has first been reduced to that of representation can representation, for instance the *cogito*'s "I represent", be understood as an action, or the movement of Leibniz's representation as that of a real action and its essence, the essence of force (p. 212). The reciprocal reduction (action as objectification, representation as act) explains Chapter V's Leibniz: Heidegger can call perception *vis* only because he already thinks action as production.

These presuppositions form the mute ground of all idealism, which Schopenhauer radically overturns, since the will is an action without representation. So radical is this reversal, so hard to conceive, that the author of *The World* attempts it twice. First, action without representation can only be "blind", and since representation is the place of all disposing and organizing, of ends and means, coordinates and directions, an aberrant action: one who acts pushed from behind by the will, not drawn forward by the representation of an end, unable to set his landmarks before himself, flails like a puppet, producing, as Schopenhauer said, "a comic effect". The first version of the reversal still measures will by representation.

One must measure everything that, with this bracketing of representedness, is expelled from the sphere of an action now immanent, transcending toward nothing, preceded and lit by no project: not only finality in general, which consists precisely in inserting representation into the will, but also the common claim to attain in practical life any "assurance", and finally certainty, inevitably understood as the pursuit and obtaining of evidence, the teleological fulfilment of representational and intentional consciousness. Deprived of it and of everything grounded on it, of every form of knowledge, the will acts alone; its action is the simple inner actualization of an essence that ignores the world. The rejection of "assurance" echoes Chapter III's critique of Heidegger's *Sicherung*: action's certainty is not the security of the represented.

And here is the second moment of the reversal, which opens wide the way to a philosophy of life, first Nietzsche's: action so defined, foreign to the light of ecstasis, ignorant of the world and of what happens in it, of the obstacles it will meet and against which, not seeing them, it should break, "cette action aveugle réussit, elle est sûre, certaine et, mieux que cela, infaillible" (this blind action succeeds; it is sure, certain and, better still, infallible, p. 213). So much so that, perfect in and by itself, indifferent to all calculation and reckoning, to rational evaluation and knowledge, it is rather the insertion of knowledge into it that seriously disturbs and threatens its exercise: "with the advent of reason, this certainty and infallibility [of the will's immediate manifestations] almost entirely vanishes". Will without reason, action without representation, is instinct. The term does not merely designate this process externally and a posteriori; its semantic charge carries the astonishment such blind yet successful behaviour arouses, the prestige surrounding it: "the sureness of instinct", "the infallibility of instinct", "the mystery of instinct". Henry reaches the positive concept he needs: an action certain of itself without representation. This is the certainty of the *videor* (Chapter I) transposed into praxis; *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps* had found it in Biran's effort.

In post-Schopenhauerian thought, Eduard von Hartmann's famous *Philosophy of the Unconscious* dwelt most complacently on the miraculous character of instinct, celebrating this astonishing activity at work everywhere in the universe and in us, and giving thanks to its author, the Most High, God, "à l'Inconscient !" (to the Unconscious!, p. 213). How significant that the new idol of modern times makes its entrance as this majestic figure. Before it, a crowd of dumbfounded spectators: ecstatic admirers of the marvellous and their grumbling detractors; believers hoping to recover their lost God by this strange route; scientists delighted to hear ill spoken of their oldest enemy, consciousness (whose implementation their whole activity is), but furious to see return the other adversary they thought they had disposed of long ago (hence, they remarked, the phenomena of instinct must be reduced to their proper proportions, objective phenomena that science explains or will explain by its laws, the contrary of a miracle; besides, instinct is far from infallible, it often errs, the sphex...). And behind this agitated crowd, "la foule des psychanalystes à venir" (the crowd of psychoanalysts to come, p. 214), trying to form an opinion: bravo for the unconscious, but ours has nothing to do with Hartmann's! And this although Hartmann problematized nothing but Schopenhauer's will, whose status, according to Freud himself, defines that of the drives: "the great thinker Schopenhauer, whose unconscious 'will' is equivalent to the psychical instincts of psycho-analysis" ("A Difficulty in the Path of Psycho-Analysis", 1917). But who speaks this way? To whom must action exercised outside knowledge, ignorant of its means, aimless, appear blind, unconscious, catastrophic? To the thought that places the possibility of being in representability. To whom must such action, if it succeeds, appear miraculous? To the same thought. Schopenhauer and Hartmann think the same: Schopenhauer that a blind action can only produce ruinous, contradictory consequences and ravage the earth; Hartmann that a blind action that reaches its goal must be a God's, that the Unconscious is divine. In this first, divine phase of its existence the Unconscious reveals what its night hides: "plutôt qu'une pulsion inavouable, l'absurdité d'une métaphysique qui situe la possibilité de l'être dans sa représentabilité" (rather than an unavowable drive, the absurdity of a metaphysics that places the possibility of being in its representability) and the necessity for it to turn immediately into its contrary. The satirical tableau has a strict point: pessimism and theology of the unconscious are two evaluations of one presupposition, that action is intelligible only as represented. The sphex, Fabre's wasp, was the standard example in the instinct debates (Bergson's *Creative Evolution*).

Before being lost as tragic or miraculous consequences in the field of vision of representedness, it is representedness itself that the thesis of the immanence of action radically rejects. That action is never effected in the open space of ecstasis, that it bears no aims or means, nothing representable, still less the power to bring itself before itself in representation, does not make it absurd or incomprehensible: "la non-représentabilité de l'action constitue bien plutôt sa possibilité même" (the non-representability of action is rather its very possibility, p. 215). Its possibility is the remaining-in-itself and being-with-itself of the acting being, which is thereby always already in possession of the powers that constitute it and able to deploy them. The being that is one with its own powers and receives its essence from its capacity to deploy them is the body. The body is defined by the set of its powers (seeing, hearing, feeling, touching, grasping, moving) only insofar as these powers are its own, insofar as it draws its essence from the possibility of exercising them. This possibility is identically that of each power taken in itself, what makes each of them a power and a power of the body. "La corporéité originelle est l'âme au sens de Descartes, elle est l'immanence, elle est la vie" (Original corporeity is the soul in Descartes' sense; it is immanence, it is life). The identification of body and Cartesian soul closes the circle opened in Chapter I: the "soul" of the Second Meditation was the subjective body's self-givenness, which Descartes' mechanism excluded.
