If Schopenhauer's will is in turn a body, it is for the same reason: its ultimate condition lies not in mere willing but in the inherence of willing in itself and its coherence with itself. The will needs no body or life as an external manifestation serving as its mirror. Its manifestation is its force, which consists in being-with-itself and in the original Unity by which everything is what it is and receives the power to be. The will thus rests on a force prior to it, and Nietzsche will grasp it as such. Schopenhauer already thinks this force under the title of will, if the will was originally defined by its heterogeneity to representation. For the ecstatic relation, notably the ecstatic relation to oneself, is absent wherever a force releases its power. On the ground of this force the will wards off aims and means, projects, reckonings and calculations; on this ground, far from being reducible to production, it insurmountably excludes it. "La réfutation de la métaphysique de la représentéité et de son origine dans la φύσις n'est pas théorique mais pratique, elle consiste dans la simple reconnaissance du fait de la corporéité" (the refutation of the metaphysics of representedness and of its origin in *physis* is not theoretical but practical; it consists in the simple recognition of the fact of corporeity, p. 215). Wherever in the world, that is, outside it, a body expends its force, the metaphysics of ecstasis is already out of play. "In the world, that is, outside it" is exact: the body acts in the world but its acting is not of the world. The formula "practical refutation" recalls Marx's eleventh thesis on Feuerbach, reread by Henry in *Marx* as a thesis about subjective praxis.

To separate will and body, when the body is only the object of intuitive representation, Schopenhauer points out that the body is preserved through a set of needs that run through it, to which it is subject and whose tireless fulfilment escapes its own will. In drinking, eating and procreating the body appears essentially passive. Speaking of "the contradiction between the will and its own visible form", Schopenhauer adds: "in vain does the body realize outwardly, through the organs of generation, the will to perpetuate the species; this perpetuation itself is not willed" (p. 216). But should not what is said of the body be said of the will? The will wills, but its act of willing is in no way willed by it. Precisely because the will wills without ever being master of its willing, this condition is also the body's, which is only the will, and the body's needs too never stop willing without ever being willed themselves. The illusory doubling of the will in the objective body makes one believe the will could will or not will, not will this body, its needs. "L'ek-stasis de la représentation crée l'espace d'une liberté, laquelle se fonde sur le ne... pas... de son néant" (the ecstasis of representation creates the space of a freedom founded on the "not" of its nothingness): not willing this object, which the will can as well not will as will. But the original, real body does not differ from the will. Like it, it knows no ecstasis, has no freedom to unfold beyond or short of itself the space of a distance by which it could escape itself, present itself as an object, will or not will itself. Like the will, "il est bloqué en lui-même, enfermé dans son être propre : il est immanent, il est vivant" (it is locked in itself, enclosed in its own being: it is immanent, it is living). Freedom of indifference is located in the nothingness of ecstatic distance, Sartre's freedom included; the living body has no such freedom, because it cannot step back from itself.

With the immanence of the will we are led back to the essence of life. Life is "l'épreuve originelle de soi dans le se souffrir soi-même" (the original self-experience in self-suffering, p. 216), such that, on the ground of this essence, whatever lives is riveted to itself to be forever what it is. Man, for Schopenhauer, is nailed to Ixion's wheel, condemned to will with an endless willing. This being-riveted-to-itself, the will's condition, is not given by the will to itself; it results neither from its willing nor from its power. "Dans l'essence du vouloir réside son anti-essence" (in the essence of willing lies its anti-essence), the inability of this willing to will or not will itself. "Ce ne pas pouvoir est la plus grande force" (This inability is the greatest force, p. 217). In the will lies a force greater than it, preceding it, against which it can do nothing: the force that delivers it to itself and to its own being. This force is life's, the force of being, "le rassemblement édificateur qui donne toute chose à elle-même" (the edifying gathering that gives each thing to itself). Such a force, not the will's action, not an action but its contrary, is "la passion de l'être, le souffrir primitif" (the passion of being, the primitive suffering) by which the essence of being is identically that of life. After immanence, and as its ultimate condition, every philosophy of life meets its second essential determination: affectivity. The inversion "inability is the greatest force" restates *L'essence de la manifestation*, §§ 52–70: the passivity of life toward itself is the source of every power. Here it is applied to the Schopenhauerian will and will be applied to the Freudian drive.

It is the most remarkable feature of Schopenhauer's work to place affectivity at the centre of its discourse, not as an explicit theme but as what it meets everywhere, what pervades existence and determines its relation to every form of being. Affectivity, practically excluded from philosophical debate or present only episodically as a marginal problem, becomes its sole object. "Suffering", says Schopenhauer, "is the ground of all life" (p. 217). All modalities of life are modalizations of this single suffering: weariness here, there shame, regret and remorse, boredom, disgust, infinite fatigue. One who considers himself need not obey the Socratic "know thyself"; it suffices to weep. Relation to others is not a fact of knowledge either; it is pity, or cruelty. The main forms of common existence, groups and associations, which Schopenhauer calls communities, are of two kinds: "formal" ones resting on the intellect, and "material", that is, real, communities such as family and classes, which are affective: "the essential thing in such associations is feelings" (p. 218). Particular but highly important problems of love life never discussed before, such as the motivation of the glances lovers exchange, become the object of long developments. The list of affective topics (pity, communities, the metaphysics of sexual love) reads as the agenda of the later psychoanalytic theory of affects; "it suffices to weep" displaces self-knowledge by affect.

The world is an enigma, a "frightful dream", a procession of miseries, and yet its secret can be pierced. Affectivity reveals it: "this common essence of things ... reveals itself precisely to each of us, but *in concreto*, through feeling". And when this revelation finally allows escape from the play of pain, affective modalities again form the concrete shape of deliverance: "joy and heavenly peace", "profound calm", "inner serenity". Between the misery of man given over to desire and the salvation of the ascetic and saint who have accomplished the self-negation of the will-to-live, there is only the space from one affective tonality to another, "une sorte de dialectique de la vie affective elle-même" (a kind of dialectic of affective life itself, p. 218): "then, instead of the eternal passage from desire to fear, from joy to pain, instead of hope never satisfied and never extinguished that turns man's life into a dream while the will animates it, we behold that peace higher than all reason, that ocean-like calm, that deep repose of the soul, that unshakeable serenity" (§ 71). The reading anticipates Chapter VII on Nietzsche: salvation is not a passage from feeling to knowledge but from one affect to another, the thesis of *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 70, on the conversion of suffering into joy.

Schopenhauer's metaphysics is one of will, but "will" is often only a title for affectivity itself: "impulses and modifications of the will include not only volition and resolution in the narrow sense, but every striving, desire, aversion, hope, fear, love, hatred, in short everything that directly constitutes happiness or suffering, pleasure or pain"; and "everything that is a matter of the will in the widest sense, such as desire, passion, joy, pain, kindness, wickedness, and what the Germans call *Gemüth* ... everything attributed to the heart" (p. 219). The enumerations support Henry's thesis that Schopenhauer's will is affectivity named by its ontic form.

Affectivity is not, in life, an empirical character merely noted, still less a synthetic determination foreign to its being and coming from outside: "suffering does not infiltrate us from outside; we carry within us the inexhaustible source from which it springs". Here a wholly new philosophical *intentio* takes shape: to tear affective existence from the realm of facticity to which it was traditionally abandoned, to institute an eidetics of affectivity making an a priori discourse on it possible; more, to give this hitherto unnoticed essence an exceptional status, making it not a fragment of the universe of pure laws but the essence of the absolute, the essence of life. Schopenhauer's project, "to discover by a priori reasons the deep roots by which pain belongs to the very essence of life" (§ 56), must be heard as unheard-of. Pain would no longer be what it has always been, a misfortune, a natural peculiarity or an incomprehensible fate, but the a priori structure of all that is, its innermost possibility and, as Nietzsche will say after Schopenhauer, "la Mère de l'Être" (the Mother of Being, p. 219). Henry credits Schopenhauer with the idea of an a priori eidetics of affectivity, the programme of *L'essence de la manifestation*; "Mother of Being" anticipates the Nietzsche chapter (*Birth of Tragedy*, the "Mothers of Being").

We can understand, however, why Schopenhauer's extraordinary project cannot be adequately realized, why the fundamental questions he thematizes for the first time lead not to the elucidation of a vast new field but to an impasse. The main reason is that the essence from which affectivity must be grasped eidetically is not truly that of life, its original auto-affection identical with affectivity, but what was called its ontic concept, the reductive grasp of life as will-to-live, will and desire. Affectivity is then understood not in itself, in the ontological essence of life, but from a reality other than it, no longer an essence but a fact, the fact of need, that life presents itself as a never-satisfied appetite, a drive subject to indefinite reiteration. "Une explication remplace l'analyse essentielle" (an explanation replaces essential analysis, p. 220), the analysis of the essence of the absolute. The ontic/ontological distinction now governs the critique of affect theory: explaining affect by need is an ontic explanation where an eidetic analysis was promised.

How is affectivity "explained" from life as need? In the simplest way, since every natural and especially scientific explanation, every theory that misses the problem of principled possibility, has the clarity that comes from not seeing the essential foundations. Need (will, desire), if it reaches its aim, produces pleasure, well-being, happiness: it is a satisfied need. If not, it produces pain, suffering, discomfort, dissatisfaction in all its forms. Schopenhauer repeats it: "is it [the will] checked by some obstacle placed between it and its momentary aim, that is suffering; if it reaches its aim, that is satisfaction, well-being, happiness" (§ 56). This is the model of Freud's pleasure principle: pleasure as discharge of a tension of need.

What is achieved first with this definition of affectivity as the effect of need is its determination from a foreign principle, so that the laws, properties and development of affective life are no longer its own, explainable by it, but the laws of something else; the history of affectivity is no longer grounded in its essence as its inner development but is the history of desire, with its reason in what Schopenhauer calls will. There is a prior of affectivity, something that precedes and governs it, of which it is only the consequence. Schopenhauer's claim to give an a priori theory of affectivity is illusory: "l'affectivité n'est justement pas l'a priori, elle est un a posteriori, l'a posteriori de la volonté" (affectivity is precisely not the a priori but an a posteriori, the a posteriori of the will, p. 221). The phrase echoes Schopenhauer's own formula (Chapter V): "the body is the a posteriori knowledge of the will".

For example, Schopenhauer recognizes, or rather finds, two modalities of affective life: satisfaction and dissatisfaction. He does not describe them as they are in and for themselves; he explains them by what happens to the will according to whether it reaches its aim. The nature of the will accounts for there being satisfaction and dissatisfaction in the world, pleasure and suffering, well-being and misery, because the will is essentially desire, need and lack of what it does not have, so that it is "satisfied" if it obtains it, "dissatisfied" otherwise. Only if a being is constituted as lack of being, a reality as lack of reality, can satisfaction and dissatisfaction, joy and pain, befall it and be experienced, can affectivity in general be possible. This is what Schopenhauer calls an a priori theory of affectivity: finding in the will its principled condition of possibility. Henry restates the theory's logic before refuting it: affectivity requires lack.

The will as will-to-live not only makes affectivity and its dichotomy possible, but also determines the respective status of the basic tonalities: there is no equality or ontological equivalence between them, some deemed positive, having a right to be, the others only "negative". Since the will is infinite, endless desire always renewed, clearly "no satisfaction lasts": as soon as it comes and the will thinks it has found its aim, the movement that eternally drives it forward repeats or continues, and dissatisfaction with it. More: satisfaction is not only precarious (the causal order is foreign to our wishes) and provisional; it has no specific content, no "positivity", being only a momentary suspension of pain. The purely negative character of every apparently happy form of life is a leitmotif in Schopenhauer and the ground of his pessimism: "satisfaction is in its essence nothing but negative; there is nothing positive in it. No satisfaction comes to us of itself; satisfaction and contentment can only be deliverance from a pain, a need" (§ 58, p. 222). The negativity of pleasure, pleasure as reduction of tension, is the economic thesis of *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*.

This respective situation, satisfaction always secondary to a primitive dissatisfaction, must be thought through. In the strange dialectic sketched here dissatisfaction occurs twice. On one hand it is on the same plane as satisfaction and results, like it, from the presupposition of need: it occurs if need misses its aim, satisfaction if it reaches it. On the other hand, more essentially, dissatisfaction precedes itself: it is no longer what befalls desire a posteriori when it fails to find its "object", but what belongs to it by principle, from the start, insofar as it is desire. Here affectivity does not present itself as the effect of the will and its play; it qualifies the will originally and inheres in it. The will is no longer the a priori condition of affectivity as of what proceeds from it according to its history. "Bien plutôt l'affectivité se donne maintenant pour une détermination a priori de la volonté elle-même" (Rather, affectivity now presents itself as an a priori determination of the will itself, p. 222), since the will is desire and need, and there is no desire or need not already affectively determined, already a modality of affectivity and presupposing it. Henry finds the reversal within Schopenhauer's own text: the original dissatisfaction that makes desire desire is an affect, not a consequence of desire.

The illusion of the thesis, traditional and taken over by Schopenhauer, that makes affectivity depend on a prior *conatus*, a desire to be and striving toward it, must at last be recognized. Its most naive form is the claim that pleasant tonalities result from a satisfied desire and unpleasant ones from an unsatisfied desire. In the "satisfaction" or "dissatisfaction" of a desire the affectivity supposedly explained by need reaching or missing its aim is already implied. Reaching one's aim is nothing affective in itself, nothing able to produce affectivity: "la flèche qui frappe la cible n'en retire aucune satisfaction" (the arrow that strikes the target draws no satisfaction from it, p. 223). Only a being originally constituted as affective, able as such to be affectively determined, to "experience" tonalities, can reach its aim and draw satisfaction from it. Need, as *conatus* toward what it needs, can feel the specific phenomenological tonality called "satisfaction" only insofar as it is first a need of life, whose original being is its self-appearing, identical in it with the essence of affectivity and life. Only because the will is such a need, originally determined in itself as affective, can it then modulate its affectivity according to its history. This prior affectivity of the will is what Schopenhauer's thought implies insofar as it conceives life as suffering by essence and at bottom. The arrow example is the chapter's key argument against every economic theory of affect, Freud's included: discharge, reaching an aim, reducing tension are non-affective events, which can be felt only by a being already affective in itself.

But does not life owe this suffering to being will, desire for a reality it never reaches? Is it not the impossibility for need to fill itself, to reach its aim, that gives it its principled dissatisfaction? Existence is dissatisfied, for Schopenhauer, because the infinite will has no corresponding object, because there is no *summum bonum*; but more fundamentally, as shown, because it can first experience something in general, dissatisfaction for instance, and its history is tragic only because it is "l'histoire d'une affectivité et, comme on dit, « l'histoire d'une vie »" (the history of an affectivity and, as they say, "the story of a life", p. 224). The idiom "story of a life" is turned literally: a history is tragic only if it is lived.

Thus a singular reversal occurs, though veiled, in Schopenhauer's text. The will, which constantly determined affectivity as its ground, producing tonalities according to whether it is "satisfied" or not, is suddenly placed after it. Suffering, far from resulting from the *conatus* and its failure, precedes, arouses and makes it possible. After it is recalled once more that "life is essentially bound up with pain", it appears that pain drives life forward and makes it, in this projection ahead of itself, a desire: "all desire arises from a need, a lack, a pain" (p. 224). Desire is desire only by a lack, a being constituted as lack of being. But this lack is desire and need only insofar as it affects a being able in general to be affected. Finally, it is pain, suffering itself, insofar as, unable to bear itself, it constantly aspires to surpass itself and throws itself ahead of itself, that becomes desire. This is the genetic thesis that will govern Henry's reading of the Freudian drive: desire is born from suffering's inability to bear itself, not from a lack of an object. "Unable to bear itself" is the formula of *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 70.

This secret reversal of precedence between willing and affectivity becomes visible even when Schopenhauer explicitly defends the contrary, the primacy of the will. This happens when the "explanation" of pleasure and pain goes through the body, the phenomenal appearance of the will: "every action on the body is at once and directly an action on the will; as such it is called pain when it goes against the will, and well-being or pleasure when it conforms to it" (§ 18). The rest of the text, which has the merit of wresting tonalities from representation without giving them a more precise status ("it is wrong to call pleasure and pain representations; they are only immediate affections of the will in its phenomenal form, the body"), ends with a definition showing that one goes from willing to pleasure and pain only by presupposing what is to be explained, the impressional character of pleasure and pain: "they are the necessary and momentary fact of willing or not willing the impression the body undergoes" (p. 225). The circularity lies in "impression": to will or not will an impression, the impression must already be felt.

The respective status of will and affectivity in relation to the essence of life, and the validity of each one's claim to constitute it, will be decided in the extraordinary analysis in which "le phénomène du refoulement" (the phenomenon of repression, p. 225) is recognized for the first time and circumscribed in its possibility. It is presented as an analysis of madness but, since it brings into play the system's fundamental thesis, the disjunction in the psyche of will and representation and the determination of the second by the first, it underlies other developments. Its principle is that the will, in which all power is concentrated, represents nothing, while representation wills nothing, that is, can do nothing: "the reason for this reciprocal relation is that the will by itself does not know, and the understanding associated with it cannot will". To the efficacy of the will is opposed the inertia and passivity of representation, of the intellect. Will and representation are external to each other in power as in their capacity to manifest. When they are connected, as in man, representation, which brings the will the light it lacks but has no power, necessarily submits and obeys. It is not a collaboration like that of the blind man and the cripple, but a diktat, since the power of illumination has no power, so that what is illuminated depends not on what illuminates it but on another principle (p. 226). With "the phenomenon of repression" the chapter reaches its title. The blind-and-lame fable (Schopenhauer's own image in Supplements ch. 19) is replaced by "diktat", the word used in Chapter V for representation's reign in Freud; here the diktat runs the other way.

A paradoxical, almost inconceivable situation arises once the intellect truly becomes the will's "valet": thought through, the intellect is not only relegated to a subordinate position; one may ask whether it is not destroyed, since the "making-see" that is its essence has turned into its contrary, a "not making-see", a "hiding". The very meaning of representational life is not altered but reversed when representation no longer aims to exhibit what is. Schopenhauer at least partly accepts this: "in these reciprocal relations the will nevertheless keeps supremacy and shows it when, weary of serving as the intellect's plaything, it makes the intellect feel its sovereign power in the last resort, forbidding it certain representations, certain trains of thought ... at this moment it reins in the intellect and forces it to turn its attention elsewhere" (Supplements, ch. 19). Henry's point: repression presupposes a light that hides, a contradiction in the concept of representation, which the Freudian theory will inherit.

From this fundamental passivity of the intellect, which finds the principle of its action elsewhere, follows a theory of memory and of forgetting. Memory is only apparently a faculty of representation, since what brings the represented content before the mind is not the capacity to set it before, to produce it as object, but the power, foreign in itself to this production, that allows or forbids it. Speaking of "the influence that presides over all retention and all recollection", Schopenhauer says "the will is its condition and permanent basis". As it is the condition of remembering, the will is identically the condition of forgetting: not willing that a content enter the light of representation belongs to the same principle that sustains memory, as its negative determination. Forgetting explains many surprises. Schopenhauer enjoys the example of a man who listed every conceivable outcome of an affair concerning him and later discovers with amazement that he had not considered the most likely one, which indeed occurred: "and this explains the forgetting and the surprise: while the intellect believed it was making a complete review of the possibilities, the worst of all escaped it, because the will kept it covered, as it were, with its hand" (p. 227). The theory of memory recalls Chapter II's: memory is a power to produce representations, not a store. The anecdote is a forerunner of Freud's motivated forgetting in *The Psychopathology of Everyday Life*.

The theory of madness exposes the inner difficulties of these now self-evident conceptions. It is in fact a theory of repression, repeating and deepening the problematic of memory and forgetting. While "true health of mind consists in perfect recollection", Schopenhauer considers madness a disorder of memory, and this disorder is what modern psychology will call repression. It comes down to the will's refusal to let into the mind a representation contrary to it: "let us recall with what reluctance we think of things that strongly injure our interests, pride or wishes, with what difficulty we bring ourselves to submit them to the intellect's serious and precise examination, how easily we break off from them or gradually detach ourselves without being aware of it, whereas pleasant things enter the mind of themselves, slip back if driven out and hold our attention for hours. In this reluctance of the will to let what is contrary to it come into the light of the intellect lies the breach through which madness can burst into the mind" (Supplements, ch. 32). This is the passage that Otto Rank and Freud acknowledged as anticipating repression (*On the History of the Psycho-Analytic Movement*); Henry quotes it at length as the founding text.

Schopenhauer conceives experience as a painful process by which representations contrary to the will must nevertheless reach consciousness and be acknowledged, insofar as together and in their necessary connection they make up the order of the world. Experience is the difficult entry into the intellect of what, being contrary to the will, is a painful impression, which once accepted and assimilated gradually weakens (p. 228). Suppose instead the will's categorical refusal to let into the mind a representational content so displeasing as to be "unbearable"; suppose the infinite will has the radical power to reject it from consciousness, to "repress" it: then a gap appears in the whole of representations, something like a "hole" in the fabric of the world, which the will sets about filling with other representations it invents or displaces for this purpose. A truncated totality of representations, an arbitrary connection of phenomena, an arranged, fabricated past is produced, and madness with it. "But if, even in a single case, the will's reluctance and resistance to admitting a truth reach a degree at which this operation is no longer carried out purely; if certain events or details are thus entirely withheld from the intellect ... and if then, from the need for a necessary connection, the resulting gap is arbitrarily filled, then madness is there" (Supplements, ch. 32). "Hole" and "filling" anticipate Freud's account of psychosis, where delusion is "a patch over the place where originally a rent had appeared in the ego's relation to the external world" ("Neurosis and Psychosis", 1924); Henry quotes Schopenhauer so that the Freudian echo is audible without naming it.

This shows clearly what the illness called madness consists in: not in an affection of memory, though memory is disturbed, since the content of memories is falsified and their thread broken; not in a weakening of reason, though reason, the necessary causal order of phenomena, is also disturbed; not in a disorder of sensible intuition, though the perception of the present and surrounding objects may be altered and even overturned in meaning. In truth, despite appearances, the madman's memory, reason and perception remain intact; proof is that these faculties resume their normal functioning as soon as the disturbing principle, which does not reside in them, stops interrupting their play from outside. "One cannot deny madmen reason or understanding: they speak and understand, often reason very correctly, and usually have a quite exact view of what goes on before them and grasp the connection of causes and effects" (§ 36, p. 229). Since conception and intuition of the present are not at issue, it seems that the relation to the past, the past itself, is defective: "madmen are mostly not mistaken in their knowledge of what is immediately present; their ravings always concern what is absent or past, and so only the relation of the absent or past to the present. Hence their illness seems to me to affect chiefly memory" (§ 36). Henry stresses that the disturbing principle lies outside the faculties: the representational apparatus is intact; the disorder comes from a power that is not representation.

But, as shown, it is not the passive capacity to propose memories one after another, memory as a representational faculty, that is abolished, but only the possibility for these potential memories to enter consciousness. The damage is not internal to memory: "it does not abolish it entirely (many madmen know a great deal by heart and sometimes recognize people they have not seen for a long time); rather it breaks the thread of memory, destroys its continuous connection and makes any regularly coordinated recollection of the past impossible" (§ 36). What "breaking the thread" means has also been seen: the absence of a representation, a memory, does not come from a deficiency of memory, unable to supply it, but from a power other than memory forbidding it. The disorder of memory is strictly parallel to those of reason and intuition and explained the same way: always a gap in the whole of representation and its artificial filling, the gap never stemming from representation but from the split in the mind between two heterogeneous faculties, one passive, the other all-powerful, and from the second's power to repel the first, "du pouvoir de la volonté de refouler la représentation" (the will's power to repress representation, p. 229). Schopenhauer's model is already Freud's first topography: an intact representational system and a force that excludes contents from it.

What is the possibility and essence of repression? How can the will forbid a representation access to consciousness? Must it not know this representation to know it is unsuitable and to want to exclude it, represent it, already be conscious of it? But if it is already conscious of it, how can it at the same time not be, or at once cease to be? Where does light, or any principle, get this capacity to abolish itself suddenly? "De telles difficultés affectent toute théorie du refoulement dans une métaphysique de la représentation, c'est-à-dire aussi bien dans une philosophie de l'inconscient" (such difficulties affect every theory of repression within a metaphysics of representation, that is, within a philosophy of the unconscious, p. 230). In Schopenhauer they take this form: how can the will want to repress the inopportune representation when it represents nothing, knows nothing of it? Explaining madness by repression, chapter 32 of the Supplements to Book III said: "if certain events or details are entirely withheld from the intellect because the will cannot bear the sight of them [*weil der Wille ihren Anblick nicht ertragen kann*]". But nothing ever offers any "sight" to a will foreign to all representation. Chapter 19 ran into the same aporia, describing how the will makes the intellect feel its omnipotence "by forbidding it certain representations, certain trains of thought, because it [the will] knows, or rather because the intellect has taught it, that these representations would arouse in it one of the movements ..." (Henry's emphasis). But how could the intellect teach the will that certain representations will arouse aversion in it? How could the will learn anything, it, "la faculté de la nuit" (the faculty of night), blind by principle, determined in its being by *Erkenntnislosigkeit*? Is it not joined to representation in man? But where is the principle of this junction? Where can an absolute power that excludes the structure of representation identify itself with it and benefit from its light (p. 231)? The enigma of repression is identically that of this union, since in repression the blind power that repels the representation unfailingly bears in itself "ce petit savoir en cachette qui appartient toujours à l'inconscient" (that little hidden knowledge which always belongs to the unconscious) and lets it do so subtly all it does. Here the will first sees out of the corner of its eye the representation it will, as Schopenhauer so nicely said, "cover with its hand" so as not to see it any more. This is the classical objection to repression (Sartre's in *Being and Nothingness*, the censor that must know what it represses, i.e., bad faith). Henry formulates it as a structural aporia of representational metaphysics, not as an argument for consciousness: his own solution will be neither Sartre's bad faith nor a conscious censor.

Let us ask essential questions. Why do will, desire, drive repress the representation? "Non point à cause de son contenu représentatif mais de son affectivité" (not because of its representational content but because of its affectivity, p. 231). "If the grief caused by this thought or memory is cruel enough to become absolutely unbearable and exceed the individual's strength, then nature, seized by anguish, resorts to madness as its last resource"; "let us recall with what reluctance we think of things that wound ..." If repression belongs to the basic constitution of the psyche as one of its most constant and deepest laws, it is first because the representation to be repelled, or admitted, is affectively determined; only because of this affectivity is it rejected, and can and must be. A second question inevitably arises: how and why can a representation be affectively determined at all, or, as Freud will say, "invested by an affect"? This question is crucial and prior to any theory of repression, since repression always presupposes the affectivity of the repressed representation and is motivated only by it. Henry's first answer to the aporia: the agent of repression knows what to repress not through representation but through affect. The question "how is a representation affective?" targets the Freudian notion of cathexis (*Besetzung*).

Empirical psychology at once offers its services with its favourite explanatory principle: association. Because a certain representation was linked to a traumatic event, it takes on a certain affective charge capable of provoking its exclusion (p. 232). But if a representation's affectivity comes from association, it is contingent to it, external to its being; some representations may be charged, others not, and repression, far from being a general law of the psyche, the principle of the formation or non-formation of all representational contents, is only a contingent, occasional, locatable mechanism. What this theory lacks, despite its naive evidence and its wealth of examples, is its principled possibility: the possibility by which every representation is originally and necessarily determined as affective, insofar as it presupposes "l'acte proto-fondateur de l'extériorité" (the proto-founding act of exteriority) which, simply in order to be and be accomplished, affects itself and bears in itself the essence of affectivity. That a representation is and can be affective does not come to it a posteriori from a chance encounter with other empirical elements, but a priori from its very arising and constitution. Precisely because every representation is in itself affective can it take on in the course of experience, and seem to owe to experience, an affectivity that experience only modulates. "Si elle désigne autre chose que l'assemblage inconcevable d'éléments hétérogènes et impénétrables ... l'association n'est qu'un nom pour la structure de l'être" (if it designates anything but the inconceivable assemblage of heterogeneous and impenetrable elements, "representation" and "affectivity", association is only a name for the structure of being). Henry's thesis: the act that produces a representation is itself self-affecting; the affect of a representation is the affect of its producing power. Freud's "quota of affect" attached to an idea becomes an ontological necessity, not a contingent cathexis.

Having grounded the affectivity of representation as the precondition of repression, a third question concerns its effectiveness, its passage to act: why does the will repel the representation? Precisely because of its tonality, its unpleasantness, ultimately its intolerability, so that madness is sometimes better than keeping it before consciousness. But a pure power, in itself non-affective, would have no reason to reject any representation, which can be unpleasant or intolerable only for it, insofar as it is itself originally constituted as affective (p. 233). "Let us recall with what reluctance we think of things that wound ..."; "reluctance of the will to let what is contrary to it come into the light of the intellect". So it is never the will as such, pure willing, that excludes the representation, but only "un vouloir préalablement déterminé en soi comme affectif, comme répugnance, blessure, dégoût, honte" (a willing already determined in itself as affective, as reluctance, wound, disgust, shame). The affects Henry lists (disgust, shame) are those Freud names as the "dams" of repression in the *Three Essays*.

"Already" must be heard: it is not, in the end, the will that represses, but only the affectivity in it; affectivity wills or does not will this representation; the movement of willing is only the movement of affectivity itself, and its self-movement, "car ce dont l'affectivité ne veut pas, ce qu'elle tente de repousser de toutes ses forces, c'est elle-même" (for what affectivity does not want, what it tries with all its strength to repel, is itself, p. 233), or some of its determinations. Disgust itself, as unbearable, unable to bear itself and wanting to suppress itself, drives out the incriminated representation and prevents it from forming. In Schopenhauer's economy of the psyche the dependence of willing and affectivity must therefore be reversed: the first does not produce the second; the second arouses the first, the process of repression, from itself and what it experiences, insofar as it can and will no longer experience it. Life always contains the law of its own development and action; they are explained from it and its essence. A last question concerns the relation between the affectivity that represses, that of "willing", and that of the repressed representation, between the will's "reluctance" and the tonality of "the things that wound it". An ultimate evidence imposes itself: "l'affectivité du vouloir et celle de la représentation est la même : une seule et même tonalité, une seule et même détermination de la vie" (the affectivity of willing and that of the representation is the same: one and the same tonality, one and the same determination of life). Since the proto-founding act of exteriority underlying every representation affects itself, the formation of representation always depends on affectivity; the affectivity of the representation is that of the power that forms it, or does not form it. The latter case is repression, whose enigma is now revealed (p. 234). If, to repel a representation from consciousness, one must somehow know it to measure its inopportuneness, since this representation is not formed it cannot instruct us about itself; what lets the representation form or forbids it is not its representational content but its affectivity, "savoir sans représentation, savoir avant la représentation, savoir secret de toute représentation" (knowledge without representation, knowledge before representation, the secret knowledge of all representation), which already knows what it will represent. And we understand this too: the repressed representation (whose formal reality prevents its objective reality) does not exist in some unconscious and does not subsist there as a monstrous psychical entity: "elle n'est pas formée, voilà tout" (it is not formed, that is all). And the unconscious supposed to receive it does not exist either. What remains is a tonality, appearing in the first sphere of being as an accident or unfolding as a habitus. "Point n'est donc besoin pour rendre compte du refoulement de la mythologie des topiques ni de leurs personnages ... le savoir de la vie y suffit" (no need, then, to account for repression, of the mythology of topographies and their characters more or less crudely borrowed from the world of representation; the knowledge of life suffices). This is the most important positive thesis of the book. Henry solves the aporia of the censor: repression is affectivity refusing itself by not forming the representation in which it would be expressed. What persists is not an unconscious representation but an affect. It reverses Freud's doctrine that what is repressed is the representative, while the affect is displaced or transformed; Henry keeps the second half and abolishes the first. The vocabulary of formal and objective reality (Chapter II) returns: the repressed is an idea that has formal reality (it is a mode of life) without objective reality (it represents nothing).

The fundamental significance of affectivity as the elementary determination of the psyche, shown in the analysis of repression, is found in Schopenhauer himself as an implication. Affectivity does not simply result from the will, as his most frequent statements say, nor simply precede it, as when desire proceeds from suffering. The decisive character of tonalities is asserted beyond doubt when they arise and actualize their being while the will is out of play: far from presupposing it, they are accomplished in its absence. A crucial eidetic situation arises in which the essence of the psyche can no longer be defined by the will, which has become a contingent determination, while its affectivity remains as an invariant. This situation is not merely theoretical; it is the *telos* of Schopenhauer's thought, as salvation. Salvation lies in suppressing willing (p. 235), and that it occurs as self-suppression does not change the fact that at the end of the process the will is no longer there. If the will is the essence of life, this state means death. But this is an appearance the whole system aims to unmask: "for those whom the will still animates, what remains after its total suppression is indeed nothing. But conversely, for those in whom the will has turned and denied itself, this very real world of ours, with all its suns and galaxies, is nothing" (§ 71). What makes this state something other than nothingness, what subsists in life when the will is abolished, is affectivity: "infinite bliss in the midst of death", "happy the life of the man whose will is ... completely annihilated". Salvation is always described in affective terms, first in aesthetic experience, where the annihilation of the will goes with the release of positive tonalities, calm, joy, love: because the painter's eye escapes the will, it can absorb itself in objects "to conceive them with so perfect a love". The eidetic variation is Husserlian: remove the will, and affectivity remains; so affectivity, not will, is essential. Schopenhauer's salvation furnishes the experiment.

Schopenhauer's embarrassment is significant: lacking a systematic elaboration of the basic concepts to which his philosophy leads, he is a prisoner of classical categories and sent from one to another, the elimination of the will releasing the possibility of aesthetic pleasure, "pleasure that comes down to the joy that pure knowledge and the paths leading to it give us". Affectivity arises in the will's absence only to be paradoxically referred to knowledge restored to its ecstatic essence, on whose ground no affective tonality ever arises. So Schopenhauer contradicts himself once more. To all the texts in which the positive tonalities of joy, rapture and peace are credited to a gaze finally freed from desire (p. 236), to the explicit definition of salvation by an affectivity having its principle in pure knowledge ("then we behold that peace higher than all reason, that ocean-like calm, that deep repose of the soul, that unshakeable serenity of which Raphael and Correggio have shown us only the reflection in their figures; it is truly the gospel ... only knowledge remains, the will has vanished"), is opposed in the same passage, in the same eidetic situation, the same definition of salvation in which affectivity now excludes knowledge as well as will: "what is called ecstasy, rapture, illumination, union with God, etc.; but strictly speaking this state cannot be called knowledge, since it no longer has the form of object and subject; besides, it belongs only to personal experience" (§ 71). The mystical state "without subject and object" is the immanent revelation of Chapter V's § 22; Schopenhauer's text oscillates between Plato (contemplation) and Eckhart (union).

Two approaches to affectivity thus divide Schopenhauer's thought. How the first, determining life's essential tonalities and their fate from an external principle, inevitably distorts them must be specified, since this improper thematization weighs on all modern thought and lies behind Nietzsche's efforts. Satisfaction and dissatisfaction depend on willing. The stronger it is, the deeper the accompanying dissatisfaction and suffering. In reality, since willing is infinite, dissatisfaction and suffering have no end. Provisional end of desire, satisfaction is provisional too; or rather, on closer inspection, it is impossible, and here the determination of affectivity by a foreign principle shows its absurdity. Satisfaction presupposes desire; but it is also desire's suppression, hence the suppression of its own presupposition, of its own condition. Schopenhauer formulates this series of absurdities: "no satisfaction comes to us of itself; it must be the satisfaction of a desire. Desire, privation, is the preliminary condition of all enjoyment. Now with satisfaction desire ceases (p. 237), and consequently enjoyment too. Hence satisfaction, contentment, can only be deliverance from a pain, a need" (§ 58). Henry formalizes the paradox of hedonism: if pleasure is the cessation of desire and enjoyment requires desire, then enjoyment abolishes itself.

The sophism, which is found in Freud, "surdéterminé par la conception non moins absurde de l'entropie" (overdetermined by the no less absurd conception of entropy, p. 237), lies in the premise. Once affectivity is grafted onto willing and rests on it, once satisfaction is the satisfaction of a desire and possible only as its suppression, it is possible only as its own suppression; it vanishes at the moment it should occur. One must accept this: far from being explained by desire, every satisfaction is made impossible by it. Schopenhauer expresses this principled impossibility by saying that satisfaction is "nothing but negative", and the conclusion says what this means: "satisfaction ... can only be deliverance from a pain, a need". But deliverance from a pain in the sense of its pure interruption, its suppression, as death is deliverance from life, is nothing at all. "Un état affectif négatif, stricto sensu, est un cercle carré" (a negative affective state, strictly speaking, is a square circle). One must not merely say with Hartmann that there are also positive pleasures; one must say that every pleasure, satisfaction, deliverance, every affective tonality whatever, is positive. Its positivity lies in its phenomenality, its affectivity, which constitutes the original essence of all revelation and of all possible being: a tonality is not only what it is; in it each time the first upsurge of being is essentialized, "et il n'y a rien avant elle" (and there is nothing before it). The reversal of Schopenhauer's categories is finally rooted in the essence of being and its innermost structure: far from depending on willing, affectivity grounds it in its being-possible (p. 238), determining it a priori as affective, "comme libido et comme désir" (as libido and as desire). The "square circle" is Henry's verdict on Freud's economic definition of pleasure as tension reduction (Nirvana principle). Every affect is a positive phenomenological presence; the "libido" is a modality of affectivity, not its cause.

No thought can wholly ignore the radical phenomenological significance of affectivity, only falsify it. In Schopenhauer this falsification takes several forms. First, the revealing power of affectivity seems recognized, and in its metaphysical scope (in his sense): it is the revelation of the will, its "wholly immediate feeling". But manifesting the will has two totally different senses. Either affectivity reveals the will as its auto-affection and so constitutes its very being, which unfolds only in and through it; as this radical ontological and phenomenological condition, affectivity defines the will's innermost possibility, an essence outside which no willing exists. Henry states the first sense, his own, before showing that Schopenhauer adopts the second.

But for Schopenhauer it is quite otherwise: affectivity manifests the will, but as other than it, as something not the feeling itself and not contained in it, indeed as something that in itself does not manifest itself. To the inner coincidence of affectivity and willing, the first exhibiting in itself the being of the second as its constitutive auto-affection, is opposed Schopenhauer's split between feeling reduced to a phenomenon, a "phenomenal form", and a will which, far from showing itself in this phenomenon, holds itself outside it, in its night. "La relation de l'affectivité et de la volonté est devenue celle du phénomène kantien et de la chose en soi" (the relation of affectivity and will has become that of the Kantian phenomenon and the thing in itself, p. 238). This is the model of Freud's "drive" (unknowable in itself) and its "affect" (its psychical representative).

So when Schopenhauer writes, of events to which we react affectively without having had or taken time to understand them (p. 239), that "from the depths of the soul, uncalled, the ever-ready will arises" and "manifests itself as fear, dread, hope, joy, desire, envy, sadness, eagerness, anger, fury ...", one must note that these tonalities, exhibiting themselves, do not exhibit the will's being in itself; they are only its sign, its index: their presence means that elsewhere something that does not manifest itself produces them. "Les tonalités sont la fumée qui, s'élevant au-dessus de la maison, donne à penser qu'en celle-ci un feu brûle" (tonalities are the smoke rising above the house that makes one think a fire is burning inside), the nature of the combustion being only a matter of conjecture. Willing is not desire, this drive lived as discomfort that gradually resolves into pleasure or increased suffering. The process of life has doubled: "une pulsion inconsciente s'est éveillée quelque part et l'effectuation vivante du désir n'en est que la traduction phénoménologique toujours suspecte" (an unconscious drive has awakened somewhere, and the living accomplishment of desire is only its always suspect phenomenological translation). The inevitable connection between ignoring affectivity's specific revealing power and the rise of theory, its interpretation from the unconscious will, becomes evident. The smoke and fire is the semiotic model of the symptom: affect as index of a hidden cause. "Always suspect" anticipates the hermeneutics of suspicion.

Conversely, the theory of affects as effects of an unconscious instance increases this ignorance, definitively veiling the radical phenomenological significance of affectivity. It led Schopenhauer to the absurd denial of the very fact of pleasure, its phenomenality. It also leads to the strange assertion that all feelings and all trials will be given to each. Not by an eidetic analysis of affectivity showing them as principled possibilities, in a sense already effective, but again by a transcendent construction: because the (unknown-unconscious) will is infinite and its whole essence is present in each willing, all its effects too, the indefinite series of passions and torments, are inscribed in it as its destiny. Thus the Hindu myth of transmigration is explained (p. 240): if you have willed, if you have killed an animal, whoever and wherever you are, you will one day be killed in turn. Thus, enclosed in reasoning, the myth of the Eternal Return takes shape; a note adds that it is also tied to Schopenhauer's theory of history, in which, under the appearance of the other, the Same is always reproduced. Explaining affectivity, like experience in general, by an instance outside it is the very project of the "Metaphysics of Sexual Love", chapter 44 of the Supplements to Book IV. Love is an appearance whose meta-phenomenological, "metaphysical" ground is the will. Meta-phenomenological determinations of the metaphysical will, unconscious determinations of the unconscious will, will account for the "phenomenon" of love. Now (unconscious determination) "the will desires life absolutely and forever"; it desires it not merely here or there, in a particular individual; it is not "mere instinct of personal preservation" but, since it wills itself eternally and "has in view an infinite series of generations", it is more essentially "sexual instinct". The sexual instinct is thus not one instinct among others; it constitutes, for Schopenhauer, the ground of being, metaphysical reality itself: "the sexual instinct ... is in itself only the will to live". Since will and will-to-live are identical (the first inevitably changing into the second), and so will and sexual instinct, explaining affectivity by the will is identically explaining it by sexuality: "every passion, whatever illusory appearance it takes, has its source in the sexual instinct". However trivial and prosaic this reduction of love to sexuality seems, it is tied not to the positivism of an objectivist, "scientific" psychology but to a prior metaphysics (p. 241): "my conception of love will seem to them too physical, too material, however metaphysical and transcendent it is at bottom", precisely the "Metaphysics of Love", which placed affectivity in appearance in the sense of mere appearance and sought its principle in a *conatus* = x. Here the genealogy of Freud's pansexualism is drawn: the reduction of all passion to sexuality is a metaphysical thesis about the unconscious will, not an empirical finding. The Eternal Return as a "reasoned" transmigration anticipates Chapter VII's reading of Nietzsche against this version.

How this explanation from an unconscious principle distorts affectivity's specific revealing power and finally denies its phenomenological reality is accomplished as follows. The will wills the whole of life at each point of its being, which reduces to the one point where it is exercised, where it wills everything. This infinite will is present in every individual insofar as he wills: "in each individual the whole will-to-live appears". The individual's will is not, or not only, his own, seeking his preservation, ends and personal pleasure. More fundamentally, it is the whole will that wills everything, the infinite series of generations, the sexual will or will of the species. From the gap between these two wills arises an illusion: believing he pursues his own aims and selfish pleasure, the individual actually pursues the species' aims, those of the infinite will as such. Indeed this will itself produces the illusion in the individual, since, accomplished in him, it must lead him to accomplish its own aims: "nature can attain its end only by producing in the individual a certain illusion, by which he regards as a personal advantage what is really an advantage only for the species, so that he works for the species while imagining he works for himself". "This illusion is instinct", Schopenhauer declares, because instinct "makes the individual act for the good of the species". Or rather, since the sexual instinct, identical with the will, is unconscious like it (p. 242), the illusion is really its phenomenological effect on individual subjectivity, namely love, a "stratagem" of nature, since in the passion in which the individual seeks "his personal enjoyment" what is really at stake is only the procreation of a new individual, as well-constituted as possible. The "ruse of the species" is the template for Freud's account of the drive using the ego for ends not its own; Henry highlights the structure "illusion produced by the unconscious", the matrix of the theory of rationalization.

So men desire women with large breasts because through their gaze the "Genius of the species" looks and says to itself: well, well, the little one will be well fed. So blonds love brunettes, short men tall women, and vice versa; each unknowingly seeks in the other the correction of his own defects, complementary traits whose union is meant to produce the individual most conformed to the ideal type prescribed by the species. Love is the anticipated enjoyment of an infinite bliss the lover believes he will find in the beloved's arms, which the will dangles before him as a lure, this enjoyment being the presentation to consciousness of the willing by which the will undertakes to realize itself in the species. That is how, "seeking not its own interest but that of a third yet to be born", love is blind, how, explained by the will, affectivity in general is an illusion, its revealing power "non pas altéré ni méconnu, mais nié proprement et renversé" (not altered or misunderstood but properly denied and reversed, p. 242). Henry's comic paraphrase ("tiens, tiens!") makes the point by reductio: the explanation of feeling by an unconscious teleology is a just-so story.

What does affectivity's revealing power consist in when it is so reversed? In no way in itself, in its affectivity (so neither affectivity nor its revealing power is really at issue in this discussion, as in all classical and modern philosophy; neither is noticed), but in a conscious aim, a representation: "all love is grounded in an instinct aiming solely at the child to be procreated" (p. 243). This aim, the supposedly real aim of all love, is the species'. Opposed to it is the individual's aim, in which he believes he pursues his personal enjoyment. This aim is illusory; this representation is false. "Here truth has taken the form of an illusion in order to act on the will." The truth: that sexuality aims at the perpetuation and excellence of the species. The illusion: that it aims at the individual's enjoyment. The interpretation-explanation of affectivity from the will means and presupposes complete ignorance of affectivity's proper revealing power, its reduction to that of the will, or rather, the will being blind, to that of the representation linked to it in individual consciousness. Only at the price of this reduction, of its confusion with representation, can affectivity be declared illusory. "Car il n'y a pas d'illusion possible du sentiment lui-même, lequel est toujours par principe ce qu'il est" (for there is no possible illusion of feeling itself, which is always by principle what it is), since its being lies in its phenomenality, identical with its affectivity. The principle of *Passions* art. 26 (Chapter I) is turned against Schopenhauer: only representations can be false; feelings cannot be illusory. Freud's "false connection" of affect and idea is an error of the idea, never of the affect.

A new consequence appears: ignoring affectivity's specific revealing power as original and absolute leads to questioning the very reality of feeling, which is no longer an absolute, the unshakeable term on which every interpretation attached to it breaks, but an uncertain, undetermined being whose place is no longer assignable, "dont on ne sait même plus de qui il est le sentiment" (of which one no longer even knows whose feeling it is, p. 243). Since affectivity no longer rests on itself and no longer determines, in this rest in itself, the site and essence of an absolute subjectivity, but is explained from a willing as its effect outside it, the problematic faces an inextricable situation. For there is not one willing but, so to speak, two, the individual's and the species', and two series of tonalities result: mediocre feelings commensurate with the limited being projecting only its own preservation, and (p. 244) infinite feelings aroused by an infinite willing, the feelings of love: "the passion of love attaches the idea of endless happiness to the possession of a particular woman and that of unspeakable pain to the thought of not possessing her; this longing and pain of a lover's heart cannot have as their sole material the needs of an ephemeral individual; they are the sighs of joy of the Genius of the species when it succeeds in seizing a unique opportunity to realize its designs, or its deep groans when it loses one. The species alone has eternal life and alone is therefore capable of eternal wishes, eternal satisfactions and eternal pains" (Supplements, ch. 44). But when, the act of generation accomplished, love's vertigo ends and each lover feels "that prodigious disappointment" that undoes the illusion and returns him to himself, his own subjectivity replaces, "dans une transsubstantiation fantastique" (in a fantastic transubstantiation), that of the Genius of the species; under each feeling an ontological abyss opens. The question "whose feeling is it?" is the decisive objection: an affect attributed to the species is no one's, which is a contradiction, since affect is ipseity. Freud's "id" (*Es*) inherits the problem.

The reduction of affectivity's specific revealing power to that of representational knowledge is constant in Schopenhauer, despite occasional attempts to separate intellectual and affective understanding (it is in any case a matter of "understanding") and despite the final remark that there are "two paths of deliverance", one through "pure knowledge of suffering", the other through "suffering directly undergone". The examination of the tonalities that play a decisive role in the System is generally falsified in principle: a meaning joined to them synthetically in the light of ecstasis inevitably replaces the essence of their own phenomenality. Remorse is indeed a tonality, but it proceeds from knowledge, the metaphysical knowledge of the nature of things in me, of this blind, obstinate will I have not yet managed to escape (p. 245): "remorse ... is a grief arising from the knowledge one acquires of one's own nature in itself, that is, as will; it presupposes the clear insight that one has not ceased to be this same will". Likewise shame is shame at the act of generation, of which human life is "the paraphrase", at the body as objectification and focus of willing, at the will; it is reduced to knowledge, that of "the riddle of the world": "the shame aroused by the act of generation extends even to the parts that serve it ... proof that not only actions but even the human body can be regarded as the phenomenal form, the objectification and work of one's will". Sadness, for a last example, "proceeds from the disinterested consciousness of the vanity of all goods and the nothingness of all pains". Each affect is defined by a cognitive content; this is the "cognitive theory of emotions" Henry rejects, and the reason Freud's affects are always "about" a representation.

Bound to knowledge and more or less confused with it, affectivity depends on the *principium individuationis*, which constitutes a decisive dividing line for knowledge. There are two kinds of knowledge in Schopenhauerianism, that which succumbs to this principle and that which escapes it. This line is identically that of all our feelings, divided into those deceived by the illusion of individuality and those that overcome it, the deceived ones being so on the ground of representation and its illusion, the others overcoming it only through the action in them of a gaze able to pierce the *principium individuationis*. Cruelty belongs to the first kind: seeking in the sight of another's suffering a lessening of his own, or even pleasure, the cruel man believes his feeling differs from his victim's, indeed opposes it (p. 246). Whereas, proceeding from one essence and produced by it, all feelings are finally identical, their distribution among apparently different individuals and so their difference being only illusion. The "identity of all feelings" is the metaphysical basis of Schopenhauer's ethics of pity; Henry has already shown (Chapter V) that egoism and cruelty require real individuals, so this identity contradicts the phenomenology of affect.

Schopenhauer gave a striking theory of this illusion. One whose eyes are covered by the veil of Maya "does not see the essence of things, which is one; he sees their appearances, distinct, divided, innumerable. He takes joy for one reality and pain for another; he sees in one man a torturer and murderer, in another a sufferer and victim; he places crime here and suffering there ..." (§ 63). Thus, within this grand conception, the theory of affectivity is falsified by the principle of individuation, that is, by its reduction to knowledge. Precisely because the specific phenomenality of affectivity, consisting in this affectivity itself, is ignored or rather explicitly denied, the reality of tonalities is denied too: joy is no longer defined by itself, "elle n'est plus de la joie, elle n'est plus une réalité" (it is no longer joy, no longer a reality, p. 246). It is not different from pain, which is not pain either, not a reality other than joy, and the torturer is not separable from his victim. This reversal of the order of things, which Nietzsche will restore with extreme violence ("the strong", "the weak"), appears in the same way in the theory of the tonalities belonging to the second kind of knowledge. Here again it is knowledge, "the vision of the Ideas", which "pierces the principle of individuation through and through", that holds the revealing power attributed to gentleness, *caritas*, holiness and mysticism and finally constitutes their whole reality. The torturer-victim identity is the ethical consequence of denying the reality of affects: if suffering is an appearance, cruelty is too. Nietzsche's typology of the strong and the weak is introduced as the reassertion of the reality and singularity of affect.

Not recognized and delimited in its specificity, the revealing power of affectivity is finally wholly occulted: affectivity falls under the will, under whose concept it is most often understood (p. 247). Like the will, it is reduced to the condition of the known, not the knowing, every conceivable revealing power being, as in the tradition, explicitly referred to ecstasis and the mode of knowledge that draws its possibility from it. The theory of consciousness, of manifestation in general, shows this: "self-consciousness contains a knowing and a known element ... As the known element in self-consciousness we find exclusively the will. For not only volition and resolution, but every striving, desire, aversion, hope, fear, love, hatred, in short everything that directly constitutes happiness or suffering ... Now in all knowledge the known, not the knowing, part is the first and essential element ... In consciousness, then, the will, the known element, is first and essential; the knowing subject is the secondary part, added on, the mirror" (Supplements, ch. 19). Affectivity is thus referred, through the will, to essentiality only to be definitively deprived of revealing power and to fall, as in classical thought, to the rank of a blind facticity. The final quotation sums up the chapter's diagnosis: Schopenhauer gives affect primacy as the known, never as the knowing. Freud will inherit exactly this: the affective id is primary, but only as what consciousness (the ego, "the mirror") must come to know.

Despite these inadequacies, *The World as Will and Representation*, by placing affectivity at the centre of its themes, opens to modern thought the way of life, making possible the decisive progress that will be accomplished by the one who would say, even in his last writings: "my great teacher Schopenhauer" (p. 247). The chapter closes on Nietzsche, the subject of Chapter VII.

## Chapter VII. Life and Affectivity according to Nietzsche

The two essential characters of life, immanence and affectivity, are what Nietzsche's thought thinks through to the end, even if it does not thematize them explicitly but lets itself be carried by them, abandoning itself to their implications. Nietzsche's concept of life is borrowed from Schopenhauer and at first has only an ontic meaning. Life is will, but will is the essence of what is, its mode of being and, in this "metaphysical" sense, being itself. By the same process as in Schopenhauer, this will, first grasped in us as identical with our life, extends its reign to the whole world, insofar as we conceive it not as a representational velleity but as an effective power and the essence of all power. Wherever in nature we find marks of it, effects, we perceive at work the same force that is unleashed in us: every process expressing energy is a manifestation of this will which, as the form of all possible real energy, is called will to power: "if nothing is 'given' as real except our world of desires and passions, if we can rise or descend to no other reality than that of our drives ... may we not ask whether this given does not suffice to understand, from what resembles it, the so-called mechanical (or material) world (p. 250) ... as a reality of the same order as our passions? ... In short, we must suppose that wherever we recognize 'effects', will acts on will, that every mechanical process, insofar as it manifests energy, is precisely volitional energy, an effect of will. Supposing finally that this hypothesis suffices to explain our whole instinctual life ... that we could reduce all organic functions to this will to power ... we would then have the right to call all active energy will to power. The world seen from within, the world defined and designated by its 'intelligible character', would be 'will to power' and nothing else" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 36). Henry begins from the aphorism Heidegger made the basis of his reading of Nietzsche as metaphysician; he accepts its structure (inner experience as access to the essence of all) but will reinterpret the "within".

Nietzsche's will differs from Schopenhauer's in being qualified as "power". What power is, the text says: causality, true causality, efficacious, power in its effective accomplishment, real action, force: "in the end the question is whether we consider the will as really acting, whether we believe in the causality of the will. If so, and at bottom our belief in it is nothing but our belief in causality itself ..." Nietzsche's critique of causality thus touches only the rational representation of causality as a system of laws imposing order and rule on Becoming. But for one who grasps Becoming's essence "from within", causality is nothing but this absolute causality identical with the real exercise of a force, power as power in act (p. 250). Henry separates Nietzsche's critique of causal law (directed at representation) from his affirmation of felt efficacy (Biran's "feeling of effort" as the origin of the idea of cause).

But in Schopenhauer too the will is true causality, the sole power whose irruption makes up the sole reality. If the will is without cause, it is because it is true causality, and an absolute causality depends on nothing, drawing from itself the energy to produce all it does. Schopenhauer puts us on the way to understanding (p. 251) that there is no possible force in or through the world of representation, that the being of force can come to it neither from anything else nor from its own exteriority to itself, but only from itself. That is why, for Schopenhauer as for Nietzsche, only in the interiority of a force, "in us", can what force is, and the whole universe insofar as the being of every being is force, be apprehended and grasped, "given", as Nietzsche says. "Given" (*gegeben*) in Nietzsche's aphorism is read as Henry's "givenness" of life to itself.

Yet between Schopenhauer's will and the will to power there is much more than a gap: the first, as shown, is enigmatically affected by a lack, being desire for a being it does not have, indeed that is not; whereas, as Heidegger saw, the will to power is not the will for a power it lacks, toward which it would merely tend. If it were, how could this will, separate from power, ever reach it, or first launch itself toward it? By what power would it set itself in motion? "Le point de départ, dans la volonté de puissance, c'est la puissance elle-même" (the starting point of the will to power is power itself, p. 251). "Will" designates only the expansion of this power, its deployment, possible in it, from it and by it: its self-movement. Henry agrees with Heidegger (*Nietzsche* I, "The Will to Power as Art") that will to power is not a striving for power; it is power's self-enhancement. The disagreement will be about whether this self-enhancement is ecstatic.

The "difference" between the two wills shows on the plane of affectivity, because in and through tonalities these wills enter the phenomenality that makes them effective. From Nietzsche's first writings, notably *The Birth of Tragedy*, the affective specificity of the will identical with life is recognizable. While Schopenhauer's will, as unquenchable desire, was the endless torment he described pathetically, Nietzsche, without denying the tragic character of existence, recognizing it rather as the ground of the Greek soul, juxtaposes to this desire and misery from the start, as perhaps their condition, a greater pleasure, "this eternal pleasure of existence" of which Dionysian art would persuade us (p. 252). Schopenhauer's pessimism certainly persists in a vision that ties decline and death to everything born and still celebrates "the terrors of individual existence", seeing in art a provisional salvation, "a metaphysical consolation": "for brief moments we really are the primordial being itself and feel its unbridled desire and joy in existence" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 17). Yet pleasure emerges contemporaneous with the primordial being, and desire surpasses itself in it. The "eternal joy in existence" is the affective counterpart of the Eternal Return, as Henry will argue.

More, and more explicit: the whole protean universe of becoming and annihilation, with its procession of torments, appears rather as the consequence of a limitless intoxication, that of life's superabundance, toward which press the many forms it calls into existence and which reveal, through the very play of their birth and death, the overflowing fertility of the power that engenders them. Hence "the furious sting of these torments pierces us at the very moment when we have become, as it were, one with the immeasurable primordial joy in existence, and when, in Dionysian ecstasy, we sense the indestructibility and eternity of this joy; when, in spite of fear and pity, we are the happy living beings" (§ 17). In Henry's reading the torments are a consequence of superabundance, not of lack: the exact reversal of Schopenhauer.

That life reveals itself no longer simply as the vanity of objectless desire and its eternal suffering but, through it, as the pleasure and intoxication of an indestructible power, is not a passing assertion but the revelation of tragedy, which, removing all that separates man from his essence, leads him back to it, to "the thought that life, at the bottom of things, despite all the changes of appearances, is indestructibly powerful and pleasurable" (§ 7). Because it expresses this indestructible power of life, wholly resolved into the pure essence of pleasure, the satyr chorus bursts into tragedy, the chorus of "natural beings who live ineradicably, as it were, behind all civilization and remain eternally the same despite the changes of generations and the history of nations" (p. 253). The satyr chorus "behind all civilization" anticipates the id, timeless and unchanging; Henry reads it as life's immanence, not as an unconscious.

Nietzsche's positive determination of life has a meaning that must be recognized now. The opposition between the misery of an existence that is a lack nothing can fill and the immeasurable primordial pleasure of existing, as experience of life's superabundance and super-power, does not set two conceptions of life side by side to be chosen between: one marked by the absolute pessimism to be called nihilism, the other not optimistic but sufficiently differentiated as a "pessimism of strength". Still less does it produce two contradictory evaluations of the life of suffering, one saying no to it and seeking concrete modes of this negation, the other saying yes, taking in at a glance the totality of this suffering and its eternal return. If there is one decisive evidence on which Schopenhauer's and Nietzsche's philosophies agree, it is "l'impossibilité éidétique pour la vie de prendre position à l'égard de soi" (the eidetic impossibility for life to take a position toward itself, p. 253), to separate from itself in order then to will or not will to be itself, to recover itself and coincide with itself again. Henry rejects the standard reading of Nietzsche's yes-saying as an evaluative attitude toward life; life cannot stand at a distance from itself to evaluate it. This governs his reading of the Eternal Return below.

Only once was Schopenhauer unfaithful to this ultimate prescription: when, dissociating life and will and conceiving the first as the objectification of the second, he had to make willing a mere "will-to-live", an aspiration to give itself being and life outside itself, whereas what is not can do nothing, not even aspire to being, and whereas nothing is more repugnant to life than being outside itself, life resting wholly in itself and, one with itself and in possession of itself, unable to desire itself.
