Here Nietzsche harshly corrects Schopenhauer in essential lines of *Zarathustra* (p. 254): "he certainly did not hit upon the truth who shot at it with the formula of the 'will to existence': that will does not exist! For what is not cannot will; but what is in existence, how could it still will existence? Only where there is life is there also will" ("On Self-Overcoming"). The *Zarathustra* text is Henry's proof text against Schopenhauer's "lack": a living being cannot desire life because it already is life.

The radical refutation of the will-to-live thesis, specific and inessential in Schopenhauer himself, means something quite other than the external designation of life and being as will to power; it concerns the very status of life and being, their essence, and so the status and essence of the will to power itself, insofar as it is and can claim to define life. What matters for a fundamental ontology is not that life is will to power (why not simple willing?), but what makes the will to power living, what lets it construct structurally and constitute by its own means, if it can, the essence of being and life. Nietzsche says two things. Aphorism 693 of *The Will to Power* declares it "the innermost essence of being", giving the analysis of the will to power the task of disclosing the essence of being. The *Zarathustra* text says instead that will is possible only on the ground of being ("what is not cannot will"), that the site of willing is not its own but exclusively that of life: "only where there is life is there will". From this apparent contradiction: a single problematic, taking the will to power as theme, is guided only by the aim of elucidating the essence of being as such and its own truth (p. 255). Why does such a problematic, ontological in a radical sense, deal with will and, overdetermining Schopenhauer's approach, feel obliged to correct it by making will more rigorously a will to power? Henry claims Nietzsche for "fundamental ontology", but in a sense opposed to Heidegger's: the being of the will to power is life.

Let us not forget that in Schopenhauer the will is not desire as a mere velleity cut off from reality. As shown, it is the original body, not the represented body, the body-object of philosophical tradition, but the real body, the body of real movements and real bodily accomplishments, and that these accomplishments, those of desire for instance, reach no true "satisfaction" and leave us hungry changes nothing about their reality or that of the body. With the reality of this body Schopenhauer thinks nothing less than being itself, which is why he defines being, being in itself and not its representation, as will. Schopenhauer's will is never distinct from power; it is power, all real power in the world gathered in the one essence where it is concentrated and possible. More, the whole power of this power is concentrated in each of its points and determinations, whence the dizzying character of its action and the hallucinatory character of Schopenhauer's universe. Henry defends Schopenhauer against Nietzsche's own correction: his will was already power.

What then does Nietzsche's addition of "power" to this will, already power through and through and all-powerful, meeting nothing other than itself as obstacle and having as differential element only representation, which it bends like a toy, mean? That whatever the degree of this power and mode of its action, whatever the intensity and range of the force it unleashes (and here Schopenhauer was doubtless far more radical than Nietzsche), power and force must first be, and this being consists in the prior, presupposed power by which power and force have already seized themselves and their own essence, by which they are. "Volonté de puissance veut dire, dans une perspective nietzschéenne, puissance de la volonté" (will to power means, in a Nietzschean perspective, the power of the will, p. 255): not merely that will is power and, as Schopenhauer brilliantly announced, body, but the more fundamental fact (p. 256) that all power, all force and the body itself are only through the work of a more original power that throws them into themselves and compels them to be. Only in this power of the origin do power and force have licence to unfold. The inversion "will to power = power of the will" designates the self-givenness of power that precedes any exercise; in *L'essence de la manifestation* this is the passivity of auto-affection.

Supposing there are degrees of power, "quanta of force", and that from their crossings and conflicts arise internal modifications of these forces, the power by which they are and in which, despite their vicissitudes, they remain knows no degree or quantity, no increase or decrease, no modification or alteration: it is "l'hyperpuissance omniprésente et omnipuissante en toute puissance" (the hyper-power omnipresent and all-powerful in every power, p. 256), delivering each to itself so that it can be what it is. In every power: the weakest as the strongest. No force, however insignificant, does not bring with it the immeasurable of this hyper-power, which is a measure for no force and can be measured by none, being in each before it is exercised, takes and gives its measure: the irresistibility of the bond by which it seizes itself. That this hyper-power prior to all power is not drawn from it by abstraction appears in that it lies at the ground of its "experience" and makes it possible. Hyper-power does not make possible our experience of the world's forces; it is not what gives us access to them, but only what lets each force reach itself in the unconditionality of a coherence with itself where there is nothing but it, nothing of the world, "mais seulement un Soi, le soi-même de cette force et de ce qu'elle fait" (only a Self, the itself of this force and of what it does). "Hyper-power" sets Henry against the quantitative reading of Nietzsche (Deleuze's *Nietzsche and Philosophy*, with its active and reactive quanta): differences of force presuppose the same self-givenness in every force, the strong and the weak alike. The point anticipates the critique of Freud's economic "quantities".

The unconditionality of the force's coherence with itself, reaching itself as a force that is a "self", does not befall it afterward; it is the condition of its arising and birth, that by which every force (p. 257) comes to life. Here the decisive event of modern philosophy must be considered: with Schopenhauer and Nietzsche being explicitly receives for the first time the meaning of life. Interpreting being as life implies no forgetting of being. Far from reducing being to a mode of being of a privileged being, the determination of force as living force sets before thought the irresistible and unconditional embrace with itself of what arises originally in itself, grows from itself and, in the intoxication of this self-experience and self-growth, which neither begins nor ends, is Life, what the young Nietzsche calls "the immeasurable and eternal pleasure of existing", in mythical language: Dionysus. The elaborated concept of the will to power, this original self-growth included in the unconditional embrace with itself, the "more" as the "richer in itself" that alone determines the "need of itself", is the very concept of being understood at bottom as life. "La vie ne trouve pas sa condition dans l'être ... elle en est l'accomplissement phénoménologique initial et ainsi la condition" (life does not find its condition in being; it is being's initial phenomenological accomplishment and so its condition). But life's primitive coming into itself, growing from itself and experiencing itself in the intoxication of this growth, is immanence. The phrase "implies no forgetting of being" answers Heidegger's charge that Nietzsche thinks being as the beingness of beings (will to power as the last metaphysics). For Henry the interpretation of being as life is the overcoming of the forgetting, not its consummation.

Nietzsche thought the immanence of life in many ways and figures whose power must be recognized. But immanence is affirmed immediately in the crucial, repeated proposition that life is forgetting. To forget is not to think of. To forgetting one opposes remembering, which consists in thinking of what one did not think of. But forgetting and remembering are opposed only in thought, as two modes of it, the first negative, meaning that thought does not yet turn toward what it turns toward in remembering. That life is forgetting Nietzsche says in a quite different sense (p. 258). For life, forgetting is not thinking of, not by distraction or some removable disposition, but because it does not bear in itself the essence in which the possibility of thinking of something in general, of remembering for instance, lies. Life is forgetful by nature as immanence, which insurmountably expels ecstasis and so every possible form of thought. Nietzsche represents life as immanent in the figure of the animal that runs through his work, rightly, if the aim is to express the absence of thought, which traditionally defines man's humanity and specifies him as rational animal. By an eidetic necessity the animal, insofar as it figures the essence of life, which excludes thought, is determined in its being by forgetting: man, "this necessarily forgetful animal ..." (*Genealogy of Morals*, II, 1). Forgetting is the obverse of remembering in the economy of representation; Nietzsche's "forgetting" is a different thing, the immanence of life. This distinction will dismantle Freud's "forgetting" in Chapter IX.

Because the forgetting that belongs to life by principle formulates life's radical immanence and its rejection of the ecstatic dimension in which all thinking-of moves, the possibility for such forgetting to change into its opposite, remembering, a possibility self-evident as long as the first is secretly homogeneous with the second (both modes of thought), no longer exists. Then it is by external intervention, "à coups de trique" (with blows of the cudgel), that this life, incapable of it in itself, must be given not the capacity (it does not have it and never will) but the habit of remembering. By habit a behaviour is acquired that corresponds to no prior inner disposition and is in no way its actualization. This forced acquisition, imposed on life against its essence, Nietzsche names: breeding (*Züchtung*, *dressage*), raising a constellation of concepts grouped around animality whose inner coherence covers a prescription of essence. Because this essence proscribes the ecstatic Dimension of all thinking-of (p. 259) and so the play-space of remembering, forgetting does not come from the weakening of an unused faculty but from the structure of life and its unconditional willing. Together with this Dimension, everything we call consciousness, every form of representedness, is put out of play, and this putting out of play is forgetting. "Forgetting", writes Nietzsche forcefully, "is no mere *vis inertiae* ... it is rather an active and in the strictest sense positive faculty of repression, responsible for the fact that what we experience and absorb ... does not enter consciousness" (II, 1). What stands outside the Dimension, short of it, is therefore not nothing, nothing unconscious in any case; it is the whole of our experiences, the totality of what we live, and it is insofar as we live it that we must live it outside consciousness and in forgetting. Forgetting makes life possible, not in the sense that by removing cares it lets us go forward unimpeded by remorse, but as the condition of possibility of life as the inner gathering by which it coheres with itself in self-growth; it is the force prior to all force, the power of all power and, as this ultimate condition identical with life's essence, what Nietzsche calls health: "this necessarily forgetful animal, in whom forgetting represents a force, a form of robust health". That is why forgetting is the "doorkeeper of psychical order" (*Türwärterin der seelischen Ordnung*): what builds from within, delimits and preserves the essence of the psyche, the original dimension, heterogeneous to all consciousness, where being is essentialized as life. Nietzsche's *aktives Hemmungsvermögen* is translated in French as "faculté d'inhibition active"; the English "faculty of repression" (Kaufmann) shows how close this text is to Freud. Henry reads it as the opposite of Freudian repression: what is not conscious is lived, not stored.

How can what is forgetful by essence remember, give itself the possibility of interiorizing all that is by referring to it in the thinking-of that snatches it from time and annihilation? This is the paradox Nietzsche inevitably meets and that forces him to test his ultimate theses once more. It opens the second essay of the *Genealogy* (p. 260): "to breed an animal with the right to make promises", that is, to remember, at the very time when the essence of life excludes thought. Remarkably, to cut this Gordian knot of the eidos Nietzsche resorts to violence. To the "force of forgetting" expressing the essence's will, he opposes not mere memory, which as a representational faculty is neither included in nor permitted by this essence, but a will to memory, "an active will not to rid oneself [of the promise], a will that goes on willing what it once willed, really a memory of the will" (II, 1), which as will proceeds from life itself, not from ecstasis. Will is here only a name for life; it refers to its innermost possibility and suddenly reveals it. § 3 of the second essay refers every faculty of memorization, as will to memory, to affectivity and roots it explicitly there: "if something is to stay in the memory it must be burned in: only that which never ceases to hurt stays in the memory". As always when it discovers life's ground, affectivity, suffering, Nietzsche's text catches fire, the images crackle, the conflagrations of history are summoned, each proof is a blaze, some monstrous torture or unimaginable suffering we are invited to relish. What it took for man to make himself a memory: "tortures, martyrdoms, the most horrible sacrifices and vows ... the most repulsive mutilations ... the cruellest rituals". And for the German: "the most terrible means ... stoning ... breaking on the wheel ... impaling, tearing apart, trampling by horses, boiling the criminal in oil ... flaying ... cutting flesh". Everywhere pain is "the most powerful aid to mnemonics"; suffering takes the place of thought and ultimately grounds it. "Mais ces remarques sont prématurées" (but these remarks are premature, p. 260). The thesis that memory is grounded in pain, and so in affect, will return in Chapter IX against Freud's mnemic traces. Henry's stylistic comment on Nietzsche's text is ironic but not dismissive: the vehemence marks the place where the text touches life.

Prior to all force, power of all power, life's unconditional coherence in itself is the condition (p. 261) of their being set in motion, the condition of all possible action. Consider the figure of the birds of prey swooping on the lambs to devour them (*Genealogy*, I, 13). This difficult but essential analysis presents itself, in its apparent content, as a critique of morality, Nietzsche-style. It refutes the argument by which the lambs try to save their lives by condemning the birds' action, a condemnation they need only get the birds to share in order to be spared. The lambs' argument rests wholly on presupposing a doubling of force, its separation from itself, the denial of its radical immanence. This doubling, which Nietzsche calls in turn an illusion of language, of the people and of science, consists in a split, instituted in every action, between a subject able to determine itself or not to this action and the action itself as the effect of this subject's free will; for example, between lightning and its flash. "The popular mind separates the lightning from its flash and takes the latter for an action, for the operation of a subject called lightning ... it doubles the deed ... The scientists do no better when they say 'force moves, force causes', and the like" (I, 13). The parable is read not morally but ontologically: the doubling of force is ecstasis applied to action.

When affectivity and its fundamental determinations intervene, the lambs' hatred and vengeance, they seize on this fictitious separation of force from itself in order to evaluate in its light the being and doing of the birds of prey, regarding them as neutral substrates, subjects free to exercise their force or not, free to be birds of prey or not. In this freedom to be or not be birds of prey, in this freedom of force not to be force, not to eat them, lies the lambs' salvation. But neither force nor life in general possesses this freedom not to be itself. "La non-liberté, l'impossibilité de ne pas être soi, telle est l'essence qui régit la relation de la vie à elle-même" (non-freedom, the impossibility of not being oneself, is the essence governing life's relation to itself, p. 261), a relation (p. 262) constitutive of life, its self-experience in the irresistibility of the bond that ties it to itself to be forever what it is, its eternity, what Nietzsche calls "the eternal return of the Same". This is Henry's central interpretive claim about Nietzsche: the Eternal Return names the non-freedom of life toward itself, auto-affection's being riveted to itself (Chapter VI's "riveted to itself"), not a cosmological doctrine or an ethical test.

As the irresistibility of the bond delivering life to itself, non-freedom is the hyper-power in which being gathers and seizes itself in the original self-experience that makes it life. As non-freedom, being's hyper-power is identically its powerlessness, life's ultimate inability to rid itself of itself. § 13 of the first essay thinks this ultimate powerlessness rigorously. It is a matter neither of birds of prey nor of lambs. Nor does it mean only that they are not free to do other than they do, but more essentially that they cannot be other than they are. They are so only on the ground of being in them, insofar as being's structure is non-freedom, its insurmountable passivity toward itself, the inability to rid itself of itself of what coheres in itself as life. "Ce qui ne peut se défaire de soi, c'est le Soi" (what cannot rid itself of itself is the Self, p. 262). That by which each thing is, is identically that by which it is what it is, by which it is a Self and so life. "La structure unitaire de l'être, de l'identité, de l'ipséité et de la vie, c'est la structure de la subjectivité absolue" (the unitary structure of being, identity, ipseity and life is the structure of absolute subjectivity). Being, identity, selfhood and life are unified: identity is not a logical relation but life's inability to escape itself.

The text of § 13 presents itself as a critique of "the subject or, more popularly, the soul", and it has been naively taken as such. Which "subject" is Nietzsche questioning, which "soul", its "popular" substitute? Nietzsche refuses the doubling of force, asserting that there is no neutral substrate free to manifest its force or not: "there is no such substratum; there is no 'being' behind doing, effecting, becoming; 'the doer' is merely a fiction added to the deed, the deed is everything. The people double the deed" (I, 13). The subject is the substrate under the phenomenon, the lightning-subject under the flash, only through the doubling of action, which, as being's doubling from itself (p. 263), as op-position to itself, posits it twice: as phenomenon, opposed, ob-ject, and as the subject by which the ob-ject is ob-ject. "Nietzsche ne critique pas le « sujet », il critique une interprétation de l'essence de ce sujet, à savoir l'interprétation de l'essence de la subjectivité en tant qu'ek-stasis" (Nietzsche does not criticize the "subject"; he criticizes an interpretation of its essence, the interpretation of the essence of subjectivity as ecstasis). That force rests on no subject placed under it, that there is nothing outside it, beyond or short of it, that it cannot withdraw behind itself to present itself to itself and so stand before itself, that it rests in itself and there is nothing but it ("the deed is everything"), is its immanence. This is Henry's answer to the poststructuralist Nietzsche (Foucault, Deleuze) who reads § 13 as the death of the subject: Nietzsche abolishes the represented subject, the substrate, and so frees the immanent self.

The immanence of force is what compels it to be itself and so to act. Because of this immanence the will, Schopenhauer's and Nietzsche's name for force, cannot not will and, as § 1 of the third essay says, "rather than will nothing, it wills nothingness". Life's immanence explains nearly all its figures in Nietzsche's work and their properties, for instance the "egoism" of "the noble soul": the mode of being and acting of what is "everything" insofar as, gathered in itself, it is all it is and all there is, enclosing nothing else. From the non-freedom of this being in itself with itself, which underlies Nietzsche's critique of free will and freedom in general, comes the mechanical bearing of its action, its resemblance to the order of things, its naive way of being itself, but also, in the unsurpassable fullness of what surpasses itself only from itself and so touches every point of its being and fills everything, being in the perfection of its accomplishment, justice itself. "The noble soul accepts the fact of its egoism ... it sees in it ... something that may be grounded in the primordial law of things: if it sought a name for it, it would say 'it is justice itself' ... it moves among its equals ... with the same assurance of modesty and delicate reverence that it has in relating to itself (p. 264), according to an innate heavenly mechanics understood by all stars ... every star is egoistic in this way" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 265). "Rather will nothingness than not will" is the formula of nihilism; Henry reads it as proof that the will cannot escape willing, i.e., cannot escape itself. Noble "egoism" is ipseity, not selfishness.

Resting on itself, coinciding with itself, exhausting its being in itself, drawing from itself all it is, force in its deployment comes only from itself and constantly assures itself of what it does in this self-effecting. Having nothing outside itself, nothing beyond or short of itself and its immanence, it ignores everything implying any transcendence, the space of a difference, every ground as other in the alterity of this difference, every reason, cause, pretext, justification or legitimation, everything that would precede it and, proceeding from extrinsic consideration, borrow its possibility from the world of representation, calculation, intention, promise and foresight. What differs from it, not existing in it or in its action, has no position to take toward it either, in praise or blame, love or hatred. Speaking of "the strong", that is, of force, Nietzsche poetically expresses this condition of its action, not a psychological trait but the structure of being: "they come like fate, without cause, reason, consideration or pretext; they appear as lightning appears, too terrible, too sudden, too convincing, too 'different' even to be hated" (*Genealogy*, II, 17). "Self-assurance" here replaces Descartes' and Heidegger's "security" (Chapter III): force is certain of itself without representation.

Force, effecting itself in itself and holding its effecting from itself, is therefore at the same time wholly unable to account for itself through the external approach of a self-representation, foreign by principle to its being. "L'action n'est ainsi possible que pour autant qu'elle est incomprise et incompréhensible si l'acte de comprendre se tient dans l'ek-stasis" (action is possible only insofar as it is not understood, and incomprehensible if understanding resides in ecstasis, p. 264). Since action's possibility is identically its incomprehensibility, it is called instinct. As in Schopenhauer, the apparent paradox of instinct expresses only the condition of life. That is why Nietzsche mentions "the incapacity of the noble Athenians (p. 265), who, like all noble men, were men of instinct and could never give satisfactory information about the reasons for their actions". Socrates played with and mocked this incapacity until "he found the same difficulty and incapacity in himself", from which he escaped only by "a kind of self-deception", declaring that one must "do justice to [the instincts] but also to reason" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 191). The Socratic demand for reasons is the demand that action appear in representation; Henry links it to the analytic demand to "explain" one's acts.

Doing justice to reason, summoning the world of representation with its causes and laws, projects and motives, is precisely no longer being able to do justice to life: throwing a horizon of understanding beyond it and so putting it outside itself, no longer disposing of itself and its being-itself as its sole justification and possible meaning, no longer finding in itself the secret of its being. Nietzsche described this pathetically: "something was lacking, a tremendous void surrounded man; he did not know how to justify, explain or affirm himself; he suffered from the problem of his meaning" (*Genealogy*, III, 28). Beyond everything, ecstasis hollows out the space of the question "why", but life, which does not bear this space, ignores this question and need not answer it. "The noble soul", Nietzsche said, "accepts the fact of its egoism without question". The eidetic structure of life must therefore be restated: its irresistible coherence with itself in itself, excluding any self-transcendence, any possibility of going outside, before, beside or above itself ("every aspiration", Nietzsche says, "toward a beside, a beyond, an outside, an above ..."), so that only this radical exclusion of what is outside and beyond it, the ideal for instance, restoring life to the immanence of its being-itself, restores it to itself, plunging it back into the essence from which it draws its possibility of being, what Nietzsche calls reality. Nietzsche celebrates this condition of life lyrically (p. 266), speaking of "the redeeming man of great love and great contempt, the creative spirit whose compelling strength will not let him rest in any aloofness or beyond, whose isolation is misunderstood by the people as if it were flight from reality, while it is only his absorption, immersion, penetration into reality, so that, when he one day emerges again into the light, he may bring home the redemption of this reality from the curse that the ideal has laid upon it" (*Genealogy*, II, 24). "The problem of his meaning" is the problem the analytic interpretation answers; for Henry it arises only where life has been put outside itself.

How far this structure of immanence, the inability of what remains in itself to be outside itself, constitutes life's most extreme and decisive possibility appears in that its shaking is identically that of the foundations of being: all Nietzsche's thought proceeds from his avowed terror before the abyss of nihilism and his pathetic effort to ward it off. This effort is expressed in a well-known distinction that runs through his work, not without embarrassing the reader: the strong and the weak, masters and slaves. To grasp its meaning, four connected questions must first be asked, calling for one answer. Who are the strong, those beings unfailingly celebrated and adulated; how are they possible, what does their strength consist in? Who are the weak, unfailingly disparaged and despised; how are they possible, how is weakness possible? Given Nietzsche's constant thesis that the weak always prevail over the strong ("one always has to defend the strong against the weak"), why is this so: what is the strength of the weak and the weakness of the strong? Henry announces four questions but lists three; the fourth, implicit, is how the weak's victory is possible at all, answered with the ascetic priest. The "embarrassment" is addressed directly: the typology is to be read eidetically, not socially or racially.

The strength of the strong is easy to account for. The strong are strong insofar as they are, insofar as the essence of being is will to power, force itself. Their nature is all the easier to understand from the essence of being (p. 267) since they are nothing but a projection of this essence, the mythical figure of what has nothing to do with a category of unduly privileged individuals but is precisely the inner structure of being as life. Is life forgetful by nature? Then the strong are forgetful; they have nothing to forgive, not even to "forget": so full of themselves and dependent on nothing, they pass over the earth like horsemen, swift and out of reach. Offence, if there is any, is only an occasion for more strength. Leaving the traces of memory and its rumination behind, life shows in them only its "plasticity", its capacity to invent and heal, always its growth. Is this life, foreign to representation and the principle of reason, without "cause" as it is without memory, without ground outside itself? Then the strong too "come like fate, without cause, without reason ...". That is why the eidetic analysis of the structure of being as life has taken the form of a recollection of mythical figures: with each of them an element of the eidos, an invariant of life, is aimed at. The "strong" are an eidetic figure, not a social class: this neutralizes the political reading of Nietzsche's aristocratism.

Does the existence of a class or caste of masters not follow from what Nietzsche repeatedly presents as decisive, the pathos of distance in which aristocracy firmly asserts its difference and ostentatiously opposes itself to all that is less, as in this magnificent text: "without the pathos of distance that grows out of the ingrained difference between strata, out of the ruling caste's constant looking down on subjects and instruments and its equally constant practice of obedience and command, of keeping down and keeping at a distance, that other, more mysterious pathos could not have grown up either, the craving for an ever new widening of distances within the soul itself, the development of ever higher, rarer, more remote, further-stretching, more comprehensive states" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 257)? But the life of which aristocracy is here, as elsewhere, only the cipher (p. 268) is not "reactive". Far from being what it is only by opposition to what it opposes, the contrary is true. The apparent order of dependence in the text must be reversed: instead of being the condition of this "mysterious passion" of life to grow constantly from itself, the pathos of distance is its possible consequence. If the essence of aristocracy is that of life, what force makes the strength of the strong is understood: not this or that given force, greater or smaller, a power with a given fate, but "l'hyperpuissance" (hyper-power) which, throwing every force and power into themselves, gives them to grow from themselves and so to superabound. Henry reverses § 257: social distance does not produce the inner growth of the soul; inner growth (immanent) may produce external distance.

What makes the weakness of the weak is, by contrast, a problem: if the will to power is the essence of being, if everything is only through this power that superabounds from itself, how is anything like weakness possible at all? An external explanation says: everything is force, but there are quantities of force; when one meets a greater, it is weaker; the quantitative difference of forces produces their qualitative difference, weakness and strength, which now qualifies only the stronger. This qualitative differentiation is also expressed by saying that the weaker, undergoing the stronger's action, becomes "reactive", its action now determined by the stronger action it constantly undergoes, while the stronger alone remains really, wholly and properly "active". This is Deleuze's reading in *Nietzsche and Philosophy* (1962), unnamed: active and reactive forces defined by quantitative relations.

But, supposing the "quantity" of force is not already a way of thinking the qualitative differentiation of weakness it claims to explain, having surreptitiously included it, this extrinsic determination of the inner nature of force is simply at the antipodes of what Nietzsche means by will to power, which designates this inner nature (p. 269) as force superabounding from itself and never ceasing to be what it is. That there is an essence of force that cannot become something else, not even its contrary, that masters do not suddenly become slaves at a bend in the road on meeting someone more powerful, and so that mastery and servitude, strength and weakness, are not successive chance modalities, Nietzsche posits by making aristocracy a race, an essence. Likewise the plebs: weakness too must be understood not from an extrinsic determination but from its inner possibility. And this possibility is the same as strength's (there is nothing else): the essence of life, immanence. Force (no longer considered naively in its facticity) is that of immanence, "l'inquantifiable, incoercible et insurpassable force du lien qui lie la vie à elle-même" (the unquantifiable, irresistible, unsurpassable force of the bond that ties life to itself, p. 269). Nietzsche understands weakness not as lesser force but as the negation of its essence and, since this essence is immanence, as its rupture. The anti-Deleuzian thesis: force is not a quantity. "Race" is glossed as "essence", eidetically; Henry does not endorse a biological reading.

That is what nihilism means, the no said to life: not the external negation of its factual existence but the destruction of its inner essence. But this inner destruction as self-destruction (it is life, we shall see why, that says no to life), this self-negation, comes up against an impossibility of essence, the essence of life, since the bond that ties it to itself is unbreakable. The impossible self-destruction of life's inner essence, which as such never ends, is what Nietzsche calls the sickness of life, which makes man, insofar as immanence is animality and is what is to be shaken, "a sick animal", "the sickest animal". Nietzsche did nothing but consider with open eyes the unfathomable mystery of this sickness of life, this will of life to attack its own essence and so destroy itself: "what a mad, sad beast is man!" (*Genealogy*, II, 22). Nihilism as a self-destruction that cannot succeed and so never ends is Kierkegaard's "sickness unto death" (the despair of not being able to die), which Henry names explicitly in the coming units.

The end of § 13 of the third essay (p. 270) briefly answers the question "man is the sick animal: why?" by hastily listing several "causes", or rather manifestations, of this sickness: man is "the great experimenter with himself", the one inhabited by bad conscience, who has turned his instincts against himself, enjoys fighting his nature, shaping and tormenting himself. As such he is self-creator, "the unsatisfied, the insatiable", the one "whom the violence of his own desires leaves no rest", able to innovate and defy fate. This is possible only because the future, "the relentless spur in the flesh of every present", constantly tears the present, separating man from himself, launching him beyond himself, making him also "the most courageous, the most exposed" animal. But also the one who, weary of this desire that always throws him beyond himself, can only see in this distance from himself, revealing his whole past of struggle, its vanity: the man of satiety, fatigue, self-disgust, whose crushing mirror Schopenhauer never stopped holding up to Nietzsche. Considering these four "causes", they all consist in an ecstasis which, set within life's relation to itself as a gaze on itself, disgust with itself, effort against itself, leap beyond itself, as the ecstasis of the future in particular, breaks the immanence of this relation, each time affecting life in its very possibility. The "ecstasis of the future" is Heidegger's primary temporal ecstasis; Henry makes it a symptom of the sickness of life, inverting *Being and Time*'s priority of the future.

All Nietzsche's descriptions of sickness, which abound, refer ultimately to the impossibility of an eidetic situation constituted by the rupture of life's primitive immanence. To grasp what finally characterizes those Nietzsche calls the ill-constituted, the ill-favoured, the botched and failures, the vast cohort of the wretched whose misery feeds resentment, one may perceive the secret of this original morbidity where it seems most obviously related to the body and its visible deformities, what is naively called illness (p. 271): for the body appears so, misshapen or infirm, only in objectivity, hence separated from itself and from what makes it essential as a living body. Representation is not responsible for this situation; it can never give the original body to itself, only offer an image of it, leaving it intact in its real essence. Only life's deep willing can decide this putting outside itself that seems effective in the objective body. That is why the objective body and its avatars, infirmity, incapacity, illness, symbolize for Nietzsche life's metaphysical sickness, the only one he has in view. Objective appearance is nothing as long as it does not arouse the monstrous project of self-destruction. This, the rupture of self-immanence, is what the discontented aim at, always discontented with themselves. Nietzsche described every form of this project of rupture: self-doubt, loss of belief, scepticism, objectivism, scientism, self-criticism in all its forms, "on est tenté d'écrire « l'analyse »" (one is tempted to write "analysis"), all doctrines of bad faith, bad conscience, the gaze on oneself, interpretation, suspicion, all that place the truth of life outside life, making our time this "spoiled present" which, as Osip Mandelstam was to say, smells of rotten fish. All these partisans of self-division, "the ill-constituted ... it is they, the weakest, who most undermine life among men, who most dangerously poison and question our trust in life, in man, in ourselves" (*Genealogy*, III, 14). "One is tempted to write 'analysis'" is the book's most direct charge against psychoanalysis: it belongs to the forms of life's self-division Nietzsche diagnosed. "Bad faith" names Sartre, "suspicion" Ricœur.

That this rupture of life's immanence is the mark of weakness appears more clearly if, anticipating the order of analysis, one looks now at the struggle of the weak against the strong, how the former bring down the latter's strength. This destruction of force is precisely the rupture of its self-immanence, which is achieved if the weak manage to insert their own weakness into the soul of the strong, "if they succeeded in shoving their own misery, all misery, into the consciences of the fortunate (p. 272), so that one day the fortunate began to be ashamed of their good fortune and perhaps said to one another: 'it is disgraceful to be fortunate: there is too much misery!'" (III, 14). Strength and weakness are thus clearly distributed as happiness and shame, as life's immanence and its rupture. Shame is the affect of being seen, of the gaze on oneself; Henry's analysis of shame in Schopenhauer (Chapter VI) returns here as the instrument of weakness.

That this rupture is impossible leads at last to the ground of weakness and its true essence. What finally makes the weakness of the weak is not only what hides beneath the mire of shame and self-contempt ("on such soil of self-contempt, a veritable swamp, every weed, every poisonous plant grows"), the monstrous project of self-destruction: "c'est l'échec de ce projet qui constitue l'essence ultime de la faiblesse" (it is the failure of this project that constitutes the ultimate essence of weakness, p. 272). Life's willing not to be itself, the Self's willing to rid itself of itself, is weakness itself, insofar as this willing comes up by principle against a force greater than it, the greatest force, the one that builds the Self, the force of force, which gives its strength to every force and to weakness itself. The relation of weakness and strength, figured in the external relation of the strong and the weak, concerns life's relation to itself: within this relation, it is the relation of what constitutes its nature to what claims to oppose it. How, in life's inner relation to itself, the weakness of not willing to be oneself opposes the strength of this Self and so constitutes the essence of weakness, of the "ill-constituted", this limit-text says: "how can one escape that veiled glance of the man born ill-constituted, which betrays how such a man speaks to himself, that glance which is a sigh? 'If only I were someone else', sighs this glance, 'but there is no hope of that. I am who I am: how could I get free of myself? And yet I am sick of myself!'" (III, 14). Here weakness receives its true name: "elle s'appelle le désespoir" (it is called despair). The identification with Kierkegaard's despair, "not willing to be oneself", is explicit. Weakness is the self's impossible flight from itself, not a lesser quantity of force.

Third question: how do the weak prevail over the strong? An apparently insurmountable aporia if one is to understand how the weakness of life's project of ridding itself of itself (p. 273) could dominate the unsurpassable force of its coherence in itself in immanence. In truth, if weakness seems to prevail and can prevail over the greatest force, it is because it bears it within itself, insofar as it is and, even as the most signal weakness, coheres with itself in life's hyper-power: not for one instant has life's willing to rid itself of itself ceased to belong to life or to bear its essence. The weak are strong with the strength of life itself; their victory is life's victory over its own attempt at self-negation.

Nietzsche says this in the extraordinary analysis of the ascetic priest. Here, for the first time, casting retrospective light on the whole work, weakness and strength are no longer distributed as two separate entities referred to two different individuals: the ascetic priest holds both in himself, showing us their inner connection. He is weak because he is the man of bad conscience, of life turned against itself. He differs from the other weak in being their nurse, in which he still belongs to them, since to avoid contagion by this terrible sickness of life all who are in contact with the sick, especially carers, must themselves be sick. But the ascetic priest is strong, stronger perhaps than the strongest: "he must also be strong, master of himself even more than of others, unbroken in his will to power" (III, 15). His task is crushing: he must defend the herd both against the strong and against itself. Against the strong, by the brilliant invention of the ascetic ideal, which legitimates resentment by the transvaluation of values and, making the forms of weakness good and those of strength evil, secures through these inverted values the hold and domination of the weak over the strong. Against the herd itself: having defended the weak against the strong by organizing resentment, he must prevent its unleashing from breaking the herd in turn, and so channel, guide and soothe this resentment; this great magician poisons and soothes the wound at the same time. The ascetic priest is the "physician" of the sick who is himself sick, the obvious ancestor of the analyst; Henry does not yet say so, but the description (channelling resentment, poisoning and soothing the wound) is chosen with Freud in view.

Then the mysterious interweaving of weakness and strength (p. 274) appears in him, and how he converts the first into the second. In him and through him an exhausted, desperate life undertakes to survive and save itself. But what in extreme weakness first gives it this unheard-of strength to want to go on living, not to succumb before the strong and even to enslave them, what intact instinct for life? Nietzsche's hyper-lucid gaze detects it at the ground of the ascetic ideal: "the ascetic ideal springs from the protective instinct of a degenerating life that tries by all means to sustain itself and fights for its existence; it indicates a partial physiological obstruction and exhaustion against which the deepest instincts of life, which have remained intact, continually struggle with new expedients" (III, 13). The ascetic ideal turns out to be the opposite of what it was first taken for: not life turned against life, against itself, but the pathetic effort of this dying life to survive: "this ascetic priest, this apparent enemy of life ... he is among the greatest conserving and yes-creating forces of life"; "in him and through him life wrestles ... with death". What this death is against which life passionately struggles is also said: not death, but the mortal sickness, life's metaphysical sickness: "the struggle of man against death (more precisely: against disgust with life, against exhaustion, against the desire for the 'end')". How the greatest force superabounds within degenerating life to save it is now understood: as this life comes into itself in the hyper-power of its immanence. "Disgust with life, desire for the end" is the content Freud will assign to the death drive; Henry reads it as the sickness of life that life itself combats.

A doubt remains: do the preceding analyses fully account for the possibility of weakness, its origin? Why does life turn against itself? Whence this aberrant project of ridding itself of itself? Nietzsche says: from suffering. "The herd of the ill-constituted ... of those who suffer from themselves." We are referred (p. 275) to life's second eidetic determination, its most extreme possibility: affectivity. The genesis of nihilism lies in suffering: Chapter VI's thesis that desire is born from suffering's inability to bear itself is now applied to self-negation.

Affectivity fills the whole Nietzschean landscape; it is everywhere. As in Schopenhauer, "will" is often a way of designating affective life and its modes, so that the two concepts seem interchangeable. Affirming the subordination of the intellect to a power of another order that determines it, Nietzsche writes: "to eliminate the will altogether, to suspend each and every affect, supposing we were capable of this, what would that mean but to castrate the intellect?" (*Genealogy*, III, 12). Everything indeed seems subordinate to affectivity, in particular Nietzsche's own new way of interpreting the relation to the world, evaluation, and so morality in general. *Beyond Good and Evil* speaks of "the immense multiplicity of feelings of value", to declare a little later that "moralities are only a sign language of the affects" (§§ 186–187). Above all, from one end of the work to the other, affectivity presents itself as constituting in itself the essence of reality and life. The "castration of the intellect" passage is the perspectivism text; Henry reads it as the dependence of all seeing on affect.

As in Schopenhauer, the nominal primacy of will often relegates affectivity to dependence, as its mere effect. Again the history of our feelings no longer finds its principle in them but in another history, as if the coming to appearance of the principled possibilities included in affectivity's essence no longer composed the history of affectivity but that of willing. This seems to happen in Nietzsche when accounting for the transvaluation of values, which proceeds from resentment, a fundamental affective modality consisting in vengeance and hatred. But the explanation of resentment, the ultimate explanation of the reversal of the aristocratic equation of values (good = noble = beautiful = happy = beloved of the gods), refers explicitly to pure will and one of its pure determinations (p. 276): powerlessness. "The priests are the most evil enemies ... because they are the most impotent. From impotence their hatred grows to monstrous proportions" (I, 7). Yet looking at what these priests do and what makes it effective, one sees that appeal is again made to the power of willing to produce an affective tonality. Since the herd to be treated is made up of sufferers whom one must help escape at least partly from suffering's intolerability, the technique used consists for instance "in prescribing a small, easily attained joy ... the joy of giving joy (in the form of benefits, gifts, relief, help, encouragement, consolation ...)" (III, 18). To understand this joy one is referred to the will to power itself: its exercise, even in minimal doses (since doing good gives the doer a superiority, however "tiny"), produces happiness. Henry concedes the presence in Nietzsche of the Schopenhauerian derivation of affect from will (resentment from impotence, joy from a feeling of power).

Pushed to the end, this dependence leads to treating affectivity as a symptom, the "phenomenon" in the sense of a mere appearance not explained by itself but only by something outside it that does not itself appear. Feeling is then like an idea or any behaviour, offered, like them, as a dead and blind content to the examination of a genetic and critical method that seeks their naturing behind them, as behind experience in general. "Nietzsche ouvre la voie à Freud, à moins que cette lecture symptomale ne soit que la projection rétroactive sur Nietzsche du freudisme lui-même" (Nietzsche opens the way to Freud, unless this symptomal reading is only the retroactive projection onto Nietzsche of Freudianism itself, p. 276). "Symptomal reading" (*lecture symptomale*) is Althusser's term in *Reading Capital*; Henry suggests that the "master of suspicion" Nietzsche may be a construction of Freudian readers.

How can one fail to see that over this extrinsic determination of affectivity by will (shown to be impossible in principle and not really occurring even in Schopenhauer) is superimposed from the outset in Nietzsche a prior definition of willing itself (p. 277) by affectivity, at any rate the grasp of an inner connection between them? The first name of the Will to Power in Nietzsche's work is Dionysus. Dionysus is not a mysterious entity constructed by speculation and placed behind experience as a supposed explanatory principle. *The Birth of Tragedy* tells rather of Dionysus' coming among us, which occurs precisely in and through tragedy. Dionysus is certainly the hidden god who does not appear on stage or uncover his face. Yet he is there, not as advancing masked at the head of his exalted followers' procession, but as the inner principle of their exaltation, intoxication and emotion, or rather as its very reality, insofar as their joy is identically the god's and rests in him, insofar as it is "a primordial joy in the very bosom of the primordial One". Primordial joy, pain, the "contradiction and pain at the heart of the primordial One", "eternal joy of existence", "bliss of living": such are the names of the One, of the will itself, its constituents. Dionysus is the will as felt, not as hypothesized: the hidden god is present as affect, not absent behind it. This is the anti-symptomal reading of Nietzsche.

Or would this joy, this suffering, their modes, be produced simply, as in Schopenhauer, from willing's prior power, according to whether it is "satisfied" or not? Far from it. Examining closely Nietzsche's essence of tragedy, its analysis frees two superimposed dimensions of affectivity, or two successive moments of approaching it. In the first only, feeling seems to depend on desire and its prewritten history, since the happiness of satisfaction is only a provisional stage on the road to the final catastrophe. Then indeed we see the hero struggle with the dramatic course of events that will overwhelm and strike him down. But precisely in the hero's destruction an incomprehensible bliss arises in us (p. 278), spectators hitherto anguished and struck to the heart by this death: "he beholds the transfigured world of the stage and yet denies it; he sees the tragic hero before him in epic clarity and beauty and yet rejoices in his annihilation ... he feels the hero's actions to be justified and is yet still more elated when these actions annihilate their agent; he shudders at the sufferings that will befall the hero and yet anticipates in them a higher, much more overpowering joy" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 22). The annihilation of the hero, his efforts and projects, is identically that of the whole phenomenal world, this world of desire with its infinite, always vain tribulations, and in and through this collapse of the world its hidden essence is revealed, the one essence of life and will, which Nietzsche calls the primordial One. So this unveiling of life consists in this intoxication, and life is finally nothing but this self-unveiling in self-intoxication, "the bliss of living". Tragic joy is not satisfaction: it arises where desire is destroyed. Henry's reading of Aristotelian catharsis through Nietzsche: the spectator's joy is life's self-revelation once representation (the hero's world) collapses.

Thus the relation to primordial being appears, for once in Western philosophy, as accomplished in affectivity, which is not a relation to being different from it but being's own relation to itself, its auto-affection, finding its phenomenological substance in pleasure. In this self-relation of primordial being, consisting in the affectivity of pleasure, each of the god's followers identified with him stands: "we feel its unbridled desire and joy in existence". That this pleasure, or this pain "in the bosom of the primordial One", no longer results from the will's prior action as the effect of its successes or failures is clear, since the whole world of desire and its vicissitudes has been abolished so that being may be restored to its original upsurge and perceived in it. That is why Nietzsche says precisely that this pleasure is that of "primordial being", of the "primordial One", that it is "eternal" and "unbridled". And affectivity, instead of being naively taken for its own modalizations according to the play (p. 279) of their occasional causes, reveals itself as the essence that precedes and makes them possible. "For once in Western philosophy": the *Birth of Tragedy* is credited with the thesis of *L'essence de la manifestation*, that affectivity is the relation of being to itself.

We can now understand the later elaboration of the concept of will to power, its progressive identification with the essence of affectivity, explicit in the decisive assertion of aphorism 635 of *The Will to Power*: "the will to power is not a being, not a becoming, but a pathos". Formulations of this identification are scattered through the whole work on the most diverse problems. In *Beyond Good and Evil*: "it is not the strength but the duration of high feelings that makes high men" (§ 72); "the will to overcome an affect is ultimately only the will of another, or of several other, affects" (§ 117); "in the end one loves one's desire and not what is desired" (§ 175). The affective definition of life's will shows throughout the magnificent preface to *The Gay Science*, explaining how a spirit that has waited patiently "without hope" is suddenly assailed by the intoxication of recovery and with it by the wildest ideas: "this whole book is nothing but ... the need to enjoy after a long period of privation and impotence, the rejoicing of returning strength ... the sudden feeling and anticipation of a future" (Preface, § 1). The reversal of the apparent dependence of affectivity on will is evident when domination and command, usually referred to will, are explicitly subordinated to affectivity: "which groups of sensations within a soul awaken first, begin to speak and give commands?" (§ 268). The citation of *Will to Power* 635 ("pathos") is the textual pivot of the chapter: Heidegger quotes the same note (*Nietzsche* I) but reads pathos as a mode of being-in-the-world; Henry reads it as auto-affection.

But it is not a matter of naively reversing an external relation between will and affectivity, but of grasping the founding bond that unites them: power or powerlessness never present themselves alone or exist as such (p. 280), but only as feelings of power or powerlessness; likewise there is no reactive universe, a system of bare or objective forces with such a coefficient, but a "reactive pathos" (*Genealogy*, II, 11), as there is a pathos of distance. "La puissance n'existe que comme sentiment de puissance parce qu'il n'y a pas de puissance qui ne s'éprouve comme telle" (power exists only as a feeling of power because there is no power that does not experience itself as such, p. 280); this self-experience of power is its immanence, its belonging to life, to the original dimension where being comes to itself in Nietzsche's figure of animality. Only because power experiences itself and its power can it will to accomplish itself, and Nietzsche writes: "every animal, including the philosophical animal, instinctively strives for an optimum of favourable conditions under which it can expend all its strength and achieve its maximal feeling of power" (*Genealogy*, III, 7). "Reactive pathos" refutes Deleuze textually: reactivity is a feeling, not a quantitative relation between forces.

Likewise it is not powerlessness that provokes hatred but the feeling of powerlessness, powerlessness itself in the unconditionality of its bond to itself in its self-suffering, its suffering. The intolerability of this suffering, the unconditionality of this bond, arouses the project of breaking it, escaping this suffering, the mad project of self-destruction, which is hatred, and which life's genius tempers by turning it outward, toward the other, in resentment. The problematic of weakness had left a question pending, that of its origin, of life's will to rid itself of itself: it is answered here. Since power and powerlessness can unfold only by resting on themselves, they do what they do only on the ground of their affectivity. The affectivity of forces is never secondary; it results neither from their exercise nor from their encounter, as if one were affected by another and their affectivity were born of this affection. On the contrary, only what hollows itself out originally in itself as a Self and so affects itself can be affected (p. 281) by anything, insofar as affection means something other than the naive concept of an ontic causality: "loin d'être la simple conséquence de son affection préalable, l'affectivité de la force en est la condition" (far from being the mere consequence of its prior affection, the affectivity of force is its condition). Resentment is thus the outward turning of a self-hatred that cannot succeed. Henry anticipates Freud's account of aggression as the death drive turned outward (*Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, *Civilization and Its Discontents*), but grounds it in the unbearability of self-suffering, not in a drive toward the inorganic.

That the will to power is pathos means not only that it draws its being from pathos; it also finds its power there. Nietzsche's "will" is being as power. Being's power is growth, not by adding something else to itself, but as self-growth. And being's growth is not posterior to it; there is not first a being that then grows. How affectivity constitutes the will to power, the self-growth of being that forms its essence, is now evident: being grows from itself insofar as it affects itself and its auto-affection is its affectivity, the self-suffering in which being comes into itself and so grows from itself. Affectivity is not power itself, not force. "Elle est l'hyperpuissance sise en toute puissance et en toute force par laquelle toute puissance et toute force s'accroissent d'elles-mêmes" (it is the hyper-power residing in every power and force by which every power and force grows from itself, p. 281). That the hyper-power of this self-growth of power resides in its self-suffering is why the will to power is pathos. "Self-growth" (*accroissement de soi*) reinterprets Nietzsche's *Steigerung*: not more power over things, but intensification of self-experience.

The most remarkable feature of Nietzsche's analysis is that it never considers an abstract essence of affectivity but only its concrete accomplishments, standing from the start on a phenomenological plane. Suffering is the most constant of these. It is systematically disparaged, it is true, whenever the weak or Christianity are at issue. Because they have become insensitive to its dreadful demands, moderns "no longer feel the supreme horror that the paradox of 'God on the cross' held for ancient taste" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 46). But what is condemned is never pure suffering, only the hatred or vengeance it arouses, or that very particular suffering whose suffering (p. 282) engenders the will to rid oneself of oneself, which in fact denies the original essence of suffering. Everywhere else there is a real apology for suffering: "the discipline of suffering, of great suffering, do you not know that only this discipline has created all enhancements of man so far?" (§ 225). That is why Oedipus is the one who, "through his excessive suffering, exerts a magical, beneficent power that is felt even after his death" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 9). Condemned instead are those who depreciate suffering or want to eliminate it, those who preach "the universal easing of existence", "pity for all who suffer", because "suffering itself is to them something that must be abolished" (§ 44). Once more, and paradoxically, Christianity and "the democratic movement", its "heir", are condemned: "they share a deadly hatred of suffering generally, an almost feminine incapacity for remaining spectators of suffering, for letting suffer" (§ 202). Henry's distinction between condemned "suffering from oneself" (despair) and praised "great suffering" is the key to Nietzsche's seeming inconsistency. The Oedipus who suffers magnificently is Sophocles', not Freud's; the contrast is implicit.

But suffering is not first the object of an evaluation, since it co-constitutes the original essence from which every evaluation proceeds, the essence of life, insofar as life's principled possibility is self-suffering. That is why this suffering has its place in the bosom of the primordial One, whose unity with itself, as effective and phenomenological, finds the substance of its phenomenality in the affectivity of its suffering. That is why suffering, great suffering, is the sole cause of man's self-overcomings, because in it lies the essence of the primitive coming into oneself in which growth as self-growth consists. That is why "it exerts a magical, beneficent power ...". Suffering is ontological (the self-suffering of auto-affection) before being a content to be valued or disvalued.

Since life comes originally into itself in suffering's self-suffering, in suffering too, experiencing itself and growing from itself, it enjoys itself; it is enjoyment. Enjoyment (p. 283), joy, bliss, intoxication, "effusion of a primordial delight", is the second of the names Nietzsche gives life. Tragedy, because it opens us to life, stands "amid this overflow of life, suffering and joy" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 21). Suffering and joy are not two modes of affectivity; together they constitute the one essence of being as life, as original self-experience in the self-growth of self-enjoyment. Nor are they two separate, self-sufficient tonalities; rather they are the eternal passage of one into the other, since to suffer oneself in the accomplishment of suffering is each time to grow from oneself and enjoy oneself, since enjoyment has no other site or phenomenological effectiveness than the suffering of this suffering. "L'être n'est pas, il s'historialise" (being is not; it historializes itself) in the effectuation of the principled potentialities by which appearing appears to itself. This original connection of suffering and joy as together constituting the historial of being as life, "connexion saisie pour la première fois sur le mode apodictique par L'essence de la manifestation (§ 70)" (a connection grasped for the first time apodictically by *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 70), the young Nietzsche detects in Dionysus, as in this essential text: "the curious blending and duality in the emotions of the Dionysian revellers ... the phenomenon that pain arouses joy, that jubilation wrings painful sounds from the breast" (§ 2). Henry cites his own *L'essence de la manifestation* as the apodictic demonstration of what Nietzsche found mythically. Being "historializes" itself in affect: this is Henry's alternative to Heidegger's *Geschichte des Seins*, a history of tonalities, not epochs.

The historial connection of suffering and joy runs through all Nietzsche's work and serves as its unnoticed support. In this radical, rigorous sense Nietzsche's philosophy is a philosophy of life. From *The Birth of Tragedy*, the passage from extreme suffering to extreme joy felt by the spectator when the sight of the dying hero arouses anguish then jubilation is due not only to the irruption, through the collapse of the phenomenal world, of the original essence of being and life, but (p. 284) first to this essence being constituted in itself by the eternal passage from suffering to joy, which it makes possible in general, in the tragic spectator in particular. The definition of "philosophy of life" is precise: one in which suffering-joy is the structure of being.

Consider now, as a more elaborate example, the genealogy of morals. Nietzsche's analysis first offers an apparent genealogy, a pseudo-genealogy. According to it, morality issues from a first relation present in and constitutive of every society, the debtor–creditor relation. A contractual relation that makes legal persons appear ("here for the first time person measured himself against person") but itself refers "to the fundamental forms of traffic", to the real praxis of men producing and exchanging their products (II, 8). In this relation debt becomes possible and takes form, and with it the feeling of personal obligation, the need to remember and so to become an animal able to promise, the experience of guilt, the existence of damage, and of punishment as its compensation. So dense and taut is the network of moral, affective and legal relations included in the first debtor–creditor pair that it explains all the fundamental human relations that are its extension: the individual and society (the same contract, the same debts, compensations, punishments), and the no less important relation of social forms to their predecessors, to ancestors and finally to God. The conviction that each tribe subsists only through the labours and sacrifices of those before it grows dizzyingly into the monstrous image of the absolute Ancestor to whom everything is owed, so that the impossibility of ever redeeming such a debt leaves room only for "eternal punishment", unless the creditor, paying himself, sacrifices himself for the debtor's redemption: "God sacrificing himself for the guilt of man, God himself making payment to himself" (II, 21). The debt to the ancestor-God is the template of Freud's *Totem and Taboo* (the guilt toward the murdered father) and of Lacan's "symbolic debt"; Henry calls it a pseudo-genealogy.

How far this social genealogy of morals is only apparent (p. 285) one senses as soon as one recalls how far Nietzsche "stands at the opposite end of all modern ideology", far from the human sciences that seek man's secret in objective knowledge: "what of their fundamental tendency to see in the forms of existing society the virtually sole cause of all human misery and failure, in which I recognize modern stupidity par excellence?" Nor can history, the history of the first forms of social relation, supply morality's genealogy, unless one goes back beyond "world history" to what Nietzsche calls "essential history", which occurred in very ancient times ("when suffering was everywhere counted a virtue, cruelty a virtue, dissimulation a virtue, revenge a virtue ...", *Daybreak*, § 18), times which, however, do not belong to history and are not past, being not the before of the genealogy but the Same of which it is the indefinite recommencement: "taking the ancient times as reference (ancient times which exist, incidentally, at all times, or are always possible again) ...". As the history of ancient times, of what always endures, this "essential history" is nothing but the history of essence, "l'historial de l'absolu et l'éternel passage en lui de sa souffrance à sa joie" (the historial of the absolute and the eternal passage in it from its suffering to its joy, p. 285). "Ancient times that exist at all times" is the formula Henry uses to read genealogy as eidetics: the "origin" is permanent, as in the prologue's definition of the lost beginning.

Consider the decisive sequence of the genealogy. It occurs when the debtor does not repay the creditor, who is then entitled to a strange compensation, no longer an equivalent in kind, money, land or goods, but the right to strike, abuse, despise, insult and even rape, the right to cause suffering in every way. Hence the genealogy's abyssal question: "how can suffering be a compensation for debts?". And the no less abyssal answer: "because making suffer gave the greatest pleasure (p. 286), because the injured party exchanged for the loss and the displeasure an extraordinary counter-pleasure: making suffer, a real feast" (II, 6). And again: "to see others suffer does one good, to make others suffer even more". The equivalence of debt and pain is Nietzsche's famous derivation of *Schuld* from *Schulden*.

One will say: it is simply revenge. But, as Nietzsche's implacable gaze discovers, revenge refers to the same problem: "how can making suffer be a satisfaction?". It is cruelty! But cruelty too is only the enjoyment that comes from suffering: "cruelty was the great festive joy of ancient man, an ingredient of almost all his pleasures" (II, 6). Thus the genealogy's true meaning is revealed: the appeal to humanity's mythical past, where the one who caused damage must suffer so that his suffering arouses the joy of the injured and so compensates him, posits the debtor–creditor relation as explanatory principle only insofar as a more original relation is invested in it, that of life's fundamental tonalities. "C'est l'inversion de la souffrance dans la joie, c'est l'historial de l'être lui-même qu'expose la genealogie" (it is the inversion of suffering into joy, the historial of being itself, that the genealogy exposes and that makes it possible, p. 286). The social relation is the scene where the ontological relation suffering–joy is played out; this anticipates Henry's critique of Freud's sociogenesis of guilt.

In revenge and cruelty, it is true, suffering and joy no longer go together but seem separate, referred to two different subjects: the first in the debtor, the second in the creditor. The sight of the pain of the one who caused the damage gives the creditor the pleasure in which he finds compensation. This exteriority of pleasure and pain even lies at the origin of the unconvincing analysis by which Nietzsche tries to give his own answer to the most anguishing question, the meaning of suffering. This meaning, artificially reconstructed by the ascetic priest as "you suffer because you have sinned", lies, according to Nietzsche, in the pleasure (p. 287) that every suffering unfailingly arouses. But suffering does not have this meaning in itself, for the sufferer, but only for one who contemplates it from outside and delights in it. Hence Nietzsche's theme of the spectator: suffering is justified only because someone is there to contemplate and enjoy it; if there is no one, he must be invented. In the ancient world these were the gods, friends of cruel spectacles, delighting in men's tribulations and misfortunes, who even, to spice the spectacle, lent these staggering actors a will of their own and freedom. "All of antiquity was full of tender regard for 'the spectator'" (II, 7). If pessimism is to be overcome and suffering redeemed, it is solely through its bond with pleasure, though as an extrinsic bond. "Today, when suffering is always brought forward as the first argument against existence, as its gravest question mark, one does well to recall the ages in which the opposite judgment prevailed, because men were unwilling to refrain from making suffer and saw in it an enchantment of the first order, a genuine seduction to life." Only because "morbid softening and moralization" finally "taught the animal 'man' to be ashamed of all his instincts", including that of making suffer, has suffering, separated from the pleasure with which it went hand in hand, become a nonsense, an object of shame and disgust. Henry finds the "spectator" theory (suffering justified by a viewer) unconvincing because it makes the meaning of suffering lie in a representation, outside the sufferer: the same error as Schopenhauer's species.

But Nietzsche himself invites us to question the exteriority of the bond between joy and suffering, as if the first really came from the spectacle of the second, from a suffering located in another "subject" or "substrate" than joy. "To understand this one must dismiss the clumsy psychology of former times, which taught only that cruelty arises from the sight of others' suffering" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 229). Understand what? The whole set of "phenomena" in which pleasure and pain appear (p. 288) together and, correspondingly, the endless procession of those who savour them together: "the Roman in the arena, the Christian in the ecstasies of the cross, the Spaniard at the auto-da-fé or bullfight, the Japanese of today who flocks to tragedy", not to forget (and here Nietzsche's gaze pierces one of the idols of our time) "the Parisian suburban worker who longs for bloody revolutions". For the exteriority of the joy–suffering relation, which seems sufficient to account for cruelty as a mode of the experience of others, is substituted here the interiority of this relation, as the individual's relation to himself. For cruelty, in its most general and constant form, is first cruelty toward oneself, a cruelty consisting in making oneself suffer, in which the individual draws pleasure from his own suffering, and that is why he makes himself suffer. Nietzsche surveys at a glance the immense field of this cruelty: "our own suffering, the suffering we inflict on ourselves, gives abundant, superabundant enjoyment; and wherever man allows himself to be persuaded to self-denial in the religious sense, or to self-mutilation, as among Phoenicians and ascetics, or generally to desensualization, decarnalization, contrition, Puritanical penitential spasms ... he is secretly lured and pushed forward by his cruelty, by the dangerous thrill of cruelty turned against himself" (§ 229). The whole enterprise of knowledge, with the violence done to the mind's tendency to lose itself in appearance, and the whole moral enterprise, with the violence done to the instincts, proceed from this self-cruelty. Self-cruelty is Freud's "moral masochism" (*The Economic Problem of Masochism*, 1924), here traced to its Nietzschean source. Henry's interest is ontological: the pleasure in one's own suffering shows that suffering and joy belong to one self.

This self-cruelty finds its aesthetic representation in an image that runs through Nietzsche's work, that of man as a sculptor (p. 289) carving into his own flesh, to chisel a being of beauty no doubt, but even more for the immense pleasure of making himself suffer. Man, creator and creature at once. Creature: "what must be formed, broken, forged, torn, burned, made incandescent and purified, what must necessarily suffer and should suffer". Creator: the one who forms, breaks, cuts, burns, produces all this suffering and enjoys it (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 225). The sculptor image reintroduces exteriority within the self (creator over creature), which the next units examine via Scheler.

Of all the great twentieth-century thinkers influenced by Nietzsche, Max Scheler pushed his meditation furthest, and not by chance in the form of a systematic problematic of affectivity, "la première de son genre dans toute l'histoire de la pensée occidentale" (the first of its kind in the whole history of Western thought, p. 289). Reflecting on the Nietzschean relation of suffering and pleasure at the stage reached, self-cruelty, one sees that the exteriority of this relation, evident in the experience of others, is overcome here only in appearance. It is the same who enjoys and suffers. But insofar as he behaves toward himself as a sculptor toward a "matter", a creator toward his creature, a space opens between the pleasure of the one who strikes and the pain of the one struck. Scheler thinks this space radically: he recognizes the possibility of two different tonalities, pain and pleasure, coexisting in the same individual at the same moment, and he grounds this difference by identifying it with the intentional relation. The traditional status of affectivity then wavers before his eyes. It is not only a psychical content offered to a different power of apprehension consisting in an intentional perception directed at this content; this power of apprehension, this perception, is itself affective. There is thus a possible affective perception of every possible affective content, such that the affectivity of the perception and that of the content are two different tonalities separated by the intentional relation that unites them (p. 290). That is precisely the structure of the bond of suffering and joy in self-cruelty: an intentional bond, and so one of radical exteriority, between the pleasure of striking, wounding, contemplating the pain caused, and this pain itself. In Scheler's explanation self-cruelty is only a particular case: instead of enjoying a suffering one can suffer from it, or suffer from a joy (a shameful pleasure), or enjoy it; the difference and simultaneity of tonalities is grounded in principle. Henry discussed Scheler's "intentional feeling" (*Fühlen von*) at length in *L'essence de la manifestation*, §§ 64, 66, 68 (Scheler's *Formalism*, F); here he summarizes that critique: intentional feeling reintroduces ecstasis into affectivity.

With this astonishing attempt to give a philosophical foundation to the most extreme theses of Nietzsche's thought, Scheler remains infinitely far from it. At most he has shown how suffering and joy can occur together in one individual and occupy his mind at once. How can one be sad and joyful at once, sad in the flesh and happy in spirit, like Luther before his dead daughter? But Nietzsche is not at all concerned to know how two different or even opposed tonalities can go together despite their difference; he does not seek to ground this difference in the intentional relation as creative of exteriority, while the being-together of the two heterogeneous tonalities would also lie in this relation, as it intentionally unites, for instance, the perceiving pleasure and the perceived pain.
