United by the exteriority of the ecstatic bond, but even more separated by it, the two tonalities keep their irreducible qualitative difference, each facing the other in its own sufficiency, indifferent to the other. Nietzsche's decisive and still misunderstood project is rather to recognize and try to explain "comment la souffrance produit le plaisir" (how suffering produces pleasure), for that is what motivates revenge and cruelty: not the mere fact that suffering and joy can cohabit in one individual but, what is quite different and even "the very opposite", that the will to make suffer always awakens with the unthought certainty that making suffer is giving pleasure and, ultimately, that making oneself suffer will bring this pleasure to its height. So that the upsurge (p. 291) of suffering is identically that of enjoying, each mode of the first a mode of the second, each spasm of suffering a spasm of pleasure. Luther at his daughter's deathbed is Scheler's own example of layered feelings (*Formalism*, sensory vs. spiritual feelings). Henry's objection: coexistence is not production.

To designate the horrible ground of things on which the gaze hardly dares rest, Schopenhauer and Nietzsche use the same word, "contradiction". In Schopenhauer it means that desire has no end, that reality is "a hungry reality"; as the contradiction of desire, it means eternal suffering. Likewise in Nietzsche: "the contradiction at the heart of the world", "the primordial contradiction concealed in things" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 9). For him too contradiction is suffering: "the truly existent primordial One, as eternal suffering and contradiction" (§ 4). But the text does not say, as in Schopenhauer, that contradiction is suffering, but rather that suffering is contradiction. How is suffering contradictory? In producing pleasure of itself and by itself: "the contradiction, the bliss born of pain" (§ 4). Nowhere, in truth, has Nietzsche really explained how pain engenders pleasure of itself and by itself, how pleasure is born in the very flesh of pain and is consubstantial with it. The original unity of pain and pleasure as constitutive of true being, the primordial One, which is precisely their "contradiction", their unity, is never more in Nietzsche than the object of an observation. Yet this observation is the ground of all his analyses, the *hypokeimenon* to which they all finally refer. The inversion ("suffering is contradiction") is precise: the contradiction is internal to suffering, which turns into its opposite. Henry's critique is that Nietzsche states this without grounding it; the next units supply the ground.

Can we understand the original unity of suffering and joy? On condition of seeing first that neither can unfold its being outside the other, that there could be no joy without something like a primitive suffering, not elsewhere, outside joy, in the exteriority of an intentional relation (p. 292), but in it, as its innermost support and its very flesh. In the essence of life the self-enjoyment constitutive of this essence finds its principled possibility in a self-experience which is originally a self-suffering, the very possibility of suffering. Suffering is in enjoying its very accomplishment, not theoretically but phenomenologically. Likewise enjoying is inherent in this suffering as what it inevitably produces, since to suffer oneself is to come into one's own being and enjoy it. That is how, according to Nietzsche's decisive assertion, pleasure is born from pain. This birth must be taken for what it is: the perpetual coming into itself of what thus constantly becomes and comes about in itself as a Self. "L'être n'est pas, il est une venue, l'éternelle venue en soi de la vie" (being is not; it is a coming, life's eternal coming into itself). This coming does not arrive from the future or go off into the past; it is the coming of enjoyment from suffering, such that suffering, in the phenomenological accomplishment of its self-suffering, supplies enjoyment with what it enjoys. The "coming" (*venue*) not from the future rejects Heidegger's *Zukunft* (*Zu-kunft*, coming-toward) and Derrida's *à-venir*: the self-coming of life is not temporal ecstasis. This is Henry's theory of the Eternal Return: the eternal coming of joy from self-suffering.

Since suffering constitutes the contribution, the self-contribution, of enjoying, they go together and grow together. Just as their terms are not external to each other, their relation is not fixed. How it evolves and develops we can now understand: the more strongly life experiences itself in the intensification of its suffering and finally at the paroxysm of its suffering, the more powerful and deep its self-seizure, the more intense its enjoyment. Thus between suffering and joy an oscillation arises such that suffering not only constantly passes into joy and changes into it, but for this reason the excess of one is the superabundance of the other. That is why, when a form of life has aged and, abandoning oscillation, its pathos is immobilized in the boredom of an aborted destiny, the time has come to return to those ancient times when, as Nietzsche said, revenge was a virtue, cruelty a virtue, because, by awakening the fundamental tonalities and if need be unleashing them (p. 293), the aim is, in the end, "de rendre l'absolu à son histoire propre et au jeu éternel de la vie en lui" (to restore the absolute to its own history and to the eternal play of life in it). The proportionality of suffering and joy is the "oscillation" Henry will oppose to Freud's pleasure principle, where pleasure is the reduction of tension. Henry's endorsement of returning to cruelty is interpretive, not normative: it restates Nietzsche's diagnosis of boredom as the arrest of life's history.

The exteriority of the fundamental tonalities, real in Scheler, is thus in Nietzsche only a figure. This figure is cruelty, revenge, in which suffering and pleasure seem to fall outside each other, whereas, if enjoying rests on suffering and has its site in it, both are in the torturer as in the victim. If the suffering of the one who first suffered the damage, the creditor, can change, through the sight of the suffering he inflicts on the other, into the pleasure this sight gives him, it is because in him, precisely, suffering can change into joy, because the passage from suffering to joy is possible in principle, finding its possibility in suffering itself, in self-suffering as the essence of life's enjoyment. Joy and suffering are therefore never facing each other like the torturer his victim; their external relation is only the representation of their inner connection in each of those who enjoy and suffer. This representation is the representation of the absolute; it ex-poses and dis-joins the original components of the One, gives them to be seen. Among the horrors of ancient Greece that so fascinated Nietzsche was the sacrifice of a young man whose lacerated, bloody limbs were scattered so that his blood would fertilize the earth and give it life. "La philosophie de Nietzsche est ce meurtre rituel, elle est la dis-jonction et la pro-jection agrandie dans le ciel du mythe de la structure de la subjectivité absolue" (Nietzsche's philosophy is this ritual murder; it is the dis-junction and magnified pro-jection, into the sky of myth, of the structure of absolute subjectivity, p. 293). The sparagmos of Dionysus-Zagreus is Henry's image for Nietzsche's method: his mythical figures (strong and weak, torturer and victim, creditor and debtor) are the scattered limbs of one living self. Henry's own reading reassembles them. The chapter closes the "genealogy" proper and opens onto Freud's theory of the gods (Chapter VIII).

## Chapter VIII. The Gods Are Born and Die Together

In the light of the conceptions just set out, the many contradictions and paradoxes of Nietzsche's thought become clear. The gravest and most constant concern the problem of truth. They stem from the fact that, perhaps for the first time, at any rate so violently, truth is put in question in itself and as such. Not this or that content of experience or thought, this or that established "truth", is at issue, but the very idea that something is or could be true and so differs in principle from what does not attain "being true", hitherto more or less identified with the condition of existence and being. "What forces us at all to suppose that there is an essential opposition between 'true' and 'false'? Is it not enough to assume degrees of apparentness?" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 34). The chapter title is Nietzsche's, from "the gods die together", read with Freud's theory of religion in view; the opening question of truth prepares the reinterpretation of Freud's "reality" and "illusion".

Since truth becomes a problem or, more radically, does not exist, all the procedures aiming at it, all of knowledge and science, are shaken in their very *intentio* and raison d'être: the "will to truth" is at stake (p. 296). Nietzsche spoke pathetically of "seekers of knowledge"; he wanted to see in the English psychologists "at bottom brave, generous and proud animals who know how to control their hearts and pains and have learned to sacrifice all desirability to truth, every truth", however bitter or repugnant (*Genealogy*, I, 1). From moderns, instead of their "moralistic mendaciousness", their "dishonest lying", he expected a "genuine", "authentic", "deliberate", "honest" lie, which "would demand what one cannot demand of them, that they open their eyes to themselves, that they know how to distinguish 'true' and 'false' in themselves" (III, 19). He praised the French moralists who knew how to "clean their minds", while pitying "suffering men who do not want to admit to themselves what they are ... who fear only one thing: becoming conscious". The praise of the courageous psychologist willing to know the bitter truth about himself is the ethos psychoanalysis claimed for itself; Henry sets it up in order to show Nietzsche's reversal of it.

But if one asks who these suffering men are who want anything but clarity about themselves, the truth, who fear above all the light of consciousness, the text answers: precisely those inhabited by the teleology of consciousness who have undertaken to bring it to its end, the scientists. So the seekers of knowledge, those proud and courageous animals who disregarded their beliefs and most intimate convictions to dare face the whole truth, are condemned too: "we seekers after knowledge today, we godless anti-metaphysicians, still take our fire from the flame lit by a faith thousands of years old, the Christian faith which was also Plato's, that God is truth, that truth is divine" (*Gay Science*, § 344; *Genealogy*, III, 24). The antinomy that makes consciousness and the movement toward it their contrary, the principle or effect of blindness, reaches its highest tension where traditionally truth returns to itself to ground itself, the blessed land (p. 297) of self-knowledge as self-certainty. Here resounds the saying that, reversing Terence, shatters this gathering of truth in itself and the principle of all assured knowledge: "each is farthest from himself" (*Genealogy*, Preface, § 1; *Gay Science*, § 335). The reversal of Terence's "proximus sum egomet mihi" (I am nearest to myself) is the formula of self-opacity that psychoanalysis will adopt; Henry has to reconcile it with Nietzsche's "I am who I am".

But the same text declares, a few pages later, no less abruptly: "I am who I am" (*Genealogy*, III, 14). Considering this assertion, on which we have already relied, one sees that it is not marginal but the ultimate principle determining the problematic. The task was to understand "the sickness of life", how and why life turns against itself and aspires to destroy itself. What appeared was that the immense process of resentment and bad faith running through the human world and giving it its hideous face rests on an unshakeable ground: the suffering of the one who can no longer bear himself. If every empirical or worldly figuration of the one called weak is bracketed, a pure suffering remains whose phenomenological effectiveness is exhausted in its own affective tonality. Thus arises the idea of an original revelation constituted by affectivity as such and identical with it. This revelation alone defines being for Nietzsche: "« je suis » est devenu le cri de la souffrance ou plutôt sa matérialité même et sa chair" ("I am" has become the cry of suffering, or rather its very materiality and flesh, p. 297). The despair text of Chapter VII ("I am who I am: how could I get free of myself?") is now read as Nietzsche's *cogito*.

Considering this radical *cogito* of Nietzsche, in which being is established from a first affective appearing and as identical with its phenomenological substance, the texture of suffering and malaise, one sees that it is not exhausted in the mere positing of being but in a much more essential determination that refers being to itself and gives it to itself as it is. Insofar as representation is dismissed and appearing is no longer the mere showing of what is left there (p. 298), enigmatic and unexplored in itself, by the light of ecstasis falling on it; insofar as what appears is no longer separable from appearing, which no longer stands beyond it in its difference from it; insofar as what appears is now appearing itself in the original auto-affection of its affectivity, then being, this appearing, is no longer delivered as what simply is in general but, thrown into itself and referred to itself, delivered and bound to itself, experiencing itself and nothing but this pure self-experience, it is what it is. In suffering and through it, the positing of being is no longer written "I am" but, as Nietzsche decisively wishes, "I am who I am". The tautology of Exodus 3:14 is read as the formula of auto-affection: being given to itself as itself, not being in general. Henry will develop this in *C'est moi la vérité* (1996).

Nietzsche's *cogito* also proceeds from a reduction; it is what subsists at the end of the universal shaking, which, shown in the hyperbolic analysis of weakness, is no longer doubt but despair: within suffering and carried by it, its project-desire to escape itself, life's mad decision to break the bond that ties it to itself and constitutes its essence. What remains at the end of the shaking is precisely this bond, stronger than the project of breaking it and making that project weakness: the bond of being to itself as the suffering that throws it into itself and cannot be abolished: "I am what I am", forever, and to be so again eternally, in the eternal return of the same, "du Même que je suis en tant que je suis ce que je suis" (of the Same that I am insofar as I am what I am, p. 298). Despair replaces hyperbolic doubt; the residue is not a thinking thing but the self's bond to itself. The Eternal Return is again the eternity of this bond.

That Nietzsche's reduction is both sickness and liberation of life, what results from them or rather precedes and makes both possible, is his decisive intuition, since he always understands life from itself, as what proceeds and unfolds from itself. What this unfolding means is shown by interpreting life as will to power: it is not an ontic process, its triggering; it qualifies the original structure of being, the structure of absolute subjectivity as coming into itself in self-growth. The unfolding is ontological, not a biological development.

Nietzsche's thought is a thought of fullness. Fullness is not a state but the coming into itself of what never stops coming (p. 299) into itself and so being what it is. Nietzsche's being is thus never what it is in tautology but in becoming as becoming-oneself, which is the Present of life, this eternal coming into itself. To sickness, "those who never become themselves the present", Nietzsche opposes the essence of the present, life: "we, however, want to become those we are, human beings who are new, unique, incomparable, who give themselves laws, who create themselves!" (*Gay Science*, § 335). It is this fullness of life, in which life gives itself to itself as what it is full of and superabounds with, that religion originally aimed at and preludes: "a strange means of allowing a few particular men to enjoy the divine condition of self-sufficiency". So that one may ask whether "without this religious school and prehistory man would ever have learned to feel hunger and thirst for himself and to find satiety and fullness in himself" (§ 300). "Become who you are" (Pindar) as the Present of life is Henry's answer to the temporal ecstasis of Chapter VII: life's present is not a now between past and future but its self-coming. Religion as the prehistory of self-fullness prepares the chapter's thesis on God.

With this metaphysics of fullness a new concept of desire arises. If life is auto-affection and as such self-profusion in itself, how is anything like lack or need still possible? "Need is considered the cause of what comes to be; in truth it is often only an effect of what has come to be" (*Gay Science*, § 205). What has come to be, life in its inner building, what it gives itself, itself, is what it desires, as "need of itself", "hunger and thirst for oneself", as the historial of the absolute, the eternal coming into itself of what never stops coming to itself as what it is. "Pure adhésion à soi, l'être n'est que le désir de soi ; désir de soi, il n'est que pure adhésion à soi" (pure adherence to itself, being is only desire of itself; desire of itself, it is only pure adherence to itself, p. 299). This is the thought that came to Nietzsche to greet the New Year in Genoa, January 1882: "*Amor fati*: let that be my love henceforth! I do not want to wage war against what is ugly (p. 300). I do not want to accuse; I do not even want to accuse those who accuse. Looking away shall be my only negation. And all in all: some day I wish to be only a Yes-sayer" (§ 276). The thought of *amor fati* is that of eternal return. To "the question in each and every thing, 'do you want this once more and innumerable times more?'", it is life's innermost structure in its auto-affection that produces the answer: "how well disposed would you have to become to yourself and to life to crave nothing more fervently than this ultimate eternal confirmation and seal?" (§ 341). Desire as the effect, not the cause, of fullness is Henry's anti-Lacanian thesis: desire is life's adherence to itself, not the metonymy of a lack. *Amor fati* and the Eternal Return are the same thought: life's yes to itself, given by its structure, not by a decision.

The fullness of life, its eternal coming into itself, makes transparent its ultimate figure, nobility. That it is desire and embrace of itself means negatively the passionate refusal of all self-refusal: "nobility, whose characteristic will always be not to be afraid of itself, to expect nothing shameful of itself" (*Gay Science*, § 294). It is the mark of weakness and of all the negative virtues it begets, by contrast, to hold life back from itself and from the movement constitutive of its essence by which it never stops coming to itself to give itself to itself as it is: "I do not wish to strive consciously for my own impoverishment; I do not like negative virtues whose essence is negation and self-denial" (§ 304). Shame is again the affect of weakness, fear of oneself.

Such is the true reproach addressed, rightly or wrongly, to Christianity: that it does not adhere to this process of life's self-adherence, a reproach brandished, for once in a relaxed and humorous mode, in the antithesis of antiquity: "it [Christianity] has destroyed in each individual man the belief in his 'virtues'; it has made disappear forever those great virtuous figures in which antiquity abounded, those popular men convinced of their perfection who walked about with the dignity of a matador" (*Gay Science*, § 122). So certain of itself (p. 301) is life in its unconditional self-adherence that the "preachers of morals", all who persist in "finding nothing good in life", lie: "in truth they are irredeemably certain of their life, furiously in love with it". In antiquity itself Stoicism is only the lying misunderstanding of life's primordial certainty that precedes every stance toward it and secretly inhabits it: "we do not find ourselves badly enough off to have to feel bad in the Stoic manner" (§ 306). "Rightly or wrongly": Henry, who in *C'est moi la vérité* will read Christianity as a phenomenology of life, marks his distance from Nietzsche's critique of Christianity without arguing it here.

The radical phenomenological meaning of the structure of being as life, its unconditional coming into itself at every point of its being in the self-certainty of self-growth, introduces us to the heart of Nietzsche's problematic of values. The question of value immediately doubles, since every value proceeds from a prior evaluation that grounds it and so refers to an ultimate principle of evaluation, life. Why does life evaluate? How does it determine values? Does the principle of all evaluation itself have a value? If so, who confers this value on it, what principle other than it, whose value would itself be problematic? Or does it confer value on itself? For if the principle of all evaluation were without value, what value would its evaluation have, and how would the very idea of such evaluation and of value in general even occur to it? The regress of values parallels the regress of reflection in Chapter I and the regress of grounds in Chapter III; the answer will again be self-givenness.

Nietzsche answered these questions precisely. Things, beings in general, have no value in themselves. Nature has no value. Life alone confers on things all the value they can take on: "it knows that it is itself what first confers honour on things; it is value-creating". How does it know? Because it feels it: "the noble type of man [and we know it is nothing but the figure of life] feels itself as determining values". But what exactly does it feel (p. 302) while determining values? Answer: itself. What life feels insofar as it feels itself, its essence lying in auto-affection, determines values, so that these values, originally nothing but what life feels insofar as it feels itself, are originally nothing but this life itself and its own content. Restored in full, the text reads: "it knows that it is itself what first confers honour on things; it is value-creating. Everything it knows as part of itself it honours: such a morality is self-glorification" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 260). The step from "feels itself as determining values" to "feels itself" is Henry's: valuing is a modality of self-feeling.

What life finds in itself is not only its content of the moment. This content is such only insofar as it is given to life to be felt as what life itself is, insofar as life comes into it and never stops coming into it as into itself, insofar as its "feeling this content" is a "feeling itself". What life feels ultimately is itself in this ultimate sense: the fact of feeling itself. What life honours, what it glorifies, is its own essence. That is why the rest of the text, listing everything life finds in itself, honours and glorifies, lists something quite other than features proper to this life and its circumstances: the original phenomenological structures of life in general, the ontological determinations constitutive of its essence, which the text tirelessly restates: "such a morality is self-glorification. In the foreground is the feeling of fullness, of power that seeks to overflow, the happiness of high tension, the consciousness of wealth that would give and bestow". If a particular experience or action appears in the list and claims its share of praise, it is again the essence of life that is at issue: "the noble man too helps the unfortunate, but not, or almost not, from pity, rather from an urge begotten by excess of power" (§ 260). The list in § 260 (fullness, tension, overflow) is read as an inventory of the eidos of life.

Every value, we said, proceeds from a prior evaluation and depends on it. We now see that the opposite is true (p. 303). The original evaluation is life's self-glorification; it establishes as positive values the ontological characters of life, all that together compose its essence: power, happiness, fullness, will to give and bestow, superabundance, force, need born of the superabundance of force. But this evaluation, the positing of these values, is in reality rooted in them, or rather in what they glorify and value, life itself and its essence. It is life itself, the original, unconditional and eternal coming into itself of life that makes it life, that is of value, originally, unconditionally, and as such constitutes the principle of all possible evaluation and of all values, values that are nothing but the repetition, "sur le mode de l'irréalité archétypale" (in the mode of archetypal unreality, p. 303), of the vital accomplishments from which they proceed and under which they may subsume their eventual replicas. There are thus two series of values: what is of value originally, before any act of evaluation; and the values resulting from this act as the archetypal representation of what it itself proceeds from. Values as "archetypal unreality" are representations: the represented double of a living self-experience. This prepares the analysis of gods and ideals as represented life.

Why is life's original, unconditional, eternal coming into itself what is of value absolutely? "The 'good' ... the noble, powerful, high-stationed and high-minded, who felt and established themselves and their doings as good" (*Genealogy*, I, 2). "The 'well-born' felt themselves to be simply 'the happy'" (I, 10). On the ground of this primordial experience, in which the good feel themselves good and the well-born experience themselves as the happy, "the aristocratic mode of valuation" arises, spontaneously forming "its basic concept" of good, "a positive concept, full of life and passion", which is nothing but the doubling in immediate speech of what is said in it: "we the noble, the good, the beautiful, the happy!" (I, 10). "Good" designates nothing but the self-feeling (p. 304) of those who, feeling themselves so, then feel themselves good, the self-experience of those who, experiencing themselves so, experience themselves as the happy. "« Bien » désigne l'essence de la vie" ("good" designates the essence of life, p. 304). The aristocratic equation (good = noble = happy) is reduced to auto-affection.

Why is life's coming into itself the good, why is life good? Because in the self-suffering that constitutes it, life's coming into itself is its self-enjoyment and so enjoyment, Happiness. Good is happiness. Because the essence of life bears in itself, as what it necessarily produces, happiness, it is good. The chain suffering–joy–good derives value from Chapter VII's ontology of affect: life is good because self-suffering turns into self-enjoyment.

In the essence of life lies original phenomenality, truth in an absolute sense. In this absolute sense Truth is Life. This truth consubstantial with the essence of life, with their being, the good in turn formulate: "we the truthful" (*Genealogy*, I, 5). Not that they mean that they tell the truth and despise lying, but first and more essentially that they are in themselves Truth through and through, in the depths of their being, and Truth in its original form as it inhabits every conceivable form of truth and makes it possible: in themselves they are the Parousia. "*Gennaios*, 'noble by birth', stresses the nuance of 'sincerity' and perhaps 'naivety'", which is why "the noble man lives in trust and openness with himself" (I, 10); so that this frankness toward oneself is only the consequence, or rather the way of naming, life's original relation to itself as consisting in absolute Truth, its native essence, what it is "by birth". That the capacity to tell the truth, first about oneself and more, to say oneself as truth, rests on the original condition of Life as absolute Truth and thereby as the original essence of being and reality, is indicated by the comment on the word *esthlos* formed by the Greek nobility to designate itself: "*esthlos* means by its root one who is, who has reality, who is real (p. 305), true; then, by a shift of meaning, the true man as the truthful man" (I, 5). Henry's "Truth is Life" anticipates *C'est moi la vérité*. Nietzsche's etymology of *esthlos* (from *esti*) is used to connect truth with being and being with life.

But if each is in himself the Parousia of being and bears its essence, how can one also claim that "each is farthest from himself"? Did Nietzsche grossly contradict himself? Or did he mean two totally different things? Or, beneath the apparent contradiction, one and the same? The question frames the solution: two truths.

Since life, radically immanent, excludes ecstasis and with it whatever presents itself before, in the outside of any exteriority, according to the variable modes of a proximity whose essence is distance and is identical with it, then each, insofar as he is a living being and never stands in the proximity of the distant but far from it, much farther than the farthest horizon, each indeed "is farthest from himself". Insofar as he stands before himself and takes himself for what is there, for this man, at this moment of his life, in this situation resulting from this state of affairs and so on, he cannot do otherwise than take himself for something other than he is ("we cannot do otherwise than take ourselves for something other than we are", *Genealogy*, Preface, § 1), something other than life's eternal and unconditional coming into itself as what he is. That is why the Greeks built a theatre and isolated the stage with high walls that separate it forever from the City: because everything we experience in what is called life, everyday life and its busyness, the empirical individuals we think we see and know, all that presents its aspect or face in ecstasis, must be set aside and ignored, if need be covered with a mask, if life's coming into itself in the only conceivable form of its suffering and joy is to be accomplished and Dionysus to be present. "Farthest from oneself" means: life is infinitely farther than any horizon, because it is not in the space of distances at all. Self-opacity is a property of self-representation, not of life. The theatre's walls and masks figure the reduction.

Thus knowledge cannot be what brings us closer to the Essential by degrees of increasing proximity and, at the limit, "immediate" proximity. It stands infinitely far from it (p. 306), by principle. Rightly Nietzsche expressed his "distrust of the possibility of self-knowledge, which has led me", he says, "to sense a *contradictio in adjecto* even in the idea of 'immediate knowledge'" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 281). Only a few lines after this critique of immediate knowledge comes the affirmation of an absolute certainty inherent in and constitutive of the essence of life, unconditionally rejecting all ecstasis, the very possibility of seeking, approaching, finding, or departing and losing. Having denounced the craving for aristocratic values ("this craving is fundamentally different from the needs of a noble soul and is the eloquent and dangerous sign of its absence"), the text suddenly reveals the essence of the noble soul, of life: "some fundamental certainty that a noble soul has about itself, something that cannot be sought, nor found, nor perhaps lost" (§ 287). Henry's two quotations, a few lines apart, embody the two truths: immediate knowledge is contradictory (knowledge is ecstatic), yet there is a certainty not sought or found (life's self-givenness).

This being-with-itself of life, this way of being what one is such that one cannot aspire to it, desire or refuse it, such rather that desire is only the tireless accomplishment of this being-with-itself, of suffering what one is, enjoying and loving it, Nietzsche expresses in a still unheard-of concept of respect in which the original structuring of being is given as the first word of ethics: "the noble soul has reverence for itself" (§ 287). This self-respect Nietzsche also calls "faith in oneself, pride in oneself" (§ 260), faith, pride and self-confidence whose inner essence, being's Parousia in itself, helps us better understand the "egoism" of the stars and explains negatively the repeated critique of "selflessness", which claims to circumvent the law of being that throws it into itself to fill it with what it is. Self-reverence as "the first word of ethics" sketches Henry's ethics of life, developed in *La barbarie*.

That this Parousia of being in itself (p. 307) is not knowledge, that it excludes knowledge and ignores even its possibility, that it is pure ignorance in this sense, must be kept in mind not to misunderstand life's absolute certainty in Nietzsche's sense, which is nothing but the essence of life itself, its being-with-itself as living. This ignorance makes life's naivety and explains more precisely why, never rising above itself, it cannot see, perceive or understand itself as what it is. "Exceptional men", says Nietzsche, "do not experience themselves as exceptions". The higher nature, whose taste goes to the exception, "usually believes it judges by no singular standard; it rather sets up its values and disvalues as generally valid". It does not compare because it does not know. It does not know because it is pathos and, locked in itself, experiencing only itself, passion believes in nothing other than itself: "it believes in its passion as the secret passion of all" (*Gay Science*, § 3). That is why life has such difficulty imagining what is the negation of its essence: "vanity is perhaps one of the things hardest for a noble man to understand" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 261). What is inconceivable is the structure of a being whose being would be its appearing-to-others, a structure that reigns wherever knowledge extends its essence, since being by principle escapes knowledge, since each is farthest from himself. Vanity (being as being-seen) is incomprehensible to life: Henry's point against every theory of the self as constituted by the gaze of others (Sartre, Lacan's mirror stage).

What happens with Nietzsche, following Schopenhauer's decisive work, is that the essence of truth no longer resides in knowledge or its ground. Such is the decisive mutation, the displacement of original phenomenality, identical with truth, from the medium of ecstasis to the essence of life. If what knowledge ultimately aims at, the Parousia of being, is still to be possible, it is only in and through life. "Life as a means to knowledge" is the discovery of *The Gay Science*, celebrated lyrically (p. 308): "from the day the great liberator came to me, the thought that life could be an experiment of the seeker for knowledge" (§ 324). But such knowledge is possible only if it presents nothing other than itself, no ob-ject, if it is no dehiscence but the very essence of life: "we want to be our own experiments and guinea pigs" (§ 319). The gay science does not designate a happy knowledge, the joy of knowing or of fruitful theoretical work, but a knowledge consisting in gaiety, a knowledge whose sapiential essence is exhausted in the revealing phenomenality of affectivity as such. The *gaya scienza* is affectivity as knowledge: Henry's most compressed formula for the "knowledge of life" (*savoir de la vie*) invoked in the repression analysis (Chapter VI).

For Nietzsche there are therefore two dissimilar and heterogeneous truths. In their light the contradictions affecting the apparent content of Nietzsche's discourse on truth dissolve. Juxtaposed to the celebration of the truthful essence of life, the critique of ecstatic knowledge is radical. To it *The Gay Science* explicitly refers the principled impossibility of self-knowledge: "'each is farthest from himself': all who try the reins know this to their discomfort, and the maxim 'know thyself', addressed to human beings by a god, is almost malicious" (§ 335). Why the Socratic maxim has no meaning for Nietzsche, and why its failure, far from concerning the inner essence of life or of the self as living, depends on knowledge and expresses only its own powerlessness, the nescience of knowledge as such, is shown by the critique of Socratism. Socrates claimed to judge life's primordial knowledge by the measure of the secondary knowledge of cognition. He then no longer understood the perfection of immediate action in the absence of knowledge and saw in this perfection of life only the sign of ignorance and absurdity (p. 309): "what surprised him was to find that the celebrities had no correct and sure insight even about their own professions and practised them only by instinct"; "wherever it directs its probing glance, it sees lack of insight and the power of illusion, and it concludes from this lack that what exists is inwardly wrong and objectionable" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 13). "Testers of souls" (*Seelenprüfer*) are the psychologists, of whom the analyst is the latest: Henry's choice of § 335 links the critique of self-knowledge to the analytic situation.

The critique of knowledge concerns ordinary knowledge, its systematic development in science, and consciousness in general. What disqualifies every mode of spontaneous or reflective knowledge, what places it from the start outside what matters and makes it unable ever to meet it, is life's principled impossibility of appearing in the open medium of ecstasis. "La critique de la connaissance a un a priori. Cet a priori, c'est l'essence de la vie" (the critique of knowledge has an a priori: the essence of life, p. 309). Only one who represents to himself the essence of life understands a priori why knowledge, and science in particular, inevitably misses it: "the problem of science cannot be recognized on the ground of science", Nietzsche says in a decisive proposition (*Birth of Tragedy*, "Attempt at a Self-Criticism", § 2). One who knows, as long as he lives in the intention of knowledge and is guided by it, does not know that he knows nothing, nothing essential, and would never know it if the essential, the essence of life, were not given to him elsewhere, in and by life. "One who represents to himself the essence of life" is a careless phrase in Henry's own text; his argument requires that this essence be given, not represented.

No less important, aphorism 344 of *The Gay Science* declares that "the truthful man, in that daring and ultimate sense presupposed by faith in science, thereby affirms another world than that of life". Nietzsche calls the other of life metaphysical; metaphysical, therefore, knowledge, science, truth as the truth of knowledge and science. Taking up the *Gay Science* aphorism ("it is still a metaphysical faith on which our faith in science rests"), the *Genealogy* can denounce "faith in a metaphysical value, a value in itself (p. 310) of truth" (III, 24), insofar as this truth is not that of life. For it is precisely the truth of life that condemns the truth of knowledge and dispossesses it a priori of any real content. "Metaphysics" in Nietzsche's sense (another world than life) coincides with Henry's ecstasis: the world as the outside of life.

Knowledge not only ignores the original essence of life; it actually proceeds from it, as the doing of a life turned against itself, a reactive life inhabited by resentment that secretly aspires to rid itself of itself. What ultimately determines the project of trusting knowledge, the project of science, is weakness. That a sick life is the origin of the grandiose productions of human, especially scientific, knowledge is Nietzsche's disconcerting but constant intuition: "science rests on the same foundation as the ascetic ideal: a certain impoverishment of life, a cooling of the affects" (*Genealogy*, III, 25). And: "consider the periods in history when the scholar steps into the foreground: they are times of exhaustion, often of evening and decline, deserted by overflowing strength and certainty of life" (III, 25). The genealogy of science from weakness sets up the critique of Freud's scientism in Chapter IX.

What happens when life has lost its self-certainty, the certainty in which it consists, that is, when it is no longer there, is a rigorous ontological situation, the one disclosed by the scientific view of the world: a world where everything is objective and nothing subjective. Speaking of "the objective man" who "is only an instrument", "a mirror", who is "no 'end in himself'", Nietzsche adds: "whatever still remains in him of a 'person' seems to him accidental, arbitrary ... troublesome, so much has he become a passageway and reflection of foreign forms and events" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 207). Man is then no longer the dwelling in which being comes to itself in the absolute subjectivity of Life, no longer its historial or Parousia; he is no longer, says Nietzsche, "child of God", he has become "more arbitrary" and, in accordance with "his will to self-diminution", which "has been progressing steadily since Copernicus", something very small indeed (p. 311), something objective and so a tiny particle of the objective universe whose importance, Kant (quoted by Nietzsche) said, astronomy had "annihilated" (*Genealogy*, III, 25). The insertion of life, stripped of its inner essence, into the scientific domain makes it explicable by that domain, so that "the cheerfulness of theoretical man ... believes that knowledge can correct the world and science guide life, and is in fact able to confine the individual within a very narrow circle of soluble problems, from which he can cheerfully say to life: 'I want you; you are worth knowing'" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 18). The "objective man" as "mirror" recalls Schopenhauer's knowing subject as "mirror" (end of Chapter VI). The Copernican humiliation is the first of Freud's three "blows to human narcissism" ("A Difficulty in the Path of Psycho-Analysis"); Henry sets Nietzsche's reading of it against Freud's pride in it.

But life cannot be known. That is why scholars "are no longer useful where the 'great hunt' begins", whose "predestined hunting ground" is "the human soul ... the range of man's inner experiences hitherto, the heights, depths and distances of these experiences, the whole previous history of the soul and its still unexhausted possibilities" (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 45). Nietzsche's harsh and strange judgments on scholars ("their 'scientific drive' is their boredom"; "they are all vanquished men brought back under the rule of science") reduce to tautological propositions expressing only an observation: the pure and simple exteriority of life to the domain of science and of knowledge in general. By contrast the task of the philosopher appears unusual, who "demands of himself a judgment, a Yes or No, not about the sciences but about life and the value of life", a judgment that is not one and designates rather what by principle escapes all theory: a mode of life. "He feels the burden and the duty of a hundred attempts and temptations of life: he risks himself constantly, he plays the dangerous game" (§ 205). That life is essentialized (p. 312) as the other of knowledge and its developments and, in its original affectivity, as man's innermost being, the mythical world of Greece shows in an archetypal image: "nature untouched by knowledge, as yet unbolted against the intrusions of culture, that is what the Greek saw in his satyr ... it was the archetype of man, the expression of his highest and strongest emotions" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 8). The "great hunt" in the soul is the territory psychoanalysis claimed; Nietzsche, Henry stresses, denies it to science.

Is it really science, though, or knowledge, that is at issue in all these texts where the traditional concept of truth collapses? Is it not rather life itself, its declining forms more precisely, insofar as humanity's theoretical thought draws its ultimate motivation from weakness and lets itself be determined by it? But what is weakness? Not, as has been said enough, a form of life, a decadent life, as a quick reading suggests, but life's anti-essence, at any rate its project, that of breaking immanence. Such a rupture is nothing but ecstasis. Precisely when the ecstatic process of knowledge turns toward life, as with the imperative "know thyself", the ontological heterogeneity of the first and the second comes to light, as Nietzsche recognized in contesting the existence of immediate knowledge, and as weakness itself shows more radically still, as life's impossible ecstasis. But if life and ecstatic dehiscence are incompatible, how can they cohabit in man; how is their relation to be understood? The question restates the problem of Schopenhauer's repression (Chapter VI): how can a blind will and a powerless representation be joined?

It is Nietzsche's genius to have seen from the start the problem Schopenhauer left gaping and to have brought it, instinctively, a still unheard-of answer, by which a radical phenomenology leads back to the ultimate foundations of being. Schopenhauer: "the world as will and representation", two heterogeneous, mutually irreducible essences, since "will" bears no representation in itself and representation no will, no power. In *The Birth of Tragedy* will (p. 313) has become Dionysus, the essence of life, the way it never stops impressing itself according to the eternal play of the fundamental tonalities of suffering and joy. Representation has become Apollo, or rather, and this is Nietzsche's second contribution, the projection of exteriority is never left to itself and its illusory autonomy but grasped in its essential interweaving with affectivity, or rather in its own affectivity, insofar as the ecstatic bursting that constantly brings about a world and the medium of all possible affection never ceases, in the tireless accomplishment of its transcendence, to affect itself and so to experience itself as life. Apollo is not simply representation, nor, as transcendental imagination, what ensures its deployment and its principled condition; he is the innermost possibility of this deployment itself, "l'Imago du monde aperçue dans son Fond affectif" (the Imago of the world seen in its affective Ground, p. 313). Stronger in *The Birth of Tragedy* than the opposition of the two principles is their unity, an essential unity that drives Nietzsche's thought and makes Dionysus and Apollo bound by a secret affinity, so that far from fighting, or beneath their apparent fight, they go together, help each other, "naissent et meurent en même temps" (are born and die together). That is why, when the sacrilegious Euripides claimed to bend myth to the service of thought instead of letting it speak one last time the Dionysian ground of life, he killed it, and music with it. "And because you had abandoned Dionysus, Apollo abandoned you" (§ 10). But the relation of appearance to its affective ground is complex; its history in the young Nietzsche's problematic must be relived step by step. The chapter title is explained: Apollo (representation) lives only from Dionysus (affect). "Transcendental imagination" names Heidegger's *Kantbuch* reading of the common root; Henry places Apollo below it, as the affective possibility of imagination itself.

At a first stage (not chronological but a degree in the series of implications the analysis traverses), still wholly determined by Schopenhauer's explicit theses, representation is understood as what delivers us from the will. Representation could deliver from the will (p. 314) only if it were itself delivered from the will. This at least is the power Schopenhauer lends to what has no power. As if setting before oneself really emptied the power accomplishing this setting of what it had borne until then, as if objectification were a real objectification, a real translation of what was hitherto within life, projected for good outside it, into a real exteriority. That happens at least once according to Schopenhauer, in art. Aesthetic contemplation is the true objectification that, by putting outside us this horrible ground of desire and pain, properly frees us from it. This real transubstantiation has a secondary, quasi-magical effect: transforming the horrible into the beautiful. From Schopenhauer, then, Nietzsche borrows the answer to "the fundamental question" he then asks, "the question of the Greek's relation to pain". Except that representation has received the name Apollo, that is, the world in general has become an aesthetic world, whose appearance, by itself and through a power and beauty of its own, those of appearing as such, frees us from the terrors of Dionysus: "the world as at every moment the attained deliverance of God, as the eternally changing, eternally new vision of the most suffering, most contradictory, most discordant being, who can find deliverance only in appearance" ("Attempt at a Self-Criticism", § 5). This shows the ultimate motive of this "imaginary desire for appearance" that is identically that of art, that is Apollo, for, as Nietzsche says, "the more I become aware in nature of those all-powerful art impulses and, in them, of an ardent longing for appearance", the more the hypothesis forces itself on him that "the truly existent primordial One, as eternal suffering and contradiction, also needs, for its continuous (p. 315) redemption, the rapturous vision, the pleasurable appearance" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 4). A note adds that in *The Birth of Tragedy* the will is still "the unaesthetic in itself", so that music can only reproduce it, whereas later the essence of life will constitute beauty itself ("we the beautiful", *Genealogy*, I, 10); another notes that "pleasurable appearance" is taken from Schopenhauer. Representation as "deliverance by projection" is the model of what Freud will call defence: projection, sublimation, the dream as wish-fulfilment. Henry reconstructs it in Nietzsche before criticizing it.

What does "deliverance" consist in if it no longer concerns the "will" but what the will has become in Nietzsche, the passion of being, its being riveted to itself in the eternal play of its tonalities? It is no longer from an ontic principle designated from outside, willing, but from the inner structure of absolute subjectivity as life, from itself, that life must now rid itself. That is how Nietzsche understands it: "this Apollinian illusion ... has the effect of relieving us of the Dionysian burden and excessive weight" (§ 21). Likewise when, regarding music, which is the immediate reproduction of life and so bears in itself the play of fundamental affective tonalities and their indefinite modulation, he speaks of the relief lyric poetry, as "imitative effulguration of music in images and concepts", can bring to this too heavy Dionysian content, the same word "discharge" [*entladen*] comes spontaneously to Nietzsche's pen: "the process by which music discharges itself in images ..." (§ 6). Much later a significant occurrence arises when the ascetic priest undertakes to relieve the countless herd of the ill-constituted, those who suffer from themselves and are as such "fiercely discontented with themselves": "every sufferer instinctively seeks a cause for his suffering ... an agent ... a guilty agent ... on whom he can, actually or in effigy, on some pretext or other, discharge his affects: for the discharge of affects is the greatest attempt at relief on the part of the sufferer" (*Genealogy*, III, 15). The illusory denunciation of the causes of suffering fabricated by resentment changes nothing in the fundamental situation that supports this whole process, which is, once again, the pure and simple fact of suffering, or rather, hidden in it, the essence of life as self-suffering. *Entladung*, discharge: the central term of Freud's economics appears in Nietzsche in exactly the sense Freud will give it (discharge of affect as relief). Henry's genealogy of Freud is here at its most precise.

"Discharge" then takes on a singular sense (p. 316) if it is understood with regard to what is charged with itself forever. To be unable to discharge oneself of oneself and yet undertake to do so, to undertake the projection outside oneself of what one bears in oneself as too heavy, is to objectify it no longer in the sense of a real objectification capable of accomplishing the real translation from inside to outside, but in the sense of mere representation, to produce its content in exteriority, but as an unreal image. A decisive intuition of Schopenhauer allows Nietzsche to resolve masterfully the aporetic problem of the representation of the "will": the unreality of the world makes it possible to posit desire and suffering in it, insofar as they will appear there only in this unreality, as a dream. To discharge oneself, then, of what life, as bearing itself, contains of a priori unbearable is to project its image into a world born of this very projection as a dream world, where desire seeks, in the contemplation of its own unreal plastic representation, forgetfulness and appeasement of its malaise. Like art, and similar to it, the mythical universe of Greece is the product of this projection: "the same impulse that calls art into being as the complement and consummation of existence, seducing one to continue living, was also the cause of the Olympian world in which the Hellenic 'will' held up a transfiguring mirror to itself"; "the Greek knew and felt the terror and horror of existence; to be able to live at all, he had to interpose between himself and life the radiant dream-birth of the Olympians" (§ 3). This is the key to the chapter's title and to Henry's coming critique of Freud's theory of religion (*The Future of an Illusion*): the gods are life's projected dream, a discharge that does not discharge. The unreality of the image is what makes the "projection" possible; nothing is actually removed from life.

That affectivity does not objectify itself, but only its image, and that, further, the process of exteriorization that throws before does not throw itself before but remains in itself in its very accomplishment, insofar as it never stops impressing itself, means: "irréelle dans son contenu transcendant, la représentation est affective dans son principe et dans son fond" (unreal in its transcendent content, representation is affective in its principle and ground, p. 316). This is from the start Nietzsche's intuition and his first departure (p. 317) from the Schopenhauerian corpus. The dreamer, or if one prefers the aesthete, or one who perceives the world by seeing it as it is, is never the mere spectator of what takes form before his eyes; he bears its original content in himself as what he enjoys and suffers and finally as his very life. Speaking of the man capable of artistic emotion, Nietzsche says that "the whole 'divine comedy' of life, including the *Inferno*, passes before him, not like a mere shadow play, for he lives and suffers in these scenes". But since the essence of life is the historial of its own tonalities, and "projecting" its suffering "outside oneself" to "discharge" it is only to leave it where it is to accomplish on itself the inner movement of its transformation into joy, it must also be said that the ground of our being "experiences in dream a profound pleasure and a joyous necessity" (§ 1), and the "deep inner joy produced by dream contemplation" must be recognized (§ 4). Henry rereads the dream: it does not remove suffering into an image but occasions the self-transformation of suffering into joy. This is the alternative to Freud's dream as disguised wish-fulfilment, where pleasure comes from discharge.

The critique of the Schopenhauerian, and first Kantian, conception of beauty becomes explicit in the *Genealogy*. Kant is reproached precisely for confusing "the attributes of the beautiful" with those of knowledge (impersonality, universality), substituting the spectator's point of view for the creator's in defining aesthetic experience. But the "creator" here is not only, evidently and explicitly, the creator of the work, the artist; it is the process of producing representation in general, which is implied in every process of artistic creation (just as artistic creation is no doubt already virtually present in every process of representation, insofar as the world as such is an aesthetic phenomenon). Because this process of production is affective at its ground, its affectivity determines the creator's act (hence Nietzsche's irony about Kant's famous "beautiful is what pleases without interest") but also, insofar as it is itself representation, the being of the spectator: "if only our aestheticians had known this 'spectator' well enough, namely as a great personal fact and experience, as an abundance of vivid authentic experiences, desires, surprises and delights in the realm of the beautiful!" (p. 318; *Genealogy*, III, 6). The spectator too lives what he sees; disinterested contemplation is a fiction. Henry prepares *Voir l'invisible*, on Kandinsky, where painting expresses the invisible affective ground of seeing.

To discharge what is too heavy in life by giving oneself its appeased representation in the Apollinian dream does not abolish the affective ground of existence, as we now know, but only gives it occasion, by producing the dream image, to transform itself according to its own laws, actualizing the fundamental phenomenological potentialities that constitute it. This profound view leads Nietzsche not to be satisfied with the Apollinian conception of art, because the dream, in its affectivity, leads back to affectivity. This surpassing of representation toward what, always hidden in it, produces and ultimately grounds it, is shown by the genesis of tragic myth, of which Nietzsche says that "it shares with the Apollinian sphere of art the full delight in appearance and contemplation, and yet it denies this delight and finds still higher satisfaction in the annihilation of the visible world of appearance". That is why the Apollinian image in its highest forms appears to us as "only a likeness that we wish to draw aside like a curtain to see behind it the primordial image". Likewise in musical dissonance, Nietzsche asks, "is there not the same will to hear and at the same time the longing to go beyond hearing?" (§ 24). Thus a circular process arises by which suffering, in order to detach itself from itself, projects the image of the drama, the heroes and their suffering, the reflection of its own, its aesthetic and appeasing double, yet such that, the obscure and ever-present source of its ecstatic vision, it never ceases to experience itself in itself as what it is. "Ainsi Apollon ne nous sauve-t-il qu'en apparence du drame originel qui continue de se jouer en nous" (Thus Apollo saves us only apparently from the original drama that continues to be played in us, p. 318). Nietzsche's aim is therefore not (p. 319) to ask for salvation from the "saving magic of Apollo" alone but to abandon oneself to the invisible power that produces it and to its own play. But the ultimate understanding of the relation of Dionysus and Apollo in Nietzsche takes the path of individuality, which cannot be dispensed with. "Saves only apparently from the drama played in us": the same verdict will fall on Freud's cure, which saves by representation.

Representation was, for Schopenhauer, the principle of individuation. Nietzsche's critique of the individual, or more exactly that of *The Birth of Tragedy* alone, merely repeats the critique of representation, the assertion that what constitutes the reality of being lies beyond it. Certainly this representation in its unreality is not nothing, since it is first asked to deliver us from reality. This threefold meaning of representation (individuation, unreality, deliverance), which also constitutes the first concept of aesthetics, determines the being of Apollo and his mission: "Apollo appears to us as the deification of the *principium individuationis*, in which alone the eternally attained goal of the primordial One, its redemption through appearance, is consummated". Nietzsche circumscribed at a stroke the ontological, aesthetic, ethical and epistemological dimension of the Apollinian, of representation secretly understood from its ground: "Apollo, as ethical deity, demands due proportion of his followers and, so that they may observe it, self-knowledge" (§ 4). Apollo's "know thyself" links the critique of self-knowledge to the principle of individuation: knowledge of oneself is knowledge of the individuated, represented self.

The admirable commentary on the third act of *Tristan* described unforgettably Apollo's saving work in the light of this fundamental category of individuality. "When one has, as here, put one's ear to the heart chamber of the world will and felt the roaring desire for existence pouring from it into all the veins of the world, as a thundering current or as the gentlest brook, how could one fail (p. 320) to break suddenly? How could one, enclosed in the wretched glass bell of human individuality, endure to hear the echo of countless shouts of pleasure and woe from 'the wide space of the world night' ..." From this emotion too strong and that would break us the Apollinian myth saves us by diverting our attention onto it, so that "however powerfully pity affects us" for its heroes, "it nevertheless saves us from the primordial suffering of the world" (§ 21). In this diversion consists the illusion by which Apollo protects us: chained to these individuals who are the protagonists of the drama, to the scene where Tristan lies motionless and dying ("the sea is empty and waste"), we believe we see "only a particular image of the world" instead of experiencing in ourselves the overflowing effusion of its essence in universal pain. And of the music that immediately reproduces this pain, the "saving magic" of myth makes us believe that it "is meant to represent an Apollinian content". Pity for represented individuals is a diversion from the primordial suffering: the same structure as the affect "bound" to a representation.

But do not these famous analyses already presuppose, like Schopenhauer's on egoism, pity and cruelty, the secret doubling of the concept of individuality? For the individual who "diverts our ecstasy" onto himself, "chains our pity", "satisfies our sense of beauty that longs for great and sublime forms", "excites our pity", the individual represented on the stage, is he the same as the one crushed by pain who wants to discharge its excessive weight? Is not the one before us, before himself, while the other suffocates from being himself and from being unable to institute between himself and himself the first gap by which he could escape himself and the oppressiveness of his being? "Dans une proposition qui contient le freudisme à venir" (in a proposition that contains the Freudianism to come, p. 320), Nietzsche speaks of "the danger that threatens the person from within" (§ 21). It is therefore not an adventitious danger, not even a threat tied to this person's own history. "Le danger est la personne elle-même" (the danger is the person itself, p. 321), its interiority; it is the structure of absolute subjectivity insofar as it is thrown inexorably into itself to experience what it experiences and be what it is. "Le plus grand danger est la vie" (the greatest danger is life). To this danger Nietzsche said yes. It is the greatest risk he assumes in *amor fati*, to which he consents in the thought of the eternal return of the same, which, as we have suggested, is nothing but the essence of life as its indefinite reiteration in the form of its tireless coming into itself. From this danger Apollinian art tries to save us. To this danger Dionysian art abandons and entrusts itself. "The danger that threatens the person from within" is the Freudian "internal danger" (the drive), against which the ego defends itself. Henry: the internal danger is not a drive pressing from an unconscious, but the self's being given to itself, which no defence can escape.

As in Schopenhauer, there is not one individual in Nietzsche but two. To the one that proceeds from the ecstatic structure of representation and finds in the mutual exteriority of the pure parts of the transcendental medium of intuition the principle of its place, differentiation, form, limit and beauty, is decisively opposed the one that rests in the essence of life. The first, the Apollinian individual, finds its ultimate condition of possibility in the second, being only its image, "as it were the medium through which the one truly existent subject celebrates its redemption in appearance" (§ 5): the self-image the Original Individual projects outside itself in an attempt to rid itself of itself and its suffering, of this original Self that consists in its suffering as life's self-suffering. The doubling of the individual parallels Chapter V (individuation by representation vs. the will's individuality): the Apollinian individual is the represented self, the "ego" as object; the Dionysian is ipseity.

One must therefore be careful not to distort completely Nietzsche's concept of individuality by taking as one's guide the descriptions of Dionysian art that aim at the *Erlösung*, the redemption of the individual as liberation from the chains of intuitive individuation. Such descriptions abound in *The Birth of Tragedy*. When the Apollinian Greek feels "that his entire existence, with all its beauty and moderation, rested on a hidden substratum of suffering" (§ 4) and the project of the imaginary is reversed (p. 322) into the Dionysian experience of life and its mystical jubilation, it is always the bursting of the individual that is given as essential to this experience, to the point of seeming to constitute it: "under the mystical cry of jubilation of Dionysus the spell of individuation is broken and the way lies open to the Mothers of Being, to the innermost heart of things" (§ 16). That, according to Nietzsche, is the esoteric meaning of tragedy and the mysteries: to restore, in this abolition of a world of suffering fragmented into individuals, "the oneness of everything existent" (§ 10). The "Mothers of Being" (from Goethe's *Faust*) are the image Henry earlier connected with pain as "Mother of Being". The danger he flags is reading Dionysian ecstasy as dissolution of the self into an impersonal One.

But perhaps we should recognize here what we detected at the heart of the Apollinian experience. "It is Apollo", said Nietzsche, "who shows us how this whole world of torment is necessary in order that the individual may be impelled to generate the redeeming vision" (§ 4): an individual who is therefore not first the beautiful form individuated by representation but what produces it and so necessarily precedes it, as escaping it, as "the primordial suffering of the world" that seeks to flee itself in representation before consenting to itself in Dionysus. And when the Dionysian experience occurs, with intuitive individuality put out of play, what it frees is this primordial passion of the suffering of being as the very essence of ipseity. That is why "the suffering Dionysus of the Mysteries, the god who experiences in himself the sufferings of individuation" experiences them "in himself" (§ 10). That is why it is also said that "in his heroic effort toward the universal, in his attempt to transgress the bounds of individuation and to become himself the one essence of the world, the individual must suffer in himself the primordial contradiction concealed in things" (§ 9), a contradiction which in the text's Schopenhauerian language designates the suffering that Nietzsche discovers in Dionysus to be inseparable from the intoxication of his jubilation, so that this historial of being in its suffering and enjoying (p. 323) is identically what makes it a Self and the essence of life. The repeated "in himself" (*an sich selbst*) is Henry's evidence: Dionysian suffering is borne by a self, never by an impersonal flux.

The conditions of empirical individuality, in the end, that is, representation, are set aside so that the Parousia may come: "the dithyrambic chorus is a chorus of transformed beings who have completely forgotten their civic past and social rank and, living outside all social spheres, have become the timeless servants of their god" (§ 8), so that "the shattering of the individual" can only mean "his oneness with primordial being". But with the "forgetting" that seizes the dithyrambic chorus, with the "complete self-forgetfulness" that characterizes "Dionysian emotions" (§ 1), we are led back to what we recognized as the fundamental category of life, its immanence. Reduced to its "self-forgetfulness" and defined by it, the disappearance of the individual means only its disappearance from the sphere of thought, its belonging to the original dimension of being as life's affectivity: "the individual, with all his restraint and proportion, succumbed to the self-forgetfulness of the Dionysian states and forgot the precepts of Apollo. Excess revealed itself as truth. Contradiction, the bliss born of pain, spoke out from the very heart of nature" (§ 4). That the disappearance of the individual from ecstatic appearance is only the liberation of the one who, as the Self of suffering, coincides with the ground of things, is said of the Dionysian musician, who "is himself nothing but primordial pain and its primordial echo", and of the "lyric poet", who, "as the moving centre of this world, may say 'I'", so that "this 'I' is not that of the waking, empirically real man, but the only truly existent and eternal I resting at the ground of being" (§ 5). "Self-forgetfulness" is life's immanent forgetting from Chapter VII, not loss of self. The lyric "I" resting at the ground of being is Henry's chief textual evidence for an individual, non-empirical selfhood in Nietzsche.

The rest of the work makes this emergence of the individual at the heart of reality, and its belonging to it as an essential determination, manifest everywhere (p. 324). The explicit theoretical stance toward Schopenhauer is concentrated in the critique of "the unprovable doctrine of one will" and of what ultimately grounds it, "the denial of the individual" (*Gay Science*, § 99). Because it belongs to the ground of being, the individual becomes the principle and criterion of Nietzsche's evaluation: all forms of life that exalt it are exalted, all that threaten it repudiated. The aristocratic sense, the feeling of being a Self grounded on oneself and as such independent and different from every other, is only the formulation of the original essence of life's ipseity, if self-sufficiency has its ontological ground in auto-affection, is exhausted in it and refers to it: "the aristocratic sense, the will to depend only on oneself, the power to be different ..." (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 212). While "the inner insecurity that lies at the ground of all dependent men" expresses the sickness of life, the shaking of the Ground's self-sufficiency. Nietzsche's break with Schopenhauer on the "one will" is read as the affirmation of ipseity against impersonal metaphysics.

Remarkably, in many passages the individuation of original being is established by reaffirming the radically immanent character of the essence in which ipseity unfolds. So the individual essence of every possible action implies its impossibility of occurring in the ecstatic Dimension of phenomenality, its impossibility of being the object of any appreciation, comparison, demonstration or knowledge. That is notably why morality's claim to prescribe to each how he should act in a given case is meaningless: "there are not, and cannot be, identical actions ... every action performed was performed in an altogether unique way ... all prescriptions of action relate only to the coarse exterior ... they may bring about an appearance (p. 325) of sameness, but only an appearance ... every action, when one looks at it or recollects it, is and remains impenetrable ... our opinions about what is 'good', 'noble', 'great' can never be proved by our actions, because every action is unknowable" (*Gay Science*, § 335). A note adds a related text: "through morality the individual is led to be a function and to ascribe value to himself only as a function" (§ 116). The unknowability of action rejoins the "incomprehensibility" of action in Chapter VII; ethically, it disqualifies every external norm, and epistemologically, every interpretation of acts by their motives.

Revealing the true meaning of Nietzsche's refusal of pity, the extraordinary aphorism 338 of *The Gay Science* leads the problematic of the individual back to its ultimate ontological foundations. Pity is questioned twice: is it advantageous to those who suffer? To those who pity? As to the sufferers, the very project of behaving toward their suffering as something we could appreciate, circumscribe, explain, relieve, know, misunderstands it in principle, misunderstands the decisive fact that suffering occurs entirely in a dimension of being to which there is no other access than this suffering itself. "What we most deeply and most personally suffer from is incomprehensible and inaccessible to almost everyone else." Placed under the gaze of knowledge or pity, suffering is disfigured: "but whenever people notice that we suffer, they interpret our suffering superficially". That is why "it is the very essence of the emotion of pity that it strips away from the suffering of others whatever is distinctively personal", why "our 'benefactors' are, more than our enemies, the ones who make us smaller" (§ 338). The critique of pity's "explanation" of suffering is aimed, beyond Schopenhauer, at the therapeutic interpretation of suffering: explaining it "most superficially" is what any theory does.

But here, more abyssal, is the second reason to reject all pity for the sufferer: suffering is not only what, in its radical immanence, escapes all ecstatic approach; as such, as belonging to the inner building of being and as its coming into itself, it opens us to being, being the original experience being has of itself. The pitying soul that wants to help forgets "that there is a personal necessity (p. 326) of distress, that terrors, deprivations, impoverishments, midnights, adventures, risks and blunders are as necessary for me and for you as their opposites, and that ... the path to one's own heaven always leads through the voluptuousness of one's own hell". As refusal of suffering, as "religion of comfortableness", pity is nothing less than the refusal of the law of being and its historial: "how little you know of human happiness, you comfortable and benevolent people, for happiness and unhappiness are sisters and even twins that either grow up together or, as in your case, remain small together" (§ 338). The twins of happiness and unhappiness restate the historial of suffering-joy. The objection to a therapy that would eliminate suffering is clear without being stated: it would stunt joy as well.

Once individuality has been led back to the original place where it resides, in the suffering essence of life, the Dionysus–Apollo relation can no longer be thought from the criterion of the individual, which is not only the Apollinian individual of the *principium individuationis* and plastic beauty but the "I" of Dionysus himself and of each of his followers. In truth, as shown, the Dionysus–Apollo relation is only Nietzsche's interpretation of Schopenhauer's relation of will and representation, become that of affectivity and representation. This mutation is decisive not only because it deeply inflects the general conception of life but, and this is what concerns us now, because it makes intelligible (p. 327) what remained aporetic in Schopenhauer: the very possibility of such a relation, whose impossibility in principle, as long as the will remains unconscious, was shown by the critique of Schopenhauer's theory of repression. Whence does the interweaving of the Imago of the world and the affective Ground of being derive its intelligibility in Nietzsche, if not from a radical phenomenological thought that grasps the power producing representation no longer as an ontic determinant unable to know what it does, but as "l'Archi-Révélation de l'Imago la connaissant avant de l'avoir déployée" (the Arch-Revelation of the Imago knowing it before having deployed it), insofar as it knows itself as imagination in the pathos of its suffering and joy? With Nietzsche begins the decisive philosophical situation in which the junction of the two worlds, of day and night, ceases to be an enigma, because the first finds its principle in the second and this principle has become in its way a focus of intelligibility, a phenomenological naturing. This answers the aporia of repression in Chapter VI: the power that forms or refuses to form a representation "knows" it beforehand, not by seeing it but as its own affect. The "night" is not dark; it is the Arch-Revelation.

This birth of the visible in the invisible and its production by it, tragedy describes minutely. Dionysus, as we saw, never himself appears on stage: "Dionysus, the real stage hero and centre of the vision, is at first, in the oldest period of tragedy, not actually present", and, we will add, never will be. The god's original being merges with "those forces which are merely felt and not yet condensed into images", so that experiencing these forces is experiencing the god. That is how the Dionysian experience consists first in the unleashing of forces, in the "enthusiastic throng of followers" and their frenzied dances. But it is not the mere surging of these forces but their experience, the intoxication of their passion each time, that constitutes identically the being of the god and of his "inspired" follower. That is why it is said that "in the dance the greatest strength remains only potential, betraying itself only in the suppleness and wealth of movement" (§ 8), because to the setting to work of these movements and forces is each time added (p. 328) the Dionysian pathos of hyper-power, which, in their self-growth, throws them into themselves to be what they are. "Forces merely felt, not yet condensed into images" is exactly the Freudian drive before its "representative", but given as felt, not as unconscious. "Potential" anticipates the final section, "Potentiality".

This Dionysian experience, understood as an experience, as the phenomenality of force consisting in its *Stimmung*, makes possible and arouses the production of language and image and determines them entirely. "It is this emotion, this knowledge that makes the enthusiastic throng of Dionysus' followers shout for joy, and its power is such that it enables them to transform themselves before their own eyes." The excess of passion, its too heavy weight (the figures of its ontological structure as "suffering oneself", "bearing oneself") produce its "objectification", the unreality of representation as pure Imago of the world, and what is represented in it "in the form of revived spirits of nature, satyrs", its own essence.
