It is the essence of life that represents itself to itself as the individual who bears this essence and who, bearing it, can perceive it in turn: "the Dionysian enthusiast sees himself as satyr, and as satyr, in turn, he sees the god". He sees Dionysus as the masked hero who advances and is really there on stage, not as he is in himself, however, but as he "is merely represented as present". So the process is accomplished by which the pathetic essence of life discharges itself in the unreality of its self-representation, or, as Nietzsche says literally, "the Dionysian chorus ever anew discharging itself in an Apollinian world of images" engenders "a new vision that is the Apollinian accompaniment of its state" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 8). The tragic chorus is the model of the genesis of representation from affect: self-image born of pathos.

The most difficult thought is that of the phenomenality of this image as produced by life and resting initially in its pathos. By setting this image of itself apart and so properly pro-ducing it, life accomplishes ecstasis, whose light, as the light of exteriority and identical with it, is the very phenomenality (p. 329) of this Imago and its pure phenomenological substance. But since this Imago is produced and so never rests on itself or its own phenomenality, but only on what never stops producing it, on "the Dionysian state", "le devenir-visible du monde est le devenir-invisible de son anti-essence" (the becoming-visible of the world is the becoming-invisible of its anti-essence, p. 329), which grounds it and assures itself of it at every moment. Light veils itself, and constantly, not as an effect of the finitude of the place where it appears, but because its coming into this place is the concealment of the power that produces it, a concealment that is nothing but its Arch-Revelation in the Origin, Dionysus himself or the Pathos of Life. Henry reinterprets Heidegger's *lēthē*: what conceals itself in every unconcealment is not being but life, and its "concealment" is not absence but invisible revelation.

This precarious phenomenality of the image, the becoming-visible of the world, is "the Apollinian dream state", whose emergence Nietzsche described with unheard-of depth and subtlety. The "dream state" effaces everything that makes up the everyday world for us, raising beyond it, as its own condition of visibility, the horizon that lights it and lets it be there for us. But this world of light is only Dionysus' dream, his projection outside himself, this unreal horizon with the creatures of this dream in it, the many-formed representations of life. The more intense life's self-experience in the pathos of its suffering and joy, the more vivid, luminous and intelligible the images in which it projects itself. This truth of the world, the production of representation by affectivity and its radical determination by it, every form of art, and Dionysian art above all, makes evident: "precisely through this emotional discharge, this mediating world of the stage action, the drama as a whole, attained from within a degree of clarity and intelligibility unattainable by other Apollinian arts"; "the Apollinian image, when music illuminates it from within" (§ 21). The intensity of the image is proportional to the intensity of the affect: Henry's alternative to the Freudian thesis that affect distorts representation (dream-work).

But however vivid the images, however dazzling the clarity bathing them, because this clarity affects itself, the darkness of a primordial Night extends in it and inhabits its most intense brightness. Apollo, who is after all only the Image of Dionysus, is less light (p. 330) than shadow in the light, and the three components of the "Apollinian dream state", carefully separated by Nietzsche, are there for us: the intrinsic obscurity of the being, the reflection of light on it and the play of luminous forms, the original obscurity of this light of the world, like that of a dream: "such is the Apollinian dream state, in which the world of day becomes veiled and a new world, clearer, more intelligible, more moving than the everyday world and yet more shadowy, is born" (§ 8). This obscure light of Apollo determines "the character of the hero as it rises to the surface and becomes visible (it is after all nothing but a luminous image projected onto a dark wall, that is, appearance through and through)" (§ 9). How the original essence of revelation, consisting in affectivity, has deserted the phenomenality of the world, to be in it only what does not manifest itself there, its affectivity, is said in its essential simplicity by aphorism 179 of *The Gay Science*: "thoughts are the shadows of our feelings, always darker, emptier and simpler". The "dark wall" behind the projected image anticipates the cinema metaphor and reverses Plato's cave: the shadows are on the side of the light; the source is the dark.

Nietzsche's thought is a solar thought. What this thought is as such is easily misunderstood as soon as one no longer sees that the luminosity of light does not ultimately proceed from or reside in itself, that at its highest intensity, as the light of the Sun, it reaches itself only as originally revealed to itself within the invisible dimension of revelation that is life. This ultimate condition of ecstatic phenomenality, to occur only in the accomplishment (p. 331) of its anti-essence, Nietzsche expresses poetically when he represents solar fullness as pouring itself out on beings originally constituted in themselves as living beings, that is, as proceeding from them and from the essence of life. The longing for the South is the inner thrill of joy of one who, seeing everything darken and the sea itself veiled under the excessive brightness of the "Great Noon", feels himself to be the accomplishment of this veiling that gives him to himself in the certainty and jubilation of his own being. "A man who loves the South ... as a great school of recovery for the spirit and the senses, as an irresistible solar fullness that comes to illuminate everything, to pour itself over beings sure of themselves, full of faith in themselves ..." (*Beyond Good and Evil*, § 255). The "Great Noon" (*Zarathustra*) is read as the overexposure in which the world fades and life feels itself.

That is what distinguishes the artist from the man of science: the first stands before the spectacle of the world as before a curtain whose veil cannot be lifted, with the presentiment that he himself is what the world's unveiling forever veils; the second always believes he is tearing away veils and piercing the secret: "whenever the truth is uncovered, the artist will always cling with rapt gaze to what still remains covering even after the uncovering, while theoretical man enjoys and finds satisfaction in the discarded covering, and finds his highest pleasure in the process of an ever happy uncovering that succeeds through his own efforts" (*Birth of Tragedy*, § 15). This sheds light at once on Nietzsche's whole body of thought, along with the proposition of the "Attempt at a Self-Criticism" in which, sixteen years after *The Birth of Tragedy*, Nietzsche designates the task that remains his: "to look at science in the perspective of the artist, but at art in that of life" (§ 2). Schopenhauer's thesis that life determines representation has thus acquired in Nietzsche a radicality that comes from its phenomenological transcription. It is not an ontic instance, the will, that manipulates from outside (p. 332), and unintelligibly, the power of representation; with affectivity, the innermost possibility of the exercise of this power is exhibited as its condition and what necessarily precedes it. "The 'simplest' sense processes are already governed by affects: fear, love, hatred, including the passive affects of laziness" (*Will to Power*, § 669). Nietzsche's new concept of intuition, thought and representation in general finds explicit formulation in an absolutely original theory of vision that defines it no longer by excluding its affective determinants but by them, so that the first revelation inherent in all knowledge occurs elsewhere than in its ecstatic light and before it, in the feelings that constitute the true eyes of vision, whose perfection, its phenomenological accomplishment, thus lies in affectivity itself. The "theoretical man" who delights in unveiling is the analyst's ideal type (*Aufklärung* of the unconscious). The artist, who knows that unveiling veils, is Henry's counter-type.

Then a crucial thesis of Nietzsche becomes intelligible: "there are no facts, only interpretations" (*Will to Power*, § 481). Interpretation is linked in Nietzsche to what he calls "perspective", which designates an a priori structuring of phenomenality in general, insofar as "there is only a perspective seeing, only a perspective 'knowing'" (*Genealogy*, III, 12). But the question is to understand what this structuring consists in, the nature of its a priori status, what phenomenality it governs. The hardly avoidable misreading stems from perspective being an optical metaphor complacently developed by Nietzsche's text ("a belief that belongs to the optics of life and its perspective"; "for all life rests on appearance, art, illusion, optics, the necessity of perspective and error", "Attempt", § 5), which as such seems to refer to intuitive vision and its conditions. Every vision is only a "point of view" unfolding from a (p. 333) "centre" and dependent on it for what it sees and the angle from which it sees it. The finitude of ecstasis, which notably inhabits spatial intuition and grounds it (even if it borrows its vocabulary from it), would then be what the "perspective" inherent in all knowledge and finally in "all interpretation" expresses. So this perspectival character of knowledge and representation in general marks a defect, a finitude, unavoidable in one sense but to be overcome progressively by multiplying points of view, approaches, readings, in a hermeneutic labour whose temporal unfolding is that of knowledge itself and whose ideal end would be "objectivity", a sort of "knowledge in itself" given to a "pure", "absolute" subject finally escaping the limitation of its initial takes. This is the standard (and hermeneutic) reading of perspectivism; Henry ties it to Chapter I's finitude of the horizon.

Whereas for Nietzsche the perspectival character of knowledge is not a feature of its own phenomenality but designates what escapes it, as its own ground. "Perspective" no longer means the "point of view" as that from which the distancing occurs of what, distancing itself from itself, returns to itself in the pre-given circularity of ecstasis; the point is rather "le demeurer en soi de tout ce procès" (the remaining-in-itself of the whole process, p. 333). In this way affectivity determines representation, can "have it in its power", "will" it or not "will" it, as the power that forms it and its ultimate transcendental condition of possibility. Nietzsche's "interpretation" likewise does not indicate the distance of a free consideration or appreciation but what cannot itself be interpreted and so radically conditions representation: "perspectives and interpretations of an affective order" (*Genealogy*, III, 12). The point of view is not a point in space but an affect: Henry turns perspectivism into a thesis of the affective a priori. "Will or not will" a representation repeats the repression analysis.

Then the astonishing concept takes shape of a vision, an eye, whose essence is no longer light, and it is the concept of all possible vision and knowledge. This concept implies rejecting the traditional interpretation of knowledge as ecstatic: "here we are always asked to think (p. 334) of an eye that is completely unthinkable, an eye turned in no particular direction, in which the active and interpreting forces, through which alone seeing becomes seeing something, are supposed to be lacking; these always demand of the eye an absurdity and a nonsense. There is only a perspective seeing, only a perspective 'knowing'; and the more affects we allow to speak about one thing, the more eyes, different eyes, we can use to observe one thing, the more complete will our 'concept' of this thing, our 'objectivity', be" (*Genealogy*, III, 12). The "eye without direction" is the pure subject of science; Nietzsche's "more affects, more eyes" makes objectivity a function of affect.

Thus the conception of representation that reigns through Nietzsche's work and underlies his most revolutionary aphorisms is the extension and laying bare of the one sketched brilliantly in *The Birth of Tragedy*: the determination of vision by affectivity as the ground of both its phenomenality and its meaning, which is not limited to what comes here or there into the condition of object but draws from the generality of its ground that of its own generality. Conditions of all representation, the universal structures of affectivity, their simple tonalities, subsume everything that is, communicating to it, together with its light, an infinite resonance. "Dionysian art ... has a twofold effect on the Apollinian art faculty: music incites to the symbolic intuition of Dionysian universality, and music allows the symbolic image to emerge in its highest significance" (§ 16). The "generality" of meaning comes from affect, not from concepts: Henry's theory of meaning as tonality.

The overcoming of the Schopenhauerianism of the early writings in Nietzsche's later work thus leaves intact the thesis formulated in § 52 and chapter 39 of the Supplements to Book III of *The World* in the extraordinary theory of music. Its generality stems from its reproducing affectivity, whose tonalities are the matrices of being, the laws of its constitution, so that the infinite diversity of all that is reduces, in its way of coming about and so of being possible, to these fundamental affective determinations (p. 335), suffering and joy, sadness and boredom; so that, for instance, the same music expresses the same pathos "whether the subject of the drama be Agamemnon and Achilles or the quarrels of a bourgeois family" (§ 52); so that, more ultimately, everything merely represented is represented only under the condition of a more original affection whose essence is the very essence of life. Schopenhauer's theory of music (music as the immediate copy of the will) is the one part of his system Henry fully retains.

Determined by the affectivity of its representation, everything represented is then represented as value: this is the essential modification that affects the world of representation in Nietzsche insofar as it is no longer in reality that of representation but that of life, the world of a representation that finds its principle and end in life. What is posited there before us is no longer the mere being, which has no value and does not exist by itself either, but precisely what is there for life, what is of value for it and so has value, what is by virtue of the auto-affection of its self-constitution and only under this condition. "Value" names the affective tonality of every appearance; the world is a world of values because its appearing is affective.

Value is therefore not an addition to what has already come near us and already exists as such; it belongs a priori to its coming and so to everything that comes, as an essential feature. Scheler, who, as we said, meditated on Nietzsche more than anyone, made value the correlate of an affective perception understood as the specific opening to the axiological determinants of the world: the "useful", the "threatening", the "horrible", the "lovable", the "serene", the "divine", and so on. But every perception is affective, as its own auto-affection, and, as Nietzsche saw, the whole world of representation is a world of values. Not under the gaze of a "metaphysics of values" interpreting the being of all that is from that of the living being that posits values and for which there is value, but by virtue of the original essence of being as such. The phrase "metaphysics of values" is Heidegger's charge against Nietzsche; Henry answers it next.

There are thus three senses of value in Nietzsche. First, the will to power is value as the original self-growth in which being builds itself inwardly and produces itself. Second (p. 336), values are the titles under which this inner work of being is exhibited: force (as hyper-power), overflowing, superabundance, nobility, egoism, forgetting, beauty, goodness, truth, everything positive (with the correlative horror of the negative), the ontological determinations of life, everything it "finds in itself" and, insofar as this finding is intoxication, cannot help singing and praising in self-celebration ("we the noble, the good, the beautiful, the happy"). Finally, value in a third sense designates everything in the world of representation that life represents as able to favour and increase its essence, self-growth: "values and their changes are related to increases in the power of those who posit them" (*Will to Power*, § 14). Values as the representation of the conditions of self-growth, or of this growth itself, nowhere mean the primacy of representation and its definition of the structure of being; on the contrary, they everywhere reaffirm its radical dependence on life. The three senses map onto the levels of the analysis: life itself, its eidetic determinations, and represented conditions of growth.

It is therefore completely to distort Nietzsche's problematic to claim to reduce it, as a metaphysics of values, to a metaphysics of representation, on the pretext that value is linked to representation as represented by it, whereas representation becomes value only under a condition other than itself. This falsification, the explicit insertion of the philosophy of the will to power into the history of Western metaphysics become since Descartes a metaphysics of representation, Heidegger accomplishes as follows. Linked to the will to power, representation does become value, but the description given of value tends to refer it to representation as a sufficient ontological ground, which is achieved as soon as the essence of value is exhausted in that of the "point of view" and the point of view itself is naively understood as inherent in perception and its own phenomenality, whereas, as we saw, "point of view" and "perspective" designate in Nietzsche affectivity and its determination (p. 337) of all possible representation, its determination as value. The reduction of value, through "point of view", to sight itself, to everything that takes form and face in it, is explicit: "the essence of value lies in its being a point of view. Value means what is kept in view [*was ins Auge gefaßt ist*]. 'Value' is the centre of perspective for a gaze that has its sights on something ...". That this reduction of value to the medium of vision is a reduction to the structure of representation, intended to explain by it the characters of value, notably its being a "point of view", and finally its essence, is said word for word: "from the characterization of value as point of view follows what is essential for Nietzsche's concept of value: as point of view, it is always posited by a seeing and for it. This seeing is such that it sees insofar as it has seen; it has seen insofar as it has represented and thereby posited as such what it has sighted. Only through this positing in representation does the point of reference necessary for aiming and guiding the seeing of this aim become a centre of perspective, that is, what matters for seeing and for the action guided by this sight. Values are therefore not first something in themselves that could then occasionally be taken as points of view" ("Nietzsche's Word: 'God is Dead'", *Holzwege*). The confrontation with Heidegger, begun in Chapter III, resumes on the ground of "Nietzsche's Word: 'God is Dead'" (1943).

But reducing value to representation and explaining it exhaustively by representation is nothing less than inserting Nietzsche's thought into the history of Western metaphysics, if one notes that representation is only a mode of ecstatic phenomenality, an equivalent of *idea*, *eidos* or *perceptio*. "Value is value insofar as it is valid. It is valid insofar as it is posited as what matters. It is so posited by a sighting, a looking toward what must be reckoned with. The centre of perspective, the gaze, the line of sight: all this understands 'sight' and 'seeing' here in the sense determined by the Greeks, but this sense gathers at the same time all the interpretations of *idea* in its metamorphosis from *eidos* to *perceptio*. Seeing is here (p. 338) the representing that, since Leibniz, is grasped most clearly in the basic feature of *appetitus*" (ibid.). Heidegger's lineage *eidos*–*perceptio*–*appetitus*–value is exactly the lineage Henry criticized in Chapters III and V.

Leave aside the first two tautological and empty propositions: value is what is valid, what matters. The sophism is in the third, given as evident like the two preceding ones, whereas it asserts something quite different and makes the leap: value is posited as what is valid "by a sighting". To this Nietzsche's saying is opposed, for whom value is posited by itself, insofar as "the well-born felt themselves simply as the happy" and the "good" are those "who felt themselves good" (*Genealogy*, I, 2, 10). The essence of Nietzsche's value is thus the essence of life, not at all that of representation, the representation of a value being only the representation of this pre-existing, presupposed value and in no way explaining it. Henry locates Heidegger's error in a single logical step: the move from "what matters" to "posited by a sighting".

The text's last sentence points to another sophism, concerning not only the reading of Nietzsche but the overall interpretation of the history of Western metaphysics: the identification of representation with *appetitus*. This is the Leibnizian thesis already criticized, by which movement, *appetitus*, is reduced to representation's striving toward its full accomplishment and so to its essence. Now this *appetitus* of representation is purely and simply identified by Heidegger in turn with the will to power, confused, despite all Nietzsche's assertions, with a movement of thought, the operation of a conscious intentionality. Commenting on aphorism 23 of *Beyond Good and Evil*, which proposes to build a morphology of the will to power, Heidegger does not hesitate to write: "morphology here is the ontology of the *on*, whose *morphē*, transformed with the change of *eidos* into *perceptio*, appears in the *appetitus* of this *perceptio* as will to power" (*Holzwege*, Henry's emphasis). The Leibnizian thesis in question is the one examined at the end of Chapter V.

The misreading that places Nietzsche's thought in the extension (p. 339) of Leibniz's is compounded by another reduction, that of the will to power to the classical will, to mere willing considered as a pure determination of thought, and again by assigning to this will, whose essence is to will or not will, a sufficient ground in representation. The historial support for this new falsification is Descartes, who however lies at the origin of Leibniz's problematic, so that the sequence ending supposedly in Nietzsche is unbroken. The *cogito*, converted into an "I represent", then includes the will, since self-representing is proposing oneself to oneself in order to assure oneself unconditionally of oneself and so of all things, thus willing oneself and first willing, so that the effecting of this self-representing as the effecting of this will is the effecting of the effectivity of reality itself (of what supports all reality): "will, as a self-effecting striving toward itself in accordance with a re-presenting of itself (the will to will)" (*Nietzsche* II). The fallacious reduction of the will to power to representation, resting on nothing but its categorical assertion, on the pure and simple identification of the being of action with that of the *cogito* as "I represent", is also written: "will becomes truly essential in *actualitas* only where the *ens actu* is determined by the *agere* as *cogitare*, since this *cogito* is a *me cogitare*, being conscious of oneself, in which being conscious as a state of knowing is essentially a disposing-toward-oneself. Will as the basic feature of reality" (ibid., Henry's emphasis). The "will to will" returns from Chapter V: Heidegger's formula for technology rests on the identity of action and representation that Henry rejects.

To push this reign of representing as the being of all beings to the end is to establish its "unconditional autonomous legislation". It then suffices to say that "autonomous legislation characterizes the 'will'" to find again their unity, which defines the reason of German idealism, a subjectivity (p. 340) consisting in self-representing that wills to represent itself so as to be fully master of itself in this total self-exhibition, the phenomenology of spirit: "reason as 'appetitive' representing is in itself also will. The unconditioned subjectivity of reason is willing self-knowing. That means: reason is absolute spirit" (*Nietzsche* II). Nietzsche is now placed and understood in the extension of Hegel. Heidegger's sequence Descartes–Leibniz–Kant–Hegel–Nietzsche is the history of the subject as self-representing will; Henry has disputed its first term since Chapter III.

By a reversal, it is true. But this reversal, emphatically described as the reversal of reason into animality, is not one; it establishes no new essence of being truly heterogeneous to representing and irreducible to it, as Nietzsche's will precisely is, but merely considers in this representing its *appetitus*, the movement by which it goes toward itself and so wills itself insofar as it represents itself to itself, and so posits itself before itself, in and for the (ecstatic) total self-exhibition. Heidegger's "inverted Platonism" (animality instead of reason) leaves the ecstatic structure unchanged. For Henry, Nietzsche's "animality" is immanence, a genuinely different essence.

Hence the action of this will does not differ fundamentally from a representation or a thought, being only the effecting of their essence, its self-accomplishment as the accomplishment of a representation, a thought. Such an intellectualist conception of action pervades the text quoted on value as point of view: "only through this positing in representation does the point of reference necessary for aiming and guiding the seeing of this aim become a centre of perspective, that is, what matters for seeing and for the action guided by this sight". For action in its radical immanence, Schopenhauer's and Nietzsche's instinct, precisely does not act guided by a sight it does not bear in itself, so that this exclusion of all sight, vision or aim, of the ecstatic structure of representation in general, the non-doubling of force, is precisely the condition of possibility of its exercise and its very essence. The non-doubling of force (Chapter VII's birds of prey) is the argument against Heidegger's "action guided by sight".

This distortion of the action of the will to power, compelled (p. 341) to find its condition in a representation, appears again when, interpreting externally (from the perspective of a metaphysics of representation) the fact that every surpassing of power presupposes preserving the degree of power already reached, Heidegger entrusts this preservation to a "representing production". "But such a 'substance' [the stock of power already reached] becomes a presence, that is, something constantly available, only through a presenting that constitutes it in constancy. This presenting has the character of representing production" (*Holzwege*). Heidegger's "constancy" (*Beständigkeit*) is again the stability of the represented, as in his reading of Descartes (Chapter III).

But it is not only the possession of the degree of power already reached; it is the taking-possession of effecting power itself, its being brought into presence to itself, its unity with itself, that is asked of representation: "the essential unity of the will to power can be nothing other than this will itself. It is the way in which the will to power brings itself before itself as will. It places itself before itself in its own testing, so that in such testing the will represents itself in purity and so in its highest form. But representation here is by no means an added, subsequent presentation; the presence determined by it is rather the way in which, and as which, the will to power is" (*Holzwege*, translation modified). Echoing this is the text of *Nietzsche* II in which the misinterpretation of the essence of the will to power (insofar as it excludes ecstatic phenomenality) reaches its peak: "the fullness of the essence of will cannot be determined with regard to will as a faculty of the soul; rather, will must be brought back into essential unity with appearing: *idea*, *repraesentatio*, becoming-manifest, being revealed, representing oneself and so realizing and surpassing oneself and so 'possessing oneself' and so 'being'". The texts show Heidegger asking representation to supply the will's self-unity, exactly the role Henry assigns to auto-affection. "Testing" (*épreuve*) in Heidegger's text is the word Henry uses for self-experience; Heidegger construes it as self-representation.

Graver, however, than the sophisticated interpretation that joins the essence of appearing to the will to power only to hand it over to representation (p. 342) is the interpretation that, on the ground of the same presuppositions, refuses it this representation or notes its absence only to abandon it to night. "La philosophie de l'inconscient, c'est-à-dire de la représentation, va trouver son ultime avatar dans la psychologie moderne" (the philosophy of the unconscious, that is, of representation, will find its last avatar in modern psychology, p. 342). The pivot to Freud: Heidegger gives the will to power representation; Freud, on the same premises, declares it unconscious. Both alternatives (representation or night) are the dilemma of the prologue.

## Chapter IX. Man's Ape: The Unconscious

The systematic elaboration of the basic structures of appearing, pursued through the inaugural problematics of Descartes, Schopenhauer and Nietzsche, now makes possible a radical critique of psychoanalysis, that is, a philosophical determination of the concept of the unconscious. Freud was doubtless aware that psychoanalysis entirely lacked such a determination when he tried aggressively to dispose of a question on which the whole discipline he had founded rests: "the question of the nature of this unconscious is no more judicious or promising than [that] of the nature of the conscious" (*An Autobiographical Study*, GW XIV, 57). The originality of psychoanalysis is thus, refusing every conceptual approach to the unconscious as speculative, to construct it from indisputable pathological material, as the only possible key to this analytic given, the law of intelligibility of what without it would be incoherence and enigma. From there to claiming that only the analyst who has dealt personally and concretely, through symptoms and resistances, with the unconscious at work, and so has as it were touched it ("we have become accustomed to handling (p. 344) the unconscious as something palpable", *Introductory Lectures*, GW XI, 288), knows what he is talking about and can smile at abstract refutations, is a short step. "Mais la décision d'écarter toute légitimation théorique au nom d'une pratique est toujours suspecte et Freud n'a, semble-t-il, jamais pensé que seul un croyant était habilité à traiter de religion" (the decision to dismiss all theoretical legitimation in the name of a practice is always suspect, and Freud apparently never thought that only a believer was entitled to deal with religion, p. 344). The chapter title comes from the Nietzschean image of the ape as the caricature of man (*Zarathustra*); the unconscious is the "ape" of life, its mimicry in representation. The ironic remark about religion turns *The Future of an Illusion* against the analyst's appeal to clinical experience.

The unconscious therefore has only this theoretical existence: to be the only possible principle of explanation of the pathological material, such that legitimation depends ultimately not on the pertinence of the explanatory principle but on the pathological material itself as such, as an indisputable given. In what is the analytic material an indisputable given? "En tant qu'il apparaît" (insofar as it appears, p. 344). One may verbally reject a philosophy of consciousness; the whole psychoanalytic problematic claimed to oppose it rests on the essence of consciousness already deployed. The clinical "given" presupposes a theory of givenness; this is the phenomenological lever of the chapter.

The texts in which Freud explicitly places in consciousness the starting point, or rather the very site, of his theoretical work are therefore the most numerous: "the fact of being conscious ... is the starting point of all our investigations" (*Metapsychology*, GW X, 271). There is, it is true, a double motivation for this inevitable beginning. One is explicit and repeated throughout the work: the gappy character of the conscious given, which remains unintelligible as it is and requires for its understanding the intervention of other processes, which do not appear but which analysis proves able to reconstruct. In the 1938 *Outline* Freud will still say: "everyone agrees that conscious processes do not form unbroken series complete in themselves" (GW XVII, 79–80). But whereas, faced with this situation, the philosophy of consciousness, abruptly yielding all its positions, finds itself forced to entrust to the physiological substructure the task of filling the gaps and restoring continuity, so that the organism becomes the true ground (p. 345) of conscious life, reduced willy-nilly to an epiphenomenon, psychoanalysis by contrast fights admirably to keep the principle of its explanation within the psyche. It does not avoid the great classical split between appearing and being, the first being only the appearance of the second, an appearance that hides rather than reveals it, or reveals it, in psychoanalysis, only in disguises. At least being is homogeneous with appearance, belonging like it to the psyche, so that the unity of the psyche, of man and his life, is safeguarded. Henry credits psychoanalysis with refusing physiological reductionism: the "psychical" unconscious defends the specificity of the psyche, which is its philosophical merit.

But being is not only homogeneous with the appearance it claims to ground; it is secretly dependent on it, always derives from it and is finally determined by it. For, as Nietzsche says: "what is 'appearance' for me now? Certainly not the opposite of some essence: what could I say about any essence except to name the attributes of its appearance!" (*Gay Science*, § 54). This is the true reason that compels the problematic of the unconscious to seek its origin and ground in consciousness: not the gappy and enigmatic character of its content, but its very existence as appearing, as conscious, that is, consciousness itself as such. The unconscious, constructed from appearances, inherits the structure of appearance: the thesis prepares the claim that the Freudian unconscious is a representation without light.

The concept of consciousness is ontic and ontological at once. Taken immediately and naively, as in ordinary language, it designates what is conscious: a symptom, a slip, a dream, a tremor, any behaviour in general. But the being-given of this analytic given, the fact that it shows itself, this pure fact of appearing considered in itself independently of what appears in it (this symptom, this behaviour), is consciousness in its ontological concept, pure consciousness drawing its essence from the pure fact of appearing and identical with it. The philosophy of consciousness may most often have confused what is conscious with consciousness itself and, in the "phenomenon", what shows itself (p. 346) with the showing; the latter remains its implicit theme and what makes it a philosophy, what makes there be, beside the sciences, which always thematize beings, something like the possibility and necessity of a philosophy in general. The ontic/ontological distinction is applied to consciousness: what is conscious vs. being-conscious (*Bewußtheit*).

Beyond what is conscious, and as its principle of explanation, psychoanalysis posits what is no longer so, the non-conscious, the unconscious. Like that of consciousness, the concept of the unconscious is equivocal, ontic and ontological at once. What is unconscious in the ontic sense are the drives and their representatives, unconscious representations and their arrangements, the primary processes to which they are subject (displacement, condensation, symbolization, at the origin of dreams, slips, symptoms), repressed or phylogenetic contents, a whole part of infantile experience, and so on. But such contents are unconscious, subsumed under this concept, only insofar as they lack being-conscious as such, *Bewußtheit*, foreign to consciousness in the ontological sense. Unconscious is what lies outside the field opened by appearing and circumscribed by its phenomenality. Since the concept of the unconscious, even taken first in an ontic sense, can take form and be defined only in relation to ontological consciousness, it is itself ontological. Henry insists: "unconscious" is always a phenomenological predicate, whatever contents it is applied to.

What does the unconscious mean in the ontological sense? Is it anything but a purely negative determination, the determination "being-conscious as such", "pure appearing as such", crossed out? Because the mere fact of not being conscious, not appearing, is purely negative, because it seems hardly "promising", one understands why Freud excluded it from his research, substituting as its theme the processes that actually account for conscious contents, just as these contents replaced the mere quality of being conscious, *Bewußtheit* as such, itself formal and empty. Thus occurs the shift by which psychoanalysis decisively departs from philosophy while conquering its own concept of the unconscious: no longer (p. 347) the empty negation of the formal quality of *Bewußtheit*, but the set of processes to be discovered whose coherent totality determines the human psyche and makes it what it is, the unconscious as system, "the system *Ucs*". What matters in the end are these psychical contents insofar as they determine one another, their being conscious or unconscious being only a secondary character, as Freud himself says: "consciousness or unconsciousness of a psychical process is only one of its properties" (*Introductory Lectures*, GW XI, 304). The shift from the descriptive to the systematic (topographical) unconscious is presented as an abandonment of the ontological question.

A strange doctrine, which begins noisily by rejecting the traditional primacy of consciousness in favour of an unconscious that wholly determines it, only to declare that neither, neither being conscious in itself nor not being so, really matters! And this although the conversion of the second into the first, of the unconscious into the conscious, is both the aim of its therapy and its condition. The irony fixes the contradiction: the cure aims at becoming conscious, yet consciousness is declared an inessential property.

But the discredit thrown on the unconscious as such by a theory that defines itself by it and more or less believes it invented it is less paradoxical than it seems. The pure fact of being unconscious, considered in itself, is empty only insofar as the antithetical concept from which it is built remains formal, designating pure consciousness in general, appearing, without anything being said about what constitutes being-conscious, the nature of this appearing, the effectiveness and phenomenological substance of pure phenomenality as such. Recall Freud's disconcerting admission: "there is no need to explain here what we call conscious, which is the same as the consciousness of philosophers and of everyday opinion" (*Outline*, GW XVII, 81). The absence of any ontological elaboration of the essence of phenomenality entails correlatively the total indeterminacy of the ontological concept of the unconscious, and its abandonment by Freud in favour of the various empirical contents that will take its place and serve to define it: infantile experiences, repressed (p. 348) representations, drives, and so on. Thus, with the substitution of the system *Ucs* for the quality "unconscious", itself correlative with the quality "conscious", a fall from the ontological into the ontic occurs that will crush psychoanalysis and, depriving it of its implicit philosophical meaning, make it "une psychologie grossière, engluée dans la facticité et le naturalisme" (a crude psychology, mired in facticity and naturalism, p. 348), unable to produce any a priori knowledge, doomed to err whenever it must face questions of principle like the unavoidable one of the relation of conscious and unconscious, which presupposes at any rate the ontological relation of the conscious as such and the unconscious as such and is neither possible nor conceivable without it. The *Outline* passage was quoted in the prologue; its return frames the whole book. Henry's harshest sentence against psychoanalysis concerns its loss of the ontological question, not its clinical findings.

What then does the unconscious mean ontologically? What is the philosophical scope of psychoanalysis prior to its fall into ontic naturalism? Our investigations place us before this series of evidences: the consciousness to which psychoanalysis assigns uncrossable limits is that of classical thought: representation and its ground, ecstatic phenomenality, which finds the condition of its expansion in the process of exteriorization of exteriority, in the transcendence of a world. The implicit but decisive intuition of psychoanalysis, the reason for the immense echo it found despite the inadequacy of its conceptual apparatus, is that the essence of the psyche lies neither in the world's becoming visible nor in what thereby comes into the condition of object. As the radical refusal of ecstatic phenomenality and of the claim to define the essence of the psyche by it, the unconscious guards man's innermost being: "l'inconscient est le nom de la vie" (the unconscious is the name of life, p. 348). In this respect Freud stands directly in the wake of Schopenhauer and Nietzsche (and of Descartes too, insofar as the "soul" reaches its essence, in the radical reduction of the first two Meditations, by rejecting every worldly dimension and worldliness as such); he belongs to the underground current which, within a philosophy that entrusts being to exteriority, to knowledge and finally to science, works relentlessly (p. 349) to recognize and preserve the domain of the invisible, the hidden phase of things. A note adds: that is why Freud's belief in science, what must be called his scientism, contradicts his deepest intuition. The prologue's formula returns, now demonstrated. The note on scientism names the split within Freud himself.

But as soon as the unconscious is seen in the positivity of its primitive ontological essence, its meaning doubles; the supposedly formal and empty concept requires a complex elucidation. The unconscious is not only the other of representation, the name of life. To the sphere of representation itself and to its own essence belongs, as shown, a horizon of non-presence, the principled possibility that whatever shows itself in it withdraws and slips away. The possibility, or rather necessity, for the represented to cease being so, for every ontic content to disappear, is rooted in the original law of disappearance that affects every ecstatic presence as such, so that it is the very place of light that surrounds itself with shadow, and beings vanish continually into the unconscious only by a law that is not first their own. The two unconsciouses distinguished since Chapter II (the horizon-shadow and the night of life) are now applied to Freud.

The ontological concept of the unconscious therefore necessarily takes on, by virtue of the structuring of pure phenomenality and its division into the co-original dimensions of representation and life, two fundamentally different meanings according to which it refers to. Looked at closely, the unconscious as usually taken, as pure and simple negation of phenomenality, as crossed-out consciousness (*ucs* = barred *cs*), is understood only in relation to representational consciousness: it abolishes its light, while belonging to it, as its limit, as the horizon of non-presence surrounding all ecstatic presence and determining it as essentially finite. This co-belonging of ecstatic presence and non-presence grounds the incessant passage of one into the other by which every appearance in the world is identically a disappearance, just as on the ground of this co-belonging, as a pure ontological law, rests the destiny of everything that is there, to be born and to die. The question, essential in Freudianism, of the transformation of the unconscious into the conscious and conversely (*ucs* ⇄ *cs*) (p. 350) finds its a priori condition of possibility here, as an ontological possibility. In accordance with it, such transformation is both reversible and absolutely free, every unconscious content being able to take on the quality of consciousness and enter its light, every conscious content being bound to leave it and return to the unconscious. The first unconscious is the one in which "making conscious" makes sense: the latent memory.

With this unconscious, which for simplicity we shall call the unconscious of representation (*ucs* = barred *cs*), the unconscious that secretly refers to the essence of life has nothing to do. The bar placed here on phenomenality concerns only the phenomenality of representation, and its rejection frees the original dimension of appearing in which being reveals itself to itself outside and independently of ecstasis, in the radical immanence of its auto-affection as life. Because being, according to the original essence of its self-appearing, expels ecstasis, the possibility of showing itself in it is excluded in principle. The question of the reciprocal transformation of conscious and unconscious now receives a quite different answer: from possible it has become impossible (*ucs* ⇏ *cs*). The mystery of the double destiny assigned to unconscious contents, some submitting, under suitable conditions, to becoming conscious, others obstinately refusing it, is cleared up. The existence of the latter need not be explained by some ontic process invented for the purpose, by a primal repression that is itself mysterious. Nor can it be simply observed as a factual property of certain drive representatives: it is rooted in an ontological prescription; it formulates the status of life. Thus Freud's discourse on the unconscious, far from arising solely from the work of analysis as its result, secretly refers to the basic structures of being, which it exhibits in its own way. This must be established more precisely. Primal repression (*Urverdrängung*), Freud's postulate that some contents never became conscious, is reinterpreted as the ontological impossibility for life to appear in representation.

We have already shown how it is the finitude of the place of ecstasis that, excluding all representations except the one licensed to exhibit itself there for a moment, places them "in a state of latency" (p. 351), so that, considering for instance all our virtual memories, it becomes "quite inconceivable to deny the unconscious" (see Chapter II above). The ontological meaning of the unconscious is explicit here, together with its reference to the structure of representation: representability as such makes it that, of all psychical contents, only one can "be known to consciousness". This decisive argument, which concerns only the phenomenality of consciousness-as-representation, was already formulated in the 1912 "Note on the Unconscious in Psycho-Analysis". Defining "what the term 'unconscious' has come to mean in psycho-analysis", Freud contested the philosophical identification of "psychical" and "conscious": "this is an obvious mistake that denies psychology the right to account by its own means for the commonest facts, such as memory. Let us call 'conscious' the representation present to our consciousness and that we perceive as such, and let that be the only meaning of the term 'conscious'. As for latent representations, if we have grounds to suppose that they exist in mental life, as with memory, let them be designated by the term 'unconscious'" (GW VIII, 431–432, Henry's emphasis). Freud's 1912 definition of "conscious" as "present representation" is exactly ecstatic presence; the unconscious it defines is the shadow of representation.

Representedness is therefore the starting point of the psychoanalytic determination of the unconscious. The pathological material from which the doctrine's great explanatory hypotheses, notably the unconscious, are built is indisputable, as was said, only insofar as it is given. But the being-given of this given is precisely its capacity to be represented, to come into the condition of object. What matters first is not its pathological character (psychoanalysis, like every science, must leave the domain of specificity in which it would like to enclose itself and be unassailable) but its ontological, that is, phenomenological character. That this character has the feature of ecstatic phenomenality and is exhausted in it (p. 352), that the given in question is that of representation, is seen also when it ceases to be "pathological" strictly speaking. Everyone knows the decisive role of the dream in forming psychoanalysis. The peculiarity of the dreamer's conscious life is to lose itself in its products to the point of seeming nothing but the set of dream contents and their incoherent succession. The same holds for the association of ideas. Everywhere the representational content is considered for itself, in itself; "la réalité objective de l'idée est séparée de sa réalité formelle" (the objective reality of the idea is separated from its formal reality, p. 352). Small wonder that this content, isolated from the constituting power that gave it birth, a bare presence in dead objectivity, appears fragmentary, enigmatic, meaningless and at the limit absurd. The idea of a conscious given gappy by essence comes from Freud's privileging of the dream and free association as practical supports of analytic work. The Cartesian vocabulary of Chapter II now diagnoses Freud's method: the "manifest content" is objective reality severed from the formal reality (the living act) that produced it. Its "gaps" are the effect of that severance.

Here another facet of the psychoanalytic concept of the unconscious is revealed: it designates not only the finitude of ecstasis, the zone of shadow surrounding every object-presence, but more essentially ecstasis itself, the process of ob-jection considered in itself, independently of the objectivity it produces, production as such. To the positive genealogy of psychoanalysis, Descartes, Schopenhauer and Nietzsche, must therefore be added its negative genealogy, taking account of the great deficiencies of Western thought from which it directly proceeds and which it unknowingly repeats. For the last word of the philosophy of consciousness, its limit, its paradox, the extreme point where it turns against itself and self-destructs, is the unconsciousness of pure consciousness itself as such, the unconsciousness of "transcendental consciousness" (a note refers to *L'essence de la manifestation*, Section I, and to the same situation in Kant, Chapter IV). The moment of this reversal is historically located when German idealism, unable to ground its principle and undermined from within by this major incapacity, tips over into a philosophy of nature (p. 353), which is the truth of this idealism, asserting nothing but the unconsciousness of pure consciousness itself, the unconsciousness of production. To production it belongs to become conscious of itself, to produce itself in phenomenality and so to appear only in its product, in the object, in the form of the product, never in itself, as producing, as naturing. The third unconscious of Chapter III (Schelling's unconscious production) is named as the "negative genealogy" of psychoanalysis.

With the unconsciousness of production the philosophy of nature thinks it solves the problem left pending by idealism: how can the principle that produces the world come up against it as a foreign reality? Precisely because this creation does not know itself and discovers its product, what is there, what stands before it, as an enigma. That this enigma can nonetheless be gradually penetrated and deciphered is possible only because it is the product of this production and, beneath the appearance of their difference (an appearance that is their Difference), Identity reigns. A note adds that every great aesthetic creation is, for German Romanticism, the discovery of this hidden Identity of spirit and nature, as Anne Henry showed of Proust's exemplary work (*Proust romancier: le tombeau égyptien*, 1983). This situation, "celle d'Œdipe somme toute et de la psychanalyse en général" (that of Oedipus, after all, and of psychoanalysis in general, p. 353), finds its explicit and finished formulation in the young Schelling's monumental *System of Transcendental Idealism*, whose implications are immense. Oedipus is the figure of the producer who meets his own product (his acts) as an enigma to be deciphered: Henry reads the Oedipal structure as the Schellingian structure of unconscious production. Anne Henry is the author's wife.

The historial affinity between philosophy of nature and psychoanalysis is mediated by late-nineteenth-century psychology, which also supplied Freudianism with its historical support. The interest of the first great French work on Freud, Roland Dalbiez's *La méthode psychanalytique et la doctrine freudienne* (1936), is to bring out certain determining aspects of the ideological context of nascent psychoanalysis. The realism, "a minimum of realism", to which Dalbiez entrusts the destiny of psychoanalysis derives, in an intra-psychic system (where everything is psychical) (p. 354), from this ultimate possibility, that the psychical process does not know itself and finds itself before its product as before something "real", something that comes from it without its knowing. This is precisely the case in dreams, the association of ideas, the formation of psychoneurotic symptoms. Dalbiez's neo-Thomist realism is read as an unwitting philosophy of unconscious production.

So in truth, despite the factual appearance of the analysis, it is the ontological character of the psyche as operating and naturing that is at issue. That is why Dalbiez constantly advances a theory of consciousness that reaffirms in various forms its original unconsciousness. In our perception of a tree, for instance, "we in no way know our vision; we grasp it only afterward, by a second act". And this holds not only for "external sensation", vision, but for psychical life in general. The conception of a colour exhibits only this colour, the conception, unconscious in itself, becoming conscious only after a new specific act of apprehension that makes it too, but only then, a "knowledge". The heterogeneity of the second act to the first is expressed in its contingency, in that the first in no way implies its becoming conscious, even as a later modality: "it is perfectly conceivable that sensation and intellection occur in us but remain unconscious" (Henry's emphasis; a note adds that one had already read these theses in Leibniz). Dalbiez's thesis that the act is unconscious until reflected is the exact negation of the *videor* of Chapter I; Henry links it to Leibniz's perception without apperception.

"This posteriority of consciousness relative to knowledge", which Dalbiez adds "has been brought out by the American neo-realists", meaning nothing else, if one sounds its ultimate ontological implications, than the non-phenomenality of phenomenality as such, its veiling in the phenomenon, in the ob-stance of the table, the colour, and so on, will receive its most remarkable illustration and a semblance of truth from the psychoanalytic method. The association of ideas is precisely the incessant coming of each representational content into its condition, such that the coming itself as pro-duction (p. 355) conceals itself each time and disappears into its product. So this product, cut from its root, arises as the not-understood, its understanding requiring the uncovering of the associative processes that gave birth to it. Association is here production itself, the unconsciousness of production, the unconsciousness of association. Hence the constant effort in psychoanalysis, which determines its method, to tear the associative processes from the unconscious to which they belong by principle, in order to account from them for the manifest but in itself unintelligible content of conscious life. Free association becomes the empirical form of the philosophy of nature's "unconscious production"; the analyst's reconstruction of associative chains is the attempt to see production in its products.

In any case the unconscious that proceeds from and is tied to representedness doubles: to the unconscious content that lies outside the field of intuitive presence and its horizon (the "latency" of memory, for instance) must be opposed the unconsciousness of its production, or, in psychology's naturalistic transcription, that of the associative link as such, "relational unconsciousness" (Dalbiez). Against Janet, who characterized hysteria by the narrowing of the field of consciousness, Freud explicitly distinguished the unconsciousness of facts, proper to hysteria, and the unconsciousness of relations between facts, which alone can account for obsessional cases. Likewise Frink, invoking Freud's thesis that "the emotional state as such is always justified", will try to show that even when the patient is conscious of the cause of his affective state he does not perceive it as cause: the relation as such escapes him (*Morbid Fears and Compulsions*). The "affect is always justified" thesis matters: Freud holds that the affect is never wrong, only its link to an idea is; Henry will build on it.

The representational concept of the unconscious (its determination from representedness) is overdetermined in Freud by the role of the dream in the doctrine's development. For it is never the dream itself that is analysed, for the principled reason that the constituting intentionality of the dream is imagination and as such incompatible with that of analysis, conceptual (p. 356) by essence. The dream in its specificity, as pure imaginary, is thus withdrawn a priori from analytic procedure, which can only substitute an equivalent for it. This equivalent is the dream narrative, a text, a set of meanings constitutive of language and belonging to thought strictly speaking, to a consciousness that aims at its object emptily, without really reaching it, a sense-giving consciousness, as Husserl says, a *Sinngebung*. A note recalls that Husserl showed decisively that imagination is a mode of intuitive consciousness eidetically different from the speaking consciousness, which is empty and non-intuitive in principle (*Logical Investigations* I, "Expression and Meaning"). The Husserlian distinction between intuitive presentification (*phantasia*) and empty signification grounds the objection: analysis replaces an image by a text.

An extraordinary situation then arises. On one hand, a linguistic formation having been substituted for the dream proper, that is, for a pure imaginary that as such has nothing to do with language, all the categories concerning language will be invested in a given heterogeneous to them. What was only a metaphor, the dream as "text" of analysis, its object, is taken literally as an intrinsic determination of this object's essence. The contamination or denaturing of psychoanalysis by linguistics and all the disciplines now associated with it becomes possible. "On va pouvoir déclarer sans rire que la structure de l'inconscient est celle d'un langage" (one will be able to declare without laughing that the structure of the unconscious is that of a language, p. 356). In Freud himself the consideration of words too often vitiates the delimitation of the real phenomenon and the search for its effective determinants. The target is Lacan ("the unconscious is structured like a language"). Henry's objection is precise: the "text" is an artefact of the analytic setting (the narration of the dream), not the dream's own structure.

On the other hand, since imaginary life contains by itself no meaning like that of the word (like the meaning "dog", as dog aimed at emptily, of which one has neither perception, image, memory nor concept), this meaning, created by the specific act of pure thought as *Sinngebung*, is absent from the imaginary as such: from there to believing and saying it is "unconscious" is a short step. This illusion occurs constantly because we think, and thought posits itself and so posits its products as the criteria by which it measures the other determinations of life. The hypostasis of pure meanings, which can accompany everything that is because indeed (p. 357) everything that is can be thought, "everything can be said", creates an ideal archetypal universe in the light of which all concrete formations of life and life itself appear lacking, deprived of the body of meanings they precisely do not bear. This set of hypostasized meanings will constitute the unconscious. The child, for instance, forms the image of his mother, whose presence is at times an irrepressible need for him. He does not for that form the meaning "needing his mother" or "wanting to sleep with her" and, for that, "killing his father"; he does not really even know what his "mother" is in our sense, nor his "father". "« Coucher avec sa mère et tuer son père », ce sera donc là son inconscient" ("Sleeping with his mother and killing his father" will thus be his unconscious, p. 357). This is the most original argument of the chapter: the Oedipus complex as an "unconscious meaning" results from projecting adult significations onto pre-significative life. The unconscious is the gap between a life and the meanings it does not form.

This principled critique of psychoanalysis must be taken in its widest scope. The dream is for Freud only the prototype of representation, which Schopenhauer, we recall, had reduced to a dream. The interpretation of dreams will therefore extend to all forms of representational life, especially those preceding thought strictly speaking. Not only dream contents and psychoneurotic fantasies, but all symbolic formations, artworks, myths, religious beliefs, will be subjected to a method forged in the analysis of specific data. Everywhere the same gap will appear between what such formations concretely are (symbolic, aesthetic, religious), what imaginary life in general is according to its own essence and modes, and the meaning, similar to linguistic meanings, under which one tries each time to subsume them. This gap, in which an unconscious lodges, here made of ideal meanings and indeed identical with a language, determines the "far-fetched" character of all "Freudian explanations", which their author vainly tried to justify by claiming that, the principle of explanation being unknown to the subject, the subject could only regard it with astonishment when confronted with it. Applied psychoanalysis (art, myth, religion) is the same projection of meanings onto life writ large.

Thus one of the most interesting aims of Freudianism turns against itself: to circumscribe in the psyche (p. 358) the immense part of what proceeds from its free play, its deepest impulses, to recognize, in short, the decisive role of the imaginary in life. But it is by the measure of ideal meanings that the imaginary is finally measured, interpreted, reduced. And behind these meanings of thought, the objects of thought take shape, everything included in the reality principle: to this reality in its flattest objective sense, the most down-to-earth determination, analysis leads back each time. "Le scientisme de Freud a déjà recouvert l'intuition de la vie" (Freud's scientism has already covered over the intuition of life, p. 358). The interpretation always ends in an objective fact (the father, the mother, the scene); the reality principle governs interpretation itself.

If the ideal meaning aimed at emptily by thought is foreign to the imaginary, how much more must it differ from life itself! For life has no meaning and, bearing no intentionality in itself, for instance that of forming a meaning, it cannot be placed under one either, questioned or examined in its light, judged or condemned by it. The child who loves his mother does not accomplish the ecstasis in which he would appear to himself as loving his mother, able, thanks to the distance of this ecstasis, to take a position toward himself and his love, and first to perceive it and, so perceiving, form the meaning "I, a child, loving my mother". The child perceives none of this not because he is a child but because he is a living being. "L'enfant chez Freud, comme l'animal chez Nietzsche, n'est en réalité qu'une figure" (the child in Freud, like the animal in Nietzsche, is really only a figure, p. 358). It is life that, bearing no ecstasis and so unable to perceive itself as such, relate to itself or represent itself, cannot signify itself either and so cannot, for its part, have a meaning. Having no meaning, life need not answer the question of meaning. Life is like a rose: "the rose is without why; it blooms because it blooms; it pays no attention to itself, does not ask whether it is seen" (Angelus Silesius, *Cherubinic Wanderer*, I, 289). A note observes that Heidegger commented on these lines in *The Principle of Reason* but, not taking into account the second line, which explicitly excludes ecstasis from the inner work of being, could not show how Silesius's saying relates to the original essence of life, here the rose. The "child" as figure, like the "animal": infancy in Freud is the eidetic figure of pre-reflective life, not a developmental stage. Henry's correction of Heidegger turns on "does not ask whether it is seen" (*fragt nicht, ob man sie siehet*).

Or, one may say, is not the meaning of life the movement of this pure self-experience, this pure affection (of the child) reduced to its affectivity, independently of the light of a world? But then a quite different concept (p. 359) of "meaning" arises, and with it the original signification of the concept of the unconscious beckons. A second sense of "sense" (the felt sense of life) is announced and set against Freud's.

The dream has a meaning, the slip has a meaning, symptoms have a meaning, our least gesture, silence ("he whose lips are silent chatters with his fingertips", *Dora*, GW V, 240), forgetting has a meaning, the memory that hides another (the screen memory) has a meaning; everything has a meaning, yet what must be understood each time by "meaning" remains in the most extreme equivocation. Insofar as meaning designates an ideal signification, like that of language, like the meaning of the word "dog", it is the noematic correlate of a signifying intentionality that originally creates it (though it may later, by a subsequent modification, keep it passively as an acquisition). What does the critical work of analysis consist in here? In this: the immediately intended meaning turning out to be false, it would be crossed out and replaced by another? Unlike Husserlian phenomenology, it is true, it is not the consciousness that formed the first meaning that proves able to overcome it. Only the surpassing of the "I think" and its own point of view allows the surpassing of the initial truth, that is, of the illusion. Concretely, the analyst leads his patient to recognize that the jealousy he showed toward his partner is really only a secret desire to deceive her himself. When the meaning "desire to deceive" succeeds the meaning "jealousy", an idealism of consciousness is rejected only in appearance; rather its reign extends to infinity, and psychoanalysis stands in the direct line of Western thought: what it posits are meanings, what posits is the power of positing meanings, a *Sinngebung*, a consciousness. The jealousy example is Freud's (projective jealousy, "Some Neurotic Mechanisms in Jealousy", 1922). Interpretation substitutes meaning for meaning: hermeneutics is a philosophy of consciousness extended.

But when Freud says that the dream, for instance, has a meaning (p. 360), he means something quite different: that a dream content is produced by an unconscious tendency. In the whole process of production of an imaginary representational content by an unconscious tendency there is neither meaning nor signifying consciousness, no meaning in the sense of a language. It is purely metaphorically, we repeat, that one says that someone silent speaks with his hands: he does not "speak", if speaking is intentionally forming a meaning with inner awareness of doing so. The relation between the agitation of someone whose hands tremble and this trembling is of another order: no longer the intentional relation, lived as such, of signifying something, but an external relation (psychologists will say causal) between two blind events, "unconscious" like the relation that unites them. Such a relation, like that of smoke above a house to the fire supposed to cause it, is an indication. Husserl admirably distinguished the relation by which one state of affairs indicates another, by which words for example indicate a mental state supposed in the speaker, from the intentional constitution of a meaning by the speaker in language strictly speaking. Husserl's distinction between indication (*Anzeichen*) and expression (*Ausdruck*), *Logical Investigations* I, § 1–5 (and Derrida's *Voice and Phenomenon*), sorts Freudian "meaning" into the indicative, not the expressive. The smoke and fire returns from Chapter VI.

Treating psychical phenomena in general, Freud writes: "we seek to understand them as indications [*Anzeichen*] of an interplay of forces in the mind ... in our conception the phenomena perceived must yield in importance to trends that are only hypothetical" (*Introductory Lectures*, GW XI, 62). Not only is all meaning out of play here, but the reversal of classical positions has occurred: the phenomenon is still a representational content, imaginary (dream, artwork, myth) or real (trembling of hands, psychoneurotic symptoms in general), but the power that produces it is no longer the power of representation, no longer consciousness. So when Freud declares that everything has a meaning, an assertion about which misunderstanding is general, far from reducing the psychical to a sayable offered to hermeneutic reading, he opens rather the domain in which there is no longer (p. 361) intentionality or meaning. This wholly other of representation must now be systematically elucidated. Freud's own word *Anzeichen* confirms Henry's reading against Ricœur's and Lacan's hermeneutic or linguistic Freud.

The inflection of Freud's concept of the unconscious toward the unexplored, because invisible, domain of life can be seen in the 1912 Note. The proof, the "justification" of the unconscious by the latency of most psychical contents, gives way almost at once to a quite different consideration. It is no longer the resurgence of these contents, memories for instance, after a lapse of time, that implies the hypothesis of a corresponding state of psychical unconsciousness (without which classical thought must, as said, entrust memory to the organism), but the efficacy of these unconscious thoughts while unconscious: activity as unconscious activity becomes the major argument. Hence the appeal to neurotic symptoms, which are no longer hypothetical, which are there, continually produced by an activity of which they are the immediate manifestation and so the proof, though this activity itself does not show itself. The whole mental life of the hysteric, who vomits because she fears being pregnant, "is full of effective but unconscious thoughts". "Other forms of neurosis" show "the same predominance of efficient unconscious ideas". The objection that pathological conditions cannot be extended to normal psychology falls if one notes that normal functional defects "such as slips of the tongue, errors of memory and speech, forgetting of names, etc., depend on the action of strong unconscious thoughts in the same way as neurotic symptoms" (GW VIII, 432–435). From latent (descriptive) to efficient (dynamic) unconscious: Henry sees in this shift the turn toward power, hence toward life.

More, normal defects and neurotic symptoms are here only revealers of an absolutely general phenomenon: the principled determination of everything that comes into the condition of being-represented by a power that never itself comes (p. 362) into this condition and could not. Freud does not merely correct the classical theses for which latency, unconsciousness as virtuality, would be synonymous with inefficacy and weakness ("we had become accustomed to thinking that every latent thought was so because of its weakness and became conscious as it grew strong") by asserting of his unconscious that "it designates not only latent thoughts in general but especially thoughts with a certain dynamic character". With the decisive character of an "efficient unconscious" the deliberate relocation of force and power in all their forms outside the field of representability is prescribed. One must not say that an effective action can also be accomplished in the unconscious; only as such, insofar as the power that produces it coheres with itself in the radical immanence where it first seized itself, is any action at all possible, for instance the movement of the hands of someone agitated. The change in Freud's unconscious, ceasing to designate the formal and empty negation of the quality "conscious" to take charge of the dynamism of the psyche, the totality of the "processes" of what becomes the "system *Ucs*", does not so much mark the fall of its concept from the ontological into the ontic: behind its apparent facticity is hidden what "unconsciousness" ("pure unconsciousness as such") means if it aims at the very possibility of action, its mode of being and finally the original essence of being as life. Henry corrects his earlier verdict: the dynamic unconscious is not only an ontic fall; it covertly names life as power, the "efficient" as the immanent.

How hard it is for thought to find a way out of the field of representedness and escape its power is seen in the 1915 essay "The Unconscious": no sooner is the drive recognized, as a "piece of activity", in its identity with the original forms of Energy and Force, as the wholly other of representation and so as the deepest feature of life and of the psyche itself, than its belonging to the psyche implies that it re-enters the field of representation (p. 363) to conform secretly to its structure and laws, as if the psyche were identical with representedness and drew its essence from it. This capital and catastrophic turn of Freud's problematic occurs with the institution of a dissociation between the drive and what represents it in the psyche, its psychical representative. This representative [*Repräsentanz*] is understood on the model of representation, being itself first a representation. The drive acquires psychical existence, becomes properly a psychical reality, only as this representative, only insofar as it takes on the mode of being of presenting something other than itself, the mode of being of representation as such. The *Vorstellungsrepräsentanz* is the key concept under attack.

Commentators have noted the ambiguity of the concept of drive, designating on one hand what is presented in the psyche, pure activity and the principle of all activity (which is finally what drive means in psychoanalysis), and on the other what accomplishes this presentation, presentation as such, in its essence as representative. But there is a reason for this equivocation that any radical problematic must expose: thought's powerlessness to grasp activity, power and force in themselves, and the substitution for their own essence, as soon as they are to be considered psychical, of that of representation. Representation takes back into itself what initially stood outside it. The unconscious, which originally means the other of representation, now bears representation within it. "Le concept aberrant de « représentation inconsciente » est né" (the aberrant concept of "unconscious representation" is born, p. 363). The "commentators" include Laplanche and Pontalis (*Vocabulaire de la psychanalyse*, "Représentant psychique") and Ricœur, named in note 58.

In this concept the two major errors of Freudianism meet. On one hand, one imagines there are unconscious representations because there are memories one is not currently thinking of, "latent" representations, or repressed representations: "the [unconscious] representation, once repressed, remains in the system *Ucs* as a real formation" (*Metapsychology*, GW X, 277). As if these representations were formed (p. 364), existed as effective representational contents independently of the act that forms them, of their formal reality. And as if the structure of ecstasis could have unfolded without the phenomenality it constitutes as such and by itself having been phenomenalized. The first error is the reservoir model criticized in Chapter II: a representation without its forming act and without its light.

On the other hand, the drive, which first means non-representability ("a drive can never become an object of consciousness"), existing psychically only through its representative, which is a representation, non-representability exists only in the form of representability. What cannot be transformed into the conscious in the sense of representation is nonetheless transformed into it, not even accidentally but in itself, in its being as psychical being, since the drive has being only in its psychical representative and so in its representation.
