The irreducible heterogeneity of the unconscious (here the drive) and the conscious, yet the slide of the first into the second through the drive's "representative" as its psychical being, the definition of the psychical unconscious by the structure of the representation it excludes ("but in the unconscious too the drive can be represented only by the representation"), the possibility then of the becoming-conscious of what refuses such becoming in principle, the justification, so to speak, of the whole psychoanalytic enterprise and notably its therapy: all this is contained in this key text: "I am in fact of the opinion that the antithesis of conscious and unconscious is not applicable to drives. A drive can never become an object of consciousness, only the representation that represents it. Even in the unconscious, moreover, a drive cannot be represented otherwise than by a representation. If the drive did not attach itself to a representation ... we could know nothing about it" (*The Unconscious*, § III, GW X, 275–276). This is the decisive Freudian text for Henry's whole book. The last sentence ("we could know nothing about it") shows that for Freud knowledge is representation; the drive is thereby made knowable at the cost of its essence.

Freud wanted to preserve the specificity of the psychical against any physico-biological reduction, but this is his greatest illusion. Insofar as the drive is psychically only the representative of somatic processes and, according to the teaching of the 1895 *Project for a Scientific Psychology* (p. 365), of physical energies, its psychical being, the being of the Psyche itself, is only the representative of something else, of something not psychical but a physical energetic system. So the psychical stands for a reality other than itself; it is the indication of this other reality; it has only a pseudo-autonomy, pseudo-specificity, pseudo-reality. The assertion that there is a psychical unconscious is acceptable only with this essential restriction: that the unconscious, the psychical at its ground, is only a "standing-for", an equivalent, a substitute, an ersatz. "Representative" makes the psyche the delegate of the body: Freud's anti-reductionism is defeated by his own concept.

Insofar, however, as this psychical being of the drive, of the unconscious and finally of the Psyche at its ground, is understood as a "representative", on the model of representation, it is secretly homogeneous with representation, with consciousness in the sense of representation. The unconscious, which is initially only the equivalent, the psychical transcription of a physical energetic system, in itself wholly foreign to pure consciousness, thereby acquires, through this function of representative that it assumes and because the essence of representedness inhabits this function, an affinity with consciousness itself, into which it can then be transformed. At a stroke Freud thinks he escapes the biological and grounds his method, since unconscious processes are posited as psychical and dissociated from physical states only by their resemblance to the modes of conscious life, which can then supply a key to understanding them, while the physical approach to these processes leads nowhere. Having declared that no chemical conception can give us an idea of their nature, Freud adds: "on the other hand, it is certain that they have abundant points of contact with conscious mental processes; with the help of a certain amount of work they can be transformed into, or replaced by, conscious mental processes, and all the categories we employ to describe conscious mental acts, such as ideas, purposes, resolutions and so on, can be applied to them. Indeed, we are obliged to say of some of these latent states (p. 366) that the only respect in which they differ from conscious ones is precisely the absence of consciousness" (GW X, 267). A note adds that this confirms that being conscious or not is only an added character of these "processes". Freud's own words: the unconscious is consciousness minus consciousness. This is the "representation with the light switched off" diagnosed in Chapter II.

So the original essence of the Psyche is missed twice: reduced to physical reality on one side, to representational consciousness on the other; more precisely, the first reduction is overcome only by the second, by the reduction of the psychical to the ecstatic. This second reduction governs the whole analysis. For the strange bridge thrown by the "representative" between the material processes of the energetic system it represents and the medium in which it represents them, the consciousness of classical philosophy and psychology, rests finally on the secret identity of the opposites it unites. And this identity lies in this: that the being of these material processes is only being-represented as such, consciousness itself. Matter and consciousness are identical in being objects: the physical world is the world of representation (Chapter V); so the "representative" bridges nothing.

The affinity, or rather identity, of material being and consciousness entails many consequences in Freud. First, becoming conscious receives in general the meaning of a realization. Force, energy and activity, whose inner possibility lies in the radical immanence of which Freud's unconscious was originally the figure, are on the contrary defined, in conformity with tradition, by a process of exteriorization. Not only is this becoming conscious the *telos* governing the whole doctrine, theoretical and practical, a becoming expressed in coming into being in the form of being-represented; action itself is exhausted in this coming and coincides with it. Proposing its general interpretation of morbidity, the *Introductory Lectures* declare: "the existence of the symptom presupposes that some mental process has not been carried through to its normal end so that it could become conscious. A violent opposition must have arisen against the mental process penetrating into consciousness"; and "the symptom is a substitute for something that (p. 367) repression prevented from being exteriorized" (GW XI, 303, 307). It is in relation to the process of exteriorization, within the ontological horizon of a metaphysics of representation, that this centrepiece of the doctrine, repression, is explicitly determined and understood. Freud's "normal end" of a mental process is becoming conscious: action is completed only by representation. Henry contrasts the "expression" (*Äußerung*) of a drive with its immanent accomplishment.

Deepening the concept of the unconscious, Freud spoke of "efficient unconscious thoughts" that determine it at its ground as Energy. As efficient, such "thoughts" tend toward their realization; they are tendencies, yet such that their true efficacy consists in surpassing this aspiration to reality (the status of mere "tendency") to throw themselves into reality, into exteriority, so that the process of exteriorization as such constitutes efficacy as such. Hence an insurmountable malaise, that of an unsatisfied tendency, qualifies the state of everything that, not having thrown itself into the light of ecstasis or being unable to, remains deprived of the efficacy of realization, the only one conceived as possible. "L'intuition enfouie au cœur du freudisme selon laquelle toute vie est malheur" (the intuition buried at the heart of Freudianism that all life is misery, p. 367) is wrested from its contingency as soon as it is referred to its ultimate phenomenological presuppositions: that the hither side of the world, the unconscious as such, is separated from reality and so is desire, endless desire. Beyond Nietzsche, Freud once again rejoins Schopenhauer. Freudian pessimism (*Civilization and Its Discontents*) is derived from the ontology: if reality means exteriority, the inner is by definition unrealized, i.e., desire.

The reduction of the basic modes of effective action to the process of exteriorization of representation thus leads to a representational, and ultimately fantasmatic, theory of desire whose error must be denounced. Desire is certainly accompanied by a train of representations. According to *The Interpretation of Dreams*, the experience of satisfaction of need is linked to the image of the object that allowed or procured it, so that "a mnemic image of a certain perception remains associated with the memory trace of the excitation produced by the need. As soon as this need arises again, a psychical impulse will, thanks to the link established, seek to re-cathect the mnemic image of that perception and re-evoke the perception itself (p. 368), that is, to re-establish the situation of the original satisfaction. An impulse of this kind is what we call a wish; the reappearance of the perception is the fulfilment of the wish" (ch. VII, GW II–III, 571, translation modified). So desire, which is nothing but need awakening and being fulfilled like need as a movement of the original body, in the sphere of immanence of absolute subjectivity, and whose "fulfilment" is and can only be that of this movement, the immanent passage from discomfort to pleasure, sees its being and history fundamentally modified: no longer the being and history of life itself as, experiencing itself, it experiences them, but a history of representations, the return of a memory, then of a perception, of all those that together make up the objective situation in which the primitive experience of satisfaction is supposed to have occurred. "Comme si la satisfaction en elle-même avait à voir avec une disposition objective quelconque, avec des représentations, des images" (as if satisfaction in itself had anything to do with any objective arrangement, with representations, images, p. 368). The *Traumdeutung* definition of the wish as the re-cathexis of a perception is the founding text of the "hallucinatory satisfaction of desire" that Laplanche and Lacan develop; Henry opposes to it the immanent passage from discomfort to pleasure.

With this displacement by which the drive of a desire, of a force, a real movement, is changed into a movement of representations, the historial situation already denounced in Leibniz is reconstructed, and need not be denounced again as such. A specific consequence occurs in Freud, however: the hyperdevelopment of the imaginary and with it of a fantasmatic and ultimately hallucinatory universe. From the moment one passes off as the realization of desire what in no way includes the moment of reality, a series of representations, this pseudo-realization can only reproduce itself endlessly; desire has bartered its being for a procession of symbols and imaginary fixations whose proliferation is offered to the equally indefinite play of analysis. "Il faut partir à la recherche du « moi » dans la forêt des signes, des allusions, des déguisements, dans un monde d'objets où il n'est jamais" (one must go in search of the "self" in the forest of signs, allusions and disguises, in a world of objects where it never is, p. 368). Leibniz's appetition (Chapter II) as passage from perception to perception is the precise model of the Freudian wish; the "interminable analysis" follows from it.

There remains the essential of Freud's thought: the drive's representative is not only the representation; it is also the affect. In this encounter (p. 369), at last, with the ground of the unconscious and of life, psychoanalysis will yield its secret. Its explicit presuppositions then waver, first of all the first: the dissociation of the Psyche and phenomenality. For affect is not only a representative of the drive in the same way and alongside representation; it is in reality its ground. All the great analyses of the doctrine, notably of repression, the vicissitudes of the drives, hysteria, and the analytic cure itself grasped in its ultimate possibility, will establish this primacy, implying that only the fate of the affect matters, while that of representations is constantly subordinate to it. "Or l'affect n'est pas inconscient et ne peut l'être" (now affect is not unconscious and cannot be, p. 369), nor therefore become so. Freud's declarations are categorical: "it is of the essence of a feeling that it should be perceived, that is, that it should become known to consciousness" (GW X, 276). And: "a representation can exist even if it is not perceived. A feeling, on the contrary, consists in perception itself" (note on Saussure's thesis, which criticized Freud on this point). "Ainsi le fond de l'inconscient n'est-il, en tant qu'affect, rien d'inconscient" (So the ground of the unconscious, as affect, is nothing unconscious). The book's decisive turn: Freud's own doctrine that affects cannot be unconscious, read with the primacy of affect, means that the ground of the psyche is phenomenal. Raymond de Saussure's 1922 thesis on the psychoanalytic method had Freud's preface.

The embarrassment surrounding such statements in a philosophy of the unconscious shows in the two passages cited from the 1915 essay and the note on Saussure, but Freud's clarifications, far from limiting the thesis, only underline it. Noting that in psychoanalytic practice one speaks of unconscious feelings, of hatred, of love, that one does not recoil before "the strange conjunction 'unconscious consciousness of guilt'" or "a paradoxical 'unconscious anxiety'", Freud sees in these expressions only improper language. It is the representation with which the feeling is associated that is or can be unconscious. Separated from it, the affect binds itself to another representation and is henceforth taken by consciousness for the manifestation of the latter: then it is called unconscious, when (p. 370) this designation suits only the representation to which it was originally bound. In this process, which is that of repression, the feeling has never ceased to be "known"; only its meaning, here the representation with which it was associated, has been "misrecognized". "It may happen that an affective or emotional impulse is perceived but misconstrued. Owing to the repression of its proper representative it has been forced to become connected with another representation and is now regarded by consciousness as the manifestation of that representation. If we restore the true connection, we call the original affective impulse 'unconscious', although its affect was never unconscious and only its representation had undergone repression" (GW X, 276). A note adds that the Saussure note likewise grants "the right to speak of unconscious feelings provided one remembers that it is an abbreviation". "Its affect was never unconscious" is the sentence on which Henry builds his reading of repression as a fate of affect (Chapter VI). False connection is a matter of representation; the affect is always truthful (as Frink's "always justified").

Repression is precisely the crucial experiment offered to us to decide whether a feeling can be unconscious. In the case just examined, only the representation is repressed and so becomes unconscious. But can repression not bear on the affect itself, and if the affect is repressed, does it not become unconscious? Before this limit-question of psychoanalysis, and perhaps of all philosophy, Freud's genius answered by citing a quite different process from that by which the conscious changes into the unconscious, a process that, as a structure of ecstatic phenomenality, indeed concerns only representation. What he describes is the process of affectivity itself in which, never ceasing to affect itself and so to appear, to be "known" as Freud says, it transforms itself according to the modes prescribed by its essence. On this history of affectivity as its historial, the historial of the absolute, of which we already know much, Freud's decisive contribution lies in bringing anxiety to the fore and the role assigned to it: whereas in repression the representation bound to the affect is pushed into unconsciousness, the affect is not suppressed but qualitatively modified, becoming (p. 371) this or that tonality. When these in turn are forbidden, repressed, they change into anxiety along with the original affect. The repression of affect is not its disappearance but its transformation: Henry reads Freud's three "vicissitudes" of affect (preserved, transformed, suppressed) as the historial of tonalities, with anxiety as their common point.

Repression therefore means no disappearance of the affect nor of the phenomenality that belongs to it in principle, but only its modalization into another affect and finally into anxiety. The movement of life is not interrupted, because its phenomenality is not either: what happens in this repression of affectivity is rather its declension according to its own potentialities, such that this declension inevitably ends in anxiety as their common point, their obligatory place of passage, "on est tenté de dire : à leur essence" (one is tempted to say: their essence, p. 371). Having studied the repression of a representation, the *Introductory Lectures* say: "as for the fate of the affective state associated with the repressed representation ... it consists in being transformed into anxiety, whatever its quality might have been under normal conditions. This transformation of the affective state is by far the most important part of the process of repression" (GW XI, 425). That anxiety, place of passage and end point of all affects, is thus like life's currency, the analysis of psychoneuroses, notably hysteria, says explicitly: "it is the affective state which, when repressed, is replaced by anxiety, whatever its own quality. Anxiety is therefore the universally current coinage for which any affective impulse is or can be exchanged if the representational content attached to it has been subjected to repression" (GW XI, 418–419). Before elucidating further this crucial phenomenon of anxiety in its connection with the historial of the Psyche, an objection must be removed. "The currency of all affects" was quoted in the prologue as the anxiety of life unable to escape itself. Here it is placed in Freud's first theory of anxiety (transformed libido).

As a mere "representative" of the drive too, is not affect, though more fundamental than representation and foreign to its light, like it something secondary (p. 372), a mere equivalent, the transcription of what, remaining in itself foreign to every form of manifestation, is nonetheless the ultimate naturing of all psychical reality and of affect itself? A note recalls the situation analysed in Schopenhauer, where affectivity would be only the effect of a more primitive *conatus* whose vicissitudes its tonalities reflect, and that Freud explicitly identified his "drives" with Schopenhauer's will. But the drive in turn is only a representative, that of the many excitations that constantly assail the Psyche, or rather some of them (a note quotes "Drives and their Vicissitudes": "'drive' appears to us as a concept on the frontier between the mental and the somatic, as the psychical representative of the stimuli originating within the organism", GW X, 214). Understanding Freud's thought requires taking up here the famous schema of the 1895 *Project*, which was never abandoned but determines the ultimate conceptions we now face, notably those of "Drives and their Vicissitudes" and *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*. In one sense this schema marks the extreme point of the alienation of the thought of existence, since it imposes its interpretation from the physical model of an energetic system governed by the law of entropy. But insofar as this so-called scientific model, built entirely of hypotheses, is only the unconscious translation of absolute phenomenological life itself in its deepest structures, far from determining it, it is rather its objective representation, whose features, referred to it, light up in its dazzling illumination. The method announced in the prologue is now applied: Freud's mythology of energy is the objective transcription of life's structures, to be read back into them.

The so-called "nervous system", or "organism", or "living tissue", is determined in its being by the capacity to receive two kinds of excitation: those from the external world and those originating in the organism itself, the double system φ and ψ of the *Project*. But this capacity, attributed to "neurones", to receive these two kinds of excitation is only the inscription in the organism of a double ontological receptivity: transcendental receptivity toward the world, the world's unfolding in ecstasis, on one hand (p. 373), and transcendental receptivity toward oneself, the self-receptivity of absolute subjectivity as living subjectivity, on the other, "excitation" being nothing but affection, pure manifestation itself according to the duality of the fundamental modes of its effective phenomenological accomplishment. That is why, and only why, a "without" corresponds to the first and a "within" to the second. The psycho-biological features attributed by the *Project* to φ and ψ (in Freud's text one slides from the physical to the biological and the psychical, which surreptitiously contains the form of all that precedes) are themselves only the crudely realistic translation, in the language of "science", of the structures determining the possibility of experience in general. The essential point is that, unlike external excitation, which one can escape by an appropriate motor reaction such as flight, "drive excitation ... comes from within the organism itself" (GW X, 212), so that the ego (between pages 14 and 35 of the French translation, the "organism" has become the "ego", the "individual being") "remains defenceless against drive excitations" (GW X, 226). That it is impossible to escape them results from "the drive never operating as a force giving a momentary impact but always as a constant force" (GW X, 212). Now the constancy of the excitation, that is, the constancy of affection, the impossibility of escaping it by flight, of developing a gap, a difference, the least distance from it, from oneself, the fact that, caught in itself and prisoner of itself, such affection as auto-affection qualifies nothing but absolute subjectivity and so, as immanent affection of self by self, the essence of ipseity and therefore the (p. 374) ego (no longer named from outside but implied in its innermost and most inalienable possibility), is said in the essay on repression: "in the case of the drive, flight is of no avail, for the ego cannot escape from itself" ("Repression", GW X, 248). The φ/ψ distinction becomes ecstasis/immanence; "the ego cannot escape from itself" is Freud's own formula of auto-affection.

"Drive", finally, does not designate in Freud a particular psychical impulse but the fact of impressing oneself without ever being able to escape oneself and, insofar as this self-impression is effective, the weight and burden of oneself. What is in this condition of being unable to rid itself of itself or suppress itself is need. "A better term than drive stimulus is 'need'; what removes the need is 'satisfaction'" (GW X, 212). The removal of need is only its transformation into another affective mode. The problematic of the drive, of which affect was only the representative, was to refer affectivity to a deeper substrate; it already appears that it leads back to affectivity, since, as self-impression, the drive finds its essence in affectivity itself. But let us go back a little. The drive is the burden of self: Henry's definition reverses Freud's derivation of affect from drive.

According to the *Project*, then, the model guiding the whole interpretation of the Psyche and determining it as "psychical apparatus" is that of a system of neurones cathected with quantities of energy from a double exogenous and endogenous source, such that the system tends to free itself of these quantities to return to the state Q = 0: the principle of inertia. This tendency seems realizable for exogenous excitations, because the energy they arouse can be used by the organism in its effort to flee them. Not so for endogenous excitations: they are not punctual but constant, and above all the possibility of distancing them no longer exists. According to the *Project*'s conceptualization there are therefore quantities of energy permanently cathected in the system ψ, an ego "perpetually cathected", that is, affection as auto-affection never ceases. In other (p. 375) words: "le système ne peut se débarrasser de ses quantités d'énergie parce que la vie ne peut se défaire de soi" (the system cannot rid itself of its quantities of energy because life cannot rid itself of itself, p. 375). That is why the principle of inertia changes into the principle of constance: because there is an inalienable "energy", so that the "system" can only claim to lower its level and not eliminate it entirely. The passage from the principle of inertia to that of constancy disguises and expresses, in the mythical language of science (of 1895), the structure of absolute subjectivity: "le système ψ est l'image de l'essence originelle de la Psyché" (the system ψ is the image of the original essence of the Psyche). The prologue's claim is now argued: inertia becoming constancy is the representation of auto-affection's inability to discharge itself.

Is affectivity now secondary to these quantities of energy that constitute or support the being of the drive? Or are such quantities in turn only the figuration of fundamental affective determinations? According to the explicit explanatory schema, it is the quantities of energy and the law they obey, the principle of constancy, that determine tonalities, since, the "nervous system" tending to "master excitations", to "reduce them to as low a level as possible" (GW X, 213), this lowering of tension is felt as pleasure, while its increase or maintenance at a high level causes unpleasure. But we know nothing of these quantities of energy or their supposed variations, still less of the regulation by which they determine tonalities. It is always from tonalities, in reality, from pleasure and the movement toward it, that we start. The "pleasure principle", this "fundamental intention ... inherent in the work of our psychical apparatus" (GW XI, 369), belongs to phenomenology; its explanation by the principle of constancy is only an added hypothesis. Only afterward does the text quoted, asking about the "condition" of this principle, declare that "it is related to the diminution, attenuation or extinction of the masses of excitation accumulated in the psychical apparatus". The *Metapsychology* proceeds no differently: "when ... we find that the activity of the psychical apparatus ... is subject to the pleasure principle, that is, (p. 376) is automatically regulated by feelings of the pleasure–unpleasure series, we can hardly reject the further hypothesis that these feelings reflect the manner in which the mastering of stimuli takes place ... we wish nevertheless to retain the highly indeterminate character of this hypothesis" (GW X, 214). The order of knowing is reversed: pleasure is given; quantities are inferred from it. Henry uses Freud's own caution ("highly indeterminate hypothesis").

Likewise it will appear that repression is directed not by flight from increased excitation but by flight from unpleasure, and indeed present, currently felt unpleasure, that is, that it is a process immanent to phenomenological life and identical with it: "repression has no other motive or purpose than the avoidance of unpleasure" (GW X, 256, translation modified). Repression poses a difficult theoretical problem because, satisfaction of a drive being always pleasure, one does not see why it is repressed. It can only be because of "some process by which the pleasure of satisfaction is changed into unpleasure" (GW X, 248). So the play of quantities and their variations, supposedly governed by the principle of constancy, comes down in reality to regulation by the pleasure–unpleasure series, a dialectic of affectivity itself, and is wholly absorbed into it. This confirms Chapter VI: repression is affectivity refusing itself; the energetic language adds nothing.

When, as was said, we feel in pleasure the diminution of excitation or in unpleasure its increase, the "excitation" in question is not something beyond pleasure, beyond the effective tonality; it is a word for its phenomenological content, in no way the excitation or quantity of energy cathected in neurones, of which we know nothing. It is by a grave abuse of language that the scientific explanation is integrated into experience and so seems demonstrated by it, as if it were indeed the neuronal energies that were felt directly and really in pleasure and unpleasure. "Excitation" is equivocal between felt tension and neural quantity; the equivocation is the whole credibility of the economic point of view.

Does Freud not assert the contrary? "We feel directly the increase or decrease of quantities of excitation in (p. 377) the series of feelings of tension." But this proposition, in which is concentrated the equivocation of problematics that have not been able to circumscribe the place where they move and, not having performed the reduction, confuse "physical" and "psychical" and mix their properties inextricably, comes precisely when, in 1924, in "The Economic Problem of Masochism", Freud abruptly abandons his thesis, tirelessly repeated since the *Project*, to recognize that pleasure can correspond to an increase in tension and unpleasure to a decrease: the physical content, the real neuronal energy that was supposed to decrease in pleasure and increase in pain, is thus out of play, together with the whole system built until then. What is taken into account is only a phenomenological excitation and tension, and it is of this excitation that Freud notes that it is present in pleasure, in that pleasure which consists in this excitation and coincides with it, as he notes that a feeling of slackening is present in pain, in the pain that coincides with such a feeling. Restored in full, the text says: "we feel directly the increase or decrease of quantities of excitation in the series of feelings of tension, and there can be no doubt that there are pleasurable tensions and unpleasurable relaxations" (GW XIII, 372). "En fin de compte, chez Freud lui-même, la phénoménologie fait s'écrouler le schéma spéculatif initial" (in the end, in Freud himself, phenomenology brings down the initial speculative schema, p. 377). The 1924 text on masochism is Henry's empirical refutation of the economic theory, from Freud's own pen: pleasurable tensions are possible, so pleasure is not discharge. Chapter VII's Nietzschean thesis (suffering and joy grow together) receives Freudian confirmation.

The interference of "scientific" and phenomenological discourse is expressed in the strange naming of affectivity as "quota of affect" (*Affektbetrag*). Quantity seems to designate this pure qualitative, affect as such, only because it really aims beyond it at the energetic state of the neuronal system, the quantities of excitation cathected in it, what the affect is supposed to represent. Leaving aside this mythical world-behind of the *Project*, does quantity, transposed onto the phenomenological plane (p. 378), still have a meaning? Here it is not any indifferent quantity but a "too much". When the energy charge of neurones gives way to the "charge of affect", this charge is affected by this too-much: it is "too heavy", the charge itself is this "too heavy", it is affect itself as charged with itself, bearing itself and unable to escape itself, as the essence of life. The "discharge" of neurones, the liquidation of the quantities of energy cathected in them, is in turn only the transposition, into science's ideal imagery, of this essence of life in its insurmountable passivity toward itself and of the movement by which it tries to escape what is oppressive in its being. "The true task of repression", "the liquidation of the charge of affect", the "processes of discharge" that make up the vicissitudes of drives as vicissitudes of affects (GW X, 277–278), express nothing but this "burden of existence" and existence's flight before it, before itself. This situation is expressed in turn by Freud's concept of anxiety. Quantity is phenomenologically the "too much" of self-suffering; "charge" (*Ladung*, *Besetzung*) is the weight of being given to oneself.

There is a superficial reading of Freudianism, largely responsible for its success, that reduces it to a sort of empirical history explaining man's destiny, the adult's from the child's, even the foetus's. Anxiety in particular has its source in infantile anxiety and finally in the trauma of birth, which it reproduces and repeats indefinitely. For the newborn, and then for the infant unable to provide for its own needs, there is a situation of helplessness in which a sudden influx of uncontrollable excitations is immediately translated into the psychical helplessness that is anxiety. But if we step back from this infantile anxiety that recurs in Freud's analyses as in life, we see that it is not a particular anxiety tied to determinate moments of an empirical history, childhood, but the model or prototype of true anxiety, or rather its essence (p. 379). What characterizes it is that it is not anxiety before a real external danger, before an object (*Realangst*), but before the drive. And the drive, especially libido, is provoked, we recall, by no external stimulus; it is an endogenous excitation, a self-excitation, life itself. That is why anxiety before the drive is precisely not anxiety *before* it, because then, as with fear before a foreign threat, it could turn away from it and flee. The prologue's critique of genetic explanation (Rank's birth trauma) is applied: infantile anxiety is the eidetic figure of anxiety as such.

Freud certainly often describes anxiety as flight from libido. The flight is even compared to flight from an object, so that the ego "behaves toward this internal danger as if it were an external danger" (GW XI, 420), the defensive measures here being symptom formation, against which anxiety will try, as it were, to barter its own existence and so destroy itself. Thus anxiety is in reality a flight from oneself, such that in this self-relation of anxiety there is precisely no "before" but the principled impossibility of unfolding any. The flight starts from anxiety; anxiety provokes it, wants to flee itself and, inhabiting the being of this flight it determines, never succeeds. More precisely, it is the feeling of being unable to escape itself of what is constituted in its essence by this impossibility. "L'angoisse est le sentiment de l'être en tant que la vie, elle est le sentiment du Soi" (anxiety is the feeling of being as life; it is the feeling of the Self, p. 379). Henry's definition takes up Kierkegaard and his own *L'essence de la manifestation*, § 70, against Heidegger's anxiety before nothingness and being-in-the-world.

In Freudian terms: life is the drive, the libido; anxiety is the feeling of libido, libido's self-experience not as this particular libido but as cornered by itself, in its inability to break the bond that ties it to itself, insofar as the experience of this inability is phenomenologically anxiety, this impression of narrowness and suffocation of which the child's coming into the world provides the empirical exemplification. Man is not capable of anxiety because he came into the world in the contractions of pain and the panic of asphyxia; he knew this panic and felt this pain (p. 380) only because he was capable of anxiety, because he was originally constituted in himself as a living being and a Self, as Freud declares literally: "the anxiety that signifies a flight of the ego from its libido is nevertheless generated by it. A person's libido is part of that person and cannot confront it as something external" (GW XI, 420). "Angst" from *angustiae* (narrowness): Henry's phenomenology of anxiety as constriction is etymological. Freud's sentence that libido "cannot confront [the person] as something external" states immanence.

It is not libido, strictly, that provokes anxiety, but unused libido. On one and the same page of Freud one reads that neurotic anxiety is not a secondary phenomenon, a special case of anxiety before an object: "we see, moreover, in the young child something behaving like anxiety that shares an essential feature with neurotic anxiety: its origin in unused libido"; "infantile anxiety closely resembles adults' neurotic anxiety; like it, it arises from unused libido"; "in the phobias things happen exactly as in infantile anxiety: unused libido is constantly transformed into an apparent anxiety before an object, and so the least external danger becomes a substitute for the demands of libido" (GW XI, 422–424, translation modified). Freud's first theory of anxiety (transformed, dammed-up libido) is the one Henry privileges over the second (anxiety as signal of the ego, 1926).

What is unused libido? Repressed libido. But repressed libido is not thereby put out of play; it does not fall outside experience, on the contrary, and here the theory of the repression of affect we have defended receives brilliant confirmation: repressed libido is libido whose self-experience is brought to its peak, to the point of becoming unbearable, to the degree of suffering of what, no longer able to bear itself, tries to flee and escape itself, so that anxiety is nothing but, within this suffering and its increase, the feeling of being unable to escape itself: "what facilitates the onset of a neurosis [and we have just been told it is anxiety] is the inability to bear for a longer or shorter time (p. 381) a fairly considerable repression of libido" (Henry's emphasis). Repressed libido is more felt, not less: repression intensifies self-experience. Chapter VI's thesis that the repressed persists as tonality is confirmed by Freud's dammed-up libido.

What is "used", spent, de-repressed libido, which finally frees and expresses itself? Insofar as unused, "accumulated" libido is libido that is there, that makes itself felt in all the parts of its being to the point of no longer being able to feel or bear itself, insofar, therefore, as on the ground of this essence of self-suffering it is nothing but life, to that extent the accomplishment of libido, figured in the liquidation of the energies cathected in the neuronal system and its tendency toward Q = 0, is in turn only the liquidation of life itself. "Le freudisme est le dernier jalon de cette histoire qui, contestant la définition de l'homme par la pensée, découvre au plus profond de lui la vie. Mais le freudisme n'a pris en compte la vie que pour la liquider" (Freudianism is the last milestone of the history which, contesting the definition of man by thought, discovers life at his deepest; but Freudianism took life into account only to liquidate it, p. 381). The meaning of the entropy of the *Project*'s initial speculative schema stands before us. A note adds that when posterity, what might be called popular Freudianism, took psychoanalysis for a liberation of sexuality, of the instincts and so of life, it did not know that to liberate here means to suppress. This is the book's central verdict on Freud: if libido's satisfaction is discharge to zero, liberating libido means abolishing life. The note targets the Freudo-Marxist reading (Reich, Marcuse).

Insofar as life, the essence of the Psyche, is the transcription, equivalent, representative of an energetic system tending to abolish the quantities that constitute it and so to suppress itself, it is itself only the movement of this self-destruction, the striving toward and aspiration to its own death. Phenomenological life in its deepest, affective determinations betrays this movement. Pleasure is the inner experience of this self-destruction in its accomplishment; its enjoyment is like life's secret consent to death, because death and the movement toward it are life's very essence: "everything living dies for internal reasons, returns to the inorganic: the aim of all life is death" (*Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, GW XIII, 40). The death drive follows from the entropic premise; Henry calls the conclusion what it is: life consenting to death in pleasure.

*Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, as is known, introduced a new drive, deeper than the pleasure principle or at least prior (p. 382) to its exercise, because the pleasure principle can operate only if the free energy of the organism has first been bound by a compulsion to repeat that aims to restore "the inertia of organic life" and ultimately the inorganic state: what underlies this compulsion is the death drive. As "an urge inherent in organic life to restore an earlier state of things" (GW XIII, 38), to maintain it at the lowest possible level of excitation, the death drive, far from opposing the pleasure principle or preceding it in the genesis of reality, is identical with it. The detour through the repetition compulsion and the precariousness of the theoretical means used to introduce the death drive were quite useless if the latter is only the reaffirmation of the presuppositions that have constantly guided the doctrine, if, over the whole work and bringing out its true meaning, *Beyond the Pleasure Principle* and the *Project for a Scientific Psychology* join hands. A note cites Ricœur's *De l'interprétation. Essai sur Freud* (1965), which asks whether the principle of constancy and the death instinct coincide, so that the death drive is not beyond the pleasure principle but in a sense identical with it. Henry praises this "remarkable work", "one of the rare philosophical approaches to Freudianism", but notes that Ricœur's presuppositions are radically different: for Ricœur the symbolic universe is the indispensable mediation of a self-knowledge that can only be hermeneutic, safeguarding the rights of intentional consciousness; affect has meaning only insofar as it binds itself to a representation (is it not itself a drive representative?); the concept of "representative", whose importance Ricœur showed especially in the *Metapsychology*, appears as the means of reintroducing Freudian energetics into a psychology essentially defined by representation. "Mais alors n'est-ce pas l'originalité d'une pensée de la vie qui est perdue ?" (But then is not the originality of a thought of life lost?) The note sets Henry's book against Ricœur's: same diagnosis (death drive = constancy), opposite conclusion (hermeneutics vs. life).

Against death, to maintain life at least provisionally and make there be something rather than nothing, remains Eros, whose merits *Beyond the Pleasure Principle* celebrates without transition: "Eros, which preserves and maintains everything living"; "Eros, whose function is to conserve and sustain life"; "Eros, which seeks to unite the parts of living substance and hold them together"; "Eros, which holds all living things together" (GW XIII, 56, 58, 66, 54; a note cites the *Outline*: "the aim of Eros is to establish ever greater unities and so to preserve them: binding"). How does Eros maintain life? If death consists in the progressive diminution of the quantities of energy cathected, in their liquidation, it can only be by their increase, by a sort of inversion in which the organism, instead of tending toward the inorganic, in a sort of start and revolt against its own law, opens itself to the irruption of new energies and consents to their increase, so that life is now this increase and no longer entropy, and Eros, in whom these living energies are exalted (p. 383), is opposed to the enjoyment complicit with death: "Eros contre le plaisir !" (Eros against pleasure!). The contradiction is exact: if pleasure is discharge, Eros (which increases tension) works against pleasure.

These enormous contradictions, this incoherence in which, in *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, every conceptual line is lost (the celebration of Eros, to be borrowed from Plato, is still preceded by the obsessive repetition of the entropic principle, of the hypothesis that "derives an instinct from the need to restore an earlier state"), Freud does not care about and no longer even notices. Speaking of the "renewal of life" that "is accomplished through the influx of new quantities of stimulus", he adds: "but this agrees very well with the hypothesis that the life process of the individual leads for internal reasons to an equalization of chemical tensions, that is, to death, whereas union with the living substance of a different individual increases those tensions, introducing what may be described as fresh 'vital differences' which must then be lived off" (GW XIII, 60, 62, Freud's emphasis). A note recalls that "The Economic Problem of Masochism" will recognize a pleasure linked to increased tensions. Henry shows Freud holding both entropy and anti-entropy as "agreeing very well".

Or rather, beyond these contradictions and oscillations, as their ultimate possibility and truth, is not what stands and unfolds its essence life, which is the less and the (p. 384) more, to which, within its very distress, it is given to experience its being more strongly, to be intoxicated and enjoy itself? "Le plaisir de mourir est une contradiction dans les termes, parce qu'il est une forme de la vie et lui appartient" (the pleasure of dying is a contradiction in terms, because it is a form of life and belongs to it, p. 384). Freud grasped of life only its dark ground, this place of first anxieties where, cornered by itself, it thinks first only of fleeing itself. On this path of self-liquidation he followed it to the end, recognizing of it only this atrocious face of the death drive, present from the 1895 *Project*. He did not see the meaning of these difficult beginnings: "que la douleur appartient à l'édification intérieure de l'être et le constitue, que cette naissance est une naissance transcendantale — que l'insupportable n'est pas dissociable de l'ivresse et y conduit" (that pain belongs to the inner building of being and constitutes it, that this birth is a transcendental birth, that the unbearable is inseparable from intoxication and leads to it). Henry's reply to the death drive is Nietzsche's (Chapter VII): suffering turns into joy; Freud stopped at the first half of the historial. "Transcendental birth" anticipates the theme of birth in *C'est moi la vérité* and *Incarnation*.

To sum up: "l'inconscient n'existe pas" (the unconscious does not exist, p. 384), if one sets aside the fact, the a priori law of all ecstatic phenomenality, that almost everything represented is excluded from representation. Outside representation this represented does not thereby subsist in the form of "unconscious representations", those entities for which Freudianism imagined fantastic destinies. The first conclusion is negative: the unconscious of representation is only the finitude of the horizon; there are no unconscious representations.

As for the unconscious that designates life, it cannot be reduced to the empty negation of the formal concept of phenomenality, if life is being's initial coming into itself in the form of affect, its self-growth, if, finally, the quantities of "excitation", their increase and decrease, are only the expression, in energetic imagery, of the fundamental pathos of this life. The second conclusion is positive: the unconscious of life is affect, and the economic quantities are its images.

One of Freud's decisive intuitions, which places him in the line of Schopenhauer and Nietzsche, is precisely that this pathos of life determines its representation and therefore repression (whose ultimate possibility we have shown not by recourse to the metapsychology's nested hypotheses but rather by rejecting them) as well as the return of the repressed. The best texts are those in which this primacy of affectivity shows, "this subordination of the intellect (p. 385) to life", which speak of tenacious memories "of offences and humiliations", which assert that the past need not be known in order to act, that in the return of pathogenic memories to consciousness the emotion revives before its representational content, that a "complex" is "a group of representational elements bound together and charged with affect" (*Five Lectures*, GW VIII, 30). A note adds: insofar as a complex is an affective support of representations bound by this support, it is nothing "unconscious" but a latent source of potential representations whose "thematic" unity is this source, the affect itself. Does not the cure itself show that the analysand's representation of his own situation, his conflicts and their history, is useless as long as the condition of this consciousness, a modification of life, has not occurred? The remark on the cure is the book's only positive statement about analytic practice: its efficacy lies in a change of affect (transference, abreaction), not in insight. The definition of the complex as affectively unified prepares "Potentiality".

That the unconscious does not escape every form of phenomenality but, as short of ecstasis, is the place of first appearing, its self-appearing as life and affectivity, Freud acknowledges despite himself when the *Metapsychology* declares decisively that "*Ucs* processes ... are subject to the pleasure principle" (GW X, 286); and when, at the end of his reflection on superstition, proposing a general theory of mythological, religious and metaphysical world views, *The Psychopathology of Everyday Life* explains them as an external projection of psychical reality and so as its unveiling before representational consciousness, such that this projection presupposes the obscure awareness of what it projects: "the obscure knowledge of psychical factors and facts of the unconscious (in other words, the endopsychic perception of these factors and facts) is mirrored ... in the construction of a supersensible reality, which science retransforms into a psychology of the unconscious" (ch. XII). To "obscure knowledge" (p. 386) a note adds: "not to be confused with true knowledge". But this "endopsychic perception", this "obscure knowledge ... of the unconscious", its affectivity, bring down the whole dogmatic apparatus of Freudianism; they designate it as a thought of life that was unable to rise to the level of its project. Does not a radically phenomenological determination of "psychical reality", however, leave some ultimate difficulties pending? "Endopsychic perception" is Freud's name for the self-revelation of life that the whole book has been describing; the projection of myths (Chapter VIII's Olympians) is life's "discharge" into images. The final question opens "Potentiality": if life is wholly self-revealed, how can it contain what is not actual (memories, habits, powers)?

## Potentiality

These difficulties appeared as soon as Descartes conceived his extraordinary project of defining man as the original essence of pure phenomenality, and this in turn as a sort of omni-phenomenality, an appearing bringing itself into itself, into appearing, at every point of its being, identical with this appearing itself. From that moment, as soon as the "soul" was regarded as this total self-exhibition of appearing and as absorbing the totality of its being into it, what part of this being could still escape "consciousness" so understood, not be "known" in this radical sense? The final section returns to the beginning, the Cartesian *cogito*, and to the objection Chapter II answered only partly: how can an absolutely self-revealed soul contain anything not actual?

Objections came from all sides. For unless this soul was conceived as emptiness, a translucent void, a nothingness transparent to itself, rejecting all diversity and positivity outside itself into the opacity of beings, one inevitably met the problem of the phenomenological effectiveness of the exhaustive totality of being as such, if being finds its essence in appearing and, more, in the immediate self-appearing of appearing in its totality. So Descartes, who conceived the soul not as this nothing or as an empty form but as the infinite richness and diversity of life, had to answer for this richness and diversity as regards their possibility of exhibiting themselves wholly in this self-exhibition declared identical with their being. The "translucent void" is Sartre's consciousness in *Being and Nothingness*; Henry's life is plenitude and must account for its own richness.

First came the critique (p. 388) of innate ideas, which were supposed to constitute the soul's richness, its "treasure". For if each drew the possibility of its being from appearing's initial self-appearing, they would all constantly appear to themselves, making our mind a sort of dazzling transcendental Milky Way of which we have hardly more than the concept. And the foetus in its mother's womb, once the soul was infused into it, did not possess, it was mocked, the marvellous idea of God on which Descartes would build his whole metaphysics. But it was, as we saw, the whole of conscious contents that fell under this critique, and nineteenth-century psychology, relayed by Freud, seeing only one of these contents in the light while all the others, memories we no longer think of, remained plunged in "latency", had to shatter this crystal of appearing of the Cartesian soul and put in its place the anti-essence of effective phenomenality: the Unconscious. The infant objection (Chapter I) and the latency argument (Chapter II) are recapitulated.

A radical phenomenology has shown the confusion from which these "objections" proceed. The ontological delimitation of the concept of the unconscious has rigorously dissociated the non-phenomenality belonging to the "world" as the finitude of its horizon from that of life. "Unconscious" meant two quite different things according as it referred to the darkness into which every conscious content inevitably sinks as soon as it leaves the "present" of intuition or evidence to become only a virtual representation, or to life itself as escaping by principle the light of ecstasis. This double reference occurs constantly in Freud, and we have shown how the mere latency of representational contents gives way to a deeper consideration that thematizes the unconscious in its connection with the original essence of life and the primitive mode of its immanent accomplishment: action, force, drive, Energy. Summary of Chapter IX's two unconsciouses.

The unconscious we have called that of representedness, insofar as it rests on the virtuality of most of our representations, presupposes in reality the belief in their real existence as discrete contents juxtaposed in an (p. 389) unconscious invented solely to receive them. The unconscious then appears as the ultimate illusion of a metaphysics of representation: the maintenance and persistence of the juxtaposed and the exposed as such, the ecstatic essence of phenomenality, in the absence of this phenomenality, in the absence of the phenomenological effectiveness that ecstasis constitutes in and by itself. The formula of Chapter II ("conscious and unconscious are the Same") is restated as the final verdict on the representational unconscious.

Descartes had denounced this mistake. What remains in the soul is not these representational contents in their ecstatic exposition and juxtaposition, but "the faculty of producing them". The soul is not a "storehouse of ideas" in which all these conscious entities, considered in their objective reality and subsisting as such, would be stored. The receptacle-unconscious of Bergson, Freud and the psychology of the time could be abandoned without loss to the fictional universe of clumsy speculative hypotheses or, if one prefers, to the crude realism of mythologies. The dispositional theory of Chapter II (Third Replies, *Notae in programma*) is reaffirmed.

But, as also shown, the displacement Descartes forces on us, considering the formal reality of the idea instead of its objective reality, can receive decisive meaning only if it is not merely the displacement from natured to naturing, from representational content to the power that produces it. With this thematic change, it is true, the idea of power, Force, already arises before us and offers itself to radical elucidation. Classical thought misses it because the power charged with forming representations and so accounting for their indefinite repetition, reproduction and recognition is precisely the power of representation, its production considered in and for itself, in a word, ecstatic consciousness. Such thought has only the light of ecstasis at its disposal when it inevitably meets the question of the phenomenological status of this power by which all representations are: since this status is precisely the same as that of the objective reality of ideas, the regression of ideas to their naturing, taking account of the power that forms them, has been useless. Moving from contents to the act that produces them does not help if the act is itself conceived as ecstatic (Husserl's noesis).

So one sees in Descartes' commentators (p. 390) and in Descartes himself the uncertainties about the phenomenological effectiveness of the soul's content repeated exactly when, leaving its infinitely varied objective reality, one considers the acts that produce it each time: these acts are accomplished only for a moment; the time of their actualization is identically that of their phenomenological actuality, their brief appearance in the field of consciousness, before they fall back, like the contents, into the night of unconsciousness. The finitude of the horizon applies to acts as well as contents, so long as acts are thought as appearing in the light.

These acts, it will be said, are only the actualization of the soul's various powers and faculties; it is these faculties that remain in it as its reality. But how and in what form? If total self-exhibition constitutes the essence of the soul and of all that belongs to it, all that is "in it", are not these faculties "absolute phenomena", wholly present to themselves at every instant, a transcendental Milky Way of the soul's powers this time, juxtaposed to that of its "innate ideas"? Obviously not. One must therefore admit that such faculties can be in the soul only potentially and that only their actualization, when it occurs, obeys the law of omni-phenomenality, in "phenomenological actuality". One forgets the crucial assertion that existence in potency is nothing: "the objective being of an idea cannot be produced by a being that exists only potentially, which strictly speaking is nothing, but only by a formal or actual being" (Third Meditation, AT IX, 37–38). Descartes' own principle (potency is nothing) blocks the Aristotelian solution.

But if potential existence is nothing and cannot produce the objective reality of an idea, how could it constitute the being of the faculty of which this production is only the exercise? That is why, in the very text where he grants the potential existence of the faculty, Descartes corrects himself with a more decisive assertion: the soul's possibility of seizing this faculty and using it at once, the principled possibility and essence of every power as such: "but it must be noted (p. 391) that we do have actual knowledge of the acts and operations of our mind, but not always of its faculties, except potentially; so that when we prepare to use some faculty, at once, if this faculty is in our mind, we acquire actual knowledge of it ..." (Fourth Replies, AT IX, 190, Henry's emphasis). "At once" (*tout aussitôt*) is the textual key: the faculty is available to the soul immediately, which means it is already self-given.

How, it will be asked, if the potential existence of the faculty means its unconsciousness, could the soul prepare to use it, form the idea of it? And if this idea came miraculously to mind, how could the soul recognize it as that of the faculty it needs and is about to use? Above all, how would this use be possible; how could the soul reach this faculty, this power, unite with it so as, one with it and with what it can do, do what it does and be what it is? The questions repeat the aporia of repression (Chapter VI): access to a power cannot depend on representing it.

Only a radical, that is, material, phenomenology, which does not designate appearing externally and formally but, considering its concrete accomplishment, can recognize the essential dichotomy of its effectuation, the materiality and phenomenological substance of pure phenomenality as such, can definitively overcome these uncertainties. For the shift from the objective reality of the idea to the power that produces it takes on ultimate meaning only for such a phenomenology, insofar as it leads us back from the ecstatic dimension of phenomenality and the finitude belonging to it by principle to the original seeming in which life is life, what experiences itself in the totality of its being consisting in this self-experience. And that is why, for one who can read in it the essential cleavage *videre*/*videor*, the *cogito*, after the reduction of the first, is exhausted in the second, and authorizes, or rather requires, this exhaustive phenomenological definition of "man" as a living being. The book's beginning (Chapter I) supplies its conclusion: the *videor*, not the *videre*, contains the powers.

The laws of the *videre*, of the finitude of the world, cannot therefore be applied to power; its "act", its "phenomenological (p. 392) actualization", is not its momentary, passing arrival in the light of evidence. Rather, power, every power, every force, every effective form of energy, never comes into this light. But this way of not coming and not being able to come into the light of ecstasis is precisely what makes them possible as such, as power, as force, as effective and efficacious form of energy. For if they displayed their being before me for even an instant (that of their arrival in the actuality of evidence), how could I join them? On the ground of what power would I set myself in motion toward them to try to seize them, if I did not already bear this power in me, if I did not coincide with it by the irresistible bond by which it coheres in itself as this Self that I am? "C'est en deçà du monde et jamais par son pouvoir que s'édifie intérieurement l'essence de tout pouvoir : il n'y a pas de forces naturelles" (it is short of the world and never by its power that the essence of every power builds itself inwardly: there are no natural forces, p. 392). "There are no natural forces" concludes the argument begun with Schopenhauer and Biran: force is known only from within, and what physics calls force is its representation.

Short of the world: in the invisible, in the radical immanence of absolute subjectivity. Freud says, in his mythical language: in the unconscious. So in Freud himself one was led from a first, superficial definition of the unconscious to another, more significant one. Considering the latency of virtual representations led only to their hypostasis in a realist unconscious which was in turn only the hypostasis of their ontological structure, the ecstatic-horizontal as such. With the critique of the classical theses for which virtuality (and unconsciousness as virtuality) would be synonymous with inefficacy and weakness, with the project of establishing the existence of the unconscious from its power, that of determining not only representations but behaviour itself, and not only pathological behaviour, with the assertion of an "efficient unconscious", Freud placed himself before the abyss in which the very essence of every possible power hides: its principled inability ever to come into the condition of the ob-jected and the object. The unconscious was no longer the metaphysical hypostasis of representation; it signified its dismissal. Thus, extending the radical effort of Schopenhauer and Nietzsche, issuing like them, in its historial provenance, from "la volonté de (p. 393) l'Être même de demeurer en soi et d'être la Vie" (the will of Being itself to remain in itself and be Life, p. 392), psychoanalysis, receiving the light of their decisive intuitions, became intelligible after them, within this genealogy whose difficult and painful path we have tried to retrace. The genealogy closes: psychoanalysis is placed in the lineage it belongs to, as the last avatar of life's withdrawal from the world, misnamed "unconscious".

To withdraw power from the light of ecstasis in order to protect its principled possibility is finally possible only for a thought that deliberately opposes to representation, to its ground and its forms, the effectiveness of a praxis. That is what happened when, taking up the brilliant discoveries of Maine de Biran, Schopenhauer placed the body at the centre of the debate. Not the represented body, to the exclusive and superficial consideration of which traditional philosophy had devoted itself, thereby depriving itself of any possibility of grasping the essence of power and so of any power whatever, the essence of the body in particular. Biran (*Essai sur les fondements de la psychologie*) is named as the source; Henry's own *Philosophie et phénoménologie du corps* is the background. "Praxis" recalls the *Marx* (1976).

The body is the set of our powers; its being is intelligible only from the essence of power. What must be set aside first is the idea of bodily acts, inevitably written in the plural. For if the body is considered in the exercise of its efficacious power, it is precisely not a bodily act that is at issue, which is only the representation of power, its coming into ecstasis where it is fragmented according to the categories of exposition and juxtaposition, where it becomes this plurality of acts in which it is said to actualize itself, while it de-realizes itself in them, in this illusory multiplicity that Schopenhauer recognized as the law of mere "appearance", insofar as in it reality, what he called the Will, the original essence of power, is lost. Acts are the representation of a power; the power is one.

At the Sanjusangen-dō in Kyoto, under the vast wooden roof, a thousand and one statues of Kannon Bosatsu, goddess of compassion, stand side by side, all different, it is said, and works of the greatest artists. Each statue has more than a thousand arms, of which one sees only twenty-one pairs, but these forty-two arms represent a thousand, because each saves twenty-five worlds. If one adds that the Bodhisattva Kannon can take thirty-three forms, the thousand and one effigies of the temple give us thirty-three thousand and thirty-three Kannon to see (p. 394). But it is always the same one. Because Kannon is power and so not an act but the infinite and indefinite possibility of giving and saving, such that this indefinite possibility, the essence of power, and it alone, gives each time, because it alone *can* give. The many acts of this power are only its representation by Indian, Chinese or Japanese imagery, and the senseless and oppressive proliferation of statues in the immense hall is only the formulation, by Asia's accumulative sensibility, of what hardly lends itself to this kind of exhibition. That is why the hall is too small and, before the crowd of goddesses with innumerable hands, the spectator's unease grows. The Kyoto image closes the book's Japanese frame (the Osaka seminars). The thousand arms are the represented multiplication of one power; the unease is the felt inadequacy of representation to power.

The multiplicity of acts and of their immediate means, here Kannon's hands, is a problem only for the thought that claims to hold and know everything in its own space; it is by turning our back on it that we must try to grasp power in its indefinite capacity for reproduction, its very essence. Consider one of our body's powers as power, for instance Kannon's principal attribute: the hand. Consider it not in its ecstatic appearance, in which this indefinite capacity for grasping finds its archaic figuration in the image of tens of thousands of juxtaposed hands, but in itself, as the subjective power of grasping: it is never an act, accomplished here or there, this movement locatable in space whose duration could also be identified, this act or that. And these acts are never the actualization of a power, that by which the power would acquire effectiveness by passing into act, by realizing itself in it. Rather, this act, all the possible acts of this power, are possible only in it; their ontological reality is never anything but the reality of this power; "leur substance est sa substance, leur chair est sa chair" (their substance is its substance, their flesh its flesh, p. 394). Henry reverses Aristotle: the act is not the realization of potency; potency is the reality of acts.

If power is described as the possibility of these acts, this possibility in turn must be understood: not as an ideal possibility from which reality never comes, but as the original ontological possibility that as such constitutes reality. Here, the original ontological possibility (p. 395) of grasping that constitutes the reality of the hand and finally the body itself as the fundamental "I can" that I am. "La possibilité ontologique constitutive de la réalité, nous l'appelons Potentialité" (the ontological possibility constitutive of reality we call Potentiality, p. 395). If the aim is finally to contest the existence of a primitive Unconscious by denying it the capacity to circumscribe in itself the place where every possible power is accomplished, everything that has the feature of effectiveness as desire and drive, then it is potentiality that is in question, more exactly its phenomenological status, which must be clearly established. The section's title concept is defined: potentiality is not unconscious virtuality but the self-given "I can" (Husserl's *Ich kann*, Biran's effort), constitutive of reality.

The potentiality in which our body's powers rest as in their essence is easily seen if, as is usually and rightly done, each of these powers is considered in its relation to the world to which it opens us each time. For the world is never given to a determinate or individuated act, this act of seeing or hearing, feeling or touching. Only what I have a principled possibility of access to is a world and constitutes itself as such: what I can see or hear, touch or feel as often as I wish, because I have the possibility of doing so, because each sense is a power and each of its accomplishments an accomplishment of this power, not an act come from who knows where whose possibility would be forever mysterious if it were not given to me first as this very possibility that I am. The world as correlate of an "I can" (Husserl's "and so on" of the horizon) is grounded in the self-givenness of power.

Things are never present to my body in an experience bearing the character of having to be unique, as what one will never see twice, but as what one can see in principle, as a term indefinitely evocable, on condition of a certain movement, because the principled capacity to accomplish this movement (of the eye, the hand) constitutes the very being of my body. When it seems to us, on the contrary, that we are seeing a landscape or a face we will never see again, this new meaning, which gives its tragic character to the world of human intersubjectivity and to the world itself insofar as we are only tourists passing through, this provisional and fleeting character of all experience is possible only on the ground of our permanent capacity of access to the world (p. 396) as constitutive of our being. The very idea of death, which is the representation of the global disappearance of all my body's powers, is only a negative determination of the general meaning of our experience of the world as that of this body, as experience of power. Death is thought as the negation of power; Henry's last word on the death drive: death presupposes the "I can" it negates.

But it is the phenomenological status of power, Potentiality, that can no longer be understood as the Ecstasis of a world or from it. Our body is the set of powers we have over the world; through all its senses it weaves the threads that bind us to it; it has eyes, ears, feet and hands. But the original hyper-power by which we seize each of these powers in order to set it to work, by which we can, as Descartes observed, prepare to use them when we wish, this hyper-power bears none of these powers in itself and is not accomplished through them. "Elle n'a pas besoin d'eux mais ce sont eux qui ont besoin d'elle" (it does not need them; they need it, p. 396). The "hyper-power" of Chapter VII (Nietzsche) returns, now identified with Descartes' "at once".

There is an original body, an Arch-Body in which such hyper-power resides and unfolds its essence as identical with it. The body has eyes, ears and hands, but the Arch-Body has neither eyes nor ears nor hands. And yet only through it are eyes and hands, the principled possibility of seeing and grasping, given to us, as what we are and as our body. "Ainsi sommes-nous toujours un peu plus, en réalité, que ce que nous sommes, plus que notre corps" (So we are always, in reality, a little more than what we are, more than our body). Material phenomenology is the radical theory of this "more" that Nietzsche thought as Will to Power and that is the hyper-power of Life. "La Volonté de Puissance est l'Archi-Corps en lequel notre corps parvient d'abord en soi comme tout ce qui est vivant et comme cela même qui est la vie" (the Will to Power is the Arch-Body in which our body first comes into itself as everything living and as life itself, p. 396). The "Arch-Body" (*Archi-Corps*) is the book's last coinage; it anticipates the "originary corporeity" of *Incarnation* (2000). The "more" is Nietzsche's *Mehr*.

Superficial thoughts are those of mediation. To know what we are, they always use a detour: either our true being is constituted only mediately, or the knowledge we can have of it is only mediate. To surprise the secret of our being, psychoanalysis abounded in this sense. The drive manifests itself only through its "acts", through the scattered set (p. 397) of the subject's unnoticed behaviours, representations and affects: so many indications offered to a hermeneutic reading. One must therefore move through a forest of symbols to try to locate the main lines along which the drive has tried to discharge itself, and life to rid itself of itself. To recapture this being shattered and dispersed through the ecstasis of time, there is no other means than to reconstruct it from scattered fragments, to conceive it as their sole law of intelligibility, but first to gather these fragments, to propose their exact recollection in memory: a difficult task if each memory hides another, if one must make one's way through the labyrinth of these screens. "Forest of symbols" (Baudelaire's "Correspondances") names the hermeneutic Freud of Ricœur and Lacan; the screen memories are Freud's *Deckerinnerungen*.

But the possibility of remembering in general is the precondition of this analysis: not only of the method proposed here as an instrument of knowledge, but of the very reality to be known, our being as the exposed and the shattered. And the possibility of remembering is Potentiality as such, our original and proper being, insofar as it belies everything just said of it. The precondition of anamnesis is a power of remembering, which is not itself a memory and is not dispersed.

Every thought that entrusts Being to the gathering of memory is thus prey to a contradiction. Memory joins the juxtaposed and dispersed by a sort of pre-established harmony; it is an "I think" that accompanies all our representations, drawing them one after another out of the virtuality that is nothing, that is the unconscious, to confer being on them in phenomenological actuality. But the possibility of memory is the problem, the power on which it ultimately rests. Memory is Kant's "I think" of Chapter IV turned diachronic; Henry's objection is the same: it presupposes what it cannot ground.

Consider our hand one last time as a radically subjective power of grasping. Because it is this power that grasps each time, not a discrete act separate from all others, and because what it grasps, the solid it seizes and whose edges it runs over, is accessible to it in principle, its knowledge of this solid, exhausted in this movement of grasping, is identically its recognition, the principle of the recognition of all possible objects and so our primitive memory of the world. And this insofar as this movement of grasping and running over is always the same, the accomplishment of a single power, which knows (p. 398) what it does and recognizes it as it knows itself, as it comes originally into itself in the hyper-power of its immanence. "L'essence de la puissance n'est donc pas l'Inconscient mais le premier apparaître, la venue en soi de la vie" (the essence of power is therefore not the Unconscious but first appearing, life's coming into itself, p. 398). The principle of memory is not representation but the Arch-Body in which hyper-power is effective and to which representational memory too belongs, as first a power. To entrust to memory the gathering of our being, of all these pieces of ourselves scattered in the absurd exteriority of ecstasis, of all these so-called traumatic events that mark the course of our existence, to sew up indefinitely the indefinitely broken thread of all these little stories, is to forget that this gathering has already been accomplished: "il est le Rassemblement intérieur originel en lequel réside l'essence de toute puissance et la mémoire elle-même, l'Archi-Révélation de l'Archi-Corps, l'éternelle étreinte avec soi de l'être et son pathos" (it is the original inner Gathering in which the essence of every power and memory itself reside, the Arch-Revelation of the Arch-Body, being's eternal embrace with itself and its pathos), and, before its illusory dispersion in the unreal exteriority of ecstasis, the very essence of our being. The book ends on the thesis that answers the analytic cure: the self is not to be reconstituted from the traces of its history, because it is already gathered in the self-givenness of life. "Gathering" (*Rassemblement*) answers Heidegger's *logos* as gathering (*Versammlung*), and the hand's "primitive memory of the world" (Biran's habit, Bergson's motor memory) replaces the Freudian archive. The "lost beginning" of the subtitle is here: not lost in the past, but forgotten in the present by every thought that seeks it in representation.
